Part: Basic algorithms (129)

1. Representation of numbers (21)

alg-f-001 · level facile · notes
What is the largest natural number that can be represented with $k=5$ digits in base $b=3$?
  1. $243$
  2. $242$
  3. $80$
  4. $15$
Explanation. With $k$ digits in base $b$ the largest number is $b^k-1=3^5-1=242$ (all digits equal to $b-1=2$); $3^5=243$ already needs $6$ digits.
alg-f-002 · level facile · notes
What is the minimum number of digits needed to write the natural number $n=1000$ in base $2$?
  1. $9$
  2. $11$
  3. $4$
  4. $10$
Explanation. The minimum number of digits is $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$: since $2^9=512\le 1000<1024=2^{10}$, we have $\lfloor\log_2 1000\rfloor=9$ and $10$ digits are needed.
alg-f-003 · level facile · notes
What is the representation interval of the integers in sign-magnitude with $k=8$ bits?
  1. $[-128,\,127]$
  2. $[-127,\,127]$
  3. $[-128,\,128]$
  4. $[0,\,255]$
Explanation. The magnitude uses $k-1=7$ bits, so it is at most $2^{7}-1=127$: the interval is $[-(2^{k-1}-1),\,2^{k-1}-1]=[-127,127]$. $[-128,127]$ is the one of two's complement.
alg-f-004 · level facile · notes
What is the representation interval of the integers in two's complement with $k=6$ bits?
  1. $[-31,\,31]$
  2. $[-31,\,32]$
  3. $[-32,\,31]$
  4. $[-64,\,63]$
Explanation. With $k$ bits the interval is $[-2^{k-1},\,2^{k-1}-1]=[-32,31]$: the $2^{5}$ sequences with most significant bit $1$ represent $-32,\dots,-1$, and zero has a unique representation.
alg-f-005 · level facile · notes
Which statement about the sign-magnitude representation with $k$ bits is correct?
  1. Zero has two representations, so the $2^k$ sequences represent only $2^k-1$ different integers.
  2. Zero has a unique representation, as in two's complement.
  3. Two integers are added as natural numbers, discarding the final carry.
  4. The most significant bit is $1$ for the non-negative integers.
Explanation. $(00\dots0)_2=+0$ and $(10\dots0)_2=-0$: two sequences for the same integer. Two's complement solves the problem (a unique zero) and allows adding as for natural numbers.
alg-f-006 · level media · notes
In IEEE 754 single precision ($1$ sign bit $s$, $8$ bits of biased exponent $E$, $23$ fraction bits $f$, normalized number) we have $s=0$, $E=(10000010)_2$ and $f=0100\dots0$. Which number is represented?
  1. $5$
  2. $2$
  3. $10$
  4. $20$
Explanation. $E=130$, hence $e=E-127=3$; the significand is $(1.01)_2=1.25$ and the number is $1.25\cdot 2^3=10$. Forgetting the implicit $1$ gives $0.25\cdot 8=2$; using $128$ as the bias gives $5$.
alg-f-007 · level facile · notes
In IEEE 754 single precision (significand $(1.f)_2$ with $23$ fraction bits $f$, exponent between $-126$ and $127$), what is the distance between $1$ and the next representable number (machine epsilon)?
  1. $2^{-23}$
  2. $2^{-24}$
  3. $2^{-126}$
  4. $2^{-127}$
Explanation. $1=(1.00\dots0)_2\cdot 2^0$; the next number sets to $1$ the last of the $23$ fraction bits, whose weight is $2^{-23}$. $2^{-126}$ is instead the smallest positive normalized number.
alg-f-008 · level teorema · notes
The two's complement representations with $k$ bits of $n_1$ and $n_2$ are added as natural numbers, keeping the $k$ least significant bits; there is an overflow when the result does not represent $n_1+n_2$. Which statement is correct?
  1. There is an overflow if and only if the sum produces a carry out of the most significant bit, which is discarded.
  2. There is an overflow if and only if $n_1$ and $n_2$ have the same sign and the sign bit of the result is the opposite one.
  3. There is an overflow if and only if $n_1$ and $n_2$ have different signs and the result has the sign of the larger in absolute value.
  4. There is never an overflow: the result always represents $n_1+n_2$, because the final carry is discarded.
Explanation. Overflow means $n_1+n_2\notin[-2^{k-1},2^{k-1}-1]$ (Observation 1.13). A discarded carry does not mean overflow: for instance $3+(-1)$ with $4$ bits discards a carry and correctly gives $2$. With different signs the sum lies between $n_1$ and $n_2$ and there is never an overflow.
alg-f-009 · level ipotesi · notes
Corollary 1.12 gives the minimum number of bits of an integer $n$ in two's complement ($\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+2$ if $n>0$, $\lceil\log_2|n|\rceil+1$ if $n<0$). What is needed to prove it?
  1. The binary digits of $|n|$ are $\lfloor\log_2|n|\rfloor+1$, and this number of bits is already enough for every integer $n\ne 0$.
  2. The result on addition: $n$ fits in $k$ bits if and only if $n+n$ gives no overflow with $k$ bits.
  3. The interval $[-(2^{k-1}-1),\,2^{k-1}-1]$ of sign-magnitude, which has the same number of bits for $n$ and $-n$.
  4. The minimum is the smallest $k$ with $-2^{k-1}\le n\le 2^{k-1}-1$; then the monotonicity of $\log_2$ and the definitions of $\lfloor\cdot\rfloor$ and $\lceil\cdot\rceil$.
Explanation. For $n>0$ the condition becomes $n<2^{k-1}$, i.e., $\log_2 n<k-1$; for $n<0$ it becomes $|n|\le 2^{k-1}$, i.e., $\log_2|n|\le k-1$: hence floor $+2$ and ceiling $+1$. The digits of $|n|$ alone forget the sign bit.
alg-f-010 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation 1.9 states that the two's complement representation of $n<0$ is obtained by inverting the bits of $|n|$ and adding $1$. What is the proof based on?
  1. Adding the representations of $n$ and of $|n|$ gives zero, discarding the carry out of the most significant bit.
  2. On the uniqueness of quotient and remainder in the successive divisions by $2$ that produce the digits of $|n|$.
  3. Inverting the bits of $|n|$ gives $(2^k-1)-|n|$ (geometric sum); adding $1$ gives $2^k+n$, i.e., the definition.
  4. Inverting the sign bit of the sign-magnitude representation of $n$ gives $-n$, and the $+1$ fixes the double zero.
Explanation. The proof is a direct computation: the inverted bits give $2^k-1-|n|$, plus $1$ gives $2^k-|n|=2^k+n$. One also checks that the result fits in $k$ bits, since $1\le|n|\le 2^{k-1}$.
alg-f-152 · level facile · notes
In positional notation with a fractional part, the digit $d_i$ in position $i$ (positive left of the point, negative right of it) weighs $d_i\,2^i$. Which number does $(110.101)_2$ represent?
  1. $6.101$
  2. $6.625$
  3. $6.3125$
  4. $3.625$
Explanation. $(110.101)_2=2^2+2^1+2^{-1}+2^{-3}=4+2+0.5+0.125=6.625$. Reading the digits after the point as decimal digits gives $6.101$; starting the weights from $2^{-2}$ instead of $2^{-1}$ gives $6.3125$.
alg-f-153 · level media · notes
In normalized binary scientific notation $x=(-1)^s\cdot(1.f_1f_2\dots)_2\cdot 2^e$, the sign is $s\in\{0,1\}$, the fraction $f=f_1f_2\dots$ consists of the bits after the point of the significand, and $e$ is the exponent. What are $s$, $f$ and $e$ for $x=-12.5$?
  1. $s=1$, $f=11001$, $e=3$
  2. $s=1$, $f=1001$, $e=4$
  3. $s=0$, $f=1001$, $e=3$
  4. $s=1$, $f=1001$, $e=3$
Explanation. $12.5=(1100.1)_2=(1.1001)_2\cdot 2^3$: the point moves $3$ positions, so $e=3$, and the fraction is $1001$ (the $1$ before the point is not part of $f$). The number is negative, so $s=1$.
alg-f-270 · level facile · notes
In a positional numeral system every natural number is a sequence of digits. What distinguishes it from a system like the Roman one?
  1. It uses only the digits $0$ and $1$
  2. Every digit always has the same value, wherever it is
  3. The contribution of a digit depends on its position in the sequence
  4. It cannot represent zero
Explanation. In positional notation the same digit weighs different $b^i$ according to its position $i$: in $(707)_{10}$ the two $7$s are worth $700$ and $7$. Binary is just the case with base $2$.
alg-f-271 · level teorema · notes
The base $b$ of a positional numeral system is the number of distinct digits it uses. Which statement is correct?
  1. It must be $b \ge 2$
  2. It can be $b=1$, the unary system
  3. It must be a power of $2$
  4. It must be $b \le 16$
Explanation. With a single digit the position carries no information and only the length counts: this is why the unary system is not positional. Bases like $3$, $10$ or $60$ are allowed.
alg-f-272 · level facile · notes
In sign-magnitude representation with $k=4$ bits the most significant bit is the sign ($0$ for $n \ge 0$, $1$ for $n<0$) and the other $3$ bits are the magnitude $|n|$ in binary. How is $-5$ represented?
  1. $1011$
  2. $0101$
  3. $1101$
  4. $1010$
Explanation. Sign $1$, magnitude $5=(101)_2$: $1101$. $1011$ is $-5$ in two's complement; $0101$ is $+5$.
alg-f-273 · level teorema · notes
In two's complement with $k$ bits the integers from $-2^{k-1}$ to $2^{k-1}-1$ are represented. What is the minimum number of bits to represent $n=-9$?
  1. $5$
  2. $4$
  3. $6$
  4. $3$
Explanation. With $k=4$ the interval is $[-8,7]$, which does not contain $-9$; with $k=5$ it is $[-16,15]$. So $k=5$; $4$ bits suffice for the magnitude $9$ in binary but not for the signed number.
alg-f-372 · level facile · notes
In two's complement representation with $k=8$ bits, which bit string represents the integer $-1$?
  1. $10000001$
  2. $11111111$
  3. $11111110$
  4. $10000000$
Explanation. In two's complement $n<0$ is written as the natural number $2^k+n=256-1=255=(11111111)_2$. $10000001$ is $-1$ in sign-magnitude, $11111110$ is just the bitwise complement of $1$ (without adding $1$), $10000000$ is $-128$.
alg-f-373 · level teorema · notes
In two's complement with $k$ bits one represents the integers from $-2^{k-1}$ to $2^{k-1}-1$. Why is the interval not symmetric, while in sign-magnitude it is?
  1. The sign bit is $1$ for positives, which takes one string from the negatives
  2. It uses $k+1$ bits, and the extra bit serves only the negatives
  3. The all-ones string represents no integer, like $-0$
  4. Zero has a single representation, so one more string is left for the negatives
Explanation. All $2^k$ strings are used: $2^{k-1}$ with first bit $0$ (from $0$ to $2^{k-1}-1$) and $2^{k-1}$ with first bit $1$ (from $-2^{k-1}$ to $-1$). In sign-magnitude zero has two representations ($+0$ and $-0$).
alg-f-374 · level media · notes
In two's complement with $k=4$ bits one adds as naturals $0110$ (that is $6$) and $0011$ (that is $3$), keeping the $4$ least significant bits. Which string is obtained and what does it represent?
  1. $1001$, which represents $-7$: overflow occurs
  2. $1001$, which represents $9$: no overflow
  3. $1001$, which represents $-1$: overflow occurs
  4. $0001$, which represents $1$: the carry is dropped
Explanation. $6+3=9=(1001)_2$. In two's complement with $4$ bits $1001$ is $9-16=-7$: the sum of two positives gives a negative, because $9>2^{3}-1=7$ is outside the interval $[-8,7]$. $-1$ is the sign-magnitude reading.
alg-f-375 · level facile · notes
In the IEEE 754 single-precision standard ($32$ bits: sign, biased exponent $E$, fractional part $f$), how many bits does the exponent have and which bias is subtracted from $E$ to get the exponent $e$?
  1. $8$ bits, and $e=E-128$
  2. $11$ bits, and $e=E-1023$
  3. $8$ bits, and $e=E-127$
  4. $7$ bits, and $e=E-63$
Explanation. Single precision: $1$ sign bit, $8$ exponent bits, $23$ fraction bits; the bias is $2^{7}-1=127$. $11$ bits and $1023$ are the double-precision values.
alg-f-376 · level facile · notes
In IEEE 754 single precision a number is $(-1)^s\cdot(1.f)_2\cdot 2^e$ with $23$ bits of fractional part $f$. Why is the decimal number $0.1$ not represented exactly?
  1. In base $2$ it has infinitely many digits after the point, and $f$ holds only $23$
  2. It is smaller than the smallest representable normalised number
  3. The exponent $e$ cannot be negative, so numbers below $1$ are lost
  4. More than $8$ bits are needed for the sign of a number below $1$
Explanation. $0.1=(0.000110011\ldots)_2$ is periodic in base $2$: it must be truncated (rounded) to $23$ fraction bits. The smallest normalised number is about $1.2\cdot 10^{-38}$, far below $0.1$, and the exponent can be negative.

2. Pseudo-code (36)

alg-f-011 · level facile · notes
In the pseudo-code of the notes, which statement about $:=$ and $\leftarrow$ is correct?
  1. A variable is declared with its initial value using $:=$ and later modified with $\leftarrow$.
  2. A variable is declared with $\leftarrow$ and modified with $:=$.
  3. $:=$ and $\leftarrow$ are synonyms and can be used interchangeably, also for variables not yet declared.
  4. $\leftarrow$ compares two values and returns true or false.
Explanation. The declaration operator is $:=$ (name and initial value); the assignment $\leftarrow$ evaluates the expression on the right and its value replaces the old value of a previously declared variable.
alg-f-012 · level facile · notes
What is the value of $\lfloor 17/5\rfloor+(17\bmod 5)$?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $5$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $17=3\cdot 5+2$: the floor of the quotient is $3$ and the remainder is $2$, $5$ in total. With the ceiling ($\lceil 17/5\rceil=4$) one would get $6$.
alg-f-013 · level facile · notes
How many iterations does the loop «for $i:=2$ to $2n$ step $2$» execute (with $n\ge 1$)?
  1. $n$
  2. $2n$
  3. $n+1$
  4. $2n-1$
Explanation. The variable $i$ takes the values $2,4,\dots,2n$, i.e., $n$ values: the step $2$ halves the number of iterations with respect to $i:=1$ to $2n$.
alg-f-014 · level facile · notes
What is the value of $sum$ at the end? <pre>sum := 0 <b>for</b> i := 1 <b>to</b> 10 <b>do</b> <b>if</b> i mod 3 = 0 <b>then</b> <b>continue</b> <b>end</b> sum ← sum + i <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $55$
  2. $18$
  3. $12$
  4. $37$
Explanation. continue skips the rest of the body for $i=3,6,9$: we add $1+2+\dots+10=55$ minus $3+6+9=18$, i.e., $37$.
alg-f-015 · level facile · notes
What is the value of $i$ at the end? <pre>sum := 0 i := 1 <b>while</b> i ≤ 10 <b>do</b> sum ← sum + i <b>if</b> sum > 12 <b>then</b> <b>break</b> <b>end</b> i ← i + 1 <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $6$
  2. $5$
  3. $4$
  4. $11$
Explanation. The partial sums are $1,3,6,10,15$: with $i=5$ we have $sum=15>12$ and break leaves the loop before the increment, hence $i=5$.
alg-f-016 · level facile · notes
We declare $s:=$ new set, then add to $s$ the even numbers from $1$ to $9$, add $4$ again and remove $5$. What is size$(s)$?
  1. $5$
  2. $3$
  3. $6$
  4. $4$
Explanation. $s=\{2,4,6,8\}$; adding an element already present and removing an absent element have no effect, so the cardinality stays $4$.
alg-f-017 · level facile · notes
Which of the following is the definition of algorithm?
  1. A sequence of unambiguous instructions that may not terminate, provided that every instruction can be executed.
  2. A finite sequence of unambiguous instructions that, starting from some data given as input, produces a result given as output in a finite number of steps.
  3. A program written in a programming language such as C++, Python or Java.
  4. A high-level description that mixes natural language and programming language-like structures.
Explanation. Finiteness, unambiguity and termination in a finite number of steps are part of the definition. The description mixing natural language and programming language-like structures is the definition of pseudo-code, which is a way to describe an algorithm.
alg-f-018 · level facile · notes
ReverseArray$(a,n)$ swaps $a[i]$ and $a[n-i+1]$ for $i:=1$ to $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$. How many swaps does it perform with $n=7$?
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $7$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $\lfloor 7/2\rfloor=3$: it swaps $a[1]\leftrightarrow a[7]$, $a[2]\leftrightarrow a[6]$, $a[3]\leftrightarrow a[5]$; the middle element $a[4]$ stays in place. Going up to $n$ would restore the original array.
alg-f-154 · level facile · notes
Which statement describes pseudo-code?
  1. A high-level description of an algorithm that mixes natural language and structures similar to those of programming languages.
  2. A language with a rigid, fixed syntax, which a compiler translates into machine code like a C++ program.
  3. A sequence of instructions that may also not terminate, provided each single instruction can be executed by the computer.
  4. The line-by-line translation of a Python program, with the keywords translated into English or Italian.
Explanation. Pseudo-code is meant to communicate the logic of an algorithm to humans, not to be executed: that is why it mixes natural language and constructs such as if, while, for, without the syntax of a specific language.
alg-f-155 · level facile · notes
In pseudo-code $:=$ declares a variable with its initial value and $\leftarrow$ assigns it a new value. What are $x$ and $y$ at the end? <pre>x := 3 y := 8 x ← y y ← x</pre>
  1. $x=8$, $y=3$
  2. $x=3$, $y=3$
  3. $x=8$, $y=8$
  4. $x=3$, $y=8$
Explanation. $x\leftarrow y$ overwrites the old value $3$ of $x$ with $8$; then $y\leftarrow x$ copies into $y$ the current value of $x$, i.e., $8$. To exchange the values a temporary variable is needed: $tmp:=x$, $x\leftarrow y$, $y\leftarrow tmp$.
alg-f-156 · level facile · notes
$\lfloor x\rfloor$ is the largest integer $\le x$, $\lceil x\rceil$ the smallest integer $\ge x$, $|x|$ the absolute value. What is $\lceil 7/2\rceil\cdot\lfloor 7/2\rfloor+|-3|+\max(-3,2)$?
  1. $17$
  2. $14$
  3. $12$
  4. $21$
Explanation. $\lceil 3.5\rceil=4$, $\lfloor 3.5\rfloor=3$, $|-3|=3$, $\max(-3,2)=2$: $4\cdot 3+3+2=17$. Using $3$ for both roundings gives $14$, and taking $-3$ as the maximum gives $12$.
alg-f-157 · level facile · notes
In pseudo-code arrays are indexed from $1$: $a:=$ new array$[1..5]$ has the components $a[1],\dots,a[5]$. What does $a$ contain at the end? <pre>a := new array[1..5] <b>for</b> i := 1 <b>to</b> 5 <b>do</b> a[i] ← 2 · i <b>end</b> a[3] ← a[2] + a[4]</pre>
  1. $[2, 4, 6, 16, 10]$
  2. $[2, 12, 6, 8, 10]$
  3. $[2, 4, 12, 8, 10]$
  4. $[2, 4, 10, 8, 10]$
Explanation. After the loop $a=[2,4,6,8,10]$; then $a[3]\leftarrow a[2]+a[4]=4+8=12$. Counting the indices from $0$ leads to adding $6+10$ and writing into the fourth component, i.e., $[2,4,6,16,10]$.
alg-f-158 · level facile · notes
In pseudo-code $b[i][j]$ is the component in row $i$ and column $j$ of a two-dimensional array, with indices from $1$. What is $b[2][3]$ at the end? <pre>b := new array[1..3][1..4] <b>for</b> i := 1 <b>to</b> 3 <b>do</b> <b>for</b> j := 1 <b>to</b> 4 <b>do</b> b[i][j] ← 4 · (i − 1) + j <b>end</b> <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $10$
  2. $11$
  3. $6$
  4. $7$
Explanation. $b[2][3]=4\cdot(2-1)+3=7$: the value numbers the components in the order in which they are visited, row by row. Swapping row and column gives $4\cdot 2+2=10$; forgetting the $-1$ gives $11$.
alg-f-159 · level facile · notes
The function IsSorted$(a,n)$ executes, for $i:=1$ to $n-1$, «<b>if</b> $a[i]>a[i+1]$ <b>then return</b> false», and after the loop «<b>return</b> true». On $a=[1,3,3,7,5,9,2]$, how many comparisons $a[i]>a[i+1]$ does it perform and what does it return?
  1. $6$ comparisons, returns false
  2. $4$ comparisons, returns false
  3. $4$ comparisons, returns true
  4. $2$ comparisons, returns false
Explanation. The comparisons for $i=1,2,3$ ($1>3$, $3>3$, $3>7$) are false; for $i=4$ we have $7>5$ and the return statement terminates the function at once. The equal pair $3,3$ does not violate the non-decreasing order.
alg-f-160 · level facile · notes
In pseudo-code a set contains distinct elements: add $x$ to $s$ is ignored if $x$ is already present, remove $x$ from $s$ if $x$ is absent, and foreach visits the elements in insertion order. After $s:=$ new set, add $5$, add $2$, add $8$, add $2$, remove $7$, add $1$ (all on $s$), what does «<b>foreach</b> $x$ <b>in</b> $s$ <b>do</b> print$(x)$» print?
  1. $1, 2, 5, 8$
  2. $5, 2, 8, 2, 1$
  3. $5, 2, 8, 1$
  4. Nothing: remove $7$ gives an error, because $7$ does not belong to $s$
Explanation. The second add $2$ and remove $7$ leave $s=\{5,2,8\}$ unchanged; then $1$ is added. By convention foreach follows the insertion order: $5,2,8,1$. Increasing order is obtained only with «sorted by».
alg-f-161 · level facile · notes
Another text writes $b_{ij}$ for the component in row $i$ and column $j$ of a matrix and uses $=$ both to declare and to assign. How is the same written in the style conventions of the pseudo-code of the notes?
  1. $b[i][j]$; $:=$ for the declaration and $\leftarrow$ for the assignment
  2. $b[i,j]$; $\leftarrow$ both for the declaration and for the assignment
  3. $b_{ij}$; $:=$ both for the declaration and for the assignment
  4. $b[j][i]$; $\leftarrow$ for the declaration and $:=$ for the assignment
Explanation. The notes use $a[i]$ and $b[i][j]$ (row, then column), declare with $:=$ and assign with $\leftarrow$. The other choices are legitimate in other texts: what matters is consistency.
alg-f-162 · level facile · notes
An author of pseudo-code writes $a_i$ for the component of index $i$ of an array and puts comments after #. What do the style conventions of the notes say about it?
  1. They are errors: pseudo-code has a fixed syntax, so one must always write $a[i]$ and // for comments.
  2. $a_i$ is legitimate, but comments are forbidden: pseudo-code must explain itself, without notes.
  3. They are legitimate choices if used consistently; the notes write $a[i]$ and comments after //.
  4. They are legitimate only if $=$ is also used to declare, because the conventions must all come from the same text.
Explanation. The notes list the common variants ($:=$ or $=$, $\leftarrow$ or $=$, $a[i]$ or $a_i$, comments with //, # or (* *)) and conclude that what matters is being consistent and clear.
alg-f-274 · level facile · notes
What is pseudo-code, in the algorithms course?
  1. A compiled programming language
  2. The machine code produced by a compiler
  3. A description fixing the type and the bit size of every variable
  4. A high-level description of an algorithm, meant for people
Explanation. Pseudo-code conveys the idea of the algorithm without the details of a real language; it is not run on a computer, but translated afterwards into C++, Python or Java.
alg-f-377 · level facile · notes
An algorithm is a finite sequence of unambiguous instructions that produces the output in a finite number of steps. Which of the following procedures is NOT an algorithm?
  1. Finding the maximum of an array by scanning it once
  2. Converting $n$ to base $2$ by repeated division by $2$
  3. Repeating «$x\leftarrow x+1$» with no stopping condition
  4. Trying the divisors $d=2,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ of $n$
Explanation. The first procedure never terminates, so it produces no result in a finite number of steps. The other three terminate on every input.
alg-f-378 · level facile · notes
In the pseudocode of the notes a variable is declared with $:=$ (name and initial value) and modified with $\leftarrow$. Which fragment follows these rules?
  1. $sum \leftarrow sum + 5$, without declaring $sum$ first
  2. $sum := 0$, then $sum := sum + 5$
  3. $0 := sum$, then $sum \leftarrow 5$
  4. $sum := 0$, then $sum \leftarrow sum + 5$
Explanation. A variable is declared once, before use, with its initial value; every later change is an assignment with $\leftarrow$. The name, not the value, stands to the left of $:=$.
alg-f-379 · level facile · notes
To find the maximum of $a[1..n]$ one declares $max := -\infty$ and then, for $i:=1$ to $n$, runs $max\leftarrow a[i]$ if $a[i]>max$. Why the initial value $-\infty$?
  1. Because $0$ is not an allowed value in a declaration
  2. So the first comparison always updates $max$, whatever $a[1]$ is
  3. Because every variable not yet assigned is $-\infty$
  4. Because then the loop can safely start at $i:=2$
Explanation. Every $a[1]$ is greater than $-\infty$, so after the first iteration $max=a[1]$. With $max:=0$ the result would be wrong on an all-negative array; starting at $i:=2$ requires $max:=a[1]$ instead.
alg-f-380 · level facile · notes
In pseudocode ($:=$ declares, $\leftarrow$ assigns: the right-hand side is evaluated first) one runs <pre>a := 4 b := a + 1 a ← 2 · b b ← a − b</pre> What are $a$ and $b$ at the end?
  1. $a=10$, $b=-5$
  2. $a=8$, $b=4$
  3. $a=10$, $b=5$
  4. $a=10$, $b=6$
Explanation. $b=5$, then $a=2\cdot 5=10$, then $b=10-5=5$. Assignment uses the current values: after the third line $a$ is already $10$.
alg-f-381 · level facile · notes
$\lceil x\rceil$ is the smallest integer $\ge x$ and $\lfloor x\rfloor$ the largest integer $\le x$. What is $\lceil 17/4\rceil-\lfloor -7/2\rfloor$?
  1. $9$
  2. $8$
  3. $7$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\lceil 4.25\rceil=5$ and $\lfloor -3.5\rfloor=-4$ (the largest integer less than or equal to $-3.5$), so $5-(-4)=9$. Rounding $-3.5$ towards zero ($-3$) gives $8$.
alg-f-382 · level facile · notes
In pseudocode $a \bmod b$ is the remainder of the integer division of $a$ by $b$. Which expression is true if and only if the integer $i$ is even and a multiple of $3$?
  1. $(i \bmod 2 = 0)$ or $(i \bmod 3 = 0)$
  2. $(i / 2 = 0)$ and $(i / 3 = 0)$
  3. $(i \bmod 2 = 0)$ and $(i \bmod 3 = 0)$
  4. $(i \bmod 2 = 1)$ and $(i \bmod 3 = 0)$
Explanation. «Even» is $i\bmod 2=0$, «multiple of $3$» is $i\bmod 3=0$, and both must hold: and. With or one of them suffices; $i/2$ is the division, not the remainder.
alg-f-383 · level facile · notes
What is the value of $y$ at the end, with $x=10$? <pre>y := 0 if x > 10 then y ← 1 else if x > 5 then y ← 2 else y ← 3 end end</pre>
  1. $2$
  2. $1$
  3. $3$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $x>10$ is false for $x=10$ (strict inequality), so the else branch runs; there $x>5$ is true and $y\leftarrow 2$.
alg-f-384 · level teorema · notes
Two pseudocode fragments are compared, with a condition $c$ and two blocks $S_1$, $S_2$. A: «if $c$ then $S_1$ end» followed by «if not $c$ then $S_2$ end». B: «if $c$ then $S_1$ else $S_2$ end». When can they behave differently?
  1. Never: the two fragments are always equivalent
  2. Whenever $c$ is false at the start
  3. Only when $S_2$ contains a while loop
  4. When $S_1$ modifies the variables $c$ depends on
Explanation. In B the condition is evaluated once. In A it is re-evaluated after $S_1$: if $S_1$ makes $c$ false, A also runs $S_2$. If $c$ is false at the start, both run only $S_2$.
alg-f-385 · level facile · notes
The loop «for $i := s$ to $e$ do … end» is equivalent to «$i := s$; while $i \le e$ do … $i \leftarrow i+1$ end». With $s=5$ and $e=3$, how many times does the body run and how many times is the condition $i\le e$ evaluated?
  1. The body $0$ times, the condition $0$ times
  2. The body $0$ times, the condition $1$ time
  3. The body $3$ times (with $i=5,4,3$)
  4. The body $1$ time, the condition $2$ times
Explanation. The condition $5\le 3$ is false at the first check, so the body never runs; the for loop does not count down without a negative step.
alg-f-386 · level facile · notes
One declares $b := $ new array$[1..3][1..4]$ (rows $1$ to $3$, columns $1$ to $4$) and sets $b[i][j]\leftarrow i\cdot j$ for every $i$ and $j$. What is the sum of row $2$, that is $b[2][1]+\dots+b[2][4]$?
  1. $12$
  2. $30$
  3. $10$
  4. $20$
Explanation. Row $2$ is $2,4,6,8$, with sum $20$. $12$ is the sum of column $2$ ($2+4+6$), $10$ the sum of the column indices alone.
alg-f-387 · level facile · notes
One declares $t := $ new array$[1..2][1..3][1..4]$ and initialises it to $0$ with three nested for loops, one per dimension. How many components does $t$ have, and how many times does the assignment $t[i][j][k]\leftarrow 0$ run?
  1. $9$ components, $24$ assignments
  2. $24$ components, $24$ assignments
  3. $24$ components, $9$ assignments
  4. $9$ components, $9$ assignments
Explanation. There are $2\cdot 3\cdot 4=24$ components, and the nested loops visit every triple $(i,j,k)$ once. $9=2+3+4$ adds the dimensions instead of multiplying them.
alg-f-388 · level facile · notes
In the pseudocode of the notes a multidimensional array is declared with $t := $ new array$[1..p][1..q][1..r]$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The declaration assigns no values: every component must be initialised
  2. The declaration automatically sets every component to $0$
  3. Indices start at $0$, so $t[p][q][r]$ does not exist
  4. At most three dimensions can be declared, like $p$, $q$, $r$
Explanation. new array creates the variables but gives them no value: nested for loops initialise them all. In the notes indices start at $1$, and there may be any number $k$ of dimensions.
alg-f-389 · level facile · notes
CountPositive$(a,n)$ sets $count:=0$ and, for $i:=1$ to $n$, runs $count\leftarrow count+1$ if $a[i]>0$; then it returns $count$. What does it return on $a=[0,-1,3,0,5]$, $n=5$?
  1. $4$
  2. $3$
  3. $2$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Only $3$ and $5$ are greater than $0$. $4$ also counts the zeros (that is, it uses $a[i]\ge 0$).
alg-f-390 · level facile · notes
In a pseudocode function, the return statement appears inside a for loop (as in IsSorted, which returns false at the first $a[i]>a[i+1]$). What happens when it is executed?
  1. Only the loop ends, and execution continues after its end
  2. Execution moves to the next iteration, as with continue
  3. The value is stored and returned at the end of the loop
  4. The whole function ends at once, returning the value
Explanation. return ends the whole function. This is what lets IsSorted stop after a single comparison on $[3,1,4,2]$.
alg-f-391 · level facile · notes
In pseudocode a set ($s := $ new set) is an unordered collection of distinct elements. Which operation can NOT be done on $s$?
  1. Checking whether $x$ in $s$ (membership)
  2. Reading $s[2]$, the second element
  3. Computing size$(s)$, the cardinality
  4. Running remove $x$ from $s$
Explanation. The elements of a set have no positions, so there is no indexing. The operations are add, remove, in, size and foreach.
alg-f-392 · level facile · notes
A function declares $s := $ new set, runs add $a[i]$ to $s$ for $i:=1$ to $n$ (add is ignored if the element is already present) and returns size$(s)$. What does it return with $a=[4,7,4,4,2,7]$, $n=6$?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. $3$
Explanation. The final set is $\{4,7,2\}$: the function counts the distinct values. $6$ also counts the duplicates.
alg-f-393 · level facile · notes
In the notes the reserved words of pseudocode (if, while, for, return, …) are written in bold. What is this convention for?
  1. To let the computer execute the pseudocode
  2. To tell them apart at a glance from variable and function names
  3. To mark the only instructions that cost running time
  4. To indicate the lines that must be commented
Explanation. Pseudocode is written for people: bold type makes the structure (constructs and keywords) stand out from the data.
alg-f-394 · level facile · notes
A pseudocode fragment uses the variables $x_1$, $x_2$, $x_3$ for a sum, a counter and a maximum, and does not indent loop bodies. Which good style practices of the notes does it break?
  1. None: pseudocode has no style rules
  2. Only the bold type for the reserved words
  3. Descriptive variable names and consistent indentation
  4. Only line numbering, which is mandatory
Explanation. The notes ask for clear names ($sum$, $count$, $max$) and indentation that shows nested blocks; line numbers are useful but not mandatory.

3. Sorting algorithms (26)

alg-f-019 · level facile · notes
When is a sorting algorithm called stable?
  1. When the extra memory it uses is constant, i.e., it does not grow with $n$.
  2. When it performs the same number of comparisons on all inputs of size $n$.
  3. When records with equal keys appear in the output in the same relative order as in the input.
  4. When it never moves an element that is already in its final position.
Explanation. This is Definition 3.4. Constant extra memory defines instead in-place sorting (Definition 3.3).
alg-f-020 · level facile · notes
InsertionSort, for $i=2,\dots,n$, inserts $\mathit{key}=a[i]$ into the sorted prefix $a[1..i-1]$: while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ it shifts $a[j]$ into $a[j+1]$. How many comparisons $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ and how many shifts does it perform on an array of $n$ elements already sorted in non-decreasing order?
  1. $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons and no shift
  2. $n-1$ comparisons and no shift
  3. $n-1$ comparisons and $n-1$ shifts
  4. No comparison and no shift
Explanation. In each iteration $i=2,\dots,n$ the first comparison $a[i-1]>\mathit{key}$ is false and stops the inner loop: one comparison per iteration, no shift. This is the best case of Observation 3.5.
alg-f-021 · level facile · notes
SelectionSort, for $i=1,\dots,n-1$, swaps $a[i]$ with the minimum of $a[i..n]$ (if different); ExchangeSort, for every pair $i<j$, swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ if $a[i]>a[j]$. With $n=6$, what is the maximum number of swaps of each of them?
  1. $15$ for both
  2. $5$ for both
  3. $15$ for SelectionSort, $5$ for ExchangeSort
  4. $5$ for SelectionSort, $15$ for ExchangeSort
Explanation. SelectionSort performs at most one swap per iteration of the outer loop, hence $n-1=5$; ExchangeSort can swap at every comparison, up to $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}=15$ (decreasing array with distinct keys).
alg-f-022 · level media · notes
InsertionSort, for $i=2,\dots,n$, sets $\mathit{key}=a[i]$, $j=i-1$ and, while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$, shifts $a[j]$ into $a[j+1]$ and decrements $j$ (with $j=0$ it exits with no comparison). How many comparisons $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ and how many shifts does it perform on $[6,5,4,3,2,1]$?
  1. $15$ comparisons and $5$ shifts
  2. $15$ comparisons and $15$ shifts
  3. $5$ comparisons and $15$ shifts
  4. $21$ comparisons and $15$ shifts
Explanation. With distinct keys in decreasing order each $\mathit{key}$ moves to the first position: iteration $i$ makes $i-1$ shifts and $i-1$ comparisons (it exits with $j=0$ without a further comparison). In total $1+2+3+4+5=15$ and $15$.
alg-f-023 · level facile · notes
SelectionSort, for $i=1,\dots,n-1$, looks for the minimum of $a[i..n]$ comparing $a[j]<a[\mathit{minIdx}]$ for $j=i+1,\dots,n$, then swaps it with $a[i]$. How many key comparisons does it perform on an array of $n=10$ elements?
  1. From $9$ to $45$, depending on the input
  2. Exactly $9$
  3. Exactly $90$
  4. Exactly $45$, on every input
Explanation. For each $i=1,\dots,n-1$ the inner loop compares $a[j]$ with $a[\mathit{minIdx}]$ for $j=i+1,\dots,n$: $\sum_{i=1}^{9}(10-i)=45$ comparisons, independently of the input.
alg-f-024 · level teorema · notes
A sort is in-place if it uses constant extra memory, stable if it preserves the relative order of records with equal keys. Which statement about Insertion Sort, Exchange Sort and Selection Sort is correct?
  1. Insertion Sort is in-place and stable; Exchange Sort and Selection Sort are in-place but not stable.
  2. All three are in-place and stable.
  3. Insertion Sort and Selection Sort are stable, Exchange Sort is not.
  4. Insertion Sort is stable but not in-place, because it shifts the elements.
Explanation. Observations 3.6, 3.8 and 3.10: all of them use a constant number of variables besides the array; only Insertion Sort never moves a key past an equal key.
alg-f-025 · level ipotesi · notes
Insertion Sort inserts $\mathit{key}=a[i]$ into the prefix $a[1..i-1]$ shifting to the right the keys $a[j]>\mathit{key}$. Which property is the proof that it is stable (preserves the relative order of equal keys) based on?
  1. On the fact that the outer loop starts from $i=2$: the prefix $a[1..1]$ is already sorted and no key jumps over it.
  2. On correctness: an array sorted in non-decreasing order always preserves the relative order of equal keys.
  3. On the strict comparison $a[j]>\mathit{key}$: $\mathit{key}$ jumps only over strictly greater keys, never over an equal key that precedes it.
  4. On the number of shifts, at most $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$, one for each pair of keys in reverse order.
Explanation. With $a[j]\ge\mathit{key}$ as condition the algorithm would still be correct but not stable. Correctness alone is not enough: Selection Sort also sorts correctly without being stable.
alg-f-026 · level ipotesi · notes
Selection Sort, for $i=1,\dots,n-1$, swaps $a[i]$ with the first minimum of $a[i..n]$. How do we prove that it is not stable (i.e., that it does not always preserve the relative order of records with equal keys)?
  1. It is enough to exhibit one input on which the relative order of two equal keys changes, e.g. $[(2,A),(2,B),(1,C)]$, which becomes $[(1,C),(2,B),(2,A)]$.
  2. We show that it performs $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons on every input, more than a stable sort needs.
  3. We show that it is not in-place, since the variable $\mathit{minIdx}$ stores a position of the array.
  4. We prove by induction that after iteration $i$ the sub-array $a[1..i]$ contains the $i$ smallest keys sorted.
Explanation. To deny a universal property («on every input the order of equal keys is preserved») one counterexample is enough: the swap of $a[1]$ and $a[3]$ moves $(2,A)$ past $(2,B)$.
alg-f-027 · level facile · notes
When is a sorting algorithm called in-place?
  1. When it uses no variable at all besides the array to be sorted.
  2. When it sorts the array without moving the elements already in place.
  3. When the amount of extra memory it requires is constant, i.e., it does not grow with $n$.
  4. When it preserves the relative order of records with equal keys.
Explanation. This is Definition 3.3: a few auxiliary variables ($i$, $j$, $\mathit{key}$, $\mathit{tmp}$) are allowed, as long as their number does not depend on $n$. Preserving the order of equal keys is stability.
alg-f-163 · level teorema · notes
ExchangeSort sorts $a[1..n]$: for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ and, inside, for $j=i+1,\dots,n$, it swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ if $a[i]>a[j]$. Which statement on its key comparisons and swaps is correct?
  1. Exactly $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons on every input; from $0$ swaps (sorted array) to $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ (distinct decreasing keys).
  2. From $n-1$ comparisons (sorted array) to $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ (decreasing keys); at most $n-1$ swaps on every input.
  3. Exactly $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons and exactly $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ swaps, whatever the initial order of the keys.
  4. Exactly $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons on every input; at most $n-1$ swaps, one per iteration of the outer loop.
Explanation. Each pair $i<j$ is compared exactly once: $\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(n-i)=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons. Each comparison causes at most one swap; none if the array is sorted, all of them if the keys are distinct and decreasing. At most $n-1$ swaps is the property of SelectionSort.
alg-f-164 · level media · notes
ExchangeSort (for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ and $j=i+1,\dots,n$, it swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ if the key of $a[i]$ is larger than that of $a[j]$) is run on records (key, label): $[(2,A),(2,B),(1,C)]$. What is the final array?
  1. $[(1,C),(2,A),(2,B)]$
  2. $[(2,A),(2,B),(1,C)]$
  3. $[(2,B),(1,C),(2,A)]$
  4. $[(1,C),(2,B),(2,A)]$
Explanation. With $i=1$: $j=2$ does not swap ($2>2$ is false), $j=3$ swaps $(2,A)$ with $(1,C)$, giving $[(1,C),(2,B),(2,A)]$; with $i=2$ the comparison $2>2$ is false. The records with key $2$ come out in the order $B,A$: ExchangeSort is not stable.
alg-f-165 · level media · notes
We sort $a=[8,7,6,5,4,3,2,1]$ in increasing order. InsertionSort shifts to the right the keys larger than $\mathit{key}$; ExchangeSort swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ for every pair $i<j$ with $a[i]>a[j]$; SelectionSort swaps $a[i]$ with the minimum of $a[i..n]$ only if it is in another position. How many shifts, swaps and swaps do they perform, respectively?
  1. $28$, $28$ and $7$
  2. $28$, $28$ and $4$
  3. $28$, $28$ and $28$
  4. $7$, $28$ and $4$
Explanation. On the decreasing array InsertionSort and ExchangeSort reach the maximum $\frac{8\cdot 7}{2}=28$. SelectionSort swaps $8\leftrightarrow 1$, $7\leftrightarrow 2$, $6\leftrightarrow 3$, $5\leftrightarrow 4$ and then finds every minimum already in place: $4$ swaps, fewer than the worst case $n-1=7$.
alg-f-275 · level facile · notes
The sorting problem asks to rearrange an array $a[1..n]$ of natural numbers. Which condition must hold at the end?
  1. $a[1]<a[2]<\dots<a[n]$
  2. $a[1] \le a[2] \le \dots \le a[n]$, with the same elements as before (a permutation)
  3. $a[1]$ is the minimum and $a[n]$ the maximum, the rest anything
  4. Equal elements have been removed
Explanation. The order is nondecreasing (duplicates are allowed) and the final array is a permutation of the initial one. Strictly increasing order would be impossible with repeated elements.
alg-f-276 · level teorema · notes
A sorting algorithm is comparison-based if it accesses the keys only by comparing them in pairs and copying them. Which statement is correct?
  1. Every sorting algorithm is comparison-based
  2. A comparison-based algorithm cannot copy the keys
  3. Comparison-based algorithms work only on natural numbers
  4. The chapter's three sorts are; using digits as indices is not
Explanation. The three algorithms of the chapter use only comparisons and copies; using the value of a key as an index (as in counting sort) leaves the model. The model works for any comparable type.
alg-f-395 · level facile · notes
A comparison-based sorting algorithm accesses the keys only by comparing them in pairs and copying them. Which operation on the keys is forbidden to it?
  1. Using the value of a key as an array index
  2. Evaluating $a[i]<a[j]$ to decide the order
  3. Copying $a[i]$ into a variable $\mathit{tmp}$
  4. Swapping $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ with three assignments
Explanation. Indexing by the value of a key (or doing arithmetic on it) is neither a comparison nor a copy. Comparisons, copies and swaps (three copies) are the allowed operations.
alg-f-396 · level facile · notes
A comparison-based sorting algorithm performs, on some input, $10$ key comparisons and $7$ swaps (each swap uses a variable $\mathit{tmp}$). How many key assignments does it perform?
  1. $14$
  2. $7$
  3. $21$
  4. $31$
Explanation. A swap is three assignments ($\mathit{tmp}:=a[i]$, $a[i]\leftarrow a[j]$, $a[j]\leftarrow\mathit{tmp}$), so $3\cdot 7=21$. Comparisons are not assignments: $31$ adds them.
alg-f-397 · level facile · notes
Sorting algorithms are evaluated by counting key comparisons and key assignments. Why are these numbers given in the best case and in the worst case?
  1. Because they also depend on the initial order of the keys, not only on $n$
  2. Because they depend on the speed of the computer
  3. Because for sorting the average case is not defined
  4. Because they depend only on $n$, and two cases suffice to check it
Explanation. For fixed $n$, different inputs give different counts (for instance Insertion Sort on a sorted or a decreasing array): the best and worst cases are the minimum and maximum over all inputs of size $n$.
alg-f-398 · level facile · notes
A sort is in-place if the extra memory it uses is constant. An algorithm copies the $n$ keys into a new array $b[1..n]$, sorts $b$ and then copies $b$ back into $a$. Is it in-place?
  1. Yes: at the end the result is in the original array
  2. No: the extra memory grows with $n$
  3. Yes: it uses only one extra variable, the array $b$
  4. It depends on the number of comparisons it performs
Explanation. Array $b$ takes space proportional to $n$, so the extra memory is not constant. What counts is the space used, not where the result ends up.
alg-f-399 · level media · notes
Records (grade, name): $(28,\mathrm{Bea}),(30,\mathrm{Ada}),(28,\mathrm{Ale}),(30,\mathrm{Cle})$. They are sorted first by name (alphabetical order) and then, with a stable algorithm, by increasing grade. What is the final order?
  1. $(28,\mathrm{Bea}),(28,\mathrm{Ale}),(30,\mathrm{Ada}),(30,\mathrm{Cle})$
  2. $(28,\mathrm{Ale}),(28,\mathrm{Bea}),(30,\mathrm{Cle}),(30,\mathrm{Ada})$
  3. $(30,\mathrm{Ada}),(30,\mathrm{Cle}),(28,\mathrm{Ale}),(28,\mathrm{Bea})$
  4. $(28,\mathrm{Ale}),(28,\mathrm{Bea}),(30,\mathrm{Ada}),(30,\mathrm{Cle})$
Explanation. After the first sort: Ada, Ale, Bea, Cle. The stable sort by grade keeps this order among equal grades: for equal grades the names stay alphabetical.
alg-f-400 · level media · notes
InsertionSort, for $i=2,\dots,n$, sets $\mathit{key}=a[i]$, $j=i-1$ and, while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$, moves $a[j]$ to $a[j+1]$ and decrements $j$; then it sets $a[j+1]=\mathit{key}$. Counting the comparisons $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ and the shifts, what does it perform on $[2,3,4,5,1]$?
  1. $4$ comparisons and $4$ shifts
  2. $10$ comparisons and $4$ shifts
  3. $7$ comparisons and $5$ shifts
  4. $7$ comparisons and $4$ shifts
Explanation. For $i=2,3,4$ one false comparison suffices. For $i=5$ ($\mathit{key}=1$) the comparisons with $5,4,3,2$ are true: $4$ shifts, then $j=0$ ends the loop with no comparison. Total $3+4=7$ comparisons. The final assignment of $\mathit{key}$ is not a shift.
alg-f-401 · level media · notes
ExchangeSort, for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ and, inside, for $j=i+1,\dots,n$, swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ if $a[i]>a[j]$. How many comparisons and swaps does it perform on $[3,1,2,0]$?
  1. $6$ comparisons and $1$ swap
  2. $6$ comparisons and $5$ swaps
  3. $6$ comparisons and $6$ swaps
  4. $3$ comparisons and $3$ swaps
Explanation. $i=1$: $[1,3,2,0]$, then $[0,3,2,1]$ ($2$ swaps). $i=2$: $[0,2,3,1]$, $[0,1,3,2]$ ($2$). $i=3$: $[0,1,2,3]$ ($1$). The comparisons are always $\frac{4\cdot 3}{2}=6$. A single swap is what Selection Sort would do.
alg-f-402 · level teorema · notes
ExchangeSort sorts $a[1..n]$: for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ it compares $a[i]$ with every $a[j]$, $j>i$, and swaps them if $a[i]>a[j]$. What holds after iteration $i$ of the outer loop?
  1. $a[1..i]$ is sorted but may not hold the smallest keys
  2. $a[n-i+1..n]$ holds the $i$ largest keys
  3. $a[i..n]$ is sorted
  4. $a[1..i]$ holds the $i$ smallest keys, sorted
Explanation. At the end of iteration $i$, $a[i]=\min(a[i..n])$; by induction $a[1..i]$ holds the $i$ smallest keys in order. An arbitrary sorted prefix is instead the property of Insertion Sort.
alg-f-403 · level facile · notes
SelectionSort, for $i=1,\dots,n-1$, finds the index $\mathit{minIdx}$ of the minimum of $a[i..n]$ and swaps $a[i]$ and $a[\mathit{minIdx}]$ if $\mathit{minIdx}\ne i$. How many swaps does it perform on $[2,3,4,5,1]$?
  1. $1$
  2. $4$
  3. $10$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $[1,3,4,5,2]$, $[1,2,4,5,3]$, $[1,2,3,5,4]$, $[1,2,3,4,5]$: one swap for each of the $n-1=4$ iterations, the maximum possible. $10=\frac{5\cdot 4}{2}$ is the number of comparisons.
alg-f-404 · level facile · notes
An already sorted array of $n=100$ distinct keys is sorted. How many key comparisons do Insertion Sort, Exchange Sort and Selection Sort perform, in this order?
  1. $99$, $99$, $99$
  2. $4950$, $4950$, $4950$
  3. $99$, $4950$, $4950$
  4. $99$, $4950$, $99$
Explanation. Insertion Sort makes one comparison for each $i=2,\dots,n$: $n-1=99$. Exchange and Selection Sort always compare every pair: $\frac{100\cdot 99}{2}=4950$.
alg-f-405 · level teorema · notes
The keys are very large records, so each key assignment costs much more than a comparison. Among Insertion, Exchange and Selection Sort, which guarantees the fewest assignments in the worst case?
  1. Selection Sort: at most $n-1$ swaps, that is $3(n-1)$ assignments
  2. Insertion Sort: in the worst case it moves no key
  3. Exchange Sort: at most $n-1$ swaps
  4. They are equivalent: all make $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ assignments in the worst case
Explanation. Selection Sort makes at most one swap per iteration. In the worst case Insertion Sort makes $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ shifts and Exchange Sort $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ swaps.
alg-f-676 · level ipotesi · notes
InsertionSort shifts right the keys $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ (strict comparison). A variant uses $a[j]\ge\mathit{key}$. What changes?
  1. It no longer sorts correctly
  2. It makes fewer shifts
  3. It becomes in-place, whereas before it was not
  4. It still sorts, but it is no longer stable
Explanation. With $\ge$ a key equal to $\mathit{key}$ is shifted right and $\mathit{key}$ jumps ahead of it: the relative order of records with equal keys is reversed. Shifts increase.

4. Linear and binary search (18)

alg-f-028 · level facile · notes
LinearSearch compares $a[i]=m$ for $i=1,2,\dots,n$ and returns the first $i$ for which the comparison is true ($-1$ if none). It looks for $m$ in an array of $n=12$ elements where $m$ appears for the first time in position $7$ (and then also in position $10$). How many comparisons $a[i]=m$ does it perform?
  1. $12$
  2. $6$
  3. $10$
  4. $7$
Explanation. It compares $a[1],\dots,a[7]$: the seventh comparison is true and the algorithm returns $7$ without looking at the rest. $12$ comparisons are needed only if $m$ is absent.
alg-f-029 · level facile · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m$ in a sorted array $a[1..n]$: at each iteration of the while loop it examines the central element $a[mid]$ of the interval $[left,right]$ and, if it is not $m$, continues in the left or right half. At most how many iterations does it execute with $n=100$?
  1. $6$
  2. $7$
  3. $8$
  4. $50$
Explanation. The bound is $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$; since $2^6=64\le 100<128$, $\lfloor\log_2 100\rfloor=6$, hence $7$ iterations.
alg-f-030 · level facile · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m$ in $a[1..n]$ comparing $m$ with the central element $a[mid]$ and discarding the left half if $a[mid]<m$, the right half if $a[mid]>m$. Which assumption on the input is needed for it to be correct?
  1. The array is sorted in non-decreasing order.
  2. The elements of the array are all distinct.
  3. The number $n$ of elements is a power of $2$.
  4. The target value $m$ is in the array.
Explanation. Only sortedness is needed: if $a[mid]<m$, all elements in positions $\le mid$ are $<m$ and can be discarded. With repeated values the algorithm is still correct, and if $m$ is absent it returns $-1$.
alg-f-031 · level facile · notes
In BinarySearch the search interval is $a[left..right]$ and the central index $mid$ is examined, rounded down when there are two central ones. In an iteration we have $left=4$ and $right=9$. Which index $mid$ is examined?
  1. $7$
  2. $5$
  3. $6$
  4. $6.5$
Explanation. $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor=4+\lfloor 5/2\rfloor=6$: the floor makes $mid$ an integer index, the leftmost of the two central ones.
alg-f-032 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the number of iterations of BinarySearch, on a sorted array of $n\ge 1$ elements, is correct?
  1. The while loop executes at most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ iterations.
  2. The while loop executes exactly $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$ iterations on every input.
  3. The while loop executes at most $\log_2 n$ iterations.
  4. The while loop executes at most $n/2$ iterations.
Explanation. This is Observation 4.4. The number depends on the input (if $a[mid]=m$ at the first iteration one iteration is enough), and $\log_2 n$ is not enough: with $n=3$ and $m>a[3]$ two iterations are needed, while $\log_2 3<2$.
alg-f-033 · level ipotesi · notes
Which fact is the proof of the bound $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ on the iterations of BinarySearch based on?
  1. Since the array is sorted, $a[mid]$ is the median of the interval, and after $\log_2 n$ medians the element $m$ has always been found.
  2. Linear search performs at most $n$ comparisons; binary search avoids half of them at each step, hence it performs at most $n/2$.
  3. Each iteration that does not return keeps at most $\lfloor L/2\rfloor$ of the $L$ elements of the interval, and each iteration needs at least one element.
  4. The elements of the array are distinct, so each comparison with $a[mid]$ excludes exactly half of the positions.
Explanation. With $L=right-left+1$ and $mid=left+\lfloor(L-1)/2\rfloor$, the new interval has $\lfloor(L-1)/2\rfloor$ or $\lceil(L-1)/2\rceil$ elements, at most $\lfloor L/2\rfloor$. Iteration $k+1$ happens only if $1\le n/2^k$, i.e., $k\le\log_2 n$.
alg-f-166 · level facile · notes
In the search problem, given an array $a[1..n]$ of natural numbers and a value $m$, one returns an index $k$ with $a[k]=m$ if it exists, and $-1$ otherwise. Why exactly $-1$?
  1. Because $0$ is a valid index in pseudo-code, where the indices of an array start from $0$.
  2. Because $-1$ signals that $m$ is smaller than all the elements, hence that it cannot be present.
  3. Because binary search, when the interval becomes empty, always returns the value $mid-1$.
  4. Because $-1$ is never a valid index, neither in pseudo-code (indices from $1$) nor in C++ (indices from $0$).
Explanation. The value returned when $m$ is missing must be distinguishable from every possible index: $-1$ is not an index in either indexing convention.
alg-f-167 · level facile · notes
The search problem asks, given $a[1..n]$ and $m$, for an index $k$ with $a[k]=m$ if it exists, and $-1$ otherwise. With $a=[4,9,4,7]$ and $m=4$, which outputs are correct according to this definition?
  1. Only $1$, the smallest index
  2. Both $1$ and $3$
  3. Only $3$, the last index
  4. The number of occurrences, $2$
Explanation. The definition asks for an index $k$ with $a[k]=m$, any of them: $a[1]=a[3]=4$. LinearSearch returns $1$ because it stops at the first occurrence, but $3$ also solves the problem.
alg-f-168 · level facile · notes
LinearSearch$(a,n,m)$ sets $i:=1$ and, while $i\le n$, returns $i$ if $a[i]=m$, otherwise executes $i\leftarrow i+1$; after the loop it returns $-1$. With $a=[4,2,7,1,5,9,3]$ and $m=6$, how many comparisons $a[i]=m$ does it perform and with which value of $i$ does the loop end?
  1. $7$ comparisons; the loop ends with $i=8$ and returns $-1$
  2. $7$ comparisons; the loop ends with $i=7$ and returns $-1$
  3. $8$ comparisons; the loop ends with $i=8$ and returns $-1$
  4. $6$ comparisons; the loop ends with $i=7$ and returns $-1$
Explanation. $6$ is not in the array: $a[i]=m$ is compared for $i=1,\dots,7$, all false, and the loop exits when $i=n+1=8$ (the condition $8\le 7$ is evaluated but there is no eighth comparison).
alg-f-169 · level teorema · notes
LinearSearch$(a,n,m)$ compares $a[i]=m$ for $i=1,2,\dots$ and returns the first $i$ for which it is true, or $-1$. Which statement on the number of comparisons is correct?
  1. It always performs $n$ comparisons, because it scans the whole array, and returns the last index $k$ with $a[k]=m$.
  2. If $k$ is the smallest index with $a[k]=m$ it performs $k-1$ comparisons, because $a[k]$ is recognized without comparing it.
  3. If $k$ is the smallest index with $a[k]=m$ it performs exactly $k$ comparisons and returns $k$; if $m$ is absent it performs $n$.
  4. It performs at most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ comparisons if the array is sorted, and $n$ comparisons otherwise.
Explanation. $i$ takes the values $1,2,\dots$ and for each $i\le n$ one comparison is made: the algorithm stops at the first $k$ with $a[k]=m$ (after $k$ comparisons), or all $n$ elements are compared. Sortedness does not help linear search.
alg-f-170 · level facile · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m$ in a sorted $a[1..n]$: with $left=1$, $right=n$, while $left\le right$ it examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$, returns $mid$ if $a[mid]=m$, otherwise continues to the right ($a[mid]<m$) or to the left. With $a=[2,5,5,5,5,8,9]$ and $m=5$, what does it return?
  1. $2$
  2. $5$
  3. $3$
  4. $4$
Explanation. The first $mid$ is $1+\lfloor 6/2\rfloor=4$ and $a[4]=5$: the algorithm returns $4$ at once. Binary search returns an index of $m$, not necessarily the first occurrence ($2$).
alg-f-171 · level media · notes
BinarySearch (with $left=1$, $right=n$; while $left\le right$ it examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$; it returns $mid$ if $a[mid]=m$, sets $left\leftarrow mid+1$ if $a[mid]<m$ and $right\leftarrow mid-1$ if $a[mid]>m$; at the end it returns $-1$) is run by mistake on the unsorted array $a=[7,3,9,1,5]$ with $m=3$. What does it return?
  1. $2$
  2. $-1$
  3. $1$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $mid=3$: $a[3]=9>3$, so $right=2$; $mid=1$: $a[1]=7>3$, so $right=0$ and the loop ends with $-1$, although $a[2]=3$. Without sortedness, discarding a half may discard $m$ itself.
alg-f-406 · level facile · notes
For the problem of searching $m$ in $a[1..n]$ the notes present linear search and binary search. Which can be used on an unsorted array?
  1. Only binary search: it halves the interval anyway
  2. Both, and they return the same index
  3. Only linear search: binary search needs a sorted array
  4. Neither: the array must always be sorted first
Explanation. Linear search compares $m$ with every element and works on any array. Binary search discards half of the array according to the comparison with $a[mid]$, which is correct only if the array is sorted.
alg-f-407 · level teorema · notes
An algorithm for the search problem receives $a[1..n]$ and $m$ and returns a value $k$. Which condition says that the result is correct?
  1. If $k\ne -1$ then $a[k]=m$; if $k=-1$, $m$ is not in $a$
  2. $k$ is the smallest index with $a[k]=m$, or $-1$
  3. $a[k]=m$ or $a[k]$ is the value closest to $m$
  4. $k\ge 1$ and $a[k]=m$, whatever the input
Explanation. The definition asks for any index with $a[k]=m$ if one exists, and $-1$ otherwise. Returning the smallest index is a property of LinearSearch, not a requirement of the problem.
alg-f-408 · level facile · notes
LinearSearch$(a,n,m)$ compares $a[i]=m$ for $i=1,2,\dots$ and returns the first $i$ for which the comparison is true, or $-1$. How many comparisons does it make in the worst case, and when?
  1. $n+1$, when $m$ is absent (one more to exit)
  2. $n$, when $m$ is absent or occurs only in $a[n]$
  3. $n/2$, when $m$ is in the middle of the array
  4. $n-1$, when $m$ does not occur in the array
Explanation. Each element is compared at most once; all $n$ comparisons are needed if the first occurrence is at position $n$ or there is none. The loop test $i\le n$ is not a key comparison.
alg-f-409 · level ipotesi · notes
BinarySearch (with $left=1$, $right=n$; while $left\le right$ it examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$, returns $mid$ if $a[mid]=m$, sets $right=mid-1$ if $a[mid]>m$, $left=mid+1$ otherwise; then $-1$) is run on the UNSORTED array $[5,1,4,2,3]$ with $m=1$. What does it return?
  1. $2$, the position of $1$
  2. $1$, since $a[1]>m$
  3. The loop does not terminate
  4. $-1$, even though $a[2]=1$
Explanation. $mid=3$: $a[3]=4>1$, $right=2$. $mid=1$: $a[1]=5>1$, $right=0$. The loop ends and returns $-1$. Without the sorted-array hypothesis, discarding the right half can remove the searched value; the loop still terminates because the interval shrinks.
alg-f-417 · level media · notes
LinearSearch$(a,n,m)$ compares $a[i]=m$ for $i=1,2,\dots$ and stops at the first true comparison. All the $10$ elements of an array $a[1..10]$ of distinct values are searched one at a time. How many comparisons are made in total?
  1. $100$
  2. $55$
  3. $45$
  4. $10$
Explanation. The value at position $k$ is found after $k$ comparisons, so the total is $1+2+\dots+10=\frac{10\cdot 11}{2}=55$. $100$ always assumes the worst case, $45$ forgets the last comparison of each search.
alg-f-677 · level ipotesi · notes
BinarySearch performs at most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ iterations because it examines the middle element. A variant always examines $mid=left$ (the first element of the interval). How many iterations can it need, in the worst case?
  1. $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$
  2. $n$
  3. $n/2$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Each iteration discards one element instead of half the interval: it becomes a linear search. The logarithmic bound uses the hypothesis that $mid$ splits the interval in half.

5. Numerical algorithms (28)

alg-f-034 · level facile · notes
In the division with remainder of $n=47$ by $b=5$, what are the quotient and the remainder?
  1. $q=9$, $r=3$
  2. $q=9$, $r=2$
  3. $q=10$, $r=-3$
  4. $q=8$, $r=7$
Explanation. $47=9\cdot 5+2$ with $0\le 2<5$: $q=\lfloor 47/5\rfloor=9$ and $r=47\bmod 5=2$. Quotient and remainder are unique precisely because of the constraint $0\le r<b$.
alg-f-035 · level facile · notes
Let $n=(2731)_8$. What are $n\bmod 8$ and $\lfloor n/8\rfloor$?
  1. $n\bmod 8=2$ and $\lfloor n/8\rfloor=(731)_8$
  2. $n\bmod 8=1$ and $\lfloor n/8\rfloor=(2730)_8$
  3. $n\bmod 8=31$ and $\lfloor n/8\rfloor=(27)_8$
  4. $n\bmod 8=1$ and $\lfloor n/8\rfloor=(273)_8$
Explanation. By Observation 5.2 the remainder of the division by the base is the least significant digit $d_0$, and the quotient is obtained by removing $d_0$: this is the principle of ChangeOfBase.
alg-f-036 · level facile · notes
PrimalityTest$(n)$ tries the divisors $d=2,3,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$ and stops at the first one that divides $n$. How many values of $d$ does it try on the prime number $n=97$?
  1. $9$
  2. $8$
  3. $47$
  4. $95$
Explanation. $\lfloor\sqrt{97}\rfloor=9$, hence $d=2,3,\dots,9$: $8$ values, none of which divides $97$. BasicPrimalityTest would try $d=2,\dots,\lfloor 97/2\rfloor=48$, i.e., $47$ values.
alg-f-037 · level facile · notes
The sieve of Eratosthenes, for every prime $p\le\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$, marks as composite the multiples $j=p^2,\,p^2+p,\dots\le n$. Why can the inner loop for $p$ start from $j:=p^2$?
  1. Because the multiples of $p$ smaller than $p^2$ are all prime numbers.
  2. Because $p^2$ is the smallest composite number smaller than $n$.
  3. To avoid marking $p$ itself; starting from $2p$ would give the same number of assignments.
  4. Because every multiple $m$ of $p$ with $p<m<p^2$ has a divisor $d$ with $2\le d<p$, hence it has already been marked by a smaller prime.
Explanation. This is the fact that $m=d\,p$ with $2\le d<p$, and $m$ is a multiple of the smallest prime dividing $d$. Starting from $2p$ would give the same result but with useless assignments.
alg-f-038 · level facile · notes
The Euclidean algorithm computes $\gcd(n,m)$ by transforming the pair $(n,m)$ at each iteration, until $m=0$. Which pair $(n,m)$ follows $(84,36)$?
  1. $(36,48)$
  2. $(12,36)$
  3. $(36,12)$
  4. $(48,36)$
Explanation. Each iteration replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\,n\bmod m)$: $84\bmod 36=12$. Then $(12,0)$ and the result is $\gcd(84,36)=12$.
alg-f-039 · level facile · notes
Two natural numbers are coprime if their only common positive divisor is $1$. Which pair consists of coprime numbers?
  1. $15$ and $28$
  2. $21$ and $35$
  3. $12$ and $18$
  4. $14$ and $49$
Explanation. Two numbers are coprime if $\gcd(n,m)=1$: $15=3\cdot5$ and $28=2^2\cdot 7$ share no divisor other than $1$. The other pairs have $\gcd$ equal to $7$, $6$ and $7$.
alg-f-040 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the divisors of composite natural numbers is correct?
  1. Every composite number $n\ge 2$ has a divisor $d$ with $\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor<d\le n/2$.
  2. Every composite number $n\ge 2$ has exactly one divisor $d$ with $2\le d\le\sqrt n$.
  3. A number $n\ge 2$ with no divisor in $\{2,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor\}$ is composite.
  4. Every composite number $n\ge 2$ has a divisor $d$ with $2\le d\le\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$.
Explanation. A divisor in $(\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor,n/2]$ is missing for $n=4$, «exactly one divisor» is false for $n=12$ ($2$ and $3$), and an $n$ with no divisor up to $\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$ is not composite: the Corollary says exactly that such an $n$ is prime.
alg-f-041 · level ipotesi · notes
Which results are used to prove Corollary 5.8 (every composite $n$ has a divisor $d$ with $2\le d\le\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$)?
  1. A divisor $d<n$ satisfies $d\le n/2$; and $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,n\bmod m)$ holds.
  2. If $d$ divides $n$ then $n/d$ divides $n$; and if $d>\sqrt n$ then $n/d<\sqrt n$.
  3. The multiples of a prime $p$ between $p$ and $p^2$ have a divisor smaller than $p$.
  4. Quotient and remainder of the division are unique; and $n\bmod b$ is the last digit of $n$ in base $b$.
Explanation. Start from a divisor $2\le d\le n-1$: if $d\le\sqrt n$ we are done, otherwise $n/d$ is a divisor (5.6) smaller than $\sqrt n$ (5.7) and at least $2$ since $d<n$.
alg-f-042 · level ipotesi · notes
SieveOfEratosthenes$(n)$ marks as composite, for every prime $p=2,3,\dots$, the multiples $p^2,p^2+p,\dots\le n$. Which result guarantees that the outer loop can stop at $p=\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$ without leaving composites marked as primes?
  1. The multiples of $p$ smaller than $p^2$ have already been marked.
  2. The remainder of the division by the base is the last digit.
  3. For every pair $n,m\ge 1$ we have $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,n\bmod m)$.
  4. Every composite $m\le n$ has a prime divisor $q\le\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$.
Explanation. Every composite $m\le n$ is marked by the inner loop of its smallest prime divisor, which is at most $\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$. The fact that the multiples of $p$ smaller than $p^2$ are already marked justifies instead the starting point $j=p^2$.
alg-f-172 · level teorema · notes
Let $n\ge 1$ have representation $(d_{k-1}\dots d_1d_0)_b$ in base $b\ge 2$ ($d_0$ is the least significant digit). Which statement on $n\bmod b$ and $\lfloor n/b\rfloor$ is correct?
  1. $n\bmod b=d_{k-1}$ and $\lfloor n/b\rfloor=(d_{k-2}\dots d_0)_b$
  2. $n\bmod b=d_0$ and $\lfloor n/b\rfloor=(d_{k-1}\dots d_1 0)_b$
  3. $n\bmod b=d_0\,b$ and $\lfloor n/b\rfloor=(d_{k-1}\dots d_1)_b$
  4. $n\bmod b=d_0$ and $\lfloor n/b\rfloor=(d_{k-1}\dots d_1)_b$
Explanation. $n=b\cdot\sum_{i\ge 1}d_ib^{i-1}+d_0$ with $0\le d_0<b$: by the uniqueness of quotient and remainder, the remainder is the last digit and the quotient is the number formed by the remaining digits. This is the fact behind the change of base.
alg-f-173 · level ipotesi · notes
A first primality test for $n\ge 2$ tries the divisors $d=2,3,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor$. Which fact guarantees that the values between $\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1$ and $n-1$ need not be tried?
  1. Every divisor $d$ of $n$ with $d<n$ satisfies $d\le\sqrt n$, because $d\cdot d\le n$.
  2. Every divisor $d$ of $n$ with $d<n$ satisfies $d\le n/2$, because $n=k\,d$ with $k\ge 2$.
  3. If $n$ is composite, then $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ is always a divisor of $n$.
  4. Every even number larger than $2$ is composite, so half of the values suffice.
Explanation. If $d$ divides $n$ and $d<n$, then $n=k\,d$ with $k\ge 2$ and $d=n/k\le n/2$. The bound $\sqrt n$ holds for the smallest divisor of a composite number, not for all divisors (for instance $6$ divides $12$ and $6>\sqrt{12}$).
alg-f-174 · level ipotesi · notes
How does one prove that $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,\,n\bmod m)$ for $n,m\ge 1$ ($\gcd$: greatest common divisor)?
  1. By induction on $n$, using only the fact that $n\bmod m<n$ and that the greatest common divisor of $n$ and $0$ is $n$.
  2. One uses that every composite number has a divisor $d$ with $2\le d\le\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$, applied first to $n$ and then to $m$.
  3. One shows that $(n,m)$ and $(m,\,n\bmod m)$ have the same common divisors, using $n\bmod m=n-q\,m$ with $q=\lfloor n/m\rfloor$.
  4. One shows that $n\bmod m$ always divides $n$, so every divisor of $n\bmod m$ is also a common divisor of $n$ and $m$.
Explanation. If $d$ divides $n$ and $m$, it also divides $n-qm=n\bmod m$; conversely, if it divides $m$ and $n\bmod m$, it divides $qm+(n\bmod m)=n$. The sets of common divisors coincide, hence so do their maxima.
alg-f-277 · level facile · notes
A natural number $d \ge 1$ is a divisor of $n$ if there is $k \in \N$ with $n=k\,d$, that is, if $n \bmod d=0$. How many divisors does $n=12$ have?
  1. $4$
  2. $6$
  3. $5$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $1,2,3,4,6,12$: six divisors. $4$ forgets $1$ and $12$; $n$ itself is also a divisor of $n$.
alg-f-278 · level facile · notes
A natural number $n \ge 2$ is prime if its only divisors are $1$ and $n$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $1$ is not prime, and $2$ is the only even prime
  2. $1$ is prime, since it is divisible only by $1$ and itself
  3. $9$ is prime, since it is odd
  4. $0$ is prime
Explanation. The definition requires $n \ge 2$, so $0$ and $1$ are not prime; every even number larger than $2$ has the divisor $2$; $9=3 \cdot 3$ is composite.
alg-f-279 · level ipotesi · notes
The primality test checks divisors only up to $\sqrt{n}$. Which fact, together with «if $d>\sqrt{n}$ then $n/d<\sqrt{n}$», justifies it?
  1. Every composite number is even
  2. There are infinitely many primes
  3. If $d$ divides $n$, so does $n/d$
  4. $\sqrt{n}$ is always an integer
Explanation. A divisor $d>\sqrt{n}$ has the companion $n/d<\sqrt{n}$, which the test would already have found. The other statements are false or not needed.
alg-f-280 · level teorema · notes
Let $n \ge 2$ and $d$ a divisor of $n$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If $d>\sqrt{n}$ then $n/d>\sqrt{n}$
  2. If $d>\sqrt{n}$ then $n/d<\sqrt{n}$
  3. Every divisor is smaller than $\sqrt{n}$
  4. If $d<\sqrt{n}$ then $d=1$
Explanation. From $d>\sqrt{n}$, dividing $n$ by both sides, $n/d<n/\sqrt{n}=\sqrt{n}$. So a composite number always has a divisor $\le \sqrt{n}$.
alg-f-281 · level facile · notes
A natural number $m$ is a multiple of $p \ge 1$ if there is $k \in \N$ with $m=k\,p$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $0$ is a multiple of no number
  2. $p$ is not a multiple of itself
  3. The multiples of $3$ below $10$ are only $3,6,9$, since $0$ is excluded
  4. $0$ is a multiple of every $p \ge 1$
Explanation. With $k=0$ we get $0=0 \cdot p$: zero is a multiple of every $p$. With $k=1$, $p$ is a multiple of itself; the multiples of $3$ below $10$ are $0,3,6,9$.
alg-f-282 · level facile · notes
The greatest common divisor $\gcd(n,m)$ of two naturals $n,m \ge 1$ is the largest common divisor. What is $\gcd(84,36)$?
  1. $6$
  2. $12$
  3. $4$
  4. $252$
Explanation. $84=2^2 \cdot 3 \cdot 7$ and $36=2^2 \cdot 3^2$: the common factors give $2^2 \cdot 3=12$. $6$ is a common divisor but not the largest; $252$ is the least common multiple.
alg-f-410 · level facile · notes
ChangeOfBase$(n,b)$ repeats, while $n>0$: $d[k]\leftarrow n\bmod b$, $n\leftarrow\lfloor n/b\rfloor$, $k\leftarrow k+1$ (with $k$ starting at $1$); it returns $d$ and the number of digits $nd$. What does it return with $n=45$, $b=4$?
  1. $d=[2,3,1]$, $nd=3$
  2. $d=[1,3,2,0]$, $nd=4$
  3. $d=[1,3,2]$, $nd=3$
  4. $d=[3,2,1]$, $nd=3$
Explanation. $45\bmod 4=1$, $\lfloor 45/4\rfloor=11$; $11\bmod 4=3$, $\lfloor 11/4\rfloor=2$; $2\bmod 4=2$, then $n=0$. So $45=(231)_4$, but $d$ stores the digits from the least significant one: $d=[1,3,2]$.
alg-f-411 · level facile · notes
ChangeOfBase$(n,b)$ converts $n\ge 1$ to base $b\ge 2$ with one iteration of the while loop per digit (it divides $n$ by $b$ while $n>0$). How many iterations does it run with $n=1000$ and $b=10$?
  1. $4$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $10$
Explanation. The digits are $\lfloor\log_{10}1000\rfloor+1=4$: $1000\to 100\to 10\to 1\to 0$. $3=\log_{10}1000$ forgets the $+1$.
alg-f-412 · level media · notes
BasicPrimalityTest$(n)$ tries the candidate divisors $d=2,3,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ and returns false at the first $d$ with $n\bmod d=0$. How many candidates does it try with $n=49$ and with $n=31$?
  1. $23$ and $14$
  2. $6$ and $15$
  3. $6$ and $14$
  4. $7$ and $30$
Explanation. $n=49$: it stops at $d=7$, after trying $2,\dots,7$ ($6$ values). $n=31$ is prime: it tries all $d=2,\dots,15$, that is $14$ values.
alg-f-413 · level facile · notes
SieveOfEratosthenes$(n)$ runs $p=2,3,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$ and, if $p$ is not yet marked as composite, marks the multiples $p^2,p^2+p,\dots\le n$. With $n=30$, for which values of $p$ does it mark multiples?
  1. $2$, $3$ and $5$
  2. $2$, $3$, $5$, $7$, $11$ and $13$
  3. $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$
  4. $2$ and $3$
Explanation. $\lfloor\sqrt{30}\rfloor=5$, so $p$ takes the values $2,3,4,5$; $4$ is already marked (a multiple of $2$) and is skipped. Primes above $5$ are not needed: every composite $\le 30$ has a prime divisor $\le 5$.
alg-f-414 · level facile · notes
EuclideanAlgorithm$(n,m)$: while $m\ne 0$, it sets $r:=n\bmod m$, $n\leftarrow m$, $m\leftarrow r$; then it returns $n$. How many loop iterations does it run with $(n,m)=(195,84)$, and what does it return?
  1. $2$ iterations, returns $27$
  2. $3$ iterations, returns $27$
  3. $4$ iterations, returns $1$
  4. $3$ iterations, returns $3$
Explanation. $(195,84)\to(84,27)\to(27,3)\to(3,0)$: three iterations, and $\gcd(195,84)=3$. $27$ is an intermediate remainder, not the greatest common divisor.
alg-f-415 · level ipotesi · notes
EuclideanAlgorithm$(n,m)$ (while $m\ne 0$: $r:=n\bmod m$, $n\leftarrow m$, $m\leftarrow r$; then it returns $n$) is called with $n<m$, for instance $(12,30)$. What happens?
  1. The loop does not terminate, since $m$ does not decrease
  2. The first iteration swaps them, $(30,12)$, and it goes on
  3. It returns $12$ at once, the smaller number
  4. A check must be added to order the two numbers
Explanation. If $n<m$ then $n\bmod m=n$, and $(n,m)$ becomes $(m,n)$. Then $(30,12)\to(12,6)\to(6,0)$: it returns $6$. The hypothesis $n\ge m$ is not needed.
alg-f-416 · level teorema · notes
Two natural numbers are coprime if their greatest common divisor is $1$. What is $\gcd(n,n+1)$ for every $n\ge 1$?
  1. $n$, because $n$ divides both $n$ and $n+1$
  2. $2$ if $n$ is even, $1$ if $n$ is odd
  3. It depends on $n$: it must be computed case by case
  4. $1$: a common divisor also divides the difference, $1$
Explanation. If $d$ divides $n$ and $n+1$, it divides $(n+1)-n=1$, so $d=1$: two consecutive integers are always coprime. Euclid confirms it: $(n+1,n)\to(n,1)\to(1,0)$.
alg-f-418 · level ipotesi · notes
ChangeOfBase$(n,b)$ requires a base $b\ge 2$: while $n>0$ it stores the digit $n\bmod b$ and then $n\leftarrow\lfloor n/b\rfloor$. What would happen calling it with $n=5$ and $b=1$?
  1. $\lfloor n/1\rfloor=n$: $n$ does not decrease and the loop never ends
  2. It returns five digits equal to $1$ (unary notation)
  3. It returns a single digit, equal to $5$
  4. It returns zero digits, because $5\bmod 1=0$
Explanation. With $b=1$ every remainder is $0$ and the quotient is $n$ itself, so the condition $n>0$ stays true forever (and $\lfloor\log_1 n\rfloor+1$ is undefined too). With $b\ge 2$ instead $\lfloor n/b\rfloor<n$ and the loop terminates.
alg-f-678 · level ipotesi · notes
The sieve of Eratosthenes marks, for each prime $p$, the multiples starting from $p^2$. If it started from $2p$ instead, what would change?
  1. The result is the same, with extra work
  2. It would also mark some primes as composite
  3. It would leave some composites unmarked
  4. The loop would not terminate
Explanation. The multiples $kp$ with $k<p$ have a prime divisor smaller than $p$, so they are already marked: starting from $2p$ marks them again, with no errors. Starting from $p^2$ is an optimisation.
alg-f-679 · level ipotesi · notes
The primality test tries the divisors $d=2,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$. A variant stops at $\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor-1$. On which input is it wrong?
  1. On $n=48$, which it declares prime
  2. On $n=47$, which it declares composite
  3. On $n=49$, which it declares prime
  4. On no input
Explanation. $\lfloor\sqrt{49}\rfloor-1=6$: the variant tries $2,\dots,6$, none divides $49$, and it declares it prime. The square of a prime has $\sqrt n$ as its only useful divisor; $48$ has the divisor $2$.

Part: Languages (175)

6. Introduction to C++ (64)

alg-f-043 · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>int a = 10, b = 3;</code>, what does <code>cout << a / b << " " << a % b;</code> print?
  1. <code>3.33333 1</code>
  2. <code>3 1</code>
  3. <code>3 0.33333</code>
  4. <code>4 1</code>
Explanation. Between two <code>int</code> values the operator <code>/</code> performs the integer division ($10/3$ gives $3$) and <code>%</code> gives the remainder ($1$).
alg-f-044 · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>int a = 10, b = 3;</code>, which expression gives the floating-point division, $3.33333$?
  1. <code>(double)(a / b)</code>
  2. <code>a / b</code>
  3. <code>(double)a / b</code>
  4. <code>a % b</code>
Explanation. The conversion must happen before the division: <code>(double)a / b</code> divides a <code>double</code> by an <code>int</code>. In <code>(double)(a / b)</code> the integer division already gives $3$, converted into $3.0$.
alg-f-045 · level facile · notes
In C++, <code>incrementByValue(int x)</code> and <code>incrementByReference(int& x)</code> both execute <code>x = x + 1;</code>. After <code>int k = 5; incrementByValue(k); incrementByReference(k);</code> what is the value of <code>k</code>?
  1. $6$
  2. $5$
  3. $7$
  4. The program does not compile
Explanation. Passing by value changes only the local copy; passing by reference (<code>int&</code>) changes the caller's variable: a single increment, $k=6$.
alg-f-046 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is the value of <code>12 & 10</code>?
  1. $14$
  2. $6$
  3. $8$
  4. $22$
Explanation. $12=(1100)_2$ and $10=(1010)_2$: bitwise AND keeps $1$ only where both bits are $1$, i.e., $(1000)_2=8$. OR would give $14$, XOR $6$.
alg-f-047 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is the value of <code>(5 << 2) + (7 >> 1)</code>?
  1. $23$
  2. $13$
  3. $34$
  4. $23.5$
Explanation. A left shift by $k$ positions multiplies by $2^k$: $5\cdot 4=20$; a right shift by one position removes the least significant bit: $(111)_2\to(11)_2=3$. Total $23$.
alg-f-048 · level facile · notes
In C++, after <code>int x = 5;</code>, what do <code>cout << ++x;</code>, <code>cout << x++;</code> and <code>cout << x;</code> print, in this order?
  1. $6$, $7$, $7$
  2. $6$, $6$, $7$
  3. $5$, $6$, $7$
  4. $6$, $6$, $6$
Explanation. The pre-increment <code>++x</code> increments and then returns the value ($6$); the post-increment <code>x++</code> returns the value ($6$) and then increments, so at the end $x=7$.
alg-f-049 · level facile · notes
Which statement about a C++ program is correct?
  1. The program is executed by an interpreter instruction by instruction, with no compilation.
  2. The execution starts from the first function written in the file.
  3. Variables need not be declared: the data type is determined during the execution.
  4. The <code>.cpp</code> source file is translated by a compiler into an executable file, and the execution starts from the function <code>main</code>.
Explanation. C++ is compiled (for instance with <code>g++</code>) and requires variables to be declared with their data type. The interpreter and dynamic typing are features of Python.
alg-f-175 · level facile · notes
Which statement about comments in C++ is correct?
  1. A single-line comment starts with <code>//</code>, a multi-line one lies between <code>/*</code> and <code>*/</code>; the compiler ignores them.
  2. A single-line comment starts with <code>#</code>, a multi-line one lies between triple quotes; the compiler ignores them.
  3. A single-line comment starts with <code>//</code> and, like every instruction, must end with the symbol <code>;</code>.
  4. A multi-line comment is written only by repeating <code>//</code> on every line: the form <code>/* */</code> does not exist.
Explanation. In C++ <code>//</code> comments until the end of the line and <code>/* … */</code> encloses a multi-line comment; <code>#</code> and triple quotes are the conventions of Python.
alg-f-176 · level facile · notes
In C++ (<code>cout</code> displays by default at most six significant digits), what does the following fragment print? <pre>bool b = false; double x = 123456.789; cout << b << " " << x;</pre>
  1. <code>false 123456.789</code>
  2. <code>0 123456.789</code>
  3. <code>0 123457</code>
  4. <code>false 123457</code>
Explanation. A false <code>bool</code> value is printed as <code>0</code>; the <code>double</code> is displayed with six significant digits, hence rounded to <code>123457</code> (the stored value does not change).
alg-f-177 · level facile · notes
In C++ every variable must be declared with its data type before use. Which instruction correctly stores the single character A in a new variable?
  1. <code>char c = 'A';</code>
  2. <code>char c = "A";</code>
  3. <code>c = 'A';</code>
  4. <code>char c = A;</code>
