Explanation. A <code>char</code> is written between single quotes. Between double quotes there is a string (data type <code>string</code>); without a data type the variable is not declared; without quotes <code>A</code> is the name of a variable.
alg-f-178 · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>using namespace std;</code>, what does <code>cout << "a" << 3 + 4 << "b" << endl;</code> print?
  1. <code>a3+4b</code>
  2. <code>a7b</code>
  3. <code>a 7 b</code>
  4. <code>a34b</code>
Explanation. The text between double quotes is printed as it is, the expression <code>3 + 4</code> is evaluated ($7$), and the operator <code><<</code> adds no spaces between the values.
alg-f-179 · level facile · notes
In a C++ program, what is needed to use <code>cout</code> and <code>endl</code> without the prefix <code>std::</code>?
  1. Only <code>#include &lt;iomanip&gt;</code>, which also contains <code>cout</code>
  2. The instruction <code>import iostream;</code> at the beginning of the file
  3. <code>#include &lt;string&gt;</code> and the function <code>main</code>
  4. <code>#include &lt;iostream&gt;</code> and the instruction <code>using namespace std;</code>
Explanation. The input-output stream library is included with <code>#include &lt;iostream&gt;</code>; <code>using namespace std;</code> avoids writing <code>std::cout</code> and <code>std::endl</code>. <code>&lt;iomanip&gt;</code> only gives manipulators such as <code>setprecision</code>.
alg-f-180 · level facile · notes
In C++ the ternary operator <code>c ? x : y</code> evaluates to <code>x</code> if the condition <code>c</code> is true, otherwise to <code>y</code>. Which value does <code>g</code> receive? <pre>int score = 75; string g = (score >= 90) ? "A" : (score >= 80) ? "B" : (score >= 70) ? "C" : "D";</pre>
  1. <code>"C"</code>
  2. <code>"B"</code>
  3. <code>"D"</code>
  4. <code>"A"</code>
Explanation. The conditions are evaluated from top to bottom: $75\ge 90$ and $75\ge 80$ are false, $75\ge 70$ is true, so <code>g</code> is <code>"C"</code>.
alg-f-181 · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>int n = 7;</code>, what does <code>cout << ((n % 2 == 0) ? n / 2 : 3 * n + 1);</code> print?
  1. $3$
  2. $3.5$
  3. $22$
  4. $21$
Explanation. $7\bmod 2=1$, so the condition is false and the third operand is evaluated: $3\cdot 7+1=22$. The second operand (integer division, $3$) is not evaluated.
alg-f-182 · level facile · notes
In C++, in the following fragment, how many times is the condition <code>i <= 5</code> evaluated and what is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>int i = 1, s = 0; while (i <= 5) { s += i; i++; }</pre>
  1. $6$ times, $s=15$
  2. $5$ times, $s=15$
  3. $6$ times, $s=21$
  4. $5$ times, $s=10$
Explanation. The body is executed for $i=1,\dots,5$ and $s=1+2+3+4+5=15$; the condition is also evaluated a sixth time, with $i=6$, when it is false and the loop terminates.
alg-f-183 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>continue</code> skips the rest of the current iteration and <code>break</code> terminates the loop. What does the fragment print? <pre>for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i++) { if (i % 3 == 0) continue; if (i > 7) break; cout << i << " "; }</pre>
  1. <code>1 2 4 5 7 8</code>
  2. <code>1 2 4 5 7 8 10</code>
  3. <code>1 2 4 5 7</code>
  4. <code>1 2 3 4 5 6 7</code>
Explanation. The multiples of $3$ are skipped by <code>continue</code>; with $i=8$ (not a multiple of $3$) the condition $i>7$ is true and <code>break</code> terminates the loop before printing.
alg-f-184 · level facile · notes
In C++ the elements of an array of size $n$ are <code>b[0]</code>, …, <code>b[n-1]</code>. What does the fragment print? <pre>int b[] = {9, 12, 3, 16}; b[2] = 5; cout << b[1] + b[2];</pre>
  1. $14$
  2. $17$
  3. $15$
  4. $21$
Explanation. <code>b[2] = 5</code> replaces the third element ($3$); then <code>b[1] + b[2]</code> $=12+5=17$. Counting the indices from $1$ would lead to $9+5=14$.
alg-f-185 · level facile · notes
Which statement about dynamic-size arrays in C++ is correct?
  1. They are allocated with <code>int* a = new int[n];</code> and their memory is freed automatically at the end of the loop that uses them.
  2. They are declared with <code>int a[] = new int[n];</code> and their elements are indexed from $1$ to $n$, as in pseudo-code.
  3. They require $n$ to be a constant written in the code: the size cannot be read as input during the execution.
  4. They are allocated with <code>int* a = new int[n];</code>, with $n$ known only during the execution, and freed with <code>delete[] a;</code>.
Explanation. The pointer <code>a</code> stores the address of the first element; the elements go from <code>a[0]</code> to <code>a[n-1]</code>, and the memory allocated with <code>new</code> must be returned with <code>delete[]</code>.
alg-f-186 · level facile · notes
In a C++ file the function <code>int addition(int a, int b)</code> is defined after <code>main</code>, without any previous declaration, and it is called inside <code>main</code>. What happens?
  1. The program works: the compiler looks for the called function in the whole file, in any position.
  2. Compilation error: a function must be defined (or declared) before the point where it is called.
  3. The program works, but <code>addition</code> is executed before <code>main</code>, because it is written after it.
  4. The program compiles, but the execution starts from <code>addition</code>, because it is the last function of the file.
Explanation. In C++ custom functions are written before <code>main</code> (or declared before it): the compiler must know them at the point of the call. The execution always starts from <code>main</code>.
alg-f-187 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum in pseudo-code executes $max:=a[1]$ and «for $i:=2$ to $n$», with the array $a[1..n]$. In C++ the array has indices from $0$ to $n-1$. Which translation is correct?
  1. <code>int max = a[1];</code> and <code>for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++)</code>
  2. <code>int max = a[0];</code> and <code>for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++)</code>
  3. <code>int max = a[0];</code> and <code>for (int i = 2; i < n; i++)</code>
  4. <code>int max = a[0];</code> and <code>for (int i = 1; i < n; i++)</code>
Explanation. The index $i$ of the pseudo-code corresponds to <code>i-1</code> in C++: $a[1]$ becomes <code>a[0]</code> and the values $2,\dots,n$ become <code>1</code>, …, <code>n-1</code>. With <code>i <= n</code> the element <code>a[n]</code>, outside the array, would be read.
alg-f-188 · level facile · notes
The C++ function <code>ArrayMaxValue(n, a)</code> sets <code>max = a[0]</code> and, for <code>i</code> from <code>1</code> to <code>n-1</code>, executes <code>max = a[i]</code> if <code>a[i] > max</code>. On <code>{9, 12, 3, 16, 19, 11, 12}</code> (with $n=7$), how many times does it execute the update <code>max = a[i]</code>?
  1. $4$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The current maximum goes from $9$ to $12$, then to $16$ and to $19$: three updates. The initialization <code>max = a[0]</code> is not an update, and the last $12$ does not exceed $19$.
alg-f-357 · level teorema · notes
In C++ the relational operators (<code>==</code>, <code>!=</code>, <code><</code>, <code><=</code>, <code>></code>, <code>>=</code>) compare two values. Which statement is correct?
  1. They return a boolean value (<code>true</code> or <code>false</code>), and <code>=</code> is instead assignment
  2. <code>=</code> and <code>==</code> are equivalent
  3. They return the difference of the two operands
  4. They can be used only between integers
Explanation. <code>a == b</code> is <code>true</code> if the values are equal; <code>a = b</code> assigns. Relational operators also work between reals and characters.
alg-f-358 · level teorema · notes
In C++ the logical operators are <code>&&</code> (and), <code>||</code> (or), <code>!</code> (not). Which statement is correct?
  1. <code>&&</code> always evaluates both operands
  2. They evaluate the second operand only if needed
  3. <code>!</code> has two operands
  4. <code>&&</code> and <code>&</code> are the same operator
Explanation. If the first operand of <code>&&</code> is false, the result is false without looking at the second (and symmetrically for <code>||</code> with true). <code>&</code> is bitwise AND.
alg-f-359 · level teorema · notes
In C++ every variable has a declared type. Which statement about types is correct?
  1. A variable may change type during execution, as in Python
  2. <code>int</code>s have unlimited precision
  3. The type is only a comment
  4. The type does not change, and an <code>int</code> has a bounded range
Explanation. C++ is statically typed: the compiler knows the type of every variable. A typical <code>int</code> has $32$ bits; Python integers, instead, have no fixed limit.
alg-f-419 · level facile · notes
The C++ program is saved in the file <code>hello.cpp</code>. Which command compiles it producing the executable <code>hello</code>?
  1. <code>g++ -o hello.cpp hello</code>
  2. <code>python3 hello.cpp -o hello</code>
  3. <code>g++ -o hello hello.cpp</code>
  4. <code>javac -o hello hello.cpp</code>
Explanation. <code>g++</code> translates the source into an executable; <code>-o</code> is followed by the executable name, then by the source file. Swapping them would overwrite the source.
alg-f-420 · level facile · notes
What happens when compiling this C++ program? <pre>#include <iostream> using namespace std; int main() { cout << "Hi" << endl return 0; }</pre>
  1. It compiles and prints <code>Hi</code>
  2. Compilation error: a <code>;</code> is missing
  3. It compiles and prints <code>Hi0</code>
  4. It compiles with a warning, then prints <code>Hi</code>
Explanation. In C++ every statement ends with <code>;</code>: without it, the compiler reads <code>endl return 0</code> as one statement and reports an error.
alg-f-421 · level facile · notes
In C++, what does the fragment print? <pre>int x = 3.9; bool b = 5; cout << x << " " << b;</pre>
  1. <code>4 1</code>
  2. <code>3.9 5</code>
  3. <code>3 5</code>
  4. <code>3 1</code>
Explanation. A real value assigned to an <code>int</code> loses its fractional part (truncation, not rounding); every non-zero value assigned to a <code>bool</code> becomes <code>true</code>, which <code>cout</code> prints as <code>1</code>.
alg-f-422 · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>#include <iomanip></code>, what does <code>cout << fixed << setprecision(2) << 3.14159;</code> print?
  1. <code>3.1</code>
  2. <code>3.14</code>
  3. <code>3.14159</code>
  4. <code>3.142</code>
Explanation. With <code>fixed</code>, <code>setprecision(k)</code> fixes the number of digits after the decimal point: two digits, rounded, give <code>3.14</code>. Without <code>fixed</code> it would count significant digits (<code>3.1</code>).
alg-f-423 · level facile · notes
In C++ the manipulator <code>endl</code> is used with <code>cout</code>, as in <code>cout << n << endl;</code>. What does it do?
  1. It ends the program after printing
  2. It prints the word <code>endl</code>
  3. It writes a newline only if the line is too long
  4. It writes a newline and flushes the output buffer
Explanation. <code>endl</code> writes a newline and forces what is in the buffer to be written at once. With <code>using namespace std;</code> it is written without the prefix <code>std::</code>.
alg-f-424 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is the value of <code>x</code> at the end? <pre>int x = 10; x += 5; x *= 2; x -= 4; x /= 3;</pre>
  1. $8$
  2. $8.67$
  3. $9$
  4. $26$
Explanation. $10\to 15\to 30\to 26\to\lfloor 26/3\rfloor=8$: <code>x /= 3</code> is <code>x = x / 3</code> between two <code>int</code>s, hence integer division (the fractional part is dropped, not rounded to $9$).
alg-f-425 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is <code>a</code> after <code>int a = 5; a *= 2 + 3;</code>?
  1. $13$
  2. $10$
  3. $25$
  4. $15$
Explanation. <code>a *= e</code> is equivalent to <code>a = a * (e)</code>: the whole right-hand side is evaluated first, $2+3=5$, then $5\cdot 5=25$. $13$ is $5\cdot 2+3$.
alg-f-426 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>i++</code> returns the value of <code>i</code> and then increments it, <code>++i</code> increments it and then returns it. What does the fragment print? <pre>int i = 3; int j = i++ + 2; int k = ++i * 2; cout << i << " " << j << " " << k;</pre>
  1. <code>5 5 10</code>
  2. <code>5 6 10</code>
  3. <code>4 5 8</code>
  4. <code>5 5 8</code>
Explanation. <code>j = 3 + 2 = 5</code> and then <code>i = 4</code>; <code>++i</code> brings <code>i</code> to $5$ and returns $5$, so <code>k = 10</code>.
alg-f-427 · level facile · notes
In C++, what happens when running the fragment? <pre>int a = 5; if (a = 0) cout << "zero"; else cout << "non zero";</pre>
  1. It prints <code>zero</code>, and <code>a</code> is $0$
  2. It prints <code>non zero</code>, and <code>a</code> is $5$
  3. It prints <code>non zero</code>, and <code>a</code> is $0$
  4. Compilation error
Explanation. <code>a = 0</code> is an assignment, not a comparison: it sets <code>a</code> to $0$ and its value is $0$, that is false, so the else branch runs. The comparison is written <code>a == 0</code>.
alg-f-428 · level facile · notes
In C++ (<code>&&</code> and, <code>||</code> or, <code>!</code> not; <code>cout</code> prints booleans as $1$ and $0$), with <code>int a = 2, b = 5;</code>, what does <code>cout << (a > 1 && !(b == 5));</code> print?
  1. <code>1</code>
  2. <code>false</code>
  3. <code>true</code>
  4. <code>0</code>
Explanation. $a>1$ is true, $b==5$ is true and hence <code>!(b == 5)</code> is false: true and false gives false, printed as <code>0</code> (without <code>boolalpha</code>, <code>cout</code> does not write <code>false</code>).
alg-f-429 · level facile · notes
In C++, what does the fragment print with <code>int x = -1;</code>? <pre>if (x > 0) cout << "a"; cout << "b";</pre>
  1. Nothing
  2. <code>b</code>
  3. <code>ab</code>
  4. <code>a</code>
Explanation. Without braces the body of the if is only the first statement; indentation does not matter in C++. So <code>cout << "b";</code> always runs.
alg-f-430 · level facile · notes
In C++, which character does <code>s</code> get with <code>int t = 25;</code>? <pre>char s; if (t > 30) s = 'A'; else if (t > 20) s = 'B'; else if (t > 10) s = 'C'; else s = 'D';</pre>
  1. <code>'B'</code>
  2. <code>'C'</code>
  3. <code>'D'</code>
  4. <code>'A'</code>
Explanation. The conditions are checked in order and only the first true branch runs: $25>30$ is false, $25>20$ is true. $25>10$ is true too, but that branch is no longer considered.
alg-f-431 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>int n = 4096, s = 0; while (n > 0) { s += n % 10; n /= 10; }</pre>
  1. $4$
  2. $4096$
  3. $19$
  4. $15$
Explanation. <code>n % 10</code> is the last decimal digit and <code>n /= 10</code> removes it: the loop adds up the digits, $6+9+0+4=19$, and stops when <code>n</code> becomes $0$. $4$ is the number of iterations.
alg-f-432 · level facile · notes
In C++, what does <code>for (int i = 10; i > 0; i -= 3) cout << i << " ";</code> print?
  1. <code>10 7 4 1</code>
  2. <code>10 7 4</code>
  3. <code>7 4 1</code>
  4. <code>10 7 4 1 -2</code>
Explanation. The initialisation gives $10$; after each iteration $i$ decreases by $3$ and the condition $i>0$ is checked before each iteration: $10,7,4,1$, then $-2$ makes it false.
alg-f-433 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>int s = 0; for (int i = 1; i <= 4; i++) for (int j = i; j <= 4; j++) s++;</pre>
  1. $16$
  2. $6$
  3. $10$
  4. $4$
Explanation. For $i=1,2,3,4$ the inner loop runs $4,3,2,1$ iterations: $10$ in all. $16$ would assume that $j$ always starts at $1$.
alg-f-434 · level facile · notes
In C++, what is <code>i</code> at the end? <pre>int i = 0; while (true) { i += 3; if (i > 10) break; }</pre>
  1. $9$
  2. $12$
  3. $10$
  4. The loop does not terminate
Explanation. <code>while (true)</code> is left only through <code>break</code>: $i=3,6,9,12$, and at $12>10$ it exits. $9$ is the last value not greater than $10$.
alg-f-435 · level facile · notes
In C++, what does <code>int a[5] = {1, 2}; cout << a[1] + a[3];</code> print?
  1. $3$
  2. An unpredictable value
  3. Compilation error
  4. $2$
Explanation. When the brace initialiser has fewer values than the size, the remaining elements are $0$: $a=\{1,2,0,0,0\}$, and $a[1]+a[3]=2+0$. $3$ counts indices from $1$.
alg-f-436 · level media · notes
In C++ a dynamic $m\times n$ matrix is allocated with <code>int** matrix = new int*[m];</code> and then <code>matrix[i] = new int[n];</code> for every row. How many allocations with <code>new</code> are needed, and how many <code>delete[]</code> statements to free it?
  1. $m+1$ and $1$
  2. $m+1$ and $m+1$
  3. $m$ and $m$
  4. $m\cdot n$ and $m\cdot n$
Explanation. One <code>new</code> for the array of row pointers and one for each of the $m$ rows; each row is freed with <code>delete[] matrix[i]</code> and then the pointer array with <code>delete[] matrix</code>.
alg-f-437 · level facile · notes
In C++, what does the program print? <pre>void f(int a[], int n) { a[0] = 99; } int main() { int b[3] = {1, 2, 3}; f(b, 3); cout << b[0]; }</pre>
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. Compilation error
  4. $99$
Explanation. An array passed to a function is not copied: the function receives the address of the first element, so it modifies <code>b</code> itself. An <code>int</code> passed by value, instead, would be a copy.
alg-f-438 · level facile · notes
In C++ execution starts from the function <code>int main()</code>, which usually ends with <code>return 0;</code>. What does that value indicate?
  1. To the terminal, that <code>main</code> printed nothing
  2. To the compiler, that there are no errors
  3. To the operating system, that the program ended well
  4. To the operating system, to rerun the program
Explanation. The value returned by <code>main</code> is the exit code of the program: by convention $0$ means success, a different value signals an error.
alg-f-439 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum in pseudocode sets $max:=a[1]$ and scans «for $i:=2$ to $n$». A C++ translation, with the array <code>a</code> of $n$ elements, writes <code>max = a[0];</code> and <code>for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++)</code>. What is the error?
  1. It reads <code>a[n]</code>, outside the array (indices $0$ to $n-1$)
  2. None: it is the exact translation of the loop
  3. It skips the last element <code>a[n-1]</code>
  4. It compares the first element twice
Explanation. In C++ indices go from $0$ to $n-1$: the right loop is <code>i < n</code>. With <code>i <= n</code> the last iteration reads a cell that does not belong to the array (C++ does not check it).
alg-f-440 · level facile · notes
A C++ function for the maximum of an array initialises <code>int max = 0;</code> and then, for <code>i</code> from <code>0</code> to <code>n-1</code>, sets <code>max = a[i]</code> if <code>a[i] > max</code>. What does it return on <code>{-5, -2, -9}</code>?
  1. $-2$
  2. $-9$
  3. $-5$
  4. $0$
Explanation. No element exceeds $0$, so <code>max</code> stays $0$. This is why FindMaximum starts from $max:=a[1]$ (in C++ <code>a[0]</code>), an element of the array.
alg-f-441 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>&</code> is bitwise AND (a result bit is $1$ only if both corresponding bits are). What is <code>13 & 7</code>?
  1. $15$
  2. $5$
  3. $10$
  4. $7$
Explanation. $13=1101$, $7=0111$: bitwise AND gives $0101=5$. $15$ is the OR, $10$ the XOR.
alg-f-442 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>&</code> is bitwise AND and <code>&&</code> logical AND. What does <code>cout << (12 & 10) << " " << (12 && 10);</code> print?
  1. <code>8 8</code>
  2. <code>1 1</code>
  3. <code>1 8</code>
  4. <code>8 1</code>
Explanation. $1100\ \&\ 1010=1000=8$. Logical AND treats every non-zero value as true: true and true gives <code>true</code>, printed as $1$.
alg-f-443 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>|</code> is bitwise OR (a result bit is $1$ if at least one of the two bits is $1$). What is <code>10 | 6</code>?
  1. $16$
  2. $14$
  3. $12$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $10=1010$, $6=0110$: OR gives $1110=14$. $16$ is the sum, $12$ the XOR, $2$ the AND.
alg-f-444 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>|</code> is bitwise OR. For which pair of naturals $(a,b)$ does <code>(a | b) == a + b</code> hold?
  1. $a=6$, $b=3$
  2. $a=5$, $b=5$
  3. $a=8$, $b=5$
  4. $a=12$, $b=10$
Explanation. OR equals the sum when the two numbers have no $1$ bits in the same position (no carry): $1000\,|\,0101=1101=13=8+5$. For $6|3=7\ne 9$ the common bit $0010$ is counted only once.
alg-f-445 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>^</code> is bitwise XOR (a result bit is $1$ if the two bits differ). What is <code>12 ^ 5</code>?
  1. $9$
  2. $13$
  3. $4$
  4. $17$
Explanation. $12=1100$, $5=0101$: the differing bits give $1001=9$. $13$ is the OR, $4$ the AND, $17$ the sum.
alg-f-446 · level facile · notes
A C++ program must compute $2^3$ and contains <code>cout << (2 ^ 3);</code>. What does it print?
  1. $8$
  2. $6$
  3. $1$
  4. $5$
Explanation. In C++ <code>^</code> is not exponentiation but bitwise XOR: $10\ \mathrm{XOR}\ 11=01=1$. For $2^3$ one uses <code>1 << 3</code> or <code>pow(2, 3)</code>.
alg-f-447 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>~</code> flips all bits. The type <code>unsigned char</code> has $8$ bits and holds the naturals $0$ to $255$. With <code>unsigned char a = 200;</code>, what is <code>(unsigned char)~a</code>?
  1. $55$
  2. $-200$
  3. $56$
  4. $255$
Explanation. Flipping the $8$ bits of $x$ gives $255-x=55$. $56=256-200$ would be the two's complement, that is the flip plus $1$.
alg-f-448 · level media · notes
In C++ an <code>int</code> is represented in two's complement and <code>~</code> flips all its bits. What does <code>int x = 5; cout << ~x;</code> print?
  1. $-5$
  2. $-6$
  3. $2$
  4. $250$
Explanation. In two's complement $-x$ is obtained by flipping the bits and adding $1$, so $\sim x=-x-1=-6$. $2=010$ flips only the $3$ bits of $101$; $250$ is the unsigned $8$-bit result.
alg-f-449 · level facile · notes
In C++ <code>x << k</code> shifts the bits of <code>x</code> left by $k$ positions, adding zeros on the right. What is <code>3 << 4</code>?
  1. $12$
  2. $7$
  3. $81$
  4. $48$
Explanation. Shifting left by $k$ positions multiplies by $2^k$: $3\cdot 16=48$ ($11\to 110000$). $12=3\cdot 4$ and $81=3^4$ mistake $k$ for the factor.
alg-f-450 · level facile · notes
In C++ (<code><<</code> left shift, <code>^</code> bitwise XOR), which expression equals $2^{10}=1024$?
  1. <code>2 ^ 10</code>
  2. <code>10 << 1</code>
  3. <code>1 << 10</code>
  4. <code>2 << 10</code>
Explanation. <code>1 << k</code> is a $1$ followed by $k$ zeros, that is $2^k$. <code>2 << 10</code> is $2^{11}=2048$, <code>10 << 1</code> is $20$, <code>2 ^ 10</code> is the XOR, $8$.
alg-f-451 · level facile · notes
In C++, for an integer $x\ge 0$, <code>x >> k</code> shifts the bits right by $k$ positions (bits falling off are lost). What is <code>100 >> 3</code>?
  1. $12$
  2. $13$
  3. $800$
  4. $97$
Explanation. Shifting right by $k$ positions gives $\lfloor x/2^k\rfloor=\lfloor 100/8\rfloor=12$: the fractional part is lost, not rounded to $13$. $800$ is the left shift.
alg-f-452 · level media · notes
In C++ (<code>>></code> and <code><<</code> right and left shifts, evaluated left to right), what does <code>int x = 45; cout << (x >> 2) << " " << (x >> 2 << 2);</code> print?
  1. <code>11 45</code>
  2. <code>180 45</code>
  3. <code>11 44</code>
  4. <code>11 11</code>
Explanation. $45=101101$: <code>>> 2</code> gives $1011=11$ and loses the two least significant bits ($01$). Shifting it back left gives $101100=44$: the lost bits do not return, so the result is $45$ rounded down to a multiple of $4$.
alg-f-453 · level facile · notes
In C++ the mask <code>1 << k</code> has only the bit in position $k$ set (positions start at $0$ on the right). What does <code>cout << (13 & (1 << 2));</code> print?
  1. $4$
  2. $1$
  3. $0$
  4. $13$
Explanation. $13=1101$ and the mask is $0100$: bit $2$ of $13$ is $1$, and the AND returns the value of the mask, $4$ (not $1$). In an if, a non-zero value counts as true.
alg-f-454 · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>int n = 15;</code> (bit positions start at $0$ on the right), what is <code>n</code> after <code>n = n & ~(1 << 2);</code>?
  1. $4$
  2. $15$
  3. $7$
  4. $11$
Explanation. <code>~(1 << 2)</code> has all bits $1$ except the one in position $2$: the AND clears that bit, $1111\to 1011=11$. $7$ clears bit $3$; $4$ is the value of the removed bit.
alg-f-662 · level ipotesi · notes
The C++ function <code>int sum(int a[], int n)</code> adds up <code>a[0]</code>, …, <code>a[n-1]</code> and assumes that the array has at least <code>n</code> elements. What happens if it is called with <code>n</code> larger than the actual size of the array?
  1. The compiler reports the error
  2. The program stops with an exception
  3. It adds up only the existing elements
  4. It reads cells outside the array, with no check
Explanation. C++ does not check indices: the cells past the end hold arbitrary values (undefined behaviour). Java would throw an exception, Python an <code>IndexError</code>.
alg-f-663 · level ipotesi · notes
A C++ function computes $n!$ in a $32$-bit <code>int</code> variable, whose maximum is $2^{31}-1=2147483647$ ($12!=479001600$, $13!=6227020800$). For which $n$ is the result correct?
  1. For every $n$
  2. For $n\le 12$
  3. For $n\le 31$
  4. For $n\le 20$
Explanation. $13!$ exceeds the maximum of an <code>int</code>: the result overflows and is no longer $13!$. The algorithm's correctness assumes the values fit in the type; $20$ is the limit of a $64$-bit <code>long long</code>.
alg-f-664 · level ipotesi · notes
In C++, the loop <code>int i = 0; while (i != n) i += 3;</code> stops only when <code>i</code> equals <code>n</code> exactly. For which integers <code>n</code> does it terminate?
  1. For every $n\ge 0$
  2. For every multiple $n$ of $3$, negative too
  3. For $n\ge 0$ a multiple of $3$
  4. Only for $n=0$
Explanation. <code>i</code> takes $0,3,6,\dots$: it meets <code>n</code> only if it is a non-negative multiple of $3$, otherwise it jumps over it. With <code>i < n</code> the loop would terminate for every <code>n</code>.
alg-f-665 · level ipotesi · notes
In C++, for $x\ge 0$, <code>x >> 1</code> equals $\lfloor x/2\rfloor$, like <code>x / 2</code> between integers. With <code>int x = -7;</code> (two's complement, sign-preserving shift), what do <code>x >> 1</code> and <code>x / 2</code> give?
  1. $-4$ and $-3$
  2. $-3$ and $-3$
  3. $-4$ and $-4$
  4. $3$ and $-3$
Explanation. <code>x >> 1</code> rounds towards $-\infty$: $\lfloor -3.5\rfloor=-4$; C++ integer division truncates towards zero: $-3$. The equivalence holds only under the hypothesis $x\ge 0$.

7. Introduction to Python (55)

alg-f-050 · level facile · notes
In Python, what are <code>-7 // 2</code> and <code>-7 % 2</code>?
  1. $-3$ and $-1$
  2. $-4$ and $-1$
  3. $-4$ and $1$
  4. $-3.5$ and $1$
Explanation. <code>//</code> computes $\lfloor a/b\rfloor$, rounding down: $\lfloor -3.5\rfloor=-4$, and the remainder is $-7-2\cdot(-4)=1$. $-3$ and $-1$ are the results of C++ and Java, which truncate toward zero.
alg-f-051 · level facile · notes
In Python, which values does <code>i</code> take in <code>for i in range(2, 11, 3):</code>?
  1. $2,5,8$
  2. $2,5,8,11$
  3. $3,6,9$
  4. $2,4,6,8,10$
Explanation. <code>range(start, stop, step)</code> starts at <code>start</code> (included) and stops before <code>stop</code> (excluded), with increment <code>step</code>: $2,5,8$ ($11$ is excluded).
alg-f-052 · level facile · notes
In Python, what happens when writing <code>x++</code> and <code>++x</code>?
  1. Both increment <code>x</code> by $1$, exactly as happens in C++.
  2. <code>x++</code> increments <code>x</code> after its use; <code>++x</code> is a syntax error.
  3. <code>x++</code> is a syntax error; <code>++x</code> is accepted but does not increment <code>x</code>: its value is <code>x</code> itself.
  4. Both are syntax errors: Python accepts neither of them.
Explanation. Python has no operators <code>++</code> and <code>--</code>: one writes <code>x += 1</code>. <code>++x</code> applies the unary plus twice and leaves the value unchanged.
alg-f-053 · level facile · notes
Which statement about the Python language is correct?
  1. It is interpreted, it uses dynamic typing, and the indentation of the blocks is mandatory.
  2. It is compiled into an executable, like C++, and the blocks are delimited by curly braces.
  3. Every variable must be declared with its data type before use.
  4. Indentation is optional: it only improves readability.
Explanation. In Python the blocks (loops, conditional statements, functions) are defined by the indentation level, introduced by «:»; the data type of a variable is determined during the execution from the assigned value.
alg-f-054 · level facile · notes
In Python, with <code>a = [4, 7, 1, 9]</code>, what is <code>a[1] + a[3]</code>?
  1. $5$
  2. $8$
  3. An index error is raised
  4. $16$
Explanation. The elements of a list are indexed from $0$: <code>a[1]</code> is $7$ and <code>a[3]</code> is $9$. With the pseudo-code indices starting at $1$ one would read $4+1=5$.
alg-f-055 · level facile · notes
In Python 3, what does <code>print(6 / 3, 7 // 2)</code> print?
  1. <code>2 3</code>
  2. <code>2.0 3</code>
  3. <code>2 3.5</code>
  4. <code>2.0 3.5</code>
Explanation. In Python 3 the operator <code>/</code> always returns a <code>float</code>, even between two integers; the integer division is obtained with <code>//</code>. This is the opposite convention with respect to C++.
alg-f-189 · level facile · notes
In Python blocks are defined by indentation. What does the program print? <pre>x = 5 if x > 10: print("a") print("b")</pre>
  1. <code>b</code>
  2. <code>a</code> and then <code>b</code>
  3. Nothing
  4. A syntax error, because the curly braces are missing
Explanation. Only <code>print("a")</code> is indented, so only it belongs to the block of the <code>if</code>, which is not executed ($5>10$ is false); <code>print("b")</code> is outside the block and is always executed.
alg-f-190 · level facile · notes
In Python one executes <code>x = 5</code> and then <code>x = "five"</code>. What happens?
  1. Error: <code>x</code> was created as an <code>int</code> and cannot receive a string.
  2. The name <code>x</code> now refers to a string: typing is dynamic and there is no error.
  3. <code>x</code> stays equal to $5$: the second assignment is ignored without messages.
  4. <code>x</code> is <code>"five"</code>, but its data type stays <code>int</code>, the initial one.
Explanation. In Python variables are created by assignment and have no declared data type: after the second assignment <code>type(x)</code> is <code>str</code>.
alg-f-191 · level facile · notes
In Python 3, what does <code>print(2 ** 100)</code> print (<code>**</code> is exponentiation)?
  1. A negative number, because of integer overflow
  2. The approximate value <code>1.2676506002282294e+30</code>
  3. An error, because the result exceeds $2^{63}-1$
  4. The exact integer $2^{100}$, with $31$ digits
Explanation. The integers of Python have no maximum value: <code>2 ** 100</code> is the exact integer <code>1267650600228229401496703205376</code>. In C++ and in Java the same value does not fit in an <code>int</code>.
alg-f-192 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>sep</code> is the separator between the values of <code>print</code> (by default a space) and <code>end</code> the text written at the end (by default a new line). What appears on the screen? <pre>print("a", "b", "c", sep="-", end="!") print("d")</pre>
  1. <code>a b c!d</code> on a single line
  2. <code>a-b-c!d</code> on a single line
  3. <code>a-b-c-!d</code> on a single line
  4. <code>a-b-c!</code> and then <code>d</code> on the next line
Explanation. The separator <code>-</code> goes only between the values; instead of the new line <code>!</code> is written, so the second <code>print</code> continues on the same line.
alg-f-193 · level facile · notes
In Python one executes <code>n = input("n? ")</code>, the user types <code>7</code> and presses enter, and then <code>print(n * 2)</code>. What is printed?
  1. <code>14</code>
  2. A type error
  3. <code>7 7</code>
  4. <code>77</code>
Explanation. <code>input()</code> always returns a string: <code>"7" * 2</code> repeats the string and gives <code>77</code>. To obtain $14$ the conversion <code>int(input(…))</code> is needed.
alg-f-194 · level facile · notes
In Python, what does the program print? <pre>x = 15 if x > 20: print("A") elif x > 10: print("B") elif x > 5: print("C") else: print("D")</pre>
  1. <code>B</code>
  2. <code>B</code> and then <code>C</code>
  3. <code>C</code>
  4. <code>D</code>
Explanation. The conditions are evaluated in order and only the first branch with a true condition is executed: $15>20$ is false, $15>10$ is true, so <code>B</code> is printed and the other branches are skipped.
alg-f-195 · level facile · notes
In Python the conditional expression <code>x if c else y</code> evaluates to <code>x</code> if <code>c</code> is true, otherwise to <code>y</code>. With <code>n = 5</code>, what is <code>"big" if n > 10 else "medium" if n > 5 else "small"</code>?
  1. <code>"medium"</code>
  2. <code>"big"</code>
  3. <code>"small"</code>
  4. A syntax error
Explanation. $5>10$ is false, so <code>"medium" if n > 5 else "small"</code> is evaluated; $5>5$ is also false, and the value is <code>"small"</code>.
alg-f-196 · level facile · notes
In Python, with <code>a = [5, 7, 9]</code>, what is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>s = 0 for i in range(len(a)): s += i</pre>
  1. $3$
  2. $21$
  3. $6$
  4. $24$
Explanation. <code>range(len(a))</code> gives the indices $0,1,2$, and the loop adds the indices, not the elements: $s=0+1+2=3$. To add the elements one needs <code>s += a[i]</code> (or <code>for x in a</code>).
alg-f-197 · level media · notes
In Python, what does the program print? <pre>m = [[0] * 2] * 3 m[0][0] = 1 print(m)</pre>
  1. <code>[[1, 0], [0, 0], [0, 0]]</code>
  2. <code>[[1, 1], [0, 0], [0, 0]]</code>
  3. <code>[[1, 0], [1, 0], [1, 0]]</code>
  4. An index error
Explanation. <code>[[0] * 2] * 3</code> creates a list with three references to the same row: modifying one modifies all of them. Three distinct rows are obtained with <code>[[0] * 2 for _ in range(3)]</code>.
alg-f-198 · level media · notes
In Python, what does the program print? <pre>def f(a, x): a[0] = 0 x = 0 b = [4, 7] k = 5 f(b, k) print(b, k)</pre>
  1. <code>[4, 7] 5</code>
  2. <code>[0, 7] 0</code>
  3. <code>[4, 7] 0</code>
  4. <code>[0, 7] 5</code>
Explanation. The parameter receives the same object as the argument: modifying an element of the list (<code>a[0] = 0</code>) is visible to the caller, while assigning a new value to <code>x</code> changes only the local name.
alg-f-199 · level facile · notes
In Python, with <code>def divide(n, m): return n // m, n % m</code>, what does <code>print(divide(17, 5))</code> print?
  1. <code>3 2</code>
  2. <code>(3, 2)</code>
  3. <code>[3, 2]</code>
  4. <code>3</code>
Explanation. Several comma-separated values after <code>return</code> form a tuple, printed between round brackets. With <code>q, r = divide(17, 5)</code> the tuple is unpacked into two variables.
alg-f-200 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum in pseudo-code executes «for $i:=2$ to $n$» on the array $a[1..n]$. In the Python translation <code>array_max_value(a)</code> the list <code>a</code> has indices from $0$ and $n$ is not a parameter. Which loop visits the same elements?
  1. <code>for i in range(2, len(a) + 1):</code>
  2. <code>for i in range(1, len(a)):</code>
  3. <code>for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):</code>
  4. <code>for i in range(2, len(a)):</code>
Explanation. The element $a[i]$ of the pseudo-code is <code>a[i-1]</code> in Python: the values $2,\dots,n$ become $1,\dots,n-1$, i.e., <code>range(1, len(a))</code> (the right end is excluded). <code>len(a)</code> replaces the parameter $n$.
alg-f-201 · level facile · notes
The Python function <code>array_max_value(a)</code> sets <code>max_val = a[0]</code> and then scans <code>a[1]</code>, …, updating the maximum. Like FindMaximum, it assumes $n\ge 1$. What happens when it is called on an empty list <code>[]</code>?
  1. The function returns $0$, the default initial value of an empty list.
  2. The function returns <code>None</code>, because the loop executes no iteration.
  3. The function returns $-\infty$, the maximum of the empty set.
  4. The program stops with <code>IndexError</code>: the access to <code>a[0]</code> fails.
Explanation. The precondition $n\ge 1$ does not hold: <code>a[0]</code> does not exist and Python signals the access outside the list with <code>IndexError</code> (in C++ the same access would not be checked).
alg-f-202 · level facile · notes
In Python integers have no fixed number of bits. What is <code>~12</code> (bitwise NOT)?
  1. $3$
  2. $-13$
  3. $243$
  4. $-12$
Explanation. In Python <code>~x</code> is always $-x-1$, so <code>~12</code> $=-13$. $3$ is the NOT on $4$ bits and $243$ the one on $8$ bits, obtained with the mask <code>~12 & 0xFF</code>.
alg-f-203 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>>></code> and <code><<</code> are the right and left shifts, <code>^</code> the bitwise XOR. What does <code>print(20 >> 3, 5 << 2, 12 ^ 10)</code> print?
  1. <code>2.5 20 6</code>
  2. <code>2 10 6</code>
  3. <code>2 20 14</code>
  4. <code>2 20 6</code>
Explanation. <code>20 >> 3</code> $=\lfloor 20/2^3\rfloor=2$; <code>5 << 2</code> $=5\cdot 2^2=20$; <code>12 ^ 10</code>: $(1100)_2$ XOR $(1010)_2=(0110)_2=6$. $14$ is the OR, not the XOR.
alg-f-204 · level facile · notes
Comparing C++ and Python, which pair of writings has the same meaning in the two languages?
  1. <code>for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)</code> in C++ and <code>for i in range(n):</code> in Python
  2. <code>c ? x : y</code> in C++ and <code>c ? x : y</code> in Python
  3. <code>x++</code> in C++ and <code>x++</code> in Python
  4. <code>pow(x, y)</code> in C++ and <code>x ^ y</code> in Python
Explanation. Both loops visit $0,1,\dots,n-1$. In Python the ternary operator is written <code>x if c else y</code>, the increment <code>x += 1</code>, and the power <code>x ** y</code> (<code>^</code> is the XOR).
alg-f-205 · level facile · notes
Which values does the expression <code>10 / 3</code> give in C++ (between two <code>int</code>) and in Python 3?
  1. $3.33333$ in C++ and $3.3333333333333335$ in Python
  2. $3$ in both languages
  3. $3$ in C++ and $3.3333333333333335$ in Python
  4. $3.3333333333333335$ in C++ and $3$ in Python
Explanation. In C++ <code>/</code> between two integers is the integer division; in Python <code>/</code> always gives a <code>float</code>, and the integer division is written <code>//</code>.
alg-f-360 · level teorema · notes
In Python a variable is a name bound to an object. Which statement is correct?
  1. The type belongs to the object, not to the name
  2. Every variable must be declared with its type
  3. Integers have $32$ bits as in C++
  4. A variable cannot be reassigned
Explanation. Python is dynamically typed: <code>x = 5</code> and then <code>x = "five"</code> is legal. Python integers have arbitrary precision.
alg-f-361 · level teorema · notes
In Python, with <code>a = [1, 2, 3]</code> and then <code>b = a</code>, one runs <code>b.append(4)</code>. Which statement is correct?
  1. <code>b</code> is a copy, and <code>a</code> stays <code>[1, 2, 3]</code>
  2. <code>b = a</code> raises an error
  3. <code>a</code> is also <code>[1, 2, 3, 4]</code>
  4. <code>append</code> creates a new list
Explanation. Assignment binds a new name to the same object; a copy needs <code>b = a.copy()</code> or <code>b = a[:]</code>. It is the same reason why <code>[[0]*2]*3</code> repeats the same row.
alg-f-362 · level teorema · notes
In Python the logical operators are <code>and</code>, <code>or</code>, <code>not</code>. Which statement is correct?
  1. They are written as words, and <code>and</code> and <code>or</code> evaluate the second operand only if needed
  2. They are written <code>&&</code>, <code>||</code>, <code>!</code> as in C++
  3. <code>and</code> always evaluates both operands
  4. <code>not</code> is a bitwise operator
Explanation. Python uses the words <code>and</code>, <code>or</code>, <code>not</code>, with short-circuit evaluation; <code>&</code>, <code>|</code>, <code>~</code> are the bitwise operators.
alg-f-455 · level facile · notes
The Python program is saved in the file <code>sum.py</code>. How is it run?
  1. First with <code>g++ sum.py</code>, then by launching the executable
  2. With <code>python3 sum.py</code>: the interpreter runs it line by line
  3. First with <code>javac sum.py</code>, then with <code>java sum</code>
  4. By typing <code>main()</code> on the first line of the terminal
Explanation. Python is interpreted: there is no separate compilation into an executable, and no mandatory <code>main</code> function. <code>g++</code> and <code>javac</code> are the C++ and Java compilers.
alg-f-456 · level facile · notes
In a Python program, how does a statement end and how is a comment written?
  1. A statement ends with <code>;</code>; a comment starts with <code>//</code>
  2. A statement ends with <code>end</code>; a comment starts with <code>#</code>
  3. A statement ends at the end of the line; a comment starts with <code>//</code>
  4. A statement ends at the end of the line; a comment starts with <code>#</code>
Explanation. In Python no semicolon is needed: normally a statement takes one line. <code>//</code> in Python is integer division, not a comment.
alg-f-457 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>int(s)</code> converts a string to an integer and <code>+</code> between two strings concatenates them. What does <code>print(int("12") + 3, "12" + "3")</code> print?
  1. <code>15 15</code>
  2. <code>15 123</code>
  3. <code>123 123</code>
  4. A type error
Explanation. <code>int("12")</code> is the integer $12$, so $12+3=15$; <code>"12" + "3"</code> concatenates two strings. The error would come with <code>"12" + 3</code>, which mixes a string and an integer.
alg-f-458 · level facile · notes
In Python, inside the braces of an f-string the format <code>:.kf</code> shows a real number with $k$ digits after the point, rounding. What does <code>print(f"{2 / 3:.2f}")</code> print?
  1. <code>0.67</code>
  2. <code>0.66</code>
  3. <code>0.6666666666666666</code>
  4. <code>{2 / 3:.2f}</code>
Explanation. $2/3=0.666\ldots$ rounded to two digits gives $0.67$ (it is not truncated to $0.66$). Without the <code>f</code> before the quotes the text would be printed as it is.
alg-f-459 · level facile · notes
In Python, with <code>a, b = 3, 4</code>, what does <code>print(f"{a}+{b}={a+b}")</code> print?
  1. <code>{a}+{b}={a+b}</code>
  2. <code>3+4=34</code>
  3. <code>3+4=7</code>
  4. <code>a+b=7</code>
Explanation. In an f-string each expression in braces is evaluated and replaced by its value: <code>{a+b}</code> is $7$, while the text outside the braces stays as it is.
alg-f-460 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>//=</code> assigns floor division, <code>**=</code> the power, <code>%=</code> the remainder. What is <code>x</code> after <code>x = 10</code>, <code>x //= 3</code>, <code>x **= 2</code>, <code>x %= 5</code>?
  1. $9$
  2. $4$
  3. $4.0$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $10//3=3$, $3^2=9$, $9\bmod 5=4$. With <code>/=</code> instead of <code>//=</code> the result would be a float.
alg-f-461 · level media · notes
In Python, in the multiple assignment <code>a, b = e1, e2</code> both expressions are evaluated first and then assigned. What does the program print? <pre>a, b = 1, 2 a, b = b, a + b print(a, b)</pre>
  1. <code>2 4</code>
  2. <code>1 3</code>
  3. <code>2 2</code>
  4. <code>2 3</code>
Explanation. On the right $b=2$ and $a+b=3$ are computed with the old values, then $a=2$, $b=3$. $2\ 4$ is what two assignments in sequence (<code>a = b</code>, then <code>b = a + b</code>) would give.
alg-f-462 · level facile · notes
In Python the C++ increment <code>++</code> and decrement <code>--</code> operators do not exist. What does the program print? <pre>x = 4 y = --x print(x, y)</pre>
  1. <code>3 3</code>
  2. <code>4 4</code>
  3. <code>4 3</code>
  4. A syntax error
Explanation. <code>--x</code> is read as $-(-x)$, two sign changes: its value is $4$ and <code>x</code> is not modified. To decrement one writes <code>x -= 1</code>.
alg-f-463 · level facile · notes
In Python comparisons can be chained: <code>0 < x <= 10</code> means <code>0 < x and x <= 10</code>. With <code>x = 7</code>, what does <code>print(0 < x <= 5, 1 < 3 > 2)</code> print?
  1. <code>True True</code>
  2. <code>False False</code>
  3. A syntax error
  4. <code>False True</code>
Explanation. $0<7$ is true but $7\le 5$ is false, hence <code>False</code>; <code>1 < 3 > 2</code> is <code>1 < 3 and 3 > 2</code>, true. In C++ the same expression would compare a boolean with a number.
alg-f-464 · level facile · notes
In Python, what does <code>print(3 == 3.0, "3" == 3)</code> print?
  1. <code>True False</code>
  2. <code>False False</code>
  3. <code>True True</code>
  4. A type error
Explanation. <code>==</code> compares values: the integer $3$ and the real $3.0$ are equal. A string is never equal to a number, but the comparison is legal and gives <code>False</code> (the error comes with <code><</code>, not with <code>==</code>).
alg-f-465 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>not</code> has lower precedence than comparisons and higher than <code>and</code> and <code>or</code>. With <code>x = 5</code>, what does <code>print(not x > 3 or x == 5)</code> print?
  1. <code>False</code>
  2. <code>1</code>
  3. <code>True</code>
  4. A syntax error
Explanation. It reads <code>(not (x > 3)) or (x == 5)</code>: <code>not True</code> is <code>False</code>, and <code>False or True</code> is <code>True</code>. Python prints booleans as <code>True</code> and <code>False</code>.
alg-f-466 · level facile · notes
In Python, what does the program print? <pre>n = 12 if n % 2 == 0: print("even") if n % 3 == 0: print("three") else: print("other")</pre>
  1. <code>even</code> and then <code>three</code>
  2. Only <code>even</code>
  3. <code>even</code>, <code>three</code> and <code>other</code>
  4. <code>even</code> and then <code>other</code>
Explanation. They are two separate if statements, not a chain with elif: both are checked. The else branch belongs to the second if, and it does not run because $12$ is a multiple of $3$.
alg-f-467 · level facile · notes
In Python the conditional expression <code>x if c else y</code> is <code>x</code> if <code>c</code> is true, otherwise <code>y</code>. With <code>a, b = 3, 8</code>, what does the program print? <pre>m = a if a > b else b s = "even" if m % 2 == 0 else "odd" print(m, s)</pre>
  1. <code>3 odd</code>
  2. <code>8 odd</code>
  3. <code>8 even</code>
  4. <code>3 even</code>
Explanation. $a>b$ is false, so $m=b=8$, the larger of the two; $8\bmod 2=0$ gives <code>even</code>.
alg-f-468 · level facile · notes
In Python, what does the program print? <pre>i = 10 while i > 0: i -= 3 print(i)</pre>
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. $-3$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. $i$ takes $10,7,4,1$ and then $-2$: the condition is checked before every iteration, and $1>0$ causes one last subtraction. The print is outside the loop (it is not indented).
alg-f-469 · level facile · notes
In Python, what is <code>c</code> at the end? <pre>x = 96 c = 0 while x % 2 == 0: x //= 2 c += 1</pre>
  1. $6$
  2. $5$
  3. $48$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $96\to 48\to 24\to 12\to 6\to 3$: five divisions, then $3$ is odd and the loop stops. $c$ is the exponent of $2$ in $96=2^5\cdot 3$.
alg-f-470 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>for x in a</code> assigns to <code>x</code>, one after the other, the elements of the list <code>a</code>. What is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>a = [3, 8, 1] s = 0 for x in a: s += x * x</pre>
  1. $74$
  2. $12$
  3. $5$
  4. $144$
Explanation. <code>x</code> takes the values (not the indices) $3,8,1$: $9+64+1=74$. $5=0+1+4$ would be the result using the indices $0,1,2$; $144=12^2$ squares the sum.
alg-f-471 · level facile · notes
In Python, what does the program print? (<code>end=" "</code> writes a space instead of a newline) <pre>for i in range(1, 10): if i % 4 == 0: break print(i, end=" ")</pre>
  1. <code>1 2 3 4</code>
  2. <code>1 2 3 5 6 7 9</code>
  3. <code>1 2 3</code>
  4. <code>4</code>
Explanation. At $i=4$ <code>break</code> leaves the loop before printing. Skipping only the multiples of $4$ would be the effect of <code>continue</code>.
alg-f-472 · level facile · notes
In Python, what is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>s = 0 for i in range(10): if i % 3 != 0: continue s += i</pre>
  1. $18$
  2. $27$
  3. $45$
  4. $0$
Explanation. <code>continue</code> skips the rest of the iteration when $i$ is not a multiple of $3$: one adds $0,3,6,9$. <code>range(10)</code> goes from $0$ to $9$; $27$ is the sum of the non-multiples of $3$.
alg-f-473 · level facile · notes
In Python a two-dimensional list is a list of rows. With <code>m = [[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]</code>, what does <code>print(len(m), len(m[0]), m[1][2])</code> print?
  1. <code>3 2 6</code>
  2. <code>2 3 5</code>
  3. <code>2 3 6</code>
  4. <code>6 3 6</code>
Explanation. <code>len(m)</code> counts the rows ($2$), <code>len(m[0])</code> the elements of the first row ($3$); <code>m[1][2]</code> is the row with index $1$ and the column with index $2$, counting from $0$.
alg-f-474 · level facile · notes
In Python, what does the program print? <pre>def double(x): y = 2 * x print(double(3))</pre>
  1. <code>6</code>
  2. <code>None</code>
  3. <code>0</code>
  4. An error
Explanation. A function that ends without <code>return</code> returns the special value <code>None</code>: <code>y</code> is computed but not returned.
alg-f-475 · level facile · notes
In Python, what happens when running the program? <pre>def g(): t = 10 return t + 1 r = g() print(r, t)</pre>
  1. It prints <code>11 10</code>, both values
  2. It prints <code>11 11</code>
  3. It prints <code>11 None</code>
  4. Error: <code>t</code> is not defined
Explanation. <code>t</code> is a local variable of <code>g</code>: outside the function the name is not defined (<code>NameError</code>). The result leaves the function only through <code>return</code>.
alg-f-476 · level facile · notes
The Python function for the maximum sets <code>max_val = a[0]</code> and then runs <code>for i in range(1, n):</code>, where <code>n = len(a)</code>. Which indices does the loop visit?
  1. $1,2,\dots,n$
  2. $1,2,\dots,n-1$
  3. $2,3,\dots,n$
  4. $0,1,\dots,n-1$
Explanation. <code>range(1, n)</code> starts at $1$ and stops before $n$; with the list indices, $0$ to $n-1$, these are all but the first, already used to initialise <code>max_val</code>.
alg-f-477 · level ipotesi · notes
FindMaximum requires an array with $n\ge 1$ elements. Its Python translation sets <code>max_val = a[0]</code> and then scans <code>a[1:]</code>. What happens calling it with the empty list <code>[]</code>?
  1. It returns $0$
  2. It returns <code>None</code>
  3. It returns $-\infty$
  4. <code>IndexError</code> on <code>a[0]</code>
Explanation. The empty list has no element with index $0$, so the initialisation fails. This is the precondition $n\ge 1$ on the input: without elements the maximum does not exist.
alg-f-478 · level facile · notes
In Python integers have no fixed number of bits and <code><<</code> is the left shift. What does <code>print(1 << 70)</code> print?
  1. $0$, because the bits fall off on the left
  2. An overflow error (<code>OverflowError</code>)
  3. $1180591620717411303424$, that is $2^{70}$
  4. $140$, that is $70\cdot 2$
Explanation. <code>1 << k</code> is $2^k$, and in Python an integer grows as needed. In C++ a $32$-bit <code>int</code> could not hold it; $140=70\cdot 2$ mistakes the shift for multiplication by $2$.
alg-f-479 · level facile · notes
In Python <code>^</code> is bitwise XOR and <code>**</code> exponentiation. What does <code>print(5 ^ 3, 5 ** 3)</code> print?
  1. <code>6 125</code>
  2. <code>125 125</code>
  3. <code>6 15</code>
  4. <code>125 6</code>
Explanation. $101\ \mathrm{XOR}\ 011=110=6$; $5^3=125$. Writing <code>5 ^ 3</code> for the power is a typical mistake (in C++ and Java too).
alg-f-480 · level facile · notes
Comparing C++ and Python, which statement about blocks and variables is correct?
  1. C++ and Python: blocks in braces; the type is declared only in C++
  2. C++: indented blocks; Python: blocks in braces, type declared
  3. C++ and Python: indented blocks, type always declared
  4. C++: blocks in braces, type declared; Python: indented blocks, no type declared
Explanation. C++: <code>{ … }</code> and <code>int x = 5;</code>. Python: colon and indentation, and <code>x = 5</code> binds the name to an object, whose type belongs to the object.
alg-f-481 · level facile · notes
The pseudocode of the notes indexes arrays from $1$ to $n$. How are a C++ array and a Python list of $n$ elements indexed?
  1. Both from $1$ to $n$
  2. Both from $0$ to $n-1$
  3. From $0$ to $n-1$ in C++, from $1$ to $n$ in Python
  4. From $1$ to $n$ in C++, from $0$ to $n-1$ in Python
Explanation. C++ and Python start at $0$: translating pseudocode, $a[i]$ becomes <code>a[i-1]</code>, and «for $i:=1$ to $n$» becomes a loop over the indices $0$ to $n-1$.
alg-f-666 · level ipotesi · notes
The Python function <code>def mean(a): return sum(a) / len(a)</code> computes the mean of a list. Which hypothesis on the input is needed, and what happens without it?
  1. None: on the empty list it returns $0$
  2. Integer elements; with reals it gives an error
  3. <code>a</code> non-empty; otherwise <code>ZeroDivisionError</code>
  4. <code>a</code> sorted; otherwise the result is wrong
Explanation. With <code>a = []</code> one computes <code>0 / 0</code>, and Python raises <code>ZeroDivisionError</code>. <code>/</code> accepts integers and reals, and order does not matter for the sum.
alg-f-667 · level ipotesi · notes
In Python, for which integers $n$ does the following function terminate? <pre>def steps(n): c = 0 while n != 1: n //= 2 c += 1 return c</pre>
  1. For every $n\ge 1$
  2. For every $n\ge 0$
  3. Only for $n$ a power of $2$
  4. For every integer $n$
Explanation. From $n\ge 1$ the halvings reach $1$. With $n=0$ one gets $0//2=0$ forever; with $n<0$, $-1//2=-1$, and the loop does not end.

8. Introduction to Java (56)

alg-f-056 · level facile · notes
How is a Java program executed?
  1. The source is executed directly by an interpreter, instruction by instruction, as in Python.
  2. The source is compiled into machine code and no other program is needed to run it, as in C++.
  3. The source is translated into C++ and then compiled with <code>g++</code>.
  4. The <code>.java</code> source is compiled by <code>javac</code> into bytecode (<code>.class</code>), which is executed by the Java Virtual Machine.
Explanation. Java is compiled and interpreted: the bytecode is platform-independent and the JVM executes it.
alg-f-057 · level facile · notes
In Java, what does <code>System.out.println(Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1);</code> print?
  1. $2147483648$
  2. $-2147483648$
  3. An error message
  4. $0$
Explanation. An <code>int</code> has $32$ bits in two's complement, with interval $[-2^{31},2^{31}-1]$: the result does not fit and «wraps around» to the other end, with no error message. With <code>(long)</code> one would get $2147483648$.
alg-f-058 · level facile · notes
In Java, with <code>int n = 42;</code>, what does <code>System.out.println("n + 1 = " + n + 1);</code> print?
  1. <code>n + 1 = 421</code>
  2. <code>n + 1 = 43</code>
  3. <code>n + 1 = 42 1</code>
  4. A compilation error
Explanation. The operator <code>+</code> is evaluated from the left: string plus <code>n</code> gives the string <code>"n + 1 = 42"</code>, to which <code>1</code> is concatenated. For the sum parentheses are needed, <code>(n + 1)</code>.
alg-f-059 · level facile · notes
In Java, what are <code>-7 / 2</code> and <code>-7 % 2</code>?
  1. $-4$ and $1$
  2. $-3.5$ and $-1$
  3. $-3$ and $-1$
  4. $-4$ and $-1$
Explanation. As in C++, the division between integers truncates the quotient toward zero: $-3$, with remainder $-7-2\cdot(-3)=-1$. $-4$ and $1$ are the results of <code>//</code> and <code>%</code> in Python.
alg-f-060 · level facile · notes
Which statement about Java is correct?
  1. It uses dynamic typing: the data type of a variable depends on the last value assigned.
  2. It is strongly and statically typed: every variable is declared with its data type, which cannot change during the execution.
  3. The entry point of a program is the first instruction of the file, outside any class.
  4. The blocks are defined by indentation, as in Python.
Explanation. In Java every program is organized into classes and starts from the <code>main</code> method of a class; blocks are between curly braces and every instruction ends with «;».
alg-f-061 · level facile · notes
In Java, after <code>int x = 7;</code>, what do <code>System.out.println(x--);</code> and then <code>System.out.println(--x);</code> print?
  1. $6$ and then $5$
  2. $7$ and then $6$
  3. $6$ and then $4$
  4. $7$ and then $5$
Explanation. The post-decrement returns $7$ and sets $x$ to $6$; the pre-decrement sets $x$ to $5$ and returns $5$.
alg-f-206 · level facile · notes
In Java an <code>int</code> has $32$ bits and a <code>long</code> $64$. What does the fragment print? <pre>int big = Integer.MAX_VALUE; // 2147483647 long b = (long) big + 1; System.out.println(b);</pre>
  1. $2147483648$
  2. $-2147483648$
  3. $2147483647$
  4. A compilation error
Explanation. The conversion <code>(long)</code> happens before the addition, which is therefore computed with $64$ bits: $2^{31}-1+1=2^{31}$. Without the conversion, <code>big + 1</code> would overflow to $-2147483648$.
alg-f-207 · level facile · notes
In Java, on a computer with English settings (decimal separator: point), what does <code>System.out.printf("%d / %d = %.2f%n", 10, 4, 10.0 / 4);</code> print? (<code>%d</code>: integer, <code>%.2f</code>: two decimal digits, <code>%n</code>: new line.)
  1. <code>10 / 4 = 2.5</code>
  2. <code>10 / 4 = 2</code>
  3. <code>10 / 4 = 2.50</code>
  4. <code>%d / %d = 2.50</code>
Explanation. The placeholders are replaced in order by the arguments: <code>%d</code> by $10$ and $4$, <code>%.2f</code> by $2.5$ written with exactly two decimal digits. The division <code>10.0 / 4</code> is not an integer division, because <code>10.0</code> is a <code>double</code>.
alg-f-208 · level facile · notes
In Java, with <code>int n = 15;</code>, one writes <code>if (n % 2) { … }</code>. What happens?
  1. The block is executed because $n$ is odd, as would happen in C++.
  2. The block is not executed, because <code>n % 2</code> is $1$ and not <code>true</code>.
  3. The program compiles, but it stops with an exception during the execution.
  4. Compilation error: in Java the condition must be of data type <code>boolean</code>.
Explanation. In Java an integer is not converted into a Boolean value: the correct condition is <code>(n % 2) == 1</code>. In C++ a non-zero value would count as true.
alg-f-209 · level facile · notes
In Java <code>c ? x : y</code> evaluates to <code>x</code> if <code>c</code> is true, otherwise to <code>y</code>. What does the fragment print? <pre>int a = 7, b = 3; String r = (a % b == 0) ? "divisible" : "remainder " + a % b; System.out.println(r);</pre>
  1. <code>divisible</code>
  2. <code>remainder 1</code>
  3. <code>remainder 2</code>
  4. <code>remainder 0</code>
Explanation. $7\bmod 3=1\ne 0$: the third operand is evaluated, the string <code>"remainder "</code> concatenated with the value of <code>a % b</code>, i.e., $1$.
alg-f-210 · level facile · notes
In Java the division <code>/</code> between two <code>int</code> values is an integer division. What is <code>c</code> at the end? <pre>int i = 10, c = 0; while (i > 1) { i = i / 2; c++; }</pre>
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $5$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $i$ goes from $10$ to $5$, to $2$ and to $1$: three iterations, then the condition $1>1$ is false. With non-integer division ($5$, $2.5$, $1.25$, $0.625$) there would be $4$ iterations.
alg-f-211 · level facile · notes
In Java, what is <code>s</code> at the end of <code>int s = 0; for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i += 3) s += i;</code>?
  1. $12$
  2. $55$
  3. $22$
  4. $18$
Explanation. $i$ takes the values $1,4,7,10$ (the next one, $13$, makes the condition false): $s=1+4+7+10=22$.
alg-f-212 · level facile · notes
In Java, what happens when executing <code>int[] a = {9, 12, 3}; System.out.println(a[3]);</code>?
  1. The program stops with the exception <code>ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException</code>
  2. $3$, the last element of the array, is printed
  3. $0$, the default value of an <code>int</code>, is printed
  4. An unpredictable value is printed, as in C++
Explanation. The indices go from $0$ to <code>a.length - 1</code> $=2$. Java checks every access and signals index $3$ with an exception; in C++ the same access is not checked.
alg-f-213 · level facile · notes
In Java <code>add(x)</code> appends <code>x</code> at the end of an <code>ArrayList</code> and <code>set(i, x)</code> replaces the element of index <code>i</code> (indices from $0$). What does the fragment print? <pre>ArrayList&lt;Integer&gt; list = new ArrayList&lt;&gt;(); for (int i = 1; i <= 4; i++) list.add(i * i); list.set(1, 50); System.out.println(list);</pre>
  1. <code>[50, 4, 9, 16]</code>
  2. <code>[1, 50, 4, 9, 16]</code>
  3. <code>[1, 50, 9, 16]</code>
  4. <code>[1, 4, 50, 16]</code>
Explanation. After the loop the list is $[1,4,9,16]$; <code>set(1, 50)</code> replaces (does not insert) the element of index $1$, i.e., the second one.
alg-f-214 · level facile · notes
In a Java class the method <code>static int addition(int a, int b)</code> is written after <code>main</code> and called inside <code>main</code>. What happens?
  1. Compilation error: as in C++, the method must be written before <code>main</code>.
  2. It works: in Java the order of the methods inside a class does not matter.
  3. It works only if the method is declared <code>public</code> instead of <code>static</code>.
  4. The program compiles, but it stops with an error when it reaches the call.
Explanation. Unlike C++, a Java method can be called before the point where it is defined in the class. A method called from <code>main</code> without an object must be <code>static</code>.
alg-f-215 · level facile · notes
Which header does the method from which the execution of a Java program starts have?
  1. <code>int main()</code>
  2. <code>def main():</code>
  3. <code>public void start(String args)</code>
  4. <code>public static void main(String[] args)</code>
Explanation. The entry point is the method <code>main</code> of a class, with this header; <code>int main()</code> is the one of C++ and <code>def</code> defines functions in Python.
alg-f-216 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum in pseudo-code scans $a[2..n]$ with «for $i:=2$ to $n$». In Java the array <code>int[] a</code> has indices from $0$ and knows its own size. Which loop visits the same elements?
  1. <code>for (int i = 1; i <= a.length; i++)</code>
  2. <code>for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++)</code>
  3. <code>for (int i = 1; i < a.length(); i++)</code>
  4. <code>for (int i = 2; i < a.length; i++)</code>
Explanation. The second element has index $1$ and the last one <code>a.length - 1</code>. <code>length</code> is a field of the array, not a method: <code>a.length()</code> does not compile; with <code><=</code> the loop would go outside the array.
alg-f-217 · level facile · notes
The Java method computing the maximum sets <code>max = a[0]</code> and, for <code>i</code> from <code>1</code> to <code>a.length - 1</code>, compares <code>a[i] > max</code>. On <code>{9, 12, 3, 16, 19, 11, 12}</code>, how many times does it evaluate the comparison <code>a[i] > max</code>?
  1. $7$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $6$
Explanation. The comparison is made once for each <code>i</code> from $1$ to $6$, i.e., $n-1=6$ times, whatever the array; $3$ is the number of true comparisons (the updates of the maximum).
alg-f-218 · level media · notes
In Java an <code>int</code> has $32$ bits in two's complement; <code>>></code> shifts to the right keeping the sign, <code>>>></code> always adds zeros on the left. What are <code>-8 >> 1</code> and <code>-8 >>> 28</code>?
  1. $-4$ and $-1$
  2. $4$ and $15$
  3. $-4$ and $15$
  4. $-4$ and $0$
Explanation. $-8=(11\dots11000)_2$: <code>>> 1</code> copies the sign bit and gives $-4$; <code>>>> 28</code> keeps only the $4$ most significant bits, $(1111)_2=15$. With <code>>> 28</code> one would get $-1$.
alg-f-219 · level media · notes
In Java (<code>|</code>: OR, <code>&</code>: AND, <code>~</code>: bitwise NOT, <code><<</code>: left shift), what is <code>x</code> at the end? <pre>int x = 6; x = x | (1 << 3); x = x & ~(1 << 1);</pre>
  1. $12$
  2. $14$
  3. $4$
  4. $10$
Explanation. $6=(0110)_2$; the OR with <code>1 << 3</code> $=(1000)_2$ sets bit $3$ to $1$: $(1110)_2=14$; the AND with the NOT of $(0010)_2$ clears bit $1$: $(1100)_2=12$.
alg-f-220 · level facile · notes
A function must produce two results. According to the comparison among the three languages of the course, how is it usually done in C++, in Java and in Python?
  1. C++: returning a tuple; Java: parameters passed by reference; Python: returning an array
  2. In all three: parameters passed by reference, written with the symbol <code>&</code>
  3. C++: returning an array; Java: returning a tuple; Python: parameters passed by reference
  4. C++: parameters passed by reference; Java: returning an array; Python: returning a tuple
Explanation. In C++ the results are written into <code>int&</code> parameters; Java passes parameters by value and returns the results together in an array; Python returns a tuple, unpacked with a multiple assignment.
alg-f-221 · level facile · notes
What happens, in C++, in Java and in Python, when accessing an index outside the range of an array (or of a list)?
  1. In all three languages the program stops with an exception that indicates the wrong index.
  2. In C++ the access is not checked; in Java and in Python the program stops with an exception.
  3. In C++ and in Java the access is not checked; in Python the program stops with an exception.
  4. In all three languages the value $0$ is read, without errors or messages.
Explanation. Java (<code>ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException</code>) and Python (<code>IndexError</code>) check the indices; C++ does not, and the access reads or writes an unpredictable memory area.
alg-f-363 · level teorema · notes
In Java every variable has a declared type, and primitive types have fixed size. Which statement is correct?
  1. An <code>int</code> has unlimited precision
  2. The size of an <code>int</code> depends on the processor
  3. An <code>int</code> always has $32$ bits, and can overflow
  4. A variable may change type
Explanation. Java fixes the size of primitive types independently of the machine: <code>Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1</code> gives <code>Integer.MIN_VALUE</code>. Larger integers need <code>long</code> or <code>BigInteger</code>.
alg-f-364 · level teorema · notes
In Java a method receives a parameter of primitive type (for instance <code>int</code>) and modifies it inside. Which statement is correct?
  1. The change is always seen by the caller
  2. The change is not seen by the caller (pass by value)
  3. In Java primitive parameters are passed by reference, like <code>int&</code> in C++
  4. A method cannot modify its parameters
Explanation. Java always passes by value; for reference types the copied value is the reference, so a method can change the elements of an array but cannot make the caller's variable point to another array.
alg-f-365 · level teorema · notes
In Java an array is created with a size, for instance <code>int[] a = new int[5];</code>. Which statement is correct?
  1. The size grows automatically if one writes past the end
  2. Indices go from $1$ to $5$
  3. Uninitialized elements have random values, as in C++
  4. The size is fixed at creation, indices go from $0$ to <code>a.length - 1</code>, and the elements start at $0$
Explanation. A Java array has fixed length and indices from $0$; an out-of-bounds access throws an exception. For a growing list one uses <code>ArrayList</code>; Java initializes <code>int</code>s to $0$.
alg-f-482 · level facile · notes
A Java source file contains the class <code>public class Hello</code> with the <code>main</code> method. What must the file be called?
  1. <code>hello.java</code>
  2. <code>Hello.class</code>
  3. <code>main.java</code>
  4. <code>Hello.java</code>
Explanation. The file name is the name of the public class, with the same capitalisation, and extension <code>.java</code>. <code>Hello.class</code> is the bytecode file produced by <code>javac</code>.
alg-f-483 · level facile · notes
A Java program uses <code>Scanner</code> to read from the keyboard. What must be written at the start of the file, before the class?
  1. <code>#include <Scanner></code>
  2. <code>import java.util.Scanner;</code>
  3. <code>using namespace java;</code>
  4. Nothing: <code>Scanner</code> is always available
Explanation. Library classes outside <code>java.lang</code> are imported with <code>import</code>. <code>#include</code> and <code>using namespace</code> belong to C++.
alg-f-484 · level facile · notes
In Java a <code>char</code> is a numeric code (the character <code>'a'</code> has code $97$), and in an expression with an <code>int</code> it becomes an integer. What does <code>System.out.println('a' + 1);</code> print?
  1. <code>b</code>
  2. <code>a1</code>
  3. $98$
  4. Compilation error
Explanation. <code>'a' + 1</code> is a sum of integers, $97+1=98$. One would get <code>b</code> with <code>(char)('a' + 1)</code>, and <code>a1</code> with the string <code>"a" + 1</code>.
alg-f-485 · level facile · notes
In Java <code>System.out.print</code> prints without a newline, <code>System.out.println</code> adds a newline after printing. What appears on the screen? <pre>System.out.print("a"); System.out.println("b"); System.out.print("c");</pre>
  1. <code>ab</code> on one line and <code>c</code> on the next
  2. <code>abc</code> on a single line
  3. <code>a</code>, <code>b</code> and <code>c</code> on three lines
  4. <code>a</code> on one line and <code>bc</code> on the next
Explanation. After <code>a</code> there is no newline; <code>println("b")</code> writes <code>b</code> and then a newline, so <code>c</code> starts a new line.
alg-f-486 · level facile · notes
In Java, with <code>Scanner sc = new Scanner(System.in);</code>, which statement reads an integer typed by the user?
  1. <code>int n = sc.nextLine();</code>
  2. <code>int n = input();</code>
  3. <code>int n = sc.nextInt();</code>
  4. <code>int n = cin >> n;</code>
Explanation. <code>nextInt()</code> reads the next integer; <code>nextLine()</code> returns a <code>String</code>, which cannot be assigned to an <code>int</code>. <code>input()</code> is Python, <code>cin</code> is C++.
alg-f-487 · level facile · notes
In Java division <code>/</code> between two <code>int</code>s is integer division and <code>%</code> is the remainder. What does <code>System.out.println(7 / 2 * 2 + 7 % 2);</code> print?
  1. $7$
  2. $8$
  3. $6$
  4. $7.0$
Explanation. $7/2=3$ (integer), $3\cdot 2=6$, $7\%2=1$: $6+1=7$. It always holds that $(a/b)\cdot b+a\%b=a$. $8$ would use $3.5\cdot 2$.
alg-f-488 · level media · notes
In Java, with <code>int x = 10;</code>, the statements <code>x += 2.7;</code> and <code>x = x + 2.7;</code> are compared. Which statement is correct?
  1. Both compile, and <code>x</code> becomes $12.7$
  2. Only <code>x += 2.7;</code> compiles, giving $12$
  3. Neither of the two statements compiles
  4. Both compile, and <code>x</code> becomes $13$
Explanation. <code>x + 2.7</code> is a <code>double</code>, which Java does not assign to an <code>int</code> without an explicit cast. The compound operator instead includes the cast: <code>x = (int)(x + 2.7)</code>, that is $12$ (truncation).
alg-f-489 · level facile · notes
In Java, what does the fragment print? <pre>double d = 10; d /= 4; int i = 10; i /= 4; System.out.println(d + " " + i);</pre>
  1. <code>2.5 2.5</code>
  2. <code>2 2</code>
  3. <code>2.0 2</code>
  4. <code>2.5 2</code>
Explanation. <code>d /= 4</code> divides a <code>double</code>: $2.5$. <code>i /= 4</code> divides two integers: $\lfloor 10/4\rfloor=2$. The type of the variable decides the kind of division.
alg-f-490 · level media · notes
In Java operands are evaluated from left to right; <code>i--</code> returns the value and then decrements, <code>--i</code> decrements and then returns. After <code>int i = 5; int j = i-- - --i;</code>, what are <code>j</code> and <code>i</code>?
  1. $j=0$, $i=3$
  2. $j=1$, $i=4$
  3. $j=2$, $i=3$
  4. $j=2$, $i=4$
Explanation. <code>i--</code> is $5$ and brings <code>i</code> to $4$; then <code>--i</code> brings <code>i</code> to $3$ and is $3$. So $j=5-3=2$ and $i=3$.
alg-f-491 · level facile · notes
In Java, what does the fragment print? <pre>String s = new String("ciao"); String t = new String("ciao"); System.out.println((s == t) + " " + s.equals(t));</pre>
  1. <code>false true</code>
  2. <code>true true</code>
  3. <code>true false</code>
  4. <code>false false</code>
Explanation. Between objects <code>==</code> compares references: <code>s</code> and <code>t</code> are two distinct objects. <code>equals</code> compares the content, which is equal. Strings are compared with <code>equals</code>.
alg-f-492 · level facile · notes
In Java <code>double</code>s follow the IEEE 754 standard in base $2$. What does <code>System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);</code> print?
  1. <code>true</code>
  2. Compilation error
  3. <code>false</code>
  4. $0$
Explanation. $0.1$, $0.2$ and $0.3$ are not exactly representable in base $2$: the sum is about $0.30000000000000004$, different from the value stored for $0.3$. Reals are compared through $|a-b|$ with a small tolerance.
alg-f-493 · level facile · notes
In Java (<code>&&</code> and, <code>||</code> or, <code>!</code> not), which expression is equivalent to <code>!(x > 0 && y > 0)</code>?
  1. <code>x <= 0 || y <= 0</code>
  2. <code>x <= 0 && y <= 0</code>
  3. <code>x < 0 || y < 0</code>
  4. <code>!(x > 0) && y > 0</code>
Explanation. By De Morgan's laws the negation of an «and» is the «or» of the negations, and the negation of $x>0$ is $x\le 0$ (not $x<0$).
alg-f-494 · level media · notes
In Java, between two <code>boolean</code>s, <code>&&</code> evaluates the second operand only if needed, while <code>&</code> always evaluates both. What does the fragment print? <pre>int i = 0, j = 0; if (false && (++i > 0)) { } if (false & (++j > 0)) { } System.out.println(i + " " + j);</pre>
  1. <code>1 1</code>
  2. <code>0 0</code>
  3. <code>1 0</code>
  4. <code>0 1</code>
Explanation. With <code>&&</code> the false first operand is enough to decide, so <code>++i</code> is not executed. With <code>&</code> <code>++j</code> runs too, although the result is false anyway.
alg-f-495 · level facile · notes
In Java, what does the fragment print with <code>int x = -5;</code>? <pre>if (x > 0) if (x > 10) System.out.println("A"); else System.out.println("B");</pre>
  1. <code>B</code>
  2. Nothing
  3. <code>A</code>
  4. <code>A</code> and then <code>B</code>
Explanation. An else binds to the nearest if that does not have one yet, that is to <code>if (x > 10)</code>, whatever the indentation. Since $x>0$ is false, nothing runs. Braces select the intended if.
alg-f-496 · level facile · notes
In Java the ternary operator <code>c ? x : y</code> is <code>x</code> if <code>c</code> is true, otherwise <code>y</code>. What does the fragment print? <pre>int a = 4, b = 9; int m = (a > b) ? a : b; String s = (m % 2 == 0) ? "even" : "odd"; System.out.println(m + " " + s);</pre>
  1. <code>4 even</code>
  2. <code>9 even</code>
  3. <code>4 odd</code>
  4. <code>9 odd</code>
Explanation. $a>b$ is false, so $m=b=9$, the larger of the two; $9\%2=1$ gives <code>odd</code>.
alg-f-497 · level facile · notes
In Java, what is <code>c</code> at the end? <pre>int n = 1, c = 0; while (n < 1000) { n *= 3; c++; }</pre>
  1. $6$
  2. $7$
  3. $8$
  4. $333$
Explanation. $n$ takes $3,9,27,81,243,729,2187$: after $7$ multiplications $n\ge 1000$ and the loop stops. It is the smallest $c$ with $3^c\ge 1000$.
alg-f-498 · level facile · notes
In Java, what happens when compiling the fragment? <pre>int s = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) { s += i; } System.out.println(s + i);</pre>
  1. Compilation error on <code>i</code>
  2. It prints $6$, that is $3+3$
  3. It prints $3$, that is $3+0$
  4. It prints $5$, that is $3+2$
Explanation. A variable declared in the for header is visible only inside the loop. To use it afterwards it must be declared before the for.
alg-f-499 · level facile · notes
In Java, what is <code>count</code> at the end? <pre>int count = 0; for (int i = 1; i <= 3; i++) for (int j = 1; j <= 3; j++) { if (j == 2) break; count++; }</pre>
  1. $1$
  2. $9$
  3. $3$
  4. $6$
Explanation. <code>break</code> leaves only the innermost loop: for each $i$ only $j=1$ is counted, then at $j=2$ control returns to the outer loop. $1$ would assume that <code>break</code> leaves both loops.
alg-f-500 · level facile · notes
In Java, what is <code>s</code> at the end? <pre>int s = 0; for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i++) { if (i % 2 == 0) continue; s += i; }</pre>
  1. $25$
  2. $30$
  3. $55$
  4. $1$
Explanation. <code>continue</code> skips the rest of the iteration for even values: one adds the odd ones $1+3+5+7+9=25$. $30$ is the sum of the even ones, $1$ what <code>break</code> would give.
alg-f-501 · level facile · notes
In Java, after <code>int[][] m = new int[3][4];</code>, what does <code>System.out.println(m.length + " " + m[0].length);</code> print?
  1. <code>4 3</code>
  2. <code>12 12</code>
  3. <code>3 4</code>
  4. <code>2 3</code>
Explanation. A two-dimensional array is an array of rows: <code>m.length</code> is the number of rows ($3$), <code>m[0].length</code> the number of elements in a row ($4$). $2\ 3$ are the last valid indices.
alg-f-502 · level facile · notes
In Java, what does <code>int[][] m = new int[2][3]; System.out.println(m[1][2]);</code> print, with no other assignments?
  1. $0$
  2. <code>null</code>
  3. An unpredictable value
  4. Compilation error: <code>m</code> is not initialised
Explanation. In Java the elements of an array created with <code>new</code> start from the default value of the type: $0$ for <code>int</code>. In C++ an uninitialised local array would instead hold unpredictable values.
alg-f-503 · level facile · notes
In Java <code>add(x)</code> appends <code>x</code> to an <code>ArrayList</code>, <code>add(i, x)</code> inserts it at position <code>i</code> shifting the following ones right, <code>get(i)</code> reads the element with index <code>i</code> (from $0$). What does the fragment print? <pre>ArrayList<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(); l.add(5); l.add(8); l.add(0, 3); System.out.println(l.get(1) + " " + l.size());</pre>
  1. <code>8 3</code>
  2. <code>3 3</code>
  3. <code>5 3</code>
  4. <code>5 2</code>
