Explanation. After the two <code>add</code>s the list is $[5,8]$; <code>add(0, 3)</code> inserts at the front: $[3,5,8]$. The element with index $1$ is $5$ and there are $3$ elements (<code>add(i, x)</code> does not replace, unlike <code>set</code>).
alg-f-504 · level media · notes
In Java an array parameter receives a copy of the reference to the array. What does the program print? <pre>static int f(int[] a) { a[0] = 7; a = new int[]{1}; return a[0]; } // in main: int[] b = {4, 5}; int r = f(b); System.out.println(b[0] + " " + r);</pre>
  1. <code>4 1</code>
  2. <code>7 1</code>
  3. <code>1 1</code>
  4. <code>7 7</code>
Explanation. <code>a[0] = 7</code> modifies the array of <code>b</code>. Then <code>a = new int[]{1}</code> makes only the local copy point to a new array: <code>b</code> no longer changes, and the function returns $1$.
alg-f-505 · level facile · notes
The Java method <code>static int arrayMaxValue(int[] a)</code> receives only the array, without the number of elements $n$. How does it get $n$?
  1. With <code>a.length()</code>
  2. With <code>a.size()</code>
  3. With <code>len(a)</code>
  4. With <code>a.length</code>
Explanation. A Java array knows its own length: <code>length</code> is a field, without parentheses. <code>length()</code> is the <code>String</code> method, <code>size()</code> the <code>ArrayList</code> one, <code>len</code> the Python function.
alg-f-506 · level ipotesi · notes
A Java method for the maximum sets <code>int max = Integer.MIN_VALUE;</code> (the smallest <code>int</code>) and then, for every <code>i</code> from <code>0</code> to <code>a.length - 1</code>, updates <code>max</code> if <code>a[i] > max</code>. Is it correct?
  1. No: with only negative numbers it returns <code>Integer.MIN_VALUE</code>
  2. Yes, provided the array has at least one element
  3. No: <code>Integer.MIN_VALUE</code> is not an <code>int</code> value
  4. Yes, even on the empty array, for which it returns $0$
Explanation. Every <code>int</code> is $\ge$ <code>Integer.MIN_VALUE</code>, so after the loop <code>max</code> is the maximum (even if all equal <code>MIN_VALUE</code>). With zero elements the loop does not run and the result is not an element: the precondition $n\ge 1$ is needed.
alg-f-507 · level facile · notes
In Java <code>&</code>, <code>|</code> and <code>^</code> are bitwise AND, OR and XOR. What does <code>System.out.println((5 & 3) + " " + (5 | 3) + " " + (5 ^ 3));</code> print?
  1. <code>1 7 8</code>
  2. <code>0 7 6</code>
  3. <code>1 8 6</code>
  4. <code>1 7 6</code>
Explanation. $5=101$, $3=011$: AND $001=1$, OR $111=7$, XOR $110=6$. $8=5+3$ mistakes OR (or XOR) for the sum.
alg-f-508 · level media · notes
In Java an <code>int</code> always has $32$ bits in two's complement. What does <code>System.out.println(1 << 31);</code> print?
  1. $2147483648$
  2. $0$
  3. $-2147483648$
  4. Compilation error
Explanation. <code>1 << 31</code> sets the most significant bit, whose weight in two's complement is $-2^{31}$: it is <code>Integer.MIN_VALUE</code>. $2^{31}$ is not representable in an <code>int</code>, whose maximum is $2^{31}-1$.
alg-f-509 · level facile · notes
An array whose size $n$ is known only at run time is created in C++ with <code>new int[n]</code>, in Java with <code>new int[n]</code>, in Python with <code>[0] * n</code>. Who frees the memory when the array is no longer needed?
  1. In C++ the programmer (<code>delete[]</code>), elsewhere the system
  2. In all three the programmer, with <code>delete[]</code>
  3. In all three the system, automatically
  4. In Java the programmer (<code>delete[]</code>), elsewhere the system
Explanation. C++ has no automatic memory collector: what is allocated with <code>new</code> is freed with <code>delete[]</code>. Java and Python free unreachable memory by themselves.
alg-f-510 · level facile · notes
How are programs written in the three languages of the course executed?
  1. All three compiled to machine code before they are executed
  2. C++ compiled to machine code; Java compiled to bytecode for the JVM; Python interpreted
  3. C++ interpreted; Java and Python compiled to machine code
  4. C++ and Java interpreted line by line; Python compiled to bytecode for the JVM
Explanation. <code>g++</code> produces an executable for the machine; <code>javac</code> produces <code>.class</code> files run by the Java Virtual Machine; the <code>python3</code> interpreter runs the source directly.
alg-f-668 · level ipotesi · notes
In Java ($32$-bit <code>int</code>), BinarySearch computes the middle index as <code>left + (right - left) / 2</code> instead of <code>(left + right) / 2</code>. For indices $0\le left\le right$, when does the second formula give a wrong result?
  1. Never: the two formulas are always equal
  2. When <code>left + right</code> exceeds <code>Integer.MAX_VALUE</code>
  3. When <code>left</code> and <code>right</code> are odd
  4. When <code>right - left</code> is negative
Explanation. Mathematically they coincide, but the sum of two large indices overflows and becomes negative. <code>right - left</code> never exceeds <code>right</code>, so the first formula is safe.
alg-f-669 · level ipotesi · notes
In Java, <code>int n = sc.nextInt();</code> (with <code>Scanner sc</code> on the keyboard) assumes that the next item typed is an integer. What happens if the user types <code>abc</code>?
  1. <code>n</code> gets $0$, the default value
  2. <code>n</code> gets the code of the character <code>a</code>
  3. The program does not compile at all
  4. Reading fails with an exception
Explanation. The item cannot be converted to <code>int</code>, so reading fails at run time. The compiler cannot know what the user will type.

Part: Asymptotics (70)

9. Asymptotic notation (36)

alg-f-062 · level facile · notes
Which condition defines $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$?
  1. For all $c_1,c_2\in\R_{>0}$ there exists $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le c_1\,g(n)\le f(n)\le c_2\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
  2. There exist $c_1,c_2\in\R_{>0}$ and $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le c_1\,g(n)\le f(n)\le c_2\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
  3. There exist $c\in\R_{>0}$ and $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le f(n)\le c\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
  4. $0\le g(n)\le f(n)\le 2\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge 1$.
Explanation. This is Definition 9.2: two positive constants and a common threshold. The condition with a single constant, $0\le f(n)\le c\,g(n)$, defines $\bigO$; with «for all $c_1,c_2$» the condition holds for no pair of positive functions.
alg-f-063 · level facile · notes
Which condition defines $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$?
  1. There exist $c\in\R_{>0}$ and $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le f(n)<c\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
  2. For all $c\in\R_{>0}$ there exists $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le c\,g(n)<f(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
  3. $f(n)<g(n)$ for all $n\in\N$.
  4. For all $c\in\R_{>0}$ there exists $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le f(n)<c\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
Explanation. This is Definition 9.9: the inequality must hold for every positive constant, however small. With «there exists $c$» one gets (essentially) the definition of $\bigO$; with $c\,g(n)<f(n)$ one has the definition of $\omega$.
alg-f-064 · level media · notes
To prove with the definition that $f(n)=3n^2+5n=\Theta(n^2)$, which choice of constants works?
  1. $c_1=3$, $c_2=4$, $n_1=5$
  2. $c_1=3$, $c_2=3$, $n_1=1$
  3. $c_1=4$, $c_2=8$, $n_1=1$
  4. $c_1=1$, $c_2=4$, $n_1=1$
Explanation. $3n^2\le 3n^2+5n$ always holds, and $3n^2+5n\le 4n^2$ is equivalent to $5n\le n^2$, i.e., $n\ge 5$. With $c_2=3$ the upper bound never holds, with $c_1=4$ the lower one holds only for $n\le 5$, with $n_1=1$ and $c_2=4$ the upper one fails for $n<5$.
alg-f-065 · level facile · notes
Which implication holds for every pair of functions $f$, $g$ of a natural variable?
  1. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$
  3. $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)=\Omega\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ f(n)=\omega\big(g(n)\big)$
Explanation. The definition of $o$ holds for every $c>0$, in particular for $c=1$, which is the definition of $\bigO$ (Observation 9.12). The converse implications are false: $n=\bigO(n)$ but $n\ne o(n)$.
alg-f-066 · level facile · notes
Let $f$, $g$ be asymptotically positive with $\lim_{n\to+\infty} f(n)/g(n)=0^+$. Which statement is true?
  1. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$ but $f(n)\ne\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$, hence also $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$
  3. $f(n)=\Omega\big(g(n)\big)$ but $f(n)\ne\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  4. $g(n)=o\big(f(n)\big)$, hence $f(n)=\omega\big(g(n)\big)$
Explanation. By the limit criterion, $L=0^+$ gives $f=o(g)$, hence $f=\bigO(g)$; $\Theta$ and $\Omega$ require $L\in\R_{>0}$ (or $+\infty$ for $\Omega$). Instead $g=\omega(f)$ holds.
alg-f-067 · level facile · notes
What is the order of growth of $n^2+n\log_2 n+7$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2\log_2 n)$
  2. $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $\Theta(n^3)$
Explanation. The sum of asymptotically non-negative functions is $\Theta$ of the maximum (Observation 9.7), and the lower-order terms can be dropped (Observation 9.20): $n^2$ remains.
alg-f-068 · level media · notes
Let $0\le c_1\,g(n)\le f(n)\le c_2\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$. With which constants do we prove that $f(n)+g(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$?
  1. $c_1$ and $c_2$, with the same threshold $n_1$
  2. $2c_1$ and $2c_2$, with threshold $2n_1$
  3. $c_1-1$ and $c_2+1$, with the same threshold $n_1$
  4. $c_1+1$ and $c_2+1$, with the same threshold $n_1$
Explanation. Adding $g(n)\ge 0$ to the three members: $(c_1+1)\,g(n)\le f(n)+g(n)\le(c_2+1)\,g(n)$ for $n\ge n_1$ (Observation 9.6). With $c_1-1$ the constant might not be positive.
alg-f-069 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$, $g$ be asymptotically positive and let $L=\lim_{n\to+\infty} f(n)/g(n)$ exist ($0^+$, $\ell\in\R_{>0}$ or $+\infty$). Which statement of the limit criterion is correct?
  1. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$ if and only if $L\in\R_{>0}$.
  2. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$ if and only if $L=0^+$ or $L\in\R_{>0}$.
  3. $f(n)=\Omega\big(g(n)\big)$ if and only if $L=+\infty$.
  4. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$ if and only if $L=1$.
Explanation. Proposition 9.18: $\bigO$ corresponds to a finite $L$ ($0^+$ or positive), $\Omega$ to a positive or infinite $L$, $\Theta$ to any $L\in\R_{>0}$ (for instance $3n^2=\Theta(n^2)$ with $L=3$).
alg-f-070 · level teorema · notes
Which of the following properties of the asymptotic notations holds for all $f$, $g$, $h$?
  1. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ g(n)=\Omega\big(f(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ g(n)=\bigO\big(f(n)\big)$
  3. $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ g(n)=o\big(f(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$ and $g(n)=\bigO\big(h(n)\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ f(n)=\Theta\big(h(n)\big)$
Explanation. This is transpose symmetry (Proposition 9.19). Symmetry holds only for $\Theta$: $n=\bigO(n^2)$ but $n^2\ne\bigO(n)$. The «mixed» transitivity with $\Theta$ and $\bigO$ is also false: $n=\Theta(n)$ and $n=\bigO(n^2)$, but $n\ne\Theta(n^2)$.
alg-f-071 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $f$, $g$ be asymptotically positive and $L=\lim_{n\to+\infty}f(n)/g(n)$. Which results are needed to prove that $L=\ell\in\R_{>0}$ or $L=0^+$ implies $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$?
  1. Limit $+\infty\Leftrightarrow\omega$, and $\omega\Rightarrow\Omega$; then transpose symmetry.
  2. The definition of $\Theta$ and the fact that $f+g=\Theta(\max\{f,g\})$.
  3. Limit in $\R_{>0}$ $\Rightarrow\Theta\Rightarrow\bigO$; limit $0^+\Leftrightarrow o$, and $o\Rightarrow\bigO$.
  4. The transitivity of $o$ and the symmetry of $\Theta$, applied to $f/g$.
Explanation. The two cases are handled separately: $\ell>0$ gives $\Theta$ and hence $\bigO$; $0^+$ gives $o$ and hence $\bigO$. The results on $+\infty$ and $\omega$ are needed for the criterion of $\Omega$.
alg-f-072 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation 9.11 states that $\lim_{n\to+\infty} f(n)/g(n)=0^+$ if and only if $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$. Which assumption on $f$ and $g$ does it require?
  1. That $f$ and $g$ are asymptotically positive, so that one can multiply and divide by $g(n)>0$.
  2. That $f$ and $g$ are asymptotically increasing, so that the ratio has a limit.
  3. That $g$ is bounded, so that the ratio $f(n)/g(n)$ does not diverge.
  4. That $f(n)\le g(n)$ for all $n\in\N$, not only from a threshold on.
Explanation. The proof goes from $0\le f(n)/g(n)<\varepsilon$ to $0\le f(n)<\varepsilon\,g(n)$ by multiplying by $g(n)$, and back: $g(n)>0$ (and $f(n)>0$ for the limit $0^+$) is needed from some $n$ on.
alg-f-222 · level media · notes
We have $f(n)=\omega\big(g(n)\big)$ if for every $c\in\R_{>0}$ there exists $n_1\in\N$ such that $0\le c\,g(n)<f(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$. With $f(n)=n^2/4$, $g(n)=n$ and $c=10$, what is the smallest threshold $n_1$ that satisfies the condition?
  1. $40$
  2. $10$
  3. $4$
  4. $41$
Explanation. $10\,n<n^2/4$ is equivalent to $n>40$ (for $n\ge 1$), so the smallest threshold is $n_1=41$; with $n=40$ equality holds, which is not enough because the inequality is strict. For every $c$ a threshold exists ($n_1=4c+1$): $f=\omega(g)$.
alg-f-223 · level teorema · notes
Let $f(n)=\bigO(n\log_2 n)$ and $g(n)=\Theta(n^2)$; moreover, $n\log_2 n=\bigO(n^2)$. Which conclusion follows from the relational properties (transitivity, symmetry, transpose symmetry)?
  1. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$
  3. $g(n)=\bigO\big(f(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)=\Omega\big(g(n)\big)$
Explanation. By transitivity $f=\bigO(n^2)$; by symmetry $n^2=\Theta(g)$, hence $n^2=\bigO(g)$, and again by transitivity $f=\bigO(g)$. An upper bound says nothing about a lower bound: $f$ might even be constant.
alg-f-224 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ and $g$ be asymptotically non-negative functions of a natural variable, with $g(n)=\bigO\big(f(n)\big)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $f(n)+g(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)+g(n)=o\big(f(n)\big)$
  3. $f(n)+g(n)=\Theta\big(f(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)+g(n)=\Theta\big(f(n)\,g(n)\big)$
Explanation. From $0\le g(n)\le c\,f(n)$ eventually it follows that $1\cdot f(n)\le f(n)+g(n)\le(1+c)\,f(n)$: lower-order terms can be dropped. This is why $3n^2+5n$ is $\Theta(n^2)$.
alg-f-225 · level facile · notes
About an algorithm we only know that its worst-case number of steps, on inputs of size $n$, is $S(n)=\bigO(n^2)$. What can be concluded?
  1. For $n$ large enough it performs at most $c\,n^2$ steps on every input of size $n$; it might also perform far fewer.
  2. For $n$ large enough it performs at least $c\,n^2$ steps on some input of size $n$, for a constant $c>0$.
  3. It performs exactly $c\,n^2$ steps on every input of size $n$, for a constant $c>0$ that depends on the algorithm.
  4. It performs $\Theta(n^2)$ steps on every input of size $n$, because $\bigO$ describes the worst case.
Explanation. $\bigO$ is an upper bound: it does not exclude, for instance, $S(n)=\Theta(n)$. A lower bound requires $\Omega$, and the exact order $\Theta$.
alg-f-283 · level teorema · notes
One writes $f(n)=O(g(n))$ if there are $c>0$ and $n_1 \in \N$ with $0 \le f(n) \le c\,g(n)$ for every $n \ge n_1$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $3n^2+5n=O(n)$, since $c$ can be chosen large
  2. $f(n)=O(g(n))$ implies $f(n) \le g(n)$ for every $n$
  3. $O(n^2)$ means «exactly quadratic»
  4. $3n^2+5n=O(n^2)$ and also $3n^2+5n=O(n^3)$
Explanation. $O$ is an upper bound: with $c=8$ and $n_1=1$ we have $3n^2+5n \le 8n^2 \le 8n^3$. No constant keeps $n^2$ below $c\,n$ forever; the inequality holds only from $n_1$ on and with the factor $c$.
alg-f-284 · level facile · notes
One writes $f(n)=\Omega(g(n))$ if there are $c>0$ and $n_1$ with $0 \le c\,g(n) \le f(n)$ for every $n \ge n_1$. Which statement is true?
  1. $n^2/10=\Omega(n)$
  2. $n=\Omega(n^2)$
  3. $\log_2 n=\Omega(n)$
  4. $5=\Omega(n)$
Explanation. $\Omega$ is a lower bound: $n^2/10 \ge 1 \cdot n$ for $n \ge 10$. In the other options the function on the left grows more slowly than the one on the right.
alg-f-285 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation: $f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$ if and only if $f(n)=O(g(n))$ and $f(n)=\Omega(g(n))$. How are the constants of $\Theta$ ($c_1\,g(n) \le f(n) \le c_2\,g(n)$ for $n \ge n_1$) obtained from those of $O$ and $\Omega$?
  1. $c_1=c_2$ always
  2. $n_1$ equal to the minimum of the two $n_1$
  3. $c_1$ from $\Omega$, $c_2$ from $O$, and $n_1$ equal to the maximum of the two $n_1$
  4. They are not: limits of ratios are needed
Explanation. From $n_1=\max$ on both inequalities hold. With the minimum, one of the two might not hold yet; limits are only a sufficient criterion.
alg-f-286 · level teorema · notes
Let $f,g$ be asymptotically nonnegative. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\max\{f(n),g(n)\}=\Theta(f(n)+g(n))$
  2. $f(n)+g(n)=\Theta(f(n)\,g(n))$
  3. $\max\{f(n),g(n)\}=o(f(n)+g(n))$
  4. $f(n)+g(n)=\Theta(\min\{f(n),g(n)\})$
Explanation. $\max \le f+g \le 2\max$: the sum is at most twice the maximum. This is why, in an algorithm with phases, the most expensive phase counts.
alg-f-287 · level facile · notes
For asymptotically positive functions, $\lim_{n \to \infty} f(n)/g(n)=+\infty$ if and only if $f(n)=\omega(g(n))$. With $f(n)=n\log_2 n$ and $g(n)=n$, what is concluded?
  1. $n\log_2 n=\Theta(n)$
  2. $n\log_2 n=o(n)$
  3. $n\log_2 n=\omega(n)$
  4. Nothing can be concluded
Explanation. $f(n)/g(n)=\log_2 n \to +\infty$, so $n\log_2 n$ grows strictly faster than $n$. $\Theta$ would need a finite positive limit.
alg-f-288 · level teorema · notes
Which implication between asymptotic notations is always true?
  1. $f(n)=\Omega(g(n)) \Rightarrow f(n)=\omega(g(n))$
  2. $f(n)=\omega(g(n)) \Rightarrow f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$
  3. $f(n)=O(g(n)) \Rightarrow f(n)=o(g(n))$
  4. $f(n)=\omega(g(n)) \Rightarrow f(n)=\Omega(g(n))$
Explanation. $\omega$ asks $f \ge c\,g$ eventually for every $c>0$, $\Omega$ for a single $c$: the first is stronger. The converse is false: $n=\Omega(n)$ but not $\omega(n)$.
alg-f-289 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation: if $\lim_{n \to \infty} f(n)/g(n)=\ell \in \R_{>0}$, then $f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$. Which choice of constants proves it, using the definition of limit?
  1. $c_1=c_2=\ell$
  2. $c_1=\ell/2$ and $c_2=2\ell$, for large $n$
  3. $c_1=0$ and $c_2=\ell$
  4. $c_1=1$ and $c_2=\ell+1$ for every $n \ge 1$
Explanation. With $\varepsilon=\ell/2$ the ratio is eventually in $[\ell/2,3\ell/2] \subseteq [\ell/2,2\ell]$. $c_1=0$ gives no lower bound; equality holds only in the limit.
alg-f-290 · level facile · notes
Limit criterion for $\Omega$: if $\lim f(n)/g(n)$ is a positive number or $+\infty$, then $f(n)=\Omega(g(n))$. For which pair does the criterion give $f=\Omega(g)$?
  1. $f(n)=n^2$, $g(n)=n^2+n$
  2. $f(n)=n$, $g(n)=n^2$
  3. $f(n)=\log_2 n$, $g(n)=n$
  4. $f(n)=1$, $g(n)=n$
Explanation. $n^2/(n^2+n) \to 1>0$, so $n^2=\Omega(n^2+n)$ (indeed $\Theta$). In the other pairs the ratio tends to $0$.
alg-f-366 · level facile · notes
In the graphical interpretation of $f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$, what is required of the values of $f$?
  1. They must lie between $c_1\,g(n)$ and $c_2\,g(n)$ for every $n \ge 1$
  2. From a threshold $n_1$ on they must lie between $c_1\,g(n)$ and $c_2\,g(n)$; before $n_1$ nothing
  3. They must coincide with $g(n)$ from $n_1$ on
  4. They must stay below $g(n)$ for every $n$
Explanation. Asymptotic notations describe the eventual behaviour: for $n<n_1$ the function may do anything. The constants $c_1,c_2$ allow a «band» around $g$.
alg-f-367 · level facile · notes
In the graph illustrating $f(n)=O(g(n))$, the curve of $f$ lies below the curve $c\,g(n)$ from some $n_1$ on. What can be said about the curve $c\,g(n)$ with respect to $f$ for $n<n_1$?
  1. It must lie above $f$ also for $n<n_1$
  2. It must cross $f$ exactly at $n_1$
  3. It must coincide with $f$
  4. Nothing: for $n<n_1$ the curve of $f$ may lie above $c\,g(n)$
Explanation. The definition asks $f(n) \le c\,g(n)$ only for $n \ge n_1$. In the graph $n_1$ is often the crossing point, but any larger threshold works.
alg-f-368 · level facile · notes
It is known that $f(n)=O(n^2)$ and that $f(n)=\Omega(n^2)$. Which conclusion is correct?
  1. $f(n)=o(n^2)$
  2. $f(n)=\omega(n^2)$
  3. $f(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  4. Nothing can be said about $\Theta$
Explanation. An upper and a lower bound of the same order give the tight bound: $\Theta$ is equivalent to $O$ and $\Omega$ together.
alg-f-511 · level facile · notes
$f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$ if there are $c_1,c_2>0$ and $n_1$ with $c_1\,g(n)\le f(n)\le c_2\,g(n)$ for every $n\ge n_1$. Which function is NOT $\Theta(n^2)$?
  1. $n^2/100+n$
  2. $5n^2-3n$
  3. $(n+1)^2$
  4. $n^2\log_2 n$
Explanation. $n^2\log_2 n/n^2=\log_2 n$ is unbounded, so no constant $c_2$ works. The other three, divided by $n^2$, tend to a positive constant ($1/100$, $5$, $1$).
alg-f-512 · level facile · notes
$f(n)=o(g(n))$ if for every $c>0$ there is $n_1$ with $0\le f(n)<c\,g(n)$ for every $n\ge n_1$; $\omega$ is the symmetric relation. Which statement is true?
  1. $2n=o(n)$
  2. $n\log_2 n=o(n^2)$
  3. $n^2=o(n^2+n)$
  4. $n^2=\omega(n^2-n)$
Explanation. $n\log_2 n/n^2=\log_2 n/n\to 0$. Instead $2n<c\,n$ fails for $c\le 2$, and $n^2/(n^2+n)\to 1$: functions of the same order are never $o$ of each other.
alg-f-513 · level teorema · notes
We have $3n=\bigO(n)$ (there are $c>0$ and $n_1$ with $3n\le c\,n$ for $n\ge n_1$). Why instead is $3n\ne o(n)$, where $o$ requires $3n<c\,n$ eventually for EVERY $c>0$?
  1. Because $o$ requires $f$ to be decreasing
  2. Because $3n$ is not asymptotically non-negative
  3. Because $o$ is used only for logarithmic functions
  4. Because for $c\le 3$ the inequality $3n<c\,n$ fails for every $n\ge 1$
Explanation. In $\bigO$ one constant suffices ($c=3$); in $o$ all are needed, even the smallest, and with $c=1$ one would need $3n<n$. $o(g)$ means «negligible with respect to $g$».
alg-f-514 · level ipotesi · notes
Asymptotic notations are transitive: from $f=\bigO(g)$ and $g=\bigO(h)$ it follows that $f=\bigO(h)$. Suppose instead that $f(n)=\bigO(g(n))$ and $h(n)=\bigO(g(n))$. What follows between $f$ and $h$?
  1. Nothing, in general
  2. That $f(n)=\bigO(h(n))$
  3. That $f(n)=\Theta(h(n))$
  4. That $h(n)=\bigO(f(n))$
Explanation. The two hypotheses give only upper bounds with the same $g$, and transitivity does not apply. With $g=n^2$: $f=n$, $h=n^2$ gives $h\ne\bigO(f)$; $f=n^2$, $h=n$ gives $f\ne\bigO(h)$.
alg-f-515 · level teorema · notes
Among the relational properties of asymptotic notations, which holds for $\Theta$ but NOT for $\bigO$?
  1. Transitivity: $f=\Theta(g)$, $g=\Theta(h)$ give $f=\Theta(h)$
  2. Reflexivity: $f=\Theta(f)$ for every $f$
  3. Symmetry: $f=\Theta(g)$ if and only if $g=\Theta(f)$
  4. Passing to multiples: $c\,f=\Theta(f)$ for $c>0$
Explanation. $n=\bigO(n^2)$ but $n^2\ne\bigO(n)$: $\bigO$ is not symmetric (instead $f=\bigO(g)\Leftrightarrow g=\Omega(f)$ holds). Transitivity and reflexivity hold for $\Theta$, $\bigO$ and $\Omega$.
alg-f-516 · level facile · notes
Algorithm A performs $\Theta(n^2)$ steps and algorithm B $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ steps on inputs of size $n$. Which statement is correct?
  1. B is faster from some $n$ on, not necessarily before
  2. B performs fewer steps than A for every value $n\ge 1$
  3. A and B are equivalent, because both are polynomial
  4. A performs fewer steps than B for every value $n\ge 1$
Explanation. Asymptotic notations hide constants and thresholds: for instance $n^2$ against $100\,n\log_2 n$ favours A while $n<100\log_2 n$. For large $n$ the smaller order of growth wins.
alg-f-517 · level teorema · notes
An algorithm performs, on inputs of size $n$, $\Theta(n^2)$ steps in the worst case and $\Theta(n)$ in the best case (like Insertion Sort). Which statement about its number of steps $T$ on an arbitrary input of size $n$ is correct?
  1. $T=\Theta(n^2)$ on every input
  2. $T=\Theta(n)$ on every input
  3. $T=\bigO(n^2)$ and $T=\Omega(n)$
  4. $T=\Theta(n^{1.5})$ on every input
Explanation. Every input takes at most as long as the worst case and at least as long as the best. One cannot say $\Theta(n^2)$ for all inputs: on a sorted array the steps are $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-670 · level ipotesi · notes
In the definition of $f(n)=\bigO(g(n))$ one asks $0\le f(n)\le c\,g(n)$ only for every $n\ge n_1$. What would happen asking it for every $n\ge 1$?
  1. Nothing: the two definitions are equivalent
  2. Every function would be $\bigO$ of every other
  3. $n=\bigO(n\log_2 n)$ would become false, because $\log_2 1=0$
  4. One could choose $c=0$
Explanation. For $n=1$ one would need $1\le c\cdot 0$, false for every $c$. The threshold $n_1$ serves to ignore small values: only the behaviour for large $n$ counts.
alg-f-671 · level ipotesi · notes
The limit criterion requires $\lim_{n\to+\infty}f(n)/g(n)$ to exist. With $f(n)=n\,(2+(-1)^n)$ and $g(n)=n$ the ratio alternates between $1$ and $3$. What can be concluded?
  1. $f(n)=\Theta(n)$ by the definition ($c_1=1$, $c_2=3$)
  2. $f$ and $g$ are not comparable
  3. $f(n)=o(n)$, because the limit does not exist
  4. $f(n)=\omega(n)$, because the ratio does not converge
Explanation. Without a limit the criterion does not apply, but the definition does: $n\le f(n)\le 3n$ for every $n$. The criterion is a sufficient condition, not a necessary one.
alg-f-672 · level ipotesi · notes
For asymptotically non-negative functions $\max\{f(n),g(n)\}=\Theta(f(n)+g(n))$. What happens dropping the non-negativity hypothesis, for instance with $f(n)=n$ and $g(n)=-n$?
  1. It still holds, with the same constants
  2. It holds with $c_1=0$
  3. It fails: $f+g=0$ while $\max\{f,g\}=n$
  4. It fails because $\max\{f,g\}=-n$
Explanation. The proof uses $\max\{f,g\}\le f+g\le 2\max\{f,g\}$, which requires $f,g\ge 0$. With negative terms the sum can vanish.

10. Asymptotic growth (34)

alg-f-073 · level facile · notes
A function $f(n)$ has a sub-linear order of growth if $f(n)=o(n)$. Which function has a sub-linear order of growth?
  1. $n\log_2 n$
  2. $\frac{n}{2}+5$
  3. $n^{0.9}$
  4. $n^{1.1}$
Explanation. Sub-linear means $f(n)=o(n)$: for $\alpha<1$ we have $n^\alpha=o(n)$ (Observation 10.19). $\frac n2+5=\Theta(n)$ is linear, $n\log_2 n$ and $n^{1.1}$ are super-linear.
alg-f-074 · level facile · notes
Which relation holds between $\log_2(5n)$ and $\log_{10} n$?
  1. $\log_2(5n)=\omega(\log_{10} n)$
  2. $\log_2(5n)=\Theta(\log_{10} n)$
  3. $\log_2(5n)=o(\log_{10} n)$
  4. Neither is $\bigO$ of the other
Explanation. $\log_2(5n)=\log_2 5+\log_2 n$ and $\log_2 n=\log_{10}n/\log_{10}2$: the ratio tends to $1/\log_{10}2\in\R_{>0}$ (Observation 10.13). The base and constant factors in the argument do not matter.
alg-f-075 · level facile · notes
Which statement about exponential functions is true?
  1. $2^{2n}=\Theta(2^n)$
  2. $2^{n/2}=\Theta(2^n)$
  3. $3^n=\Theta(2^n)$
  4. $2^{2n}=\omega(2^n)$
Explanation. $2^{2n}/2^n=2^n\to+\infty$: a constant factor in the exponent changes the order (Observation 10.22), as does a different base (10.21). Only a shift of the exponent leaves it unchanged: $2^{n+5}=\Theta(2^n)$.
alg-f-076 · level facile · notes
What is the order of growth of the polynomial $4n^3-100n^2+7$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2)$
  2. $\Theta(n^3)$
  3. $\bigO(n^3)$ but not $\Theta(n^3)$, because of the negative coefficient
  4. $\Theta(n^5)$
Explanation. By Observation 10.20 a polynomial of degree $k$ with leading coefficient $a_k>0$ is $\Theta(n^k)$: the ratio with $n^3$ tends to $4$. Negative coefficients of the lower-degree terms do not matter.
alg-f-077 · level facile · notes
Which relation holds between $n\log_2 n$ and $n^{1.01}$?
  1. $n\log_2 n=\omega(n^{1.01})$
  2. $n\log_2 n=\Theta(n^{1.01})$
  3. It depends on the base of the logarithm
  4. $n\log_2 n=o(n^{1.01})$
Explanation. $\frac{n\log_2 n}{n^{1.01}}=\frac{\log_2 n}{n^{0.01}}\to 0^+$ because every positive power grows faster than every logarithm (Observation 10.15). For small $n$, $n\log_2 n$ is larger, but only the asymptotic behaviour counts.
alg-f-078 · level facile · notes
Which statement is true?
  1. $n!=o(100^n)$
  2. $n!=\Theta(n^n)$
  3. $100^n=o(n!)$
  4. $n^{100}=\omega(2^n)$
Explanation. For every base $b>1$ we have $b^n=o(n!)$ (Observation 10.9), also for $b=100$; moreover $n!=o(n^n)$ and $n^{100}=o(2^n)$.
alg-f-079 · level facile · notes
A function $f(n)$ is polynomially bounded if $f(n)=\bigO(n^\alpha)$ for some constant $\alpha>0$. Which function is not polynomially bounded?
  1. $1.01^n$
  2. $n^{100}$
  3. $n^5\log_2 n$
  4. $\sqrt n$
Explanation. Polynomially bounded means $f(n)=\bigO(n^\alpha)$ for some $\alpha>0$. For every $\alpha$ we have $n^\alpha=o(1.01^n)$, so $1.01^n$ is not (it is exponentially bounded); $n^5\log_2 n=\bigO(n^6)$.
alg-f-080 · level teorema · notes
Which statement on the comparison between powers of the logarithm $\log_a^\beta n$ and powers $n^\alpha$ is correct?
  1. The limit $\lim_{n\to+\infty}\log_a^\beta n/n^\alpha$ is $0^+$ only if $\alpha\ge 1$.
  2. The limit is $0^+$ only if $\alpha>\beta$.
  3. For $\alpha=\beta$ the limit is $1$.
  4. For every $\alpha\in\R_{>0}$, $\beta\in\R_{>0}$ and $a\in\R_{>1}$ we have $\lim_{n\to+\infty}\log_a^\beta n/n^\alpha=0^+$.
Explanation. Every power of the logarithm, even with a large $\beta$, is $o$ of every positive power of $n$, even with a small $\alpha$: for instance $\log_2^{10} n=o(n^{0.1})$.
alg-f-081 · level ipotesi · notes
How do we prove that $\log_a^\beta n=o(n^\alpha)$ for all $\alpha,\beta>0$ and $a>1$ ($o$: $f=o(g)$ if $0\le f(n)<c\,g(n)$ eventually, for every $c>0$)?
  1. With the change of base $\log_a n=\log_2 n/\log_2 a$, which reduces everything to $a=2$, without limits.
  2. With the limit $\lim_{n\to+\infty}\log_a^\beta n/n^\alpha=0^+$ and the limit criterion ($L=0^+\Leftrightarrow o$).
  3. With the limit $\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^\alpha/b^n=0^+$ between powers and exponentials, for $b>1$.
  4. With the fact that $\log_a^\beta n$ and $n^\alpha$ are both asymptotically increasing.
Explanation. All the observations of the hierarchy have the same structure: a known limit of the hierarchy of infinities, then the limit criterion to translate it into the notation $o$.
alg-f-226 · level facile · notes
A function $f$ of a natural variable is asymptotically increasing if there exists $n_1$ such that $f(n)<f(n+1)$ for all $n\ge n_1$. Which of the following functions is not asymptotically increasing?
  1. $f(n)=n^2-10n$
  2. $f(n)=\log_2 n$
  3. $f(n)=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$
  4. $f(n)=2^n/n$
Explanation. $\lfloor 2k/2\rfloor=\lfloor(2k+1)/2\rfloor=k$: the strict inequality fails for infinitely many $n$. $n^2-10n$ increases from $n=5$ on, and $2^{n+1}/(n+1)>2^n/n$ for $n>1$.
alg-f-227 · level facile · notes
The families of increasing functions of a natural variable include the logarithms $\log_a n$, the powers $n^\alpha$ and the exponentials $b^n$. For which values of the parameters are these functions asymptotically increasing?
  1. $a>1$, $\alpha>0$, $b>1$
  2. $a>0$, $\alpha>0$, $b>0$
  3. $a>1$, $\alpha\ge 1$, $b>1$
  4. $a\ge 1$, $\alpha>0$, $b\ge 1$
Explanation. With $0<a<1$ the logarithm decreases and with $a=1$ it is not defined; $n^\alpha$ increases for every $\alpha>0$ (also $\sqrt n$); $b^n$ increases only for $b>1$, while $1^n$ is constant.
alg-f-228 · level teorema · notes
Which statement of the hierarchy of infinities (limits as $n\to+\infty$ of functions of a natural variable) is correct?
  1. We have $\lim b^n/n!=0^+$ only for $1<b<e$; for $b\ge e$ the limit is $+\infty$.
  2. For every $b\in\R_{>1}$ we have $\lim b^n/n!=0^+$.
  3. We have $\lim n!/n^n=+\infty$, because $n!$ has $n$ factors like $n^n$.
  4. For $\alpha>b>1$ we have $\lim n^\alpha/b^n=+\infty$.
Explanation. The ratio test gives $\frac{b^{n+1}/(n+1)!}{b^n/n!}=\frac{b}{n+1}\to 0$ for every base $b$. Moreover $n!/n^n\to 0^+$ and $n^\alpha/b^n\to 0^+$ for every $\alpha>0$, $b>1$.
alg-f-291 · level teorema · notes
For every constant $\alpha>0$ and $b>1$, which statement about the hierarchy of infinities is correct?
  1. $\lim_{n \to \infty} n^{\alpha}/b^{n}=+\infty$ if $\alpha>b$
  2. $n^{100}$ grows faster than $1.01^n$
  3. $\lim_{n \to \infty} n^{\alpha}/b^{n}=0$: every exponential beats every power
  4. The limit depends on the ratio between $\alpha$ and $b$
Explanation. Whatever the exponent $\alpha$ and whatever base $b>1$, the exponential wins: even $n^{100}=o(1.01^n)$, although the overtaking happens for very large $n$.
alg-f-292 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: $\lim_{n \to \infty} n!/n^n=0$. The proof uses the ratio test. Which fact closes the computation?
  1. The ratio of consecutive terms is $(n/(n+1))^n \to 1/e<1$
  2. $n! \le n$ for every $n$
  3. $n^n$ is a polynomial
  4. The ratio of consecutive terms tends to $1$
Explanation. $\frac{(n+1)!}{(n+1)^{n+1}}\cdot\frac{n^n}{n!}=\left(\frac{n}{n+1}\right)^n \to e^{-1}<1$, so the sequence tends to $0$. A ratio tending to $1$ would not allow a conclusion.
alg-f-293 · level teorema · notes
Limit criterion: let $L=\lim f(n)/g(n)$ for asymptotically positive functions, with $L=0$, $L \in \R_{>0}$ or $L=+\infty$. Which correspondence is correct?
  1. $L=0 \Leftrightarrow f=\omega(g)$
  2. $L \in \R_{>0} \Leftrightarrow f=o(g)$
  3. $L=0 \Leftrightarrow f=o(g)$; $L \in \R_{>0} \Rightarrow f=\Theta(g)$; $L=+\infty \Leftrightarrow f=\omega(g)$
  4. $L=1 \Leftrightarrow f=O(g)$, and in the other cases nothing follows
Explanation. A ratio tending to $0$ says $f$ is negligible with respect to $g$, a diverging one that $g$ is negligible with respect to $f$, a finite positive limit that they have the same order.
alg-f-294 · level facile · notes
Among the functions $n^5$, $2^n$, $n^2\log_2 n$, $1.5^n$, which grows most slowly as $n$ tends to infinity?
  1. $n^5$
  2. $n^2\log_2 n$
  3. $1.5^n$
  4. $2^n$
Explanation. Every power is $o$ of every exponential with base $>1$, and $n^2\log_2 n=o(n^5)$. Among the exponentials, $1.5^n=o(2^n)$.
alg-f-295 · level facile · notes
Which relation between $10^n$ and $n!$ is correct?
  1. $n!=o(10^n)$
  2. $10^n=\Theta(n!)$
  3. It depends on $n$: they cannot be compared asymptotically
  4. $10^n=o(n!)$
Explanation. For every base $b>1$, $b^n=o(n!)$: from $n>2b$ on every new factor of $n!$ is much larger than $b$. For small $n$ $10^n$ is larger, but the behaviour at infinity counts.
alg-f-296 · level facile · notes
Which of the following functions grows fastest: $n!$, $n^n$, $2^{n}$, $n^{10}$?
  1. $n!$
  2. $n^n$
  3. $2^n$
  4. $n^{10}$
Explanation. The hierarchy is $n^{10}=o(2^n)$, $2^n=o(n!)$, $n!=o(n^n)$: in $n^n$ all $n$ factors equal $n$, in $n!$ only the last one.
alg-f-297 · level teorema · notes
For constants $a>1$ and $\lambda>0$, which statement about the logarithm is correct?
  1. $\log_a(n+\lambda)=\omega(\log_a n)$
  2. $\log_a(\lambda n)=o(\log_a n)$
  3. $\log_a(n+\lambda)=\Theta(n)$
  4. $\log_a(n+\lambda)=\Theta(\log_a n)$
Explanation. $\log_a(n+\lambda)/\log_a n \to 1$: shifting the argument does not change the order. Also $\log_a(\lambda n)=\log_a\lambda+\log_a n=\Theta(\log_a n)$.
alg-f-298 · level facile · notes
An algorithm $A$ takes $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ steps and an algorithm $B$ takes $\Theta(n)$. What can be said for large $n$?
  1. They are equivalent, since $\log_2 n$ is almost constant
  2. $A$ is faster, since $\log_2 n<n$
  3. $B$ is asymptotically faster, since $n\log_2 n=\omega(n)$
  4. They cannot be compared
Explanation. $n\log_2 n/n=\log_2 n \to \infty$: the quasi-linear function grows strictly faster than the linear one, though slowly. Hidden constants matter only for small $n$.
alg-f-299 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation: for $\alpha,\lambda>0$, $(n+\lambda)^{\alpha}=\Theta(n^{\alpha})$. Which computation proves it?
  1. $(n+\lambda)^{\alpha}/n^{\alpha}=(1+\lambda/n)^{\alpha} \to 1$, a finite positive limit
  2. $(n+\lambda)^{\alpha}=n^{\alpha}+\lambda^{\alpha}$
  3. $(n+\lambda)^{\alpha} \le n^{\alpha}$ for every $n$
  4. The binomial expansion holds only for integer $\alpha$, so the observation holds only for $\alpha \in \N$
Explanation. The limit criterion with limit $1$ gives $\Theta$, for every real $\alpha>0$. $(n+\lambda)^\alpha \ne n^\alpha+\lambda^\alpha$ in general.
alg-f-300 · level facile · notes
Which relation is correct for $\lambda=5$ and $\alpha=3$?
  1. $(5n)^3=\omega(n^3)$
  2. $(5n)^3=\Theta(n^{15})$
  3. $(5n)^3=\Theta(n^3)$
  4. $(5n)^3=o(n^3)$
Explanation. $(5n)^3=125\,n^3$: a multiplicative constant does not change the order of growth. Scaling the argument of an exponential, instead, does change it.
alg-f-301 · level facile · notes
Which function has sub-linear order of growth, that is, is $o(n)$?
  1. $n^{0.9}$
  2. $n^{1.1}$
  3. $n$
  4. $2n+3$
Explanation. $n^{\alpha}=o(n)$ if $\alpha<1$ and $\omega(n)$ if $\alpha>1$; $2n+3=\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-302 · level teorema · notes
For constants $b,c>1$, which statement about exponentials with different bases is correct?
  1. $b^n=\Theta(c^n)$ for every $b,c>1$
  2. $3^n=\Theta(2^n)$, since they differ by a constant
  3. If $b>c$ then $b^n=\omega(c^n)$: different bases give different orders
  4. $b^n=o(c^n)$ if $b>c$
Explanation. $b^n/c^n=(b/c)^n \to \infty$ if $b>c$: unlike logarithms, the base of an exponential matters. $3^n/2^n=1.5^n$ is not bounded.
alg-f-303 · level facile · notes
Which relation between $2^{2n}$ and $2^n$ is correct?
  1. $2^{2n}=\omega(2^n)$
  2. $2^{2n}=\Theta(2^n)$
  3. $2^{2n}=o(2^n)$
  4. $2^{2n}=2\cdot 2^n$
Explanation. $2^{2n}=4^n$ and $4^n/2^n=2^n \to \infty$: scaling the exponent changes the order of growth. $2\cdot 2^n=2^{n+1}$ is instead a shift.
alg-f-304 · level facile · notes
Which relation between $2^{n+10}$ and $2^n$ is correct?
  1. $2^{n+10}=\omega(2^n)$
  2. $2^{n+10}=o(2^n)$
  3. $2^{n+10}=\Theta(2^{10n})$
  4. $2^{n+10}=\Theta(2^n)$
Explanation. $2^{n+10}=1024\cdot 2^n$: shifting the exponent multiplies by a constant, and the order does not change.
alg-f-369 · level facile · notes
What is the order of growth of the polynomial $f(n)=0.01\,n^4-100\,n^3+5000\,n$?
  1. $\Theta(n^4)$
  2. $\Theta(n^3)$
  3. $\Theta(n)$
  4. $O(1)$, since the leading coefficient is small
Explanation. A polynomial with positive leading coefficient has the order of its highest-degree term, whatever the other coefficients: $f(n)/n^4 \to 0.01>0$.
alg-f-370 · level facile · notes
The functions $\log_2 n$, $\sqrt{n}$, $n$, $n\log_2 n$, $n^2$, $2^n$ are sorted by order of growth. Which pair is in the wrong order (the first grows faster than the second)?
  1. $\log_2 n$ before $\sqrt{n}$
  2. $n\log_2 n$ before $n$
  3. $n^2$ before $2^n$
  4. $\sqrt{n}$ before $n$
Explanation. The correct chain is $\log_2 n=o(\sqrt{n})$, $\sqrt{n}=o(n)$, $n=o(n\log_2 n)$, $n\log_2 n=o(n^2)$, $n^2=o(2^n)$: putting $n\log_2 n$ before $n$ reverses a pair.
alg-f-371 · level facile · notes
An algorithm performs $f(n)=3n^3+2n^2\log_2 n+7$ steps. What is its order of growth?
  1. $\Theta(n^2\log_2 n)$
  2. $\Theta(n^5\log_2 n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^3\log_2 n)$
  4. $\Theta(n^3)$
Explanation. $n^2\log_2 n=o(n^3)$ and constants do not matter: the dominant term is $3n^3$. Terms add up, they do not multiply.
alg-f-518 · level media · notes
A computer performs about $10^{10}$ operations per second, and a year has about $3\cdot 10^7$ seconds. An algorithm performs $2^n$ operations (with $2^{10}\approx 10^3$). How long does it take for $n=100$?
  1. About $10$ seconds
  2. About $4$ and a half hours
  3. About $100$ years
  4. About $4\cdot 10^{12}$ years
Explanation. $2^{100}=(2^{10})^{10}\approx 10^{30}$ operations, that is $10^{20}$ seconds, about $3\cdot 10^{12}$–$4\cdot 10^{12}$ years: far more than the age of the universe. With $n^2=10^4$ operations a microsecond would suffice.
alg-f-519 · level facile · notes
$f$ is asymptotically increasing if there is $n_1$ with $f(n)<f(n+1)$ for every $n\ge n_1$. The factorial has $0!=1!=1$. What is the smallest valid $n_1$ for $f(n)=n!$?
  1. $n_1=0$
  2. $n_1=1$
  3. $n_1=2$
  4. None: $n!$ is not asymptotically increasing
Explanation. For $n=0$ the inequality $0!<1!$ is false ($1<1$); for every $n\ge 1$ we have $n!<(n+1)!=(n+1)\cdot n!$. It suffices that the property holds from some point on.
alg-f-673 · level ipotesi · notes
Inside $\Theta$ the base of the logarithm does not matter: $\log_a n=\log_b n/\log_b a=\Theta(\log_b n)$ for constant bases $a,b>1$. What happens if the base depends on $n$, as in $\log_n n$?
  1. $\log_n n=1$, which is not $\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  2. It still holds: $\log_n n=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  3. $\log_n n=n$
  4. $\log_n n$ is undefined for every $n$
Explanation. $\log_n n=\log_2 n/\log_2 n=1$: the factor $1/\log_2 a$ is constant only if $a$ is constant. Here it depends on $n$ and changes the order of growth.
alg-f-674 · level ipotesi · notes
For logarithms the base does not matter inside $\Theta$. Does the same hold for exponentials, that is $2^n=\Theta(3^n)$?
  1. Yes: the bases differ by a constant
  2. Yes, because both are exponential
  3. No: $2^n=\omega(3^n)$
  4. No: $2^n/3^n=(2/3)^n\to 0$, so $2^n=o(3^n)$
Explanation. $3^n=2^{n\log_2 3}$: changing base multiplies the exponent by a constant, and this changes the order. For logarithms, instead, only a constant factor in front changes.
alg-f-675 · level ipotesi · notes
Shifting the argument of a power does not change the order of growth: $(n+1)^3=\Theta(n^3)$. Does the same hold for the factorial, that is $(n+1)!=\Theta(n!)$?
  1. Yes: $(n+1)!$ and $n!$ differ by a constant
  2. No: $(n+1)!/n!=n+1\to+\infty$
  3. Yes, as for every increasing function
  4. No: $(n+1)!=o(n!)$
Explanation. For powers $(n+1)^3/n^3\to 1$; for the factorial the ratio is $n+1$, which grows: $n!=o((n+1)!)$. Shifting is harmless only for some families of functions.

Part: Analysis (92)

11. Input size (31)

alg-f-082 · level facile · notes
What is the input size $|I|$ of an instance $I$?
  1. The number of entries of the input array, whatever their values.
  2. The value of the largest number in the input.
  3. The number of lines of pseudo-code executed on the instance $I$.
  4. The total number of bits needed to represent the instance $I$ in binary.
Explanation. This is Definition 11.1. The number $n$ of entries of an array and the value of a number are natural parameters useful to count the steps, but the input size is measured in bits.
alg-f-083 · level facile · notes
The input is the single natural number $n=1000$. What is the input size?
  1. $|I|=1000$
  2. $|I|=10$
  3. $|I|=4$
  4. $|I|=9$
Explanation. A natural number $n\ge 1$ requires $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ bits: $\lfloor\log_2 1000\rfloor+1=10$. The value $1000$ is exponential in $|I|$; $4$ is the number of decimal digits.
alg-f-084 · level facile · notes
The input is an array $a[1..n]$ whose entries can all be represented with at most a constant number of bits. Which relation holds?
  1. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  2. $|I|=\Theta(n^2)$
  3. $|I|=\Theta(n)$
  4. $|I|=\Theta(1)$
Explanation. Each entry requires at least $1$ and at most $c$ bits, hence $n\le|I|\le c\,n$ (Corollary 11.9): the order of growth is that of $n$.
alg-f-085 · level facile · notes
An algorithm receives a single natural number $n$ and executes $\Theta(n)$ steps. What is its worst-case running time as a function of $|I|$?
  1. Exponential: $T(|I|)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  2. Linear: $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|)$
  3. Logarithmic: $T(|I|)=\Theta(\log_2|I|)$
  4. Quadratic: $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|^2)$
Explanation. For a number we have $2^{|I|-1}\le n\le 2^{|I|}-1$ (Observation 11.12): the value $n$ is exponential in the input size, and so is every number of steps $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-086 · level facile · notes
The input consists of two natural numbers $n,m\ge 1$ and $M=\max\{n,m\}$. Which relation holds for $|I|=(\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1)+(\lfloor\log_2 m\rfloor+1)$?
  1. $|I|=\Theta(M)$
  2. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n\cdot\log_2 m)$
  3. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 M)$
  4. $|I|=\Theta(n+m)$
Explanation. One of the two numbers is $M$, hence $|I|\ge\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1\ge\log_2 M$; each requires at most the bits of $M$, hence $|I|\le 2(\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1)$ (Observation 11.6).
alg-f-087 · level teorema · notes
Let $a[1..n]$ be an array of natural numbers with $a[i]\ge 1$ and $M=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[n]\}$. Which statement about the input size $|I|=\sum_{i=1}^n(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1)$ is correct?
  1. $|I|=\bigO\big(n(1+\log_2 M)\big)$ and $|I|=\Omega(n)$.
  2. $|I|=\Theta(n)$ for every array, whatever the values.
  3. $|I|=\Omega\big(n\log_2 M\big)$ for every array.
  4. $|I|=\bigO(\log_2 M)$, since only the largest entry counts.
Explanation. Observations 11.7 and 11.8: each entry uses at most the bits of $M$ and at least one bit. $\Theta(n)$ holds only with entries of constant size; with a single large entry $n\log_2 M$ is not a lower bound.
alg-f-088 · level ipotesi · notes
The input is an array $a[1..n]$ and $|I|$ is the total number of bits needed to write it. Which steps prove that $|I|=\Theta(n)$ if each entry has at most $c$ bits ($c$ constant)?
  1. It is enough that $|I|=\bigO\big(n(1+\log_2 M)\big)$ with $M$ the maximum, since $\log_2 M$ is a constant.
  2. $|I|\le c\,n$ gives $\bigO(n)$; each entry has at least one bit, hence $\Omega(n)$; $\bigO$ and $\Omega$ together give $\Theta$.
  3. Each entry $a[i]$ needs $\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1$ bits, and the limit criterion on $|I|/n$ gives $1$.
  4. Two entries need $\Theta(\log_2\max)$ bits, and by induction $n$ entries need $\Theta(n)$ of them.
Explanation. The upper bound comes from the assumption on the bits, the lower one from the fact that each entry needs at least one bit; together they give $\Theta$. The bound $\bigO(n(1+\log_2 M))$ alone only gives $\bigO$.
alg-f-089 · level facile · notes
When is an algorithm called polynomial?
  1. When it executes $\bigO(n^k)$ steps, where $n$ is the value of the input number.
  2. When its pseudo-code contains a constant number of nested loops.
  3. When $T(|I|)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$.
  4. When its worst-case running time satisfies $T(|I|)=\bigO(|I|^k)$ for some constant $k\in\N$.
Explanation. Definition 11.11 uses the input size in bits. A number of steps polynomial in the value $n$ of a number can be exponential in $|I|$ (as for PrimalityTest); $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$ defines the exponential algorithms.
alg-f-229 · level facile · notes
The input size $|I|$ is the total number of bits needed to write the instance in binary (a natural number $x\ge 1$ needs $\lfloor\log_2 x\rfloor+1$ bits). The input is the array $a=[6,1,3,8]$ together with the number $n=4$ of its entries. What is $|I|$?
  1. $10$
  2. $4$
  3. $16$
  4. $13$
Explanation. The entries need $3+1+2+4=10$ bits and the number $n=4=(100)_2$ needs $3$ more: $|I|=13$. Counting only the entries (without $n$) gives $10$; counting $4$ bits for each entry gives $16$.
alg-f-230 · level facile · notes
To prove with the definition that an input size $f(n)$ is $\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$, what must be determined?
  1. One constant $c>0$ and a threshold $n_1$ with $0\le f(n)\le c\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$: the lower bound is automatic.
  2. The limit of $f(n)/g(n)$ as $n\to+\infty$, which must be exactly $1$ for $\Theta$ to hold.
  3. Two constants $c_1,c_2>0$ and a threshold $n_1$ with $0\le c_1\,g(n)\le f(n)\le c_2\,g(n)$ for all $n\ge n_1$.
  4. Two constants $c_1,c_2>0$ with $c_1\,g(n)\le f(n)\le c_2\,g(n)$ for at least one value of $n$.
Explanation. $\Theta$ requires both the upper bound ($\bigO$, constant $c_2$) and the lower bound ($\Omega$, constant $c_1$), for all $n$ from a threshold on. Any finite positive limit, not only $1$, gives $\Theta$.
alg-f-231 · level ipotesi · notes
The limit criterion recalled in the chapter states: if $\lim_{n\to+\infty}f(n)/g(n)=\ell$, then $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$. Which hypotheses does it require?
  1. $g(n)>0$ for every $n\ge 1$, so that the ratio is defined, and $\ell\in\R_{>0}$ finite and positive.
  2. $\ell=1$ exactly; with another finite value one only obtains $f(n)=\bigO\big(g(n)\big)$.
  3. $f$ and $g$ asymptotically increasing, with $f(n)\le g(n)$ for every $n\ge 1$.
  4. None: the criterion gives $\Theta$ also when the limit is $0^+$ or $+\infty$.
Explanation. With $\ell\in\R_{>0}$, for large $n$ the ratio lies between $\ell/2$ and $2\ell$, which give the constants $c_1$ and $c_2$. A limit $0^+$ gives $f=o(g)$ and a limit $+\infty$ gives $f=\omega(g)$, not $\Theta$.
alg-f-232 · level facile · notes
The input is a single natural number $n\ge 1$, hence $|I|=\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ (the number of bits of $n$). What is the order of growth of $|I|$ as a function of the value $n$?
  1. $|I|=\Theta(n)$
  2. $|I|=\Theta(1)$
  3. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  4. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2\log_2 n)$
Explanation. $\log_2 n\le\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1\le 2\log_2 n$ for $n\ge 2$ (or: the ratio tends to $1$). The value $n$ instead grows exponentially with respect to $|I|$.
alg-f-233 · level facile · notes
The input consists of the two natural numbers $n=5$ and $m=1000$, and $|I|=(\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1)+(\lfloor\log_2 m\rfloor+1)$. What is $|I|$?
  1. $13$
  2. $10$
  3. $20$
  4. $1005$
Explanation. $5=(101)_2$ has $3$ bits and $1000$ has $10$ ($2^9\le 1000<2^{10}$): $|I|=13$. With $M=\max\{n,m\}$ we always have $\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1\le|I|\le 2(\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1)$, i.e., $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 M)$.
alg-f-305 · level facile · notes
The input size of a natural number $n$ in binary is $\lfloor \log_2 n \rfloor+1$ bits. Knowing that this quantity is both $O(\log_2 n)$ and $\Omega(\log_2 n)$, what is concluded?
  1. It is $\Theta(n)$
  2. It is $\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  3. It is $o(\log_2 n)$
  4. Nothing, the exact constants are needed
Explanation. $O$ and $\Omega$ together are equivalent to $\Theta$. The size of $n$ grows as $\log_2 n$, not as the value $n$.
alg-f-306 · level facile · notes
The input consists of two naturals $n=1000$ and $m=8$ written in binary. How many bits does the input size measure?
  1. $1008$
  2. $10$
  3. $2$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $1000$ needs $\lfloor\log_2 1000\rfloor+1=10$ bits and $8$ needs $4$: $14=\Theta(\log_2 n+\log_2 m)$ in total. $1008$ adds the values, not the lengths.
alg-f-307 · level teorema · notes
An algorithm is polynomial if $T(|I|)=O(|I|^k)$ for a constant $k$, where $|I|$ is the input size. Trial-division primality testing on a natural $n$ takes $\Theta(\sqrt{n})$ divisions. Which statement is correct?
  1. It is polynomial, since $\sqrt{n}$ is a power of $n$
  2. It is not polynomial: with $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$ bits, $\sqrt{n}=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$ is exponential in the size
  3. It is linear in the input size
  4. It is polynomial only if $n$ is prime
Explanation. The size is the number of bits, not the value: $\sqrt{n}=2^{(\log_2 n)/2}$. A time polynomial in the numerical value is called pseudo-polynomial.
alg-f-308 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation: if $a_1 2^{b_1 x} \le T(x) \le a_2 2^{b_2 x}$ eventually, with positive constants, then $T(x)=2^{\Theta(x)}$. Why can one not write $T(x)=\Theta(2^x)$?
  1. Because with $b_1 \ne b_2$ the orders differ
  2. Because $T$ is not positive
  3. Because $2^{\Theta(x)}$ and $\Theta(2^x)$ are the same thing
  4. Because $a_1$ and $a_2$ differ
Explanation. $2^{2x}=4^x$ is not $\Theta(2^x)$: a constant factor in the exponent changes the base. The constants $a_1,a_2$ in front, instead, are absorbed by $\Theta$.
alg-f-351 · level facile · notes
The input size of an array $a[1..n]$ of naturals $a[i] \ge 1$ is $\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1)$ bits. What is it for $a=[1,5,8,30]$?
  1. $4$
  2. $13$
  3. $44$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $1$, $5$, $8$, $30$ need $1$, $3$, $4$, $5$ bits: $13$ in total. $4$ counts only the elements, $44$ adds the values.
alg-f-352 · level teorema · notes
Let $|I|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1)$ be the input size of an array of naturals $a[i] \ge 1$, and $M=\max_i a[i]$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $|I|=\Theta(n)$ always
  2. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 M)$
  3. $|I|=\Theta(M)$
  4. $|I|=\Omega(n)$ and $|I|=O(n(1+\log_2 M))$
Explanation. Every element needs at least one bit and at most $1+\log_2 M$ bits. With arbitrarily large values $|I|$ may grow faster than $n$; with bounded values it is $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-353 · level ipotesi · notes
Observation: the input size of an array of $n$ naturals $a[i] \ge 1$ is $\Omega(n)$. Which fact proves it?
  1. Every component needs exactly one bit
  2. Every component needs at least one bit
  3. The elements are distinct
  4. $M \ge n$
Explanation. With $c=1$ and $n_1=1$: $\sum(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1) \ge n$. The elements need not be distinct nor large.
alg-f-354 · level facile · notes
An array has $n=1000$ components, all equal to $1$. How many bits does its input size $\sum(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1)$ measure?
  1. $10$
  2. $1$
  3. $1000$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Every $1$ needs one bit: $1000$ bits. It is the case where the lower bound $|I| \ge n$ holds with equality.
alg-f-355 · level facile · notes
The input is a natural $n$ and an array $a[1..n]$ of naturals $a[i] \ge 1$; the size is $(\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1)+\sum_i(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1)$. With $n=4$ and $a=[2,3,9,1]$, what is it?
  1. $12$
  2. $9$
  3. $3$
  4. $19$
Explanation. $n=4=(100)_2$ needs $3$ bits; $2$ and $3$ need $2$ each, $9$ needs $4$, $1$ one: $9$ bits for the array. $3+9=12$ in total; $9$ forgets the number $n$, $19$ adds the values.
alg-f-356 · level teorema · notes
The input is a natural $n \ge 1$ and an array $a[1..n]$ of naturals $a[i] \ge 1$, and $M=\max\{n,a[1],\dots,a[n]\}$. Which upper bound on the input size $|I|$ (in bits) is correct?
  1. $|I|=O(\log_2 M)$
  2. $|I|=O(n)$ always
  3. $|I|=O((n+1)(1+\log_2 M))$
  4. $|I|=O(M)$ and nothing better
Explanation. Each of the $n+1$ numbers needs at most $1+\log_2 M$ bits. $O(n)$ holds only if the values have fixed size; $O(M)$ is true but much weaker.
alg-f-520 · level facile · notes
The input size $|I|$ is the number of bits needed to write the instance (a natural $x\ge 1$ needs $\lfloor\log_2 x\rfloor+1$). One compares the instance made of the single number $n=10^6$ and the array of $10^6$ components equal to $1$. What are their sizes?
  1. $10^6$ bits in both cases
  2. $20$ bits in both cases
  3. $20$ bits and $10^6$ bits
  4. $10^6$ bits and $20$ bits
Explanation. $\lfloor\log_2 10^6\rfloor+1=20$; the array has $10^6$ components of $1$ bit each. The same «$10^6$» gives very different sizes: what counts is how many bits are needed, not how large the number is.
alg-f-521 · level teorema · notes
For an instance made of a single natural number $n$, why is the input size $\Theta(\log n)$ and not $n$?
  1. Because $n$ is written with $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ bits, and the size counts bits
  2. Because numerical algorithms always perform $\log n$ steps
  3. Because $n$ is one value, and the size counts values
  4. Because base $10$ needs $\log_{10} n$ digits, and base $10$ is used
Explanation. The size measures the length of the binary representation of the instance. Counting values would give $1$ for every $n$; the base does not change the order $\Theta(\log n)$, but the definition uses bits.
alg-f-522 · level media · notes
One wants to prove from the definition that $f(n)=\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, that is $c_1\log_2 n\le f(n)\le c_2\log_2 n$ for every $n\ge n_1$. Which choice works?
  1. $c_1=1$, $c_2=2$, $n_1=1$
  2. $c_1=2$, $c_2=2$, $n_1=2$
  3. $c_1=1$, $c_2=2$, $n_1=2$
  4. $c_1=1$, $c_2=1$, $n_1=2$
Explanation. $\lfloor x\rfloor+1>x$ gives the lower bound with $c_1=1$; $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1\le 2\log_2 n$ holds if $\log_2 n\ge 1$, that is $n\ge 2$. For $n=1$ one would get $1\le 0$; with $c_2=1$ already $n=2$ fails ($2\le 1$).
alg-f-523 · level facile · notes
A natural number $n\ge 1$ is written in binary with $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ bits. How many binary digits do $255$ and $256$ have?
  1. $8$ and $9$
  2. $8$ and $8$
  3. $9$ and $9$
  4. $7$ and $8$
Explanation. $255=2^8-1=(11111111)_2$ has $8$ bits, $256=2^8$ is a $1$ followed by $8$ zeros: $9$ bits. The number of bits grows at every power of $2$.
alg-f-524 · level media · notes
A primality test receives the single natural $n$ and performs, in the worst case, about $\sqrt n$ divisions. Let $x=|I|=\Theta(\log n)$ be the input size. How does the number of divisions grow as a function of $x$?
  1. Like $\Theta(\sqrt x)$: sub-linear in $x$
  2. Like $\Theta(x)$: linear in $x$
  3. Like $\Theta(x^2)$: polynomial in $x$
  4. Like $2^{\Theta(x)}$: exponential
Explanation. $n\approx 2^x$, so $\sqrt n\approx 2^{x/2}=2^{\Theta(x)}$. Time must be expressed as a function of the input size, not of the value $n$: a «$\sqrt n$» algorithm on a number is not polynomial.
alg-f-525 · level teorema · notes
The input consists of two naturals $n,m\ge 2$, with $|I|=(\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1)+(\lfloor\log_2 m\rfloor+1)$. Which expression is also $\Theta(|I|)$?
  1. $\log_2 n\cdot\log_2 m$
  2. $\log_2(n\cdot m)$
  3. $n+m$
  4. $\sqrt{n\cdot m}$
Explanation. $\log_2(nm)=\log_2 n+\log_2 m$, which differs from $|I|$ by at most $2$ and is at least $|I|/2$ for $n,m\ge 2$. The product of the logarithms grows like $(\log n)^2$ for $n=m$; $n+m$ and $\sqrt{nm}$ are exponential in $|I|$.
alg-f-526 · level facile · notes
For an array $a[1..n]$ of naturals $a[i]\ge 1$, $|I|=\sum_i(\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1)$ and, with $M=\max_i a[i]$, $|I|\le n\,(\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1)$. With $a=[100,1,1,1]$, what are $|I|$ and the upper bound?
  1. $|I|=28$, bound $28$
  2. $|I|=10$, bound $10$
  3. $|I|=7$, bound $28$
  4. $|I|=10$, bound $28$
Explanation. $100$ has $7$ bits and each $1$ has one: $|I|=7+3=10$. The bound treats every component as the largest: $4\cdot 7=28$. It is an upper bound, not always tight.
alg-f-538 · level media · notes
An algorithm receives an array $a[1..n]$ of naturals, with $M=\max_i a[i]$, and performs $\Theta(n\cdot M)$ steps in the worst case. The input size is $|I|=\Theta(n\log M)$. Is the algorithm polynomial (steps $\bigO(|I|^k)$ for a constant $k$)?
  1. Yes: $n\cdot M$ is a product, that is a polynomial
  2. No: for fixed $n$, $M$ can be $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  3. Yes: the number of steps is linear in $n$
  4. Yes: $n\cdot M\le|I|^2$ for every input
Explanation. With $n=1$ we have $|I|=\Theta(\log M)$ and the steps are $\Theta(M)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$: exponential in the size. The value of the numbers, unlike the number of components, can be exponential in the length of their encoding.

12. Running time (36)

alg-f-090 · level facile · notes
Which assumption is made on the primitive operations to count the steps of an algorithm?
  1. Each operation requires a time proportional to the number of bits of its operands, also for fixed-size numbers.
  2. Arithmetic operations require constant time, comparisons and assignments do not.
  3. Each primitive operation requires constant time, and one step is counted for each execution of a line of pseudo-code.
  4. Only key comparisons are counted, the other operations have zero cost.
Explanation. Assuming constant time for each primitive operation makes the analysis machine-independent; counting a line as one step changes the total only by a constant factor.
alg-f-091 · level facile · notes
The body of a while loop is executed $k$ times. How many times is its condition evaluated?
  1. $k+1$
  2. $k$
  3. $k-1$
  4. $2k$
Explanation. One evaluation before each execution of the body, plus the last one, which finds the condition false and ends the loop.
alg-f-092 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum sets $max:=a[1]$ and, for $i=2,\dots,n$, executes $max\leftarrow a[i]$ if $a[i]>max$; with one step per executed line it makes $3n+1+t$ steps, where $t$ counts the assignments $max\leftarrow a[i]$. On which input does it reach the maximum, $4n$?
  1. On a strictly decreasing array
  2. On an array with all elements equal
  3. On a strictly increasing array
  4. On an array with the maximum in the first position
Explanation. The steps are $3n+1+t$, where $t$ is the number of executions of $max\leftarrow a[i]$: $t=n-1$ when every element is a new maximum, i.e., for a strictly increasing array.
alg-f-093 · level facile · notes
In the worst case InsertionSort executes $S(n)=\frac{3n^2+9n-8}{2}$ steps on an array of $n$ entries of constant size. What is its worst-case running time as a function of the input size $|I|$ (number of bits of the instance)?
  1. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|^2)$
  2. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|)$
  3. $T(|I|)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  4. $T(|I|)=\Theta(\log_2^2|I|)$
Explanation. $S(n)=\Theta(n^2)$ and, with entries of constant size, $|I|=\Theta(n)$: substituting we get $\Theta(|I|^2)$.
alg-f-094 · level media · notes
In the worst case PrimalityTest executes $S(n)=3\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor+1$ steps on the natural number $n$. What is its worst-case running time as a function of the input size $|I|$ (number of bits of $n$)?
  1. $T(|I|)=\Theta(\sqrt{|I|})$
  2. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|)$
  3. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|^2)$
  4. $T(|I|)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
Explanation. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, i.e., $n=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$, hence $\sqrt n=2^{\Theta(|I|)/2}$, which is still $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$: the algorithm is exponential in the input size.
alg-f-095 · level facile · notes
ChangeToBase2 executes $S(n)=6\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+11$ steps on the natural number $n$. What is its worst-case running time as a function of the input size $|I|$ (number of bits of $n$)?
  1. $T(|I|)=\Theta(\log_2|I|)$
  2. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|)$
  3. $T(|I|)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  4. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|\log_2|I|)$
Explanation. The number of steps is proportional to the number of binary digits of $n$, which is exactly $|I|=\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$: the running time is linear in the input size.
alg-f-096 · level teorema · notes
An algorithm executes $S(n)$ steps; the running time $T(|I|)$ expresses the steps as a function of the input size $|I|$ (number of bits). Which statement is correct?
  1. If $n$ is the value of the input number, $S(n)=\Theta(n)$ gives $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|)$, as for an array.
  2. The order of growth never changes from $S(n)$ to $T(|I|)$, whatever the meaning of $n$.
  3. If $n$ is the value of the input number, also $S(n)=\Theta(\log_2 n)$ is exponential in $|I|$.
  4. If $n$ is the value of the input number, $S(n)=\Theta(\log_2 n)$ is linear in $|I|$ and $S(n)=\Theta(n)$ is exponential.
Explanation. For an array $|I|=\Theta(n)$; for a number $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, i.e., $n=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$. This is why $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ steps are linear in $|I|$ and $\Theta(\sqrt n)$ or $\Theta(n)$ steps are exponential.
alg-f-097 · level ipotesi · notes
InsertionSort executes $S(n)=\Theta(n^2)$ steps on an array of $n$ entries; we want to conclude $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|^2)$, where $|I|$ is the number of bits of the input. What is needed?
  1. Nothing else: $S(n)=\Theta(n^2)$ is already the running time as a function of $|I|$.
  2. The relation $|I|=\Theta(n)$, valid with entries of constant size and the constants of the definitions of $\Theta$.
  3. The relation $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, valid when the input is a natural number $n$.
  4. The limit criterion applied to the ratio $S(n)/n$, which diverges.
Explanation. From $a_1 n^2\le S(n)\le a_2 n^2$ and $c_1|I|\le n\le c_2|I|$ we get $a_1c_1^2|I|^2\le S(n)\le a_2c_2^2|I|^2$. Without the relation between $n$ and $|I|$ the step is not justified.
alg-f-234 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum sets $max:=a[1]$ and, for $i=2,\dots,n$, executes $max\leftarrow a[i]$ if $a[i]>max$; counting one step per executed line it performs $3n+1+t$ steps, where $t$ is the number of assignments $max\leftarrow a[i]$. On which arrays of $n$ elements does it perform the minimum, $3n+1$ steps?
  1. Only on strictly increasing arrays, where the maximum is the last element
  2. On those in which the maximum appears only once, in any position
  3. On those with the maximum in the last position, where a single assignment is needed
  4. On those in which $a[1]$ is a maximum element, for instance with all elements equal
Explanation. $t=0$ exactly when no $a[i]$, $i\ge 2$, is strictly larger than the current maximum, i.e., when $a[1]\ge a[i]$ for every $i$. On strictly increasing arrays one gets instead the maximum, $t=n-1$.
alg-f-235 · level media · notes
ChangeToBase2 is run with $n=100$. Counting one step for each execution of a numbered line (the while condition counts at each evaluation), how many steps does it perform? <pre>1 nd := ⌊log₂ n⌋ + 1 2 d := new array[0..nd−1] 3 k := 0 4 <b>while</b> n > 0 <b>do</b> 5 q := ⌊n / 2⌋ 6 r := n mod 2 7 d[k] ← r 8 n ← q 9 k ← k + 1 <b>end</b> 10 <b>return</b> d, nd</pre>
  1. $46$
  2. $47$
  3. $53$
  4. $41$
Explanation. $100$ has $\lfloor\log_2 100\rfloor+1=7$ binary digits, so the body (lines $5$–$9$) is executed $7$ times and the condition $8$ times: $3+8+5\cdot 7+1=47=6\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+11$. Forgetting the last evaluation of the condition gives $46$.
alg-f-236 · level media · notes
PrimalityTest is run with $n=91=7\cdot 13$. Counting one step for each execution of a numbered line (the while condition counts at each evaluation), how many steps does it perform? <pre>1 <b>if</b> n < 2 <b>then</b> 2 <b>return</b> false <b>end</b> 3 d := 2 4 <b>while</b> d ≤ ⌊√n⌋ <b>do</b> 5 <b>if</b> n mod d = 0 <b>then</b> 6 <b>return</b> false <b>end</b> 7 d ← d + 1 <b>end</b> 8 <b>return</b> true</pre>
  1. $28$
  2. $19$
  3. $26$
  4. $20$
Explanation. Lines $1$ and $3$ once; for $d=2,\dots,7$ the condition (line $4$) and the test (line $5$) are evaluated: $6+6$; for $d=7$ the test is true and line $6$ returns false; line $7$ is executed for $d=2,\dots,6$: $5$. Total $1+1+12+1+5=20$. $28=3\lfloor\sqrt{91}\rfloor+1$ holds only for a prime.
alg-f-237 · level facile · notes
In the worst case FindMaximum performs $4n$ steps and InsertionSort $\frac{3n^2+9n-8}{2}$ (with $n$ entries of the array, of constant size), ChangeToBase2 $6\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+11$ and PrimalityTest $3\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor+1$ (with $n$ the value of the input number). Which of these algorithms is exponential in the input size $|I|$?
  1. InsertionSort
  2. PrimalityTest
  3. ChangeToBase2
  4. FindMaximum
Explanation. For an input number $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, i.e., $n=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$, and $\sqrt n=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$. The others are polynomial: $\Theta(|I|)$, $\Theta(|I|^2)$ and $\Theta(|I|)$.
alg-f-309 · level facile · notes
In the course, the running time of an algorithm is defined as…
  1. the time in seconds measured on a reference computer
  2. the number of lines of the pseudo-code
  3. an estimate of the number of executed steps, as a function of the input size $|I|$
  4. the memory used by the algorithm
Explanation. Time in seconds depends on computer, compiler and language; counting steps as a function of $|I|$ makes the analysis independent of these details.
alg-f-310 · level facile · notes
Which of the following is not a primitive operation (cost of one step) in the course's model?
  1. Adding two numbers of fixed size
  2. Comparing two numbers of fixed size
  3. Reading $a[i]$ given the index $i$
  4. Sorting an array of $n$ elements
Explanation. Primitive operations have constant cost: arithmetic and comparisons on fixed-size numbers, access to an element given its index, assignments. Sorting an array takes many steps.
alg-f-311 · level teorema · notes
Let $T(|I|)$ be the worst-case running time of an algorithm. Which statement is correct?
  1. $T(|I|)$ is the average number of steps over the inputs of size $|I|$
  2. $T(|I|)$ is the maximum number of steps over all inputs of size $|I|$, so it guarantees a bound valid for every input
  3. $T(|I|)$ is the number of steps on the largest possible input
  4. $T(|I|)$ depends on the specific input, not only on its size
Explanation. The worst case is a maximum at fixed size: it is a guarantee, unlike the average case, which needs a distribution of the inputs.
alg-f-332 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum$(a[1..n])$ sets $max:=a[1]$, then for $i=2,\dots,n$ runs «if $a[i]>max$ then $max:=a[i]$», and returns $max$. How many times is the condition of the for loop (the test deciding whether to run another iteration) evaluated?
  1. $n-1$ times
  2. $n$ times
  3. $n+1$ times
  4. It depends on the values of the array
Explanation. The body runs for $i=2,\dots,n$, that is, $n-1$ times, and the condition is evaluated once more, when it fails: $n$ times. Only the number of executions of $max:=a[i]$ depends on the values.
alg-f-333 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum$(a[1..n])$ sets $max:=a[1]$, then for $i=2,\dots,n$ runs «if $a[i]>max$ then $max:=a[i]$», and returns $max$. On which array of $n$ distinct elements is the assignment $max:=a[i]$ executed the largest number of times (worst case)?
  1. On an array sorted decreasingly
  2. On an array with the maximum in first position
  3. The number of assignments is always the same
  4. On an array sorted increasingly, where it runs at every iteration
Explanation. In an increasing array every $a[i]$ exceeds the current maximum, so $t(i)=1$ for every $i$; in a decreasing array the maximum is $a[1]$ and the assignment never runs.
alg-f-334 · level ipotesi · notes
For FindMaximum the worst-case number of steps is $S(n)=\Theta(n)$ as a function of the number $n$ of elements. To conclude that the time is $O(|I|)$ as a function of the input size $|I|$ (in bits), which fact is needed?
  1. $|I|=\Omega(n)$, hence $n=O(|I|)$
  2. $|I|=O(n)$, since the elements have fixed size
  3. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  4. That the array is sorted
Explanation. From $|I| \ge c\,n$ it follows $n \le |I|/c$ and so $S(n) \le a\,n \le (a/c)\,|I|$. The upper bound on $|I|$ is not needed for an upper bound on the time.
alg-f-335 · level facile · notes
An algorithm on an array $a[1..n]$ performs $S(n)=\Theta(n)$ steps, and the array elements have fixed size (a constant number of bits). What is the running time as a function of the input size $|I|$?
  1. $\Theta(|I|^2)$
  2. $\Theta(\log_2 |I|)$
  3. $\Theta(|I|)$
  4. $\Theta(2^{|I|})$
Explanation. With fixed-size elements $|I|=\Theta(n)$, so $S=\Theta(|I|)$: linear both in $n$ and in $|I|$.
alg-f-336 · level facile · notes
InsertionSort on $a[1..n]$: for $i=2,\dots,n$ it inserts $a[i]$ into the already sorted part $a[1..i-1]$, shifting right by one position the larger elements (inner loop). In the worst case (array sorted decreasingly), how many times in total is the body of the inner loop executed with $n=6$?
  1. $15$
  2. $21$
  3. $6$
  4. $36$
Explanation. At iteration $i$ all the $i-1$ previous elements are shifted: $t(i)=i-1$, and $\sum_{i=2}^{6}(i-1)=1+2+3+4+5=15=n(n-1)/2$. $21=n(n+1)/2$ counts one more iteration for every $i$.
alg-f-337 · level teorema · notes
InsertionSort on $a[1..n]$: for $i=2,\dots,n$ it inserts $a[i]$ into the already sorted part $a[1..i-1]$, shifting right by one position the larger elements (inner loop). Let $t(i)$ be the number of executions of the inner loop body at iteration $i$. Which statement about the number of steps $S(n)$ of InsertionSort is correct?
  1. $S(n)=\Theta(n^2)$ on every input
  2. $S(n)=\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ in the worst case
  3. Worst $\Theta(n^2)$ ($t(i)=i-1$), best $\Theta(n)$ ($t(i)=0$)
  4. In the best case the steps are $\Theta(n^2)$
Explanation. The sum $\sum_{i=2}^{n}(i-1)=n(n-1)/2$ gives the quadratic term of the worst case; on a sorted array the inner loop condition fails at once in every iteration.
alg-f-338 · level ipotesi · notes
For InsertionSort the worst-case number of steps is $S(n)=\Theta(n^2)$. How is $S=O(|I|^2)$ obtained, with $|I|$ the input size in bits?
  1. From $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  2. From $|I| \le n$
  3. It cannot be: the time as a function of $|I|$ is exponential
  4. From $|I| \ge c\,n$: $S \le a\,n^2 \le (a/c^2)\,|I|^2$
Explanation. The lower bound $|I|=\Omega(n)$ suffices, giving $n=O(|I|)$; squaring gives the bound on $S$. $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$ concerns a single number, not an array.
alg-f-339 · level facile · notes
InsertionSort takes $\Theta(n^2)$ steps in the worst case on an array of $n$ fixed-size elements. How is it classified with respect to the input size $|I|$?
  1. It is exponential in $|I|$
  2. It is a polynomial algorithm, quadratic in $|I|$
  3. It is linear in $|I|$
  4. It is logarithmic in $|I|$
Explanation. With fixed-size elements $|I|=\Theta(n)$, so $\Theta(n^2)=\Theta(|I|^2)$: a power of $|I|$, that is, polynomial time.
alg-f-340 · level facile · notes
ChangeToBase2$(n)$ repeats, while $n>0$, «write the digit $n \bmod 2$; $n:=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$». How many times is the loop body executed with $n=100$?
  1. $6$
  2. $50$
  3. $7$
  4. $100$
Explanation. One iteration per binary digit: $\lfloor\log_2 100\rfloor+1=6+1=7$ ($100=(1100100)_2$). $50$ would count as if $n$ decreased by $2$ at every step.
alg-f-341 · level ipotesi · notes
ChangeToBase2$(n)$ repeats, while $n>0$, «write the digit $n \bmod 2$; $n:=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$». After $s$ iterations the current value is $\lfloor n/2^s\rfloor$. Why does the loop run exactly $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ times?
  1. Because $\lfloor n/2^s\rfloor=0$ exactly when $2^s>n$, and the smallest such $s$ is $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$
  2. Because $n$ is a power of $2$
  3. Because every iteration halves the number of decimal digits
  4. Because $\lfloor n/2^s\rfloor$ is always odd
Explanation. The loop stops at the first $s$ with value $0$, that is, with $2^s>n$; for every $n \ge 1$ it is $s=\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$, the number of binary digits. $n$ need not be a power of $2$.
alg-f-342 · level teorema · notes
ChangeToBase2 on a natural $n$ performs $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ steps, and the input is $n$ written in base $10$. Which statement about the time as a function of the input size $|I|$ is correct?
  1. $|I|=\Theta(n)$, so the time is logarithmic in $|I|$
  2. The time is exponential in $|I|$
  3. $|I|=\Theta(\log_{10} n)=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, so the time is $\Theta(|I|)$: linear
  4. Changing the base of logarithms changes the order of growth
Explanation. The number of decimal digits is $\Theta(\log_{10} n)$, and $\log_{10} n=\log_2 n/\log_2 10$ differs from $\log_2 n$ by a constant. So steps and size have the same order.
alg-f-343 · level facile · notes
A natural $n$ is given as input written in base $10$. Doubling the number of decimal digits of $n$, what happens to the number of steps of ChangeToBase2 (which runs one iteration per binary digit of $n$)?
  1. It roughly doubles
  2. It gets squared
  3. It stays the same
  4. It becomes exponentially larger
Explanation. The number of binary digits is about $\log_2 10 \approx 3.32$ times the number of decimal digits, so the steps grow linearly with the input size.
alg-f-344 · level facile · notes
PrimalityTest$(n)$, for $n \ge 2$, tries the divisors $d=2,3,\dots$ while $d \cdot d \le n$, and returns «composite» as soon as $n \bmod d=0$, otherwise «prime». With $n=101$ (prime), how many times is the loop body executed?
  1. $10$
  2. $99$
  3. $50$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $d$ takes the values $2,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt{101}\rfloor=10$: $10-1=9$ iterations, with no early exit since $101$ is prime. $99$ would try every $d<n$.
alg-f-345 · level facile · notes
PrimalityTest$(n)$, for $n \ge 2$, tries the divisors $d=2,3,\dots$ while $d \cdot d \le n$, and returns «composite» as soon as $n \bmod d=0$, otherwise «prime». For which inputs does the worst case (the largest number of steps for a given $n$) occur?
  1. When $n$ is even
  2. When $n$ is prime: the loop never exits early
  3. When $n$ is a perfect square
  4. The number of steps does not depend on $n$
Explanation. A composite has a divisor $\le \sqrt{n}$ and the test exits as soon as it finds it (immediately, if $n$ is even); a prime forces trying every $d$ up to $\lfloor\sqrt{n}\rfloor$.
alg-f-346 · level ipotesi · notes
PrimalityTest$(n)$, for $n \ge 2$, tries the divisors $d=2,3,\dots$ while $d \cdot d \le n$, and returns «composite» as soon as $n \bmod d=0$, otherwise «prime». The worst-case steps are $\Theta(\sqrt{n})$ and the input size is $|I|=\Theta(\log_2 n)$. Which step proves that the time is exponential in $|I|$?
  1. $\sqrt{n}$ is a power of $n$, so it is polynomial
  2. $\log_2 n \le n$
  3. The constants $c_1$ and $c_2$ can be ignored in the exponent
  4. From $c_1\log_2 n \le |I| \le c_2\log_2 n$ one gets $2^{|I|/c_2} \le n \le 2^{|I|/c_1}$, so $\sqrt{n}=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
Explanation. Applying the increasing function $2^x$ to the inequalities expresses $n$ in terms of $|I|$; the constants end up in the exponent, where they cannot be dropped: one writes $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$.
alg-f-347 · level facile · notes
Trial-division primality testing takes $\Theta(\sqrt{n})$ steps on a natural $n$ written in binary. Adding $2$ bits to the input size (that is, multiplying $n$ by about $4$), what happens to the number of steps?
  1. It grows by $2$
  2. It roughly doubles
  3. It stays the same
  4. It grows by about $2\%$
Explanation. $\sqrt{4n}=2\sqrt{n}$: two more bits double the steps, that is, every extra bit multiplies them by $\sqrt2$. This is the behaviour of a time exponential in the size.
alg-f-348 · level facile · notes
In the step count of a pseudo-code every executed line costs one step. A loop «for $i=1$ to $n$» with a one-line body: how many steps does it cost in total (body lines plus evaluations of the for condition)?
  1. $2n+1$
  2. $n$
  3. $2n$
  4. $n+1$
Explanation. The condition is evaluated $n+1$ times (the last one fails) and the body $n$ times: $2n+1$. Forgetting the last evaluation gives $2n$.
alg-f-349 · level facile · notes
An algorithm on arrays of $n$ elements performs $3n$ steps on some inputs and $n^2$ steps on others, always with $n$ elements. What is its worst-case running time?
  1. $\Theta(n)$
  2. $\Theta((n^2+3n)/2)$, the average
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. It is undefined, since it depends on the input
Explanation. The worst case is the maximum number of steps over the inputs of the same size: $n^2$ for $n \ge 3$. The average would need a distribution of the inputs.
alg-f-350 · level facile · notes
Why is the running time of an algorithm expressed with an asymptotic notation, such as $\Theta(n^2)$, instead of an exact formula?
  1. Because the exact formula never exists
  2. Because modern computers have constant time
  3. Because only the best case matters
  4. Because constants depend on the computer, the order does not
Explanation. Counting a line as one step is a convention: another count changes the constants, not the order of growth, which is what compares algorithms on large inputs.
alg-f-680 · level ipotesi · notes
The worst-case running time is the largest number of steps over all inputs of size $n$. If it were measured only on one «typical» input of each size, what would be lost?
  1. Nothing: all inputs of size $n$ cost the same
  2. The guarantee: other inputs may need more steps
  3. Only the multiplicative constants
  4. The possibility of using $\bigO$ notation
Explanation. For instance Insertion Sort on a sorted array makes $\Theta(n)$ steps, on a decreasing one $\Theta(n^2)$. The worst case is a bound valid for every input.
alg-f-681 · level ipotesi · notes
When counting steps one assumes that every elementary operation (for instance an addition) costs constant time. When must this hypothesis be revised?
  1. Never: an addition always costs $\Theta(1)$
  2. When the algorithm is recursive
  3. When the input is an array
  4. When the numbers grow with the input
Explanation. An addition on fixed-size numbers (for instance <code>int</code>) is constant; if the operands have $k$ bits with $k$ growing, an addition costs $\Theta(k)$, as in computations on $n!$ or $2^n$.

13. Correctness (25)

alg-f-098 · level facile · notes
When is an algorithm called correct?
  1. When, for every instance $I$ of the problem, it terminates after a finite number of steps and returns the correct output for $I$.
  2. When it returns the correct output whenever it terminates.
