  3. When it terminates on every instance, whatever the output.
  4. When it returns the correct output on the examples executed step by step.
Explanation. Definition 13.1 requires both, termination and correct output, on all instances: examples are not enough to prove correctness.
alg-f-099 · level facile · notes
What is a loop invariant?
  1. A variable whose value never changes during the execution of the loop, from the first to the last iteration.
  2. A property that is true only at the end of the loop, when the condition becomes false.
  3. A property of the variables that is true every time the condition of the loop is evaluated: before the first iteration and after each iteration.
  4. A property that is true at every moment of the execution of the body of the loop.
Explanation. This is Definition 13.2: during the body the invariant may be temporarily false, as long as it is true again when the condition is evaluated.
alg-f-100 · level facile · notes
Which three properties are proved in order to use a loop invariant?
  1. Initialization, maintenance and termination
  2. Base case, inductive step and inductive hypothesis
  3. Declaration, assignment and return
  4. Termination, optimality and stability
Explanation. Initialization: it holds before the first iteration; maintenance: if it holds before an iteration it holds after it; termination: when the loop ends, the invariant gives the desired result.
alg-f-101 · level facile · notes
FindMaximum sets $max:=a[1]$, $i:=2$ and, while $i\le n$, executes $max\leftarrow a[i]$ if $a[i]>max$ and then $i\leftarrow i+1$. Which loop invariant (true at every evaluation of the condition) proves its correctness?
  1. $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[i]\}$
  2. $max=a[i-1]$
  3. $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[i-1]\}$
  4. $max\ge a[i]$
Explanation. Before the first iteration $i=2$ and $max=a[1]=\max\{a[1]\}$; each iteration compares $a[i]$ and increments $i$; at the end $i=n+1$ and $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[n]\}$. With $a[1..i]$ the initialization fails.
alg-f-102 · level teorema · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m$ in $a[1..n]$ sorted in non-decreasing order, examining the central element of the current interval and returning an index or $-1$. Which statement about its correctness is true?
  1. The algorithm terminates and returns an index $k$ with $a[k]=m$ if $m$ is in the array, and $-1$ otherwise.
  2. The algorithm always returns the smallest index $k$ with $a[k]=m$.
  3. The algorithm is correct only if the elements of the array are distinct.
  4. The algorithm terminates only if $m$ is in the array.
Explanation. This is Observation 13.6. With repeated values some index with $a[k]=m$ is returned, not necessarily the first one; if $m$ is absent, the interval becomes empty and the algorithm returns $-1$.
alg-f-103 · level ipotesi · notes
InsertionSort, for $i=2,\dots,n$, inserts $\mathit{key}=a[i]$ into the prefix $a[1..i-1]$ with an inner loop that shifts the larger keys to the right. What is used to prove its correctness?
  1. The correctness of FindMaximum applied to each prefix $a[1..i]$: the maximum always ends in position $i$.
  2. The number of comparisons, at most $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$, which guarantees both termination and sorting.
  3. The outer invariant «$a[1..i-1]$ is sorted, with the original elements» and a lemma on the inner loop for maintenance.
  4. The invariant of the merge of two sorted subarrays, applied to the prefixes $a[1..i-1]$ and $a[i..i]$.
Explanation. The lemma on the inner loop says that, if $a[1..i-1]$ is sorted, after the inner loop and the insertion of $\mathit{key}$ the sub-array $a[1..i]$ is sorted: this is exactly the maintenance of the invariant of the outer loop.
alg-f-104 · level ipotesi · notes
The Euclidean algorithm, starting from $(n,m)=(n_0,m_0)$, replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\,n\bmod m)$ while $m\ne 0$ and returns $n$. What is the proof of its correctness based on?
  1. On the fact that if $d$ divides $n$ then $n/d$ divides $n$, and one of the two is at most $\sqrt n$.
  2. On the invariant $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(n_0,m_0)$, maintained by $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,n\bmod m)$, and on $m$ decreasing.
  3. On the fact that every composite $n$ has a divisor $d$ with $2\le d\le\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$.
  4. On the invariant «$m\le n$», true before every iteration, and on $n$ decreasing.
Explanation. Maintenance uses $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,n\bmod m)$, termination the fact that $r<m$. The invariant $m\le n$ is false at the beginning if $n_0<m_0$.
alg-f-238 · level ipotesi · notes
The correctness of FindMaximum ($max:=a[1]$, $i:=2$; while $i\le n$: if $a[i]>max$ then $max\leftarrow a[i]$; $i\leftarrow i+1$) uses the invariant $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[i-1]\}$. What does the termination property give?
  1. The loop ends with $i=n+1$; substituting into the invariant gives $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[n]\}$.
  2. The loop ends with $i=n$; the invariant gives $\max\{a[1],\dots,a[n-1]\}$ and a last comparison is needed.
  3. Termination follows from the fact that $max$ strictly increases at every iteration of the loop.
  4. The loop ends when $max=a[n]$, i.e., when the last element is the maximum.
Explanation. $i$ increases by $1$ at every iteration, so the loop ends when the condition $i\le n$ becomes false, with $i=n+1$: the invariant with the negated condition gives the output. $max$ does not change when $a[i]\le max$.
alg-f-239 · level media · notes
In iteration $i=5$ of InsertionSort the prefix is $a[1..5]=[1,2,4,5,3]$ and $\mathit{key}=a[5]=3$. The inner loop starts from $j=4$ and, while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$, executes $a[j+1]\leftarrow a[j]$ and $j\leftarrow j-1$; then $a[j+1]\leftarrow\mathit{key}$. With which value of $j$ does the inner loop end, and in which position does $\mathit{key}$ go?
  1. $j=3$; $\mathit{key}$ goes to $a[3]$
  2. $j=2$; $\mathit{key}$ goes to $a[2]$
  3. $j=2$; $\mathit{key}$ goes to $a[3]$
  4. $j=1$; $\mathit{key}$ goes to $a[2]$
Explanation. $a[4]=5$ and $a[3]=4$ are larger than $3$ and are shifted to the right; with $j=2$ we have $a[2]=2\le 3$ and the loop ends. Position $j+1=3$ is the «hole» of the invariant, and the array becomes $[1,2,3,4,5]$.
alg-f-240 · level media · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m$ in the sorted array $a[1..n]$ with a while loop of condition $left\le right$, shrinking the interval $a[left..right]$. Which invariant (true at each evaluation of the condition) proves its correctness?
  1. $a[left]\le m\le a[right]$, so that $m$ always lies between the ends of the interval
  2. $1\le left$, $right\le n$ and, if $m$ is in the array, then $m$ is in $a[left..right]$
  3. The interval $a[left..right]$ contains exactly $n/2^k$ elements after $k$ iterations
  4. $m=a[mid]$ for the middle index $mid$ of the current interval $a[left..right]$
Explanation. The algorithm never discards a part that may contain $m$. If $m$ is missing, $a[left]\le m\le a[right]$ may be false from the start; when the interval becomes empty the invariant guarantees that $-1$ is correct.
alg-f-241 · level ipotesi · notes
The Euclidean algorithm, while $m\ne 0$, replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\,n\bmod m)$ and then returns $n$; its correctness uses the invariant $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(n_0,m_0)$ ($n_0,m_0\ge 1$ initial values). Why does the loop terminate?
  1. Because $n$ decreases by at least $1$ at every iteration and always stays greater than or equal to $m$.
  2. Because the value of $\gcd(n,m)$ strictly decreases at every iteration, until it reaches $1$.
  3. Because $n_0\ge m_0$ is assumed: without this hypothesis the loop might not terminate.
  4. Because $m$ is a natural number that strictly decreases ($n\bmod m<m$): after at most $m_0$ iterations $m=0$.
Explanation. The remainder $r=n\bmod m$ satisfies $0\le r<m$. The $\gcd$ instead never changes (it is the invariant), and with $n_0<m_0$ the first iteration just swaps the two numbers.
alg-f-242 · level media · notes
BadSum$(a,l,r)$ returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$; otherwise it sets $m:=\lceil(l+r)/2\rceil$ and returns BadSum$(a,l,m)+$BadSum$(a,m+1,r)$. What happens when calling BadSum$(a,1,2)$?
  1. It correctly returns $a[1]+a[2]$, like the version with $\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$.
  2. The recursion does not terminate: with $m=2$ the first call is again BadSum$(a,1,2)$.
  3. It returns only $a[1]$, because the second call is on an empty interval.
  4. It stops with an index error, because the second call uses $m+1=3>r$.
Explanation. $\lceil 3/2\rceil=2$, so BadSum$(a,1,2)$ calls itself on the same instance: the proof by induction requires every call to be on a strictly smaller instance, and this is exactly what is missing here.
alg-f-243 · level ipotesi · notes
One proves by induction on the size of the instance that a recursive algorithm is correct. In the inductive step, what is assumed?
  1. That the algorithm returns the correct output on the current instance, the one to be handled.
  2. That the algorithm terminates on every instance, without the need to prove it separately.
  3. That the recursion is a tail recursion, so that it can be turned into a loop with an invariant.
  4. That the recursive calls, made on smaller instances, return the correct output.
Explanation. Inductive hypothesis: correctness on the smaller instances; one proves that the algorithm combines their results into the correct output. Assuming correctness on the current instance would be circular.
alg-f-527 · level facile · notes
An algorithm is correct if, on every instance of the problem, it terminates and returns the right output. To establish that an algorithm is correct, is it enough to test it on many inputs?
  1. Yes, if at least $1000$ random inputs are tested
  2. No: tests find errors, but correctness must be proved
  3. Yes, if the best and the worst case are tested
  4. Yes, if all instances of size at most $10$ are tested
Explanation. Instances are infinitely many: a finite set of tests, however large, does not cover them. This is why loop invariants and induction are used.
alg-f-528 · level teorema · notes
FindMaximum sets $max:=a[1]$, $i:=2$ and, while $i\le n$, updates $max$ with $a[i]$ if larger and increments $i$. The invariant is «$max=\max(a[1..i-1])$». Why does it hold before the first iteration (initialisation)?
  1. Because $i=2$ and $max=a[1]=\max(a[1..1])$
  2. Because $max$ is the largest element of the array from the start
  3. Because $a[1..i-1]$ is empty when $i=2$
  4. Because at exit $i=n+1$
Explanation. With $i=2$ the prefix $a[1..i-1]$ is $a[1..1]$, whose maximum is $a[1]$. Exit with $i=n+1$ serves instead for termination, to conclude $max=\max(a[1..n])$.
alg-f-529 · level ipotesi · notes
For FindMaximum ($max:=a[1]$, $i:=2$; while $i\le n$: if $a[i]>max$ then $max\leftarrow a[i]$; $i\leftarrow i+1$) a student proposes the invariant «$max=\max(a[1..i])$», evaluated before each check of the condition. What is wrong?
  1. Nothing: it is equivalent to the invariant with $\max(a[1..i-1])$
  2. It holds at the start, but does not give the result at exit
  3. It fails at the start, if $a[2]>a[1]$
  4. It is not maintained, because $max$ never changes
Explanation. Before examining $a[i]$ the algorithm knows only $a[1..i-1]$. On $a=[3,7]$: with $i=2$, $max=3$ but $\max(a[1..2])=7$. The correct invariant uses $a[1..i-1]$.
alg-f-530 · level teorema · notes
In the inner loop of InsertionSort (iteration $i$, $\mathit{key}=b[i]$, where $b[1..i]$ is $a[1..i]$ at the start of the iteration), while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$, $a[j]$ is moved to $a[j+1]$. Which property is part of the invariant, at each check of the condition?
  1. The whole prefix $a[1..i]$ is already sorted
  2. The cell $a[j+1]$ already holds $\mathit{key}$
  3. Every element of $a[1..j]$ is greater than $\mathit{key}$
  4. Every element of $a[j+2..i]$ is greater than $\mathit{key}$
Explanation. The elements already moved right are those greater than $\mathit{key}$; $a[1..j]$ has not been touched and $a[j+1]$ is the «hole» where $\mathit{key}$ will go. $a[1..i]$ is sorted only after the insertion.
alg-f-531 · level facile · notes
The outer-loop invariant of InsertionSort says that, at the start of iteration $i$, $a[1..i-1]$ holds the elements originally in $a[1..i-1]$, sorted. Starting from $[5,2,4,6,1,3]$, what does $a[1..4]$ hold after iteration $i=4$?
  1. $[1,2,3,4]$
  2. $[2,4,5,6]$
  3. $[2,5,4,6]$
  4. $[1,2,4,5]$
Explanation. After iteration $4$ the invariant holds with $i=5$: the first four original elements $5,2,4,6$, in order. The four smallest keys ($[1,2,3,4]$) are instead the property of Selection Sort.
alg-f-532 · level teorema · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m$ in the sorted array $a[1..n]$: while $left\le right$ it examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$ and, if $a[mid]\ne m$, sets $right=mid-1$ or $left=mid+1$. Why does the loop terminate?
  1. Because $m$ is always in the sorted array
  2. Because $mid$ stays the same in every iteration
  3. Because it runs exactly $\log_2 n$ iterations
  4. Every iteration without return shrinks $[left,right]$
Explanation. $left\le mid\le right$, and the new interval excludes $mid$: the quantity $right-left+1\ge 0$ strictly decreases, so eventually $left>right$. The number of iterations is at most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$, not exactly $\log_2 n$.
alg-f-533 · level ipotesi · notes
In BinarySearch ($mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$; if $a[mid]<m$ one sets $left=mid+1$) one writes by mistake $left=mid$. What can happen on $a=[1,3]$ searching for $m=3$?
  1. It still returns $2$, the position of $3$
  2. The loop does not terminate
  3. It returns $-1$, because $3$ is discarded
  4. It returns $1$, the last $mid$ examined
Explanation. $mid=1+\lfloor 1/2\rfloor=1$, $a[1]=1<3$, so $left=mid=1$: the state never changes. Correctness uses that the new interval excludes $mid$, so that it shrinks at every iteration.
alg-f-534 · level teorema · notes
Euclid's algorithm, while $m\ne 0$, replaces $(n,m)$ by $(m,\,n\bmod m)$ and then returns $n$. Why does it terminate on every pair of naturals?
  1. Because $n$ decreases by exactly $1$ at each iteration
  2. Because $\gcd(n,m)$ decreases at each iteration of the loop
  3. Because $0\le n\bmod m<m$: $m$ strictly decreases
  4. Because after two iterations $m$ is always $0$
Explanation. A strictly decreasing sequence of naturals cannot be infinite, so eventually $m=0$. The greatest common divisor instead stays constant: it is the invariant used for correctness.
alg-f-535 · level facile · notes
Euclid's algorithm maintains the invariant $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(n_0,m_0)$, where $(n_0,m_0)$ is the input, and exits when $m=0$ returning $n$. Which fact closes the correctness proof?
  1. $\gcd(n,0)=n$
  2. $\gcd(n,0)=0$
  3. $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,n)$
  4. $n\bmod m<m$
Explanation. At exit the invariant says $\gcd(n_0,m_0)=\gcd(n,0)$, and every $d$ divides $0$, so the greatest common divisor of $n$ and $0$ is $n$. $n\bmod m<m$ serves instead for termination.
alg-f-536 · level facile · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,n)$ returns $a[1]$ if $n=1$, otherwise RecursiveSum$(a,n-1)+a[n]$. One proves by induction on $n$ that it returns $a[1]+\dots+a[n]$. What is checked in the base case?
  1. That for $n=0$ it returns $0$, the empty sum
  2. That the call on $n-1$ is correct
  3. That for $n=1$ it returns $a[1]$
  4. That the recursion makes exactly $n$ calls
Explanation. The base case of the induction matches the base case of the algorithm, $n=1$. Correctness of the call on $n-1$ is the inductive hypothesis, used in the inductive step.
alg-f-537 · level teorema · notes
In the inductive proof of correctness of a recursive algorithm, why is it essential that every recursive call receives a smaller instance?
  1. To apply the inductive hypothesis, and to terminate
  2. Because small instances need less memory
  3. Because the algorithm must have a single base case
  4. Because otherwise the running time would not be polynomial
Explanation. The inductive hypothesis covers instances of smaller size; on an instance of the same size it says nothing, and the recursion might never reach the base case.
alg-f-682 · level ipotesi · notes
For a loop one proves that an invariant holds at the start and is maintained by every iteration, but not that the loop terminates. What can be concluded about the output?
  1. That the output is correct
  2. Nothing: without termination there is no output
  3. That the output is correct in the worst case
  4. That the loop runs $n$ iterations
Explanation. Initialisation and maintenance give only «if the loop ends, then the invariant holds». Termination is the third property, and it is needed to conclude.

Part: Recursion (219)

14. Recursion (43)

alg-f-105 · level facile · notes
What is a recursive algorithm?
  1. An algorithm that repeats the body of a while loop as long as a condition stays true.
  2. An algorithm that calls other functions to solve parts of the problem, as long as they are different from itself.
  3. An algorithm that calls itself on the same instance until it finds the result.
  4. An algorithm that solves an instance by calling itself on one or more smaller instances of the same problem and combining their results.
Explanation. This is Definition 14.1: the calls must be on smaller instances, otherwise a base case is never reached.
alg-f-106 · level facile · notes
The function WrongFactorial$(n)$ returns $n\cdot$WrongFactorial$(n-1)$ without any base case. What happens when calling WrongFactorial$(2)$?
  1. It returns $2$: the recursion stops by itself at $n=1$, because $1\cdot$WrongFactorial$(0)$ is $1$.
  2. The recursion does not terminate: the calls continue on $1,0,-1,\dots$ until the memory reserved to the calls is exhausted (stack overflow).
  3. It returns $0$: the call on $n=0$ multiplies the result by $0$ and the recursion stops.
  4. It returns $2$, because the recursion stops at $n=0$ with value $1$.
Explanation. Without a base case the chain of calls never stops; in practice the computer stops it with a stack overflow error. Multiplying by $0$ does not help: the product is computed only when the calls return.
alg-f-107 · level facile · notes
Factorial$(n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$, otherwise $n\cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$. The recursion depth is the maximum number of calls active at the same time. What is it for Factorial$(n)$?
  1. $n$
  2. $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$
  3. $2n+2$
  4. $n+1$
Explanation. The calls with inputs $n,n-1,\dots,0$ are all simultaneously active in the call stack: $n+1$ stack frames, hence $\Theta(n)$ memory. $2n+2$ is the number of steps.
alg-f-108 · level facile · notes
An algorithm is tail recursive if the recursive call is the last operation and its result is returned unchanged. RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$ returns $n$ if $m=0$, otherwise RecursiveGCD$(m,n\bmod m)$; Factorial$(n)$ returns $n\cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$; Fib$(n)$ returns Fib$(n-1)+$Fib$(n-2)$; Hanoi makes a call, a move and another call. Which one is tail recursive?
  1. Factorial
  2. Fib
  3. RecursiveGCD
  4. Hanoi
Explanation. RecursiveGCD returns unchanged the result of RecursiveGCD$(m,n\bmod m)$: the call is the last operation. Factorial multiplies the result by $n$, Fib adds two calls, Hanoi performs a move and another call after the first one.
alg-f-109 · level facile · notes
Fib$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n\le 1$, otherwise Fib$(n-1)+$Fib$(n-2)$. How many calls in total does Fib$(4)$ execute, and what is the recursion depth (maximum number of calls active together)?
  1. $9$ calls, depth $4$
  2. $9$ calls, depth $5$
  3. $5$ calls, depth $4$
  4. $8$ calls, depth $3$
Explanation. The calls satisfy $C(n)=C(n-1)+C(n-2)+1$, with $C(0)=C(1)=1$: $C(4)=9$; the longest path is Fib$(4)\to$Fib$(3)\to$Fib$(2)\to$Fib$(1)$, with $4$ nodes. The number of steps depends on the nodes, the stack memory on the depth.
alg-f-110 · level facile · notes
Counting one step per executed line, FastPower satisfies $S(0)=2$ and $S(n)=S(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+4$ for $n\ge 1$. What is $S(13)$?
  1. $16$
  2. $18$
  3. $14$
  4. $54$
Explanation. The exponents are $13,6,3,1,0$: four calls with $n\ge 1$ of $4$ steps plus the base case of $2$, i.e., $4(\lfloor\log_2 13\rfloor+1)+2=4\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+6=18$.
alg-f-111 · level teorema · notes
A recursive algorithm receives inputs with a size in $\N$; on the base cases it returns the correct output. Which further assumption guarantees that it terminates and is correct on every input?
  1. None: if the output is correct on the base cases, by induction the algorithm is correct on every input.
  2. Every call is on an input of smaller or equal size, and the output is correct whenever the calls' outputs are.
  3. The algorithm terminates on every input: then it returns the correct output, since the base cases do.
  4. Every other input makes finitely many calls, on strictly smaller sizes, and combines their outputs correctly.
Explanation. Both assumptions are needed; the size must decrease strictly, otherwise the call on an input of size $n$ may repeat forever.
alg-f-112 · level ipotesi · notes
A recursive algorithm is correct on the base cases and, on an input of size $n$, calls itself on sizes smaller than $n$ (not necessarily $n-1$) and combines their outputs. With which technique do we prove that it is correct on every input?
  1. With a loop invariant, proving initialization, maintenance and termination.
  2. By strong induction on the size $n$ of the input: the statement is assumed for all sizes $k<n$.
  3. By induction on the number of base cases.
  4. By simple induction, assuming the statement only for size $n-1$.
Explanation. The recursive calls may be on any size smaller than $n$ (for instance $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$), so the inductive hypothesis on all sizes $k<n$ is needed: this is strong induction.
alg-f-113 · level ipotesi · notes
FastPower$(x,n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$; otherwise it computes $y=$FastPower$(x,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ and returns $y\cdot y$ ($n$ even) or $x\cdot y\cdot y$ ($n$ odd). Why does the proof that it returns $x^n$ use strong induction?
  1. Because the algorithm has two base cases, $n=0$ and $n=1$, to be handled together.
  2. Because the algorithm makes two recursive calls, one for each half of the exponent.
  3. It is not needed: simple induction from $n-1$ to $n$ is enough.
  4. Because the inductive hypothesis is used for the exponent $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, not for $n-1$.
Explanation. The call is FastPower$(x,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ and $\lfloor n/2\rfloor<n$, but in general $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\ne n-1$. FastPower has a single base case ($n=0$) and a single recursive call.
alg-f-114 · level teorema · notes
Towers of Hanoi: move $n\ge 1$ disks from one peg to another, one at a time, never a disk on a smaller one. $M(n)$ are the moves of the recursive algorithm Hanoi, $M^*(n)$ the minimum over all algorithms. Which statement is correct?
  1. Hanoi performs $M(n)=2^n-1$ moves, and no algorithm can use fewer: $M^*(n)=2^n-1$.
  2. Hanoi performs $2^n-1$ moves, but there are algorithms using $\bigO(n^2)$.
  3. Hanoi performs $n^2$ moves, and this number is optimal.
  4. Hanoi performs $2^{n-1}$ moves, since every disk moves only once.
Explanation. Observations 14.14 and 14.15: $M(n)=2M(n-1)+1$ gives $2^n-1$, and every algorithm must move the tower of the $n-1$ smaller disks twice, before and after the move of the largest disk.
alg-f-115 · level ipotesi · notes
Towers of Hanoi with $n$ disks: $M^*(n)$ is the minimum number of moves over all algorithms (one disk at a time, never on a smaller one). Which argument is the proof that $M^*(n)\ge 2^n-1$ based on?
  1. Every disk must move at least once, and disk $k$ at least $2^{k-1}$ times by induction on $k$.
  2. The algorithm Hanoi uses exactly $2^n-1$ moves, hence no algorithm can use fewer.
  3. Before and after the move of the largest disk, the other $n-1$ form a tower on another peg: $M^*(n)\ge 2M^*(n-1)+1$.
  4. The moves satisfy $M(n)=M(n-1)+M(n-2)+1$, like the calls of Fib, which grow as $2^n$.
Explanation. The existence of an algorithm with $2^n-1$ moves only gives $M^*(n)\le 2^n-1$; the lower bound requires the argument on the largest disk. «At least $n$ moves» is true but too weak.
alg-f-244 · level ipotesi · notes
Factorial$(n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$, otherwise $n\cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$. How does one prove that Factorial$(n)$ terminates and returns $n!$ for every $n\in\N$?
  1. By induction on $n$: for $n=0$ it returns $1=0!$; if Factorial$(n-1)$ returns $(n-1)!$, then $n\cdot(n-1)!=n!$.
  2. With a loop invariant, proving initialization, maintenance and termination on the loop of the calls.
  3. By strong induction on the exponent $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, as for FastPower, with the two cases $n$ even and $n$ odd.
  4. By checking the result for $n=0,1,\dots,5$: the algorithm is the same for every $n$, so one generic case suffices.
Explanation. The only recursive call is on $n-1$, so simple induction suffices: base case $n=0$, inductive step from the hypothesis on $n-1$. Examples can show that an algorithm is wrong, not that it is correct.
alg-f-245 · level facile · notes
Counting one step per executed line, Factorial (test «$n=0$?», then «return $1$» or «return $n\cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$») satisfies $S(0)=2$ and $S(n)=S(n-1)+2$ for $n\ge 1$. Which closed form does the expansion method give?
  1. $S(n)=2n$
  2. $S(n)=2^{n+1}$
  3. $S(n)=2n+2$
  4. $S(n)=n+2$
Explanation. Expanding $k$ times gives $S(n)=S(n-k)+2k$; with $k=n$ the base case is reached: $S(n)=S(0)+2n=2n+2=\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-246 · level teorema · notes
Fib$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n\le 1$, otherwise Fib$(n-1)+$Fib$(n-2)$. Let $F(n)$ be the Fibonacci numbers ($F(0)=0$, $F(1)=1$) and $C(n)$ the number of calls executed by Fib$(n)$, including the first one. Which statement is correct?
  1. $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1$ for every $n\in\N$
  2. $C(n)=F(n+1)$ for every $n\in\N$
  3. $C(n)=2F(n)-1$ for every $n\ge 1$
  4. $C(n)=n+1$ for every $n\in\N$
Explanation. $C(0)=C(1)=1$ and $C(n)=C(n-1)+C(n-2)+1$; by strong induction $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1$ (for instance $C(5)=2\cdot 8-1=15$). $n+1$ would be the number of distinct subproblems.
alg-f-247 · level ipotesi · notes
The number of calls of Fib$(n)$ satisfies $C(0)=C(1)=1$ and $C(n)=C(n-1)+C(n-2)+1$ for $n\ge 2$. On which step is the inductive step of the proof of $C(n)\ge 2^{n/2}$ based?
  1. $C(n)\ge C(n-1)+1$; repeating it $n$ times gives $C(n)\ge n$, which is larger than $2^{n/2}$ for every $n$.
  2. $C(n)\ge 2\,C(n-1)$, because the two calls have the same number of descendants; hence $C(n)\ge 2^n$.
  3. $C(n)\ge 2\,C(n-2)$, because $C(n-1)\ge C(n-2)$; with the inductive hypothesis $C(n)\ge 2\cdot 2^{(n-2)/2}=2^{n/2}$.
  4. The Master Theorem applied to $C(n)=2\,C(n/2)+1$, which gives $\Theta(n)$ calls and hence the bound.
Explanation. Strong induction with two base cases suffices ($C(2)=3\ge 2$, $C(3)=5\ge 2^{3/2}$). $C(n-1)$ and $C(n-2)$ are not equal, so $C(n)\ge 2C(n-1)$ is false; the recurrence is not in standard form.
alg-f-248 · level teorema · notes
RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$ returns $n$ if $m=0$, otherwise RecursiveGCD$(m,\,n\bmod m)$. Let $m_0=m,m_1,m_2,\dots$ be the second arguments of the successive calls; one proves that $m_{k+2}<m_k/2$. Which consequence follows?
  1. The number of calls is at most $m$, because the second argument decreases by at least $1$.
  2. The number of calls is $\Theta(\sqrt m)$, like the trial divisions of the primality test.
  3. The number of calls is at most $2$, because the second argument halves every two calls.
  4. The number of calls is at most $2\lfloor\log_2 m\rfloor+3$, i.e., $\bigO(\log_2 m)$.
Explanation. Every two calls the second argument is at least halved, so after about $2\log_2 m$ calls it becomes $0$. The bound «at most $m_0$» suffices for termination, but it is very pessimistic.
alg-f-249 · level facile · notes
Hanoi$(n)$ moves $n\ge 1$ disks: if $n=1$ it makes one move; otherwise it calls Hanoi$(n-1)$, moves the largest disk and calls Hanoi$(n-1)$ again. How many calls does it execute in total, including the first one, and what is the depth of the recursion (maximum number of calls active at the same time)?
  1. $2^n-1$ calls, depth $2^n-1$
  2. $2^n-1$ calls, depth $n$
  3. $n$ calls, depth $n$
  4. $2^n-1$ calls, depth $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$
Explanation. The calls satisfy $C(1)=1$, $C(n)=2C(n-1)+1$, hence $C(n)=2^n-1$; but at any moment only the calls on $n,n-1,\dots$ along one path of the tree are active: the depth is $n$.
alg-f-250 · level facile · notes
Among Factorial$(n)$ (one call on $n-1$), Fib$(n)$ (calls on $n-1$ and $n-2$), FastPower$(x,n)$ (one call on $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$) and Hanoi$(n)$ (two calls on $n-1$), which one performs $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ steps, and with which depth of the recursion?
  1. FastPower, with depth $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+2$
  2. Factorial, with depth $n+1$
  3. Hanoi, with depth $n$
  4. Fib, with depth $n$
Explanation. FastPower halves the exponent with constant work: $S(n)=S(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+4$, i.e., $4\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+6$ steps, with one call per level down to $n=0$. Factorial is $\Theta(n)$, Fib and Hanoi are exponential.
alg-f-312 · level ipotesi · notes
Fib$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n \le 1$, otherwise Fib$(n-1)+$Fib$(n-2)$. To prove that it returns the Fibonacci number $F(n)$, which form of induction is needed?
  1. Simple induction with the single base case $n=0$
  2. A loop invariant
  3. None: checking $n=0,\dots,5$ suffices
  4. Strong induction, with base cases $n=0$ and $n=1$
Explanation. The inductive step uses correctness for $n-1$ and $n-2$; so two base cases are needed. With the single case $n=0$, $n=1$ would not be covered.
alg-f-313 · level ipotesi · notes
RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$ returns $n$ if $m=0$, otherwise RecursiveGCD$(m,n \bmod m)$. Which two facts prove that it terminates and returns $\gcd(n,m)$?
  1. $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(n-1,m)$, and the first argument decreases
  2. $\gcd(n,m)=\gcd(m,n \bmod m)$, and the second argument strictly decreases ($n \bmod m<m$) down to $0$
  3. $n$ and $m$ are coprime
  4. The recursion is tail recursion
Explanation. Euclid's property preserves the result and $0 \le n \bmod m<m$ guarantees termination; with $m=0$, $\gcd(n,0)=n$. Tail recursion concerns memory, not correctness.
alg-f-314 · level ipotesi · notes
Hanoi$(n,from,to,aux)$ moves $n-1$ disks to $aux$, disk $n$ to $to$, then the $n-1$ disks to $to$. In the induction proof, why do the calls on $n-1$ disks never break the rule «never a larger disk on a smaller one»?
  1. Because disks are moved two at a time
  2. Because peg $aux$ is always empty
  3. Because disk $n$ is larger than the moved ones
  4. Because $n$ is even
Explanation. The statement includes the hypothesis on the disks below: while the $n-1$ disks move, disk $n$ and those under it are larger, so they do no harm. $aux$ is not necessarily empty.
alg-f-315 · level facile · notes
The number of moves of Hanoi with $n$ disks satisfies $M(1)=1$ and $M(n)=2M(n-1)+1$. How many moves are needed with $n=6$ disks?
  1. $63$
  2. $64$
  3. $32$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $M(n)=2^n-1$, so $M(6)=63$. $64=2^6$ forgets the $-1$; $12=2n$ counts as if every disk moved twice.
alg-f-539 · level facile · notes
A recursive algorithm solves an instance by calling itself on smaller instances, down to a base case. Which function, defined on the naturals, terminates for every $n\ge 0$?
  1. $G(n)$: return $G(n-1)+2$, with no base case
  2. $H(n)$: if $n=0$ return $0$, otherwise $H(n+1)-2$
  3. $K(n)$: if $n=0$ return $0$, otherwise $K(n)+2$
  4. $F(n)$: if $n=0$ return $0$, otherwise $F(n-1)+2$
Explanation. Only $F$ calls itself on a strictly smaller argument that reaches the base case. $G$ has no base case, $H$ moves away from $0$, $K$ calls the same instance again.
alg-f-540 · level facile · notes
Mystery$(n)$, with $n$ an integer, returns $0$ if $n=0$, otherwise Mystery$(n-2)+1$. For which $n\ge 0$ does the recursion terminate?
  1. Only for even $n$
  2. For every $n\ge 0$
  3. Only for odd $n$
  4. For no $n\ge 1$
Explanation. From an even $n$ one goes down by $2$ until $0$. From an odd $n$ one passes $1$ and then $-1,-3,\dots$ never meeting the base case: the size must decrease towards a base case, not merely decrease.
alg-f-541 · level facile · notes
Hanoi$(n)$, with base case $n=1$, calls Hanoi$(n-1)$ twice for $n>1$. In the recursive call tree of Hanoi$(4)$, how many nodes and how many leaves are there?
  1. $16$ nodes and $8$ leaves
  2. $15$ nodes and $4$ leaves
  3. $15$ nodes and $8$ leaves
  4. $8$ nodes and $4$ leaves
Explanation. Level $j$ has $2^j$ nodes, for $j=0,\dots,3$: $1+2+4+8=15$ nodes; the leaves are the $8$ calls Hanoi$(1)$ on the last level.
alg-f-542 · level facile · notes
FastPower$(x,n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$, otherwise it calls FastPower$(x,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ once. Each active call takes one stack frame. What is the largest number of frames in the call stack during FastPower$(x,1000)$?
  1. $11$
  2. $10$
  3. $1000$
  4. $500$
Explanation. The exponents are $1000,500,250,125,62,31,15,7,3,1,0$: $11$ nested calls, all active together when the base case is reached. The largest number of frames is the recursion depth.
alg-f-543 · level facile · notes
Correctness of an iterative algorithm is proved with a loop invariant (initialisation, maintenance, termination), that of a recursive algorithm by induction. What does maintenance of the invariant correspond to?
  1. To the base case (or cases) of the recursion
  2. To the size decreasing down to a base case
  3. To the inductive step of the proof
  4. To the number of recursive calls made
Explanation. Initialisation ↔ base cases; maintenance ↔ inductive step; loop termination ↔ size strictly decreasing towards a base case.
alg-f-544 · level teorema · notes
An iterative and a recursive algorithm solve the same problem. Which statement describes the difference in «direction» between the two correctness proofs?
  1. The iterative one goes from the original instance, the recursive one bottom-up
  2. The iterative one goes bottom-up, the recursive one from the original instance
  3. Both start from the base cases and go up to the original instance
  4. Only the iterative one needs a proof of termination
Explanation. The loop proceeds from initialisation to the last iteration; recursion reduces the original instance to smaller ones. Both must terminate: recursion needs the size to decrease towards a base case.
alg-f-545 · level ipotesi · notes
Factorial$(n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$, otherwise $n\cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$. A variant uses $n=1$ as its only base case (it returns $1$ if $n=1$). What happens calling the variant with $n=0$?
  1. It returns $1$, that is the value of $0!$
  2. It returns $0$, because it multiplies by $n=0$
  3. It returns $1$ after a single call
  4. The recursion never ends
Explanation. With $n=0$ the test $n=1$ fails and $0\cdot$Factorial$(-1)$ is computed, and so on: the base case is never reached. The inductive proof must cover every allowed instance with the base cases.
alg-f-546 · level facile · notes
Counting one step per executed line, the number of steps of Factorial satisfies $S(0)=2$ and $S(n)=S(n-1)+2$ for $n\ge 1$. What is $S(10)$?
  1. $20$
  2. $22$
  3. $12$
  4. $2048$
Explanation. Unrolling, $S(n)=S(0)+2n=2n+2$, so $S(10)=22$. $20$ forgets the two steps of the base case.
alg-f-547 · level teorema · notes
Factorial$(n)$ (base case $n=0$, call on $n-1$) has recursion depth $n+1$; each stack frame takes constant memory. How much memory does its call stack use, and how much does a loop computing $n!$ with an accumulator use?
  1. $\Theta(1)$ and $\Theta(1)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(\log n)$ and $\Theta(1)$
  4. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(1)$
Explanation. The calls on $n,n-1,\dots,0$ are all on the stack together, hence $\Theta(n)$ frames. The loop uses a fixed number of variables. The steps are $\Theta(n)$ in both cases.
alg-f-548 · level facile · notes
IterativeFib$(n)$ sets $prev:=0$, $curr:=1$, $i:=2$ and, while $i\le n$, runs $next:=prev+curr$, $prev\leftarrow curr$, $curr\leftarrow next$, $i\leftarrow i+1$. The invariant is $prev=F(i-2)$, $curr=F(i-1)$ ($F$: Fibonacci numbers, $F(0)=0$, $F(1)=1$). With $n=7$, what are $prev$ and $curr$ when the condition is checked with $i=6$?
  1. $prev=5$, $curr=8$
  2. $prev=2$, $curr=3$
  3. $prev=3$, $curr=5$
  4. $prev=5$, $curr=3$
Explanation. By the invariant $prev=F(4)=3$ and $curr=F(5)=5$. At the end $i=8$ and $curr=F(7)=13$.
alg-f-549 · level media · notes
The naive recursive version Fib performs $4F(n+1)-2$ steps, IterativeFib $5n+1$ steps ($F$: Fibonacci numbers, $F(21)=10946$). How many steps does each perform with $n=20$?
  1. $43782$ and $101$
  2. $101$ and $101$
  3. $43782$ and $43782$
  4. $400$ and $101$
Explanation. $4\cdot 10946-2=43782$ against $5\cdot 20+1=101$. Fib recomputes the same subproblems many times; IterativeFib computes each $F(i)$ only once.
alg-f-550 · level facile · notes
RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$ returns $n$ if $m=0$, otherwise RecursiveGCD$(m,\,n\bmod m)$. How many calls in all does RecursiveGCD$(84,36)$ make, and what does it return?
  1. $2$ calls, returns $12$
  2. $3$ calls, returns $12$
  3. $3$ calls, returns $36$
  4. $4$ calls, returns $6$
Explanation. $(84,36)\to(36,12)\to(12,0)$: the third call is the base case and returns $12=\gcd(84,36)$, which goes back unchanged to the first.
alg-f-551 · level facile · notes
An algorithm is tail recursive if every recursive call is the last operation and its result is returned unchanged (like RecursiveGCD). How can a tail-recursive algorithm always be transformed?
  1. Into two recursive calls on half of the input
  2. Into an algorithm without a base case
  3. Into a for loop with an array of $n$ frames
  4. Into a while loop that updates the parameters
Explanation. The tail call has nothing to do after returning, so it can be replaced by updating the parameters and repeating until the base case: RecursiveGCD becomes the iterative Euclidean algorithm.
alg-f-552 · level media · notes
RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$ (returns $n$ if $m=0$, otherwise RecursiveGCD$(m,\,n\bmod m)$) performs $2$ steps per call. How many calls and steps does it perform with $(n,m)=(89,55)$, two consecutive Fibonacci numbers?
  1. $9$ calls and $18$ steps
  2. $10$ calls and $20$ steps
  3. $10$ calls and $10$ steps
  4. $55$ calls and $110$ steps
Explanation. The remainders run through the Fibonacci numbers: $(89,55),(55,34),(34,21),(21,13),(13,8),(8,5),(5,3),(3,2),(2,1),(1,0)$, that is $10$ calls. It is the worst case: still, the second argument halves at least every two calls.
alg-f-553 · level facile · notes
FastPower$(x,n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$; otherwise it computes $y=$FastPower$(x,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ and returns $y\cdot y$ if $n$ is even, $y\cdot y\cdot x$ if $n$ is odd. Why is the extra factor $x$ needed in the odd case?
  1. Because $\lfloor n/2\rfloor=n/2$ also for odd $n$
  2. Because the base case returns $x$ instead of $1$
  3. Because $y=x^{n+1}$ when $n$ is odd
  4. Because $2\lfloor n/2\rfloor=n-1$, so $y\cdot y=x^{n-1}$
Explanation. By the inductive hypothesis $y=x^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$. If $n$ is odd, $\lfloor n/2\rfloor=(n-1)/2$ and $y^2=x^{n-1}$: multiplying by $x$ gives $x^n$.
alg-f-554 · level teorema · notes
FastPower$(x,n)$ performs $\Theta(\log n)$ steps, the repeated product $x\cdot x\cdots x$ performs $\Theta(n)$. The input is the natural $n$, with $|I|=\Theta(\log n)$. How do the two running times (FastPower, then repeated product) grow as functions of $|I|$?
  1. $\Theta(|I|)$ and $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  2. $\Theta(|I|)$ and $\Theta(|I|)$
  3. $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$ and $2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  4. $\Theta(\log|I|)$ and $\Theta(|I|)$
Explanation. $\log n=\Theta(|I|)$, while $n=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$. Halving the exponent makes the algorithm polynomial (linear) in the input size; the repeated product is exponential.
alg-f-555 · level media · notes
Hanoi with $n$ disks makes $M(n)=2^n-1$ moves. With $n=64$ disks and one move per second (a year has about $3.15\cdot 10^7$ seconds, and $2^{64}\approx 1.8\cdot 10^{19}$), how long does it take?
  1. About $64$ seconds
  2. About $68$ minutes
  3. About $5.8\cdot 10^{11}$ years
  4. About $1.8\cdot 10^{19}$ years
Explanation. $1.8\cdot 10^{19}/3.15\cdot 10^7\approx 5.8\cdot 10^{11}$ years. $1.8\cdot 10^{19}$ is the number of seconds, not years; $64$ and $4096$ seconds ($68$ minutes) would be $n$ and $n^2$ moves.
alg-f-658 · level teorema · notes
A recursive algorithm performs a constant number of steps per call and uses stack frames of constant size. What do the total number of steps and the memory used by the call stack depend on?
  1. Both on the depth of the tree
  2. Both on the number of nodes of the tree
  3. Steps on the tree's nodes; memory on its depth
  4. Steps on the depth; memory on the nodes
Explanation. Each call is a node and costs a fixed number of steps; at any time the stack holds only the calls on one path from the root. Fib$(4)$: $9$ calls, but at most $4$ frames together.
alg-f-659 · level media · notes
$G(n)$ returns $1$ if $n=0$, otherwise $G(n-1)+G(n-1)$ (two identical calls). How many calls in all does $G(5)$ make, and what is the recursion depth?
  1. $63$ calls, depth $6$
  2. $6$ calls, depth $6$
  3. $32$ calls, depth $5$
  4. $63$ calls, depth $63$
Explanation. The tree is a complete binary tree with levels $0,\dots,5$: $2^6-1=63$ nodes, but a root-to-leaf path holds only $6$. Writing $2\cdot G(n-1)$ would give $6$ calls.
alg-f-660 · level media · notes
Fib$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n\le 1$, otherwise Fib$(n-1)+$Fib$(n-2)$. While computing Fib$(6)$, how many times is Fib$(2)$ called?
  1. $3$
  2. $8$
  3. $1$
  4. $5$
Explanation. The calls with argument $k\ge 1$ in Fib$(n)$ are $F(n-k+1)$: for $k=2$, $F(5)=5$. The same subproblem is solved many times: this is the cause of the exponential number of calls.
alg-f-685 · level ipotesi · notes
In RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$ (call on $(m,\,n\bmod m)$) the second argument halves at least every two calls. Why can one not say «at every call»?
  1. Because $n\bmod m$ can exceed $m$
  2. Because the second argument can stay the same
  3. Because $n\bmod m$ can be close to $m$, as in $(13,8)\to(8,5)$
  4. One can: it always halves at every call
Explanation. $5>8/2$: in one call the remainder may drop only a little. In the next one, however, $8\bmod 5=3$ and $3\le 8/2$: two calls always suffice to halve it.

15. Recurrences (39)

alg-f-116 · level facile · notes
The recurrence $S(n)=2\,S(n/2)+c$ of RecursiveSum is in standard form $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$. What do $a$, $b$ and $f(n)$ represent?
  1. $a=2$ subproblems, each of size $n/b=n/2$; $f(n)=c$ is the cost to divide and combine.
  2. $a=2$ is the size of each subproblem, $b=2$ the number of subproblems.
  3. $a=2$ and $b=2$ are the number of base cases; $f(n)=c$ is the cost of the base cases.
  4. $f(n)=c$ is the total cost of the leaves of the recursion tree.
Explanation. Definition 15.2: $a\ge 1$ is the number of subproblems, $n/b$ (with $b>1$) the size of each of them, $f(n)$ the work to divide the problem and combine the solutions.
alg-f-117 · level facile · notes
Which identity is correct for every $n>0$?
  1. $3^{\log_2 n}=n^{\log_3 2}$
  2. $3^{\log_2 n}=3n$
  3. $3^{\log_2 n}=n^{\log_2 3}$
  4. $3^{\log_2 n}=n^3$
Explanation. We have $a^{\log_b n}=n^{\log_b a}$ (Observation 15.3): this is the identity relating the number of leaves $a^{\log_b n}$ of the recursion tree to $n^{\log_b a}$.
alg-f-118 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=2T(n/2)+c\,n$ (with $n$ a power of $2$), how many levels are there and what does each cost?
  1. $n$ levels of cost $c\,n$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  2. $\log_2 n$ levels of decreasing cost, hence $T(n)=\Theta(n)$
  3. $\log_2 n$ levels of cost $c$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(\log n)$
  4. $\log_2 n+1$ levels of cost $c\,n$ each, hence $T(n)=\Theta(n\log n)$
Explanation. Level $j$ has $2^j$ subproblems of size $n/2^j$, for a cost $2^j\,c\,n/2^j=c\,n$; the levels go from $j=0$ to the leaves $j=\log_2 n$.
alg-f-119 · level facile · notes
With the expansion method, what is the solution of $T(n)=T(n-1)+n$ with $T(1)=1$?
  1. $T(n)=n=\Theta(n)$
  2. $T(n)=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}=\Theta(n^2)$
  3. $T(n)=n\log_2 n$
  4. $T(n)=2^n-1$
Explanation. Expanding, $T(n)=n+(n-1)+\dots+2+T(1)=\sum_{i=1}^n i=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}$. The recurrence is not in standard form: the subproblem has size $n-1$.
alg-f-120 · level media · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=3T(n/4)+c\,n^2$, what is the cost of level $j$ and what is the order of growth of $T(n)$?
  1. $3^j c\,n^2$, increasing costs: $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_4 3})$
  2. $\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^j c\,n^2$, decreasing costs: $T(n)=\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  3. $\left(\frac{3}{16}\right)^j c\,n^2$, decreasing costs: $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $c\,n^2$ at every level: $T(n)=\Theta(n^2\log n)$
Explanation. Level $j$ has $3^j$ nodes of size $n/4^j$, each of cost $c\,(n/4^j)^2$: $(3/16)^j c\,n^2$ in total. The geometric series with ratio $3/16<1$ is bounded, and the root dominates.
alg-f-121 · level teorema · notes
Let $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+1$ for $n>1$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $n\le T(n)\le 2n-1$ for all $n\ge 1$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(n)$.
  2. $T(n)\le n$ for all $n\ge 1$.
  3. $T(n)=\Theta(n\log n)$, as for $2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$.
  4. $T(n)\le c\,n$ cannot be proved for any constant $c$, hence $T(n)\ne\bigO(n)$.
Explanation. This is Observation 15.6. For $n$ a power of $2$ we have $T(n)=2n-1>n$. The guess $T(n)\le c\,n$ makes the inductive step fail because of the term $+1$, but $T(n)=\bigO(n)$ is true: it is enough to strengthen the guess.
alg-f-122 · level ipotesi · notes
With the substitution method we want to prove $T(n)=\bigO(n)$ for $T(1)=1$, $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+1$. Which inductive hypothesis makes the inductive step work?
  1. $T(k)\le c\,k$ for all $k<n$, with $c$ large enough.
  2. $T(k)\le k$ for all $k<n$.
  3. $T(k)\le c\,k-d$ for all $k<n$, with $d\ge 1$ (for instance $c=2$, $d=1$).
  4. $T(k)\le c\,k^2$ for all $k<n$, which gives $T(n)=\bigO(n)$.
Explanation. With $T(k)\le c\,k$ one only gets $T(n)\le c\,n+1$; subtracting a lower-order term, $T(n)\le 2(c\lfloor n/2\rfloor-d)+1\le c\,n-2d+1\le c\,n-d$ if $d\ge 1$. A guess $c\,k^2$ would only give $\bigO(n^2)$.
alg-f-123 · level facile · notes
To solve $T(n)=2T(\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor)+\log_2 n$ we set $m=\log_2 n$ and $R(m)=T(2^m)$. Which recurrence do we obtain, and what is the order of growth of $T(n)$?
  1. $R(m)=2R(m/2)+m$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(\log_2 n\cdot\log_2\log_2 n)$
  2. $R(m)=2R(m/2)+1$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  3. $R(m)=R(m/2)+m$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  4. $R(m)=2R(m-1)+m$, hence $T(n)=\Theta(n)$
Explanation. $\sqrt n=2^{m/2}$, hence $T(2^m)=2T(2^{m/2})+m$, i.e., $R(m)=2R(m/2)+m$, whose solution is $\Theta(m\log m)$; going back to $n$ we get $\Theta(\log_2 n\cdot\log_2\log_2 n)$.
alg-f-251 · level facile · notes
With the expansion method (substituting the recurrence into itself down to the base case), what is the solution of $T(n)=2T(n-1)+1$ with $T(0)=0$?
  1. $T(n)=2^n$
  2. $T(n)=n\,2^{n-1}$
  3. $T(n)=2^n-1$
  4. $T(n)=2n-1$
Explanation. After $k$ substitutions $T(n)=2^kT(n-k)+(2^{k-1}+\dots+2+1)=2^kT(n-k)+2^k-1$; with $k=n$ one gets $T(n)=2^n\cdot 0+2^n-1$. It is the number of moves of the Towers of Hanoi.
alg-f-252 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ ($a\ge 1$, $b>1$ constants, $n$ exact power of $b$, $T(1)=\Theta(1)$), how many leaves are there and what is the cost of the level at depth $j$?
  1. $n^{\log_b a}$ leaves; level $j$ costs $a^j f(n/b^j)$
  2. $n$ leaves; level $j$ costs $a^j f(n/b^j)$
  3. $n^{\log_a b}$ leaves; level $j$ costs $a^j f(n/b^j)$
  4. $n^{\log_b a}$ leaves; level $j$ costs $f(n)/b^j$
Explanation. At depth $j$ there are $a^j$ nodes of size $n/b^j$; the size reaches $1$ at depth $\log_b n$, so the leaves are $a^{\log_b n}=n^{\log_b a}$. The leaves are $n$ only when $a=b$.
alg-f-253 · level media · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=8\,T(n/2)+n^2$ ($n$ power of $2$, $T(1)=\Theta(1)$), how do the level costs evolve and what is the order of growth of $T(n)$?
  1. They decrease going down (the root dominates): $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  2. They are all equal: $T(n)=\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  3. They increase going down (the leaves dominate): $T(n)=\Theta(n^3)$
  4. They increase going down (the leaves dominate): $T(n)=\Theta(n^3\log n)$
Explanation. Level $j$ costs $8^j(n/2^j)^2=2^j\,n^2$: the costs grow geometrically and the total is dominated by the $n^{\log_2 8}=n^3$ leaves. The factor $\log n$ appears only when the levels have equal cost.
alg-f-254 · level facile · notes
Which method is the most suitable to obtain the order of growth of $T(n)=T(n/3)+T(2n/3)+c\,n$?
  1. The Master Theorem with $a=2$ and $b=3/2$, because the largest subproblem has size $2n/3$.
  2. The recursion tree: the two branches have different lengths and each level costs at most $c\,n$.
  3. The expansion method, which always gives an exact closed form for recurrences of this type.
  4. None: a recurrence with two different recursive terms cannot be solved.
Explanation. The recurrence is not in standard form ($a\,T(n/b)$ with a single size $n/b$), so the Master Theorem does not apply. The (unbalanced) tree has depth $\Theta(\log n)$ and suggests $\Theta(n\log n)$, to be confirmed by substitution.
alg-f-255 · level facile · notes
For $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$ one already expects the solution $\Theta(n\log n)$, and wants a rigorous proof, with the floors and the boundary condition. Which method is used?
  1. The recursion tree, which with floors directly gives a complete proof.
  2. The expansion method, ignoring the floors: the result then holds for every $n$.
  3. The Master Theorem, which holds only for $n$ exact power of $2$ and therefore suffices.
  4. The substitution method, proving $\bigO$ and $\Omega$ separately by induction.
Explanation. Substitution verifies a guess that is already known, handling floors and base cases through the choice of the threshold. The tree and the expansion mainly serve to obtain the guess.
alg-f-316 · level facile · notes
A recurrence equation describes a function $T:\N \to \R$ through its values on smaller inputs. Why are initial conditions also needed?
  1. Because the recurrence holds only for even $n$
  2. Because the initial conditions give the order of growth
  3. Because without the values of the base cases the recurrence does not determine a unique function
  4. They are not needed
Explanation. $T(n)=2T(n-1)$ is satisfied by $c\,2^n$ for every $c$: the base case fixes $c$. The order of growth, instead, often does not depend on the base cases.
alg-f-317 · level ipotesi · notes
For $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$ one proves $T(n) \le 2n\log_2 n$ for $n \ge 2$ by the substitution method. In the inductive step, which fact about the logarithm is used?
  1. $\log_2\lfloor n/2\rfloor \le \log_2 n-1$
  2. $\log_2 n \le n$
  3. $\log_2(n/2)=\log_2 n$
  4. $\lfloor n/2\rfloor \ge n/2$
Explanation. $2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n \le 2\cdot 2\lfloor n/2\rfloor(\log_2 n-1)+n \le 2n\log_2 n-2n+n \le 2n\log_2 n$. Without the $-1$ the $+n$ term would stay uncovered.
alg-f-318 · level teorema · notes
For $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$ (with a base case) one proves $T(n) \le 2n\log_2 n$ for $n \ge 2$ and $T(n) \ge \frac14 n\log_2 n$ for $n \ge 1$. What is concluded?
  1. $T(n)=\Theta(n)$
  2. $T(n)=\Theta(n\log_2 n)$
  3. $T(n)=O(n\log_2 n)$ but not $\Omega(n\log_2 n)$
  4. $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
Explanation. An upper and a lower bound of the same order give $\Theta$; the constants $2$ and $1/4$ are irrelevant. It is the recurrence of Merge Sort.
alg-f-556 · level facile · notes
The number of steps of RecursiveSum satisfies $S(1)=2$ and $S(n)=S(n-1)+3$ for $n>1$. What is $S(5)$?
  1. $14$
  2. $15$
  3. $17$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $S(2)=5$, $S(3)=8$, $S(4)=11$, $S(5)=14$; in general $S(n)=3n-1$. $15=3\cdot 5$ ignores the base case.
alg-f-557 · level facile · notes
A recursive algorithm, on an input of size $n>1$, performs a constant number $c$ of steps plus two recursive calls, each on half of the input. Which recurrence describes its number of steps $S(n)$?
  1. $S(n)=S(n/2)+2c$
  2. $S(n)=2S(n-1)+c$
  3. $S(n)=2S(n/2)+c$
  4. $S(n)=S(n-2)+c$
Explanation. Two subproblems ($a=2$) of size $n/2$ ($b=2$), with constant work to divide and combine. $2S(n-1)+c$ is Hanoi's form, with subproblems of size $n-1$.
alg-f-558 · level facile · notes
A recurrence is in standard form if it is written $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ with constants $a\ge 1$ and $b>1$. Which of the following is NOT in standard form?
  1. $T(n)=T(n/2)+1$
  2. $T(n)=3T(n/4)+n^2$
  3. $T(n)=7T(n/2)+n^2$
  4. $T(n)=2T(n-1)+1$
Explanation. In $2T(n-1)+1$ the subproblem has size $n-1$, not a fraction $n/b$ of $n$ («decrease and conquer»). The others have $a=1,3,7$ and $b=2,4,2$.
alg-f-559 · level facile · notes
The chapter on recurrences studies three solution methods: expansion, substitution, recursion tree. Which one requires the form of the solution to be known (or guessed) in advance?
  1. The expansion method
  2. The substitution method
  3. The recursion-tree method
  4. None of the three methods
Explanation. Substitution checks a proposed solution by induction, for instance $T(n)\le c\,n\log_2 n$. Expansion and the recursion tree serve instead to discover it.
alg-f-560 · level facile · notes
The expansion method repeatedly substitutes the recurrence into itself. What does it give, for instance with $T(n)=T(n-1)+3$?
  1. A hypothesis to be proved by induction
  2. A recursion tree with two children per node
  3. $T(n)=T(n-k)+3k$, down to the base case
  4. Directly one of the three cases of the Master theorem
Explanation. After $k$ steps $T(n)=T(n-k)+3k$; with $k=n-1$ one reaches $T(1)$. The Master theorem does not apply: the recurrence is not in standard form.
alg-f-561 · level facile · notes
When solving $T(n)=2T(n/2)+n$ one assumes that $n$ is an exact power of $2$, ignoring the floors and ceilings $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ and $\lceil n/2\rceil$. Why is this usually legitimate?
  1. Because floors normally do not change the order of growth
  2. Because algorithm inputs always have a power-of-$2$ size
  3. Because with floors the recurrence has no solution
  4. Because floors and ceilings change only the base case
Explanation. It is a technical simplification: with $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ the solution stays $\Theta(n\log n)$, as the substitution method confirms. Real inputs have arbitrary sizes.
alg-f-562 · level teorema · notes
For $T(n)=2T(n/2)+n$, with $n$ a power of $2$, the boundary condition is changed from $T(1)=1$ to $T(1)=5$. What happens to the solution?
  1. It becomes $\Theta(5\,n\log n)$, a different order
  2. It becomes $\Theta(n^5)$, because $T(1)=5$
  3. It stays $\Theta(n\log n)$
  4. It becomes $\Theta(n)$, because the base case dominates
Explanation. $T(n)=n\log_2 n+T(1)\,n$: the $n$ leaves cost $T(1)$ each, so $n\log_2 n+5n$. This is why one simply writes $T(1)=\Theta(1)$; and $\Theta(5\,n\log n)=\Theta(n\log n)$.
alg-f-563 · level facile · notes
The geometric series $\sum_{j\ge 0} r^j$ equals $\frac{1}{1-r}$ for $0<r<1$. What is the largest possible value of $1+\frac34+\left(\frac34\right)^2+\dots+\left(\frac34\right)^k$, over all $k$?
  1. $4$
  2. $\frac34$
  3. $\frac74$
  4. It is unbounded
Explanation. $\frac{1}{1-3/4}=4$, and every partial sum is below the infinite sum. It is the computation showing that, in a recursion tree, levels with geometrically decreasing costs cost as much as the root up to a constant.
alg-f-564 · level facile · notes
The number of iterations of a search that halves the interval satisfies $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=T(n/2)+1$ ($n$ a power of $2$). Expanding the recurrence down to the base case, what is $T(n)$?
  1. $T(n)=n$
  2. $T(n)=\log_2 n$
  3. $T(n)=n/2+1$
  4. $T(n)=\log_2 n+1$
Explanation. $T(n)=T(n/2^k)+k$; with $k=\log_2 n$ one reaches $T(1)=1$, hence $\log_2 n+1$. It is the recurrence of BinarySearch.
alg-f-565 · level media · notes
One unrolls $T(n)=T(n/2)+n$, with $T(1)=1$ and $n$ a power of $2$: $T(n)=n+T(n/2)=n+\frac n2+T(n/4)=\dots$ What does one get on reaching the base case?
  1. $T(n)=n\log_2 n+1$
  2. $T(n)=2n-1$
  3. $T(n)=n+\log_2 n$
  4. $T(n)=n^2$
Explanation. $T(n)=n+\frac n2+\frac n4+\dots+2+T(1)=n+\frac n2+\dots+2+1=2n-1$: a geometric series, dominated by the first term. $n\log_2 n$ would come with two calls, $2T(n/2)+n$.
alg-f-566 · level teorema · notes
For $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$ one wants $T(n)\le c\,n\log_2 n$ by the substitution method. From the inductive hypothesis $T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)\le c\lfloor n/2\rfloor\log_2\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ one gets $T(n)\le c\,n\log_2(n/2)+n=c\,n\log_2 n-c\,n+n$. For which $c$ is the inductive step complete?
  1. For every $c>0$
  2. Only for $c\le 1$
  3. For no $c$: a stronger hypothesis is needed
  4. For every $c\ge 1$
Explanation. It suffices that $-c\,n+n\le 0$, that is $c\ge 1$. The base case then fixes the threshold on $n$ (for instance $n\ge 2$, because $\log_2 1=0$) and possibly a larger constant.
alg-f-567 · level facile · notes
In the lower bound for $T(n)=2T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$ by the substitution method one needs an estimate of $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ from below. Which inequality holds for every natural $n\ge 1$?
  1. $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\ge n/2$
  2. $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\ge (n-1)/2$
  3. $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\le (n-1)/2$
  4. $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\ge n-1$
Explanation. For even $n$, $\lfloor n/2\rfloor=n/2$, for odd $n$, $(n-1)/2$: in every case at least $(n-1)/2$. $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\ge n/2$ fails for odd $n$, and that is exactly the loss to be controlled in the proof.
alg-f-568 · level media · notes
To solve $T(n)=T(\sqrt n)+1$ one sets $m=\log_2 n$ and $R(m)=T(2^m)$, so that $T(\sqrt n)=R(m/2)$. What is the order of growth of $T(n)$?
  1. $\Theta(\log\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(\log n)$
  3. $\Theta(\sqrt n)$
  4. $\Theta(1)$
Explanation. $R(m)=R(m/2)+1$ gives $R(m)=\Theta(\log m)$, so $T(n)=\Theta(\log\log n)$: the square root halves the number of bits, and $\Theta(\log m)$ halvings are needed.
alg-f-569 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=3T(n/4)+c\,n^2$, with $n$ a power of $4$ and $T(1)=\Theta(1)$, every internal node has $3$ children and the tree has $\log_4 n$ levels above the leaves. How many leaves are there?
  1. $4^{\log_4 n}=n$
  2. $3\cdot\log_4 n=\log_4 n^3$
  3. $3^{\log_4 n}=n^{\log_4 3}$
  4. $4^{\log_3 n}=n^{\log_3 4}$
Explanation. Level $j$ has $3^j$ nodes, and the leaves are at level $\log_4 n$. With the identity $a^{\log_b n}=n^{\log_b a}$ one gets $n^{\log_4 3}\approx n^{0.79}$, less than $n$.
alg-f-570 · level teorema · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=3T(n/4)+c\,n^2$ level $j$ costs $\left(\frac{3}{16}\right)^j c\,n^2$. Why is $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$?
  1. Because the tree has $\Theta(n^2)$ levels of constant cost
  2. Because every level costs $c\,n^2$
  3. Because the leaves cost $\Theta(n^2)$ in all
  4. Costs decrease geometrically: the root dominates
Explanation. $\sum_j(3/16)^j\le\frac{1}{1-3/16}=\frac{16}{13}$, so $T(n)\le\frac{16}{13}c\,n^2+\Theta(n^{\log_4 3})=\bigO(n^2)$, and the root alone gives $\Omega(n^2)$.
alg-f-571 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=2T(n/2)+c\,n$, with $n$ a power of $2$ and base case $T(1)$, how many nodes (calls) are there in all for $n=8$?
  1. $8$
  2. $15$
  3. $16$
  4. $24$
Explanation. The levels have $1,2,4,8$ nodes (the last one made of the $8$ leaves $T(1)$): $15=2n-1$. The number of levels is $\log_2 8+1=4$.
alg-f-572 · level media · notes
For $T(n)=2T(n/2)+c\,n$ with $T(1)=c$ and $n$ a power of $2$, the recursion tree has $\log_2 n$ internal levels costing $c\,n$ each and $n$ leaves. What is $T(n)$ exactly?
  1. $c\,n\log_2 n$
  2. $c\,n^2+c\,n$
  3. $2c\,n\log_2 n$
  4. $c\,n\log_2 n+c\,n$
Explanation. The internal levels cost $c\,n\log_2 n$ in all, the $n$ leaves $c\,n$. The order is $\Theta(n\log n)$; forgetting the leaves gives $c\,n\log_2 n$, which is right only asymptotically.
alg-f-573 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=T(n/3)+T(2n/3)+c\,n$ the two children of a node of size $m$ have sizes $m/3$ and $2m/3$. How many levels does the longest root-to-leaf path have?
  1. $\log_3 n$
  2. $\log_{3/2} n$
  3. $\log_2 n$
  4. $n/3$
Explanation. The longest path always follows the $2n/3$ child: after $k$ steps the size is $(2/3)^k n$, which reaches $1$ for $k=\log_{3/2} n$. The shortest path, along the $n/3$ children, has $\log_3 n$ levels.
alg-f-574 · level teorema · notes
In the unbalanced tree of $T(n)=T(n/3)+T(2n/3)+c\,n$ every complete level costs $c\,n$, and the deepest levels are incomplete. How does one conclude that $T(n)=\Theta(n\log n)$?
  1. By the Master theorem, case 2, as for $2T(n/2)+c\,n$
  2. Because all paths have the same length
  3. By counting levels: at most $\log_{3/2} n$, the first $\log_3 n$ complete
  4. Because there are exactly $n$ leaves costing $c$ each
Explanation. Every level costs at most $c\,n$ and there are at most $\log_{3/2} n+1$ levels; down to depth $\log_3 n$ the tree is complete, and these levels alone cost $c\,n\log_3 n$. The Master theorem does not apply: the subproblems have different sizes.
alg-f-575 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=4T(n/2)+n$, with $n$ a power of $2$, level $j$ has $4^j$ nodes of size $n/2^j$. How much does level $j$ cost (not counting the leaves)?
  1. $2^j\,n$
  2. $n$
  3. $n/2^j$
  4. $4^j\,n$
Explanation. $4^j\cdot\frac{n}{2^j}=2^j\,n$: costs grow by a factor $2$ at each level. $4^j n$ forgets that each node has size $n/2^j$.
alg-f-576 · level media · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=4T(n/2)+n$ ($n$ a power of $2$, $T(1)=\Theta(1)$) level costs grow geometrically and there are $4^{\log_2 n}$ leaves. What is the order of growth of $T(n)$?
  1. $\Theta(n\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $\Theta(n^4)$
Explanation. $4^{\log_2 n}=n^{\log_2 4}=n^2$ leaves; the internal levels sum to $n(1+2+\dots+2^{\log_2 n-1})<n\cdot n$. When costs grow, the leaves dominate.
alg-f-577 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ the leaves cost $\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$ in all and the root $f(n)$. For $T(n)=9T(n/3)+n$, which part dominates and what is the solution?
  1. The leaves: $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  2. The root: $T(n)=\Theta(n)$
  3. All levels alike: $\Theta(n\log n)$
  4. The leaves: $T(n)=\Theta(n^3)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_3 9}=n^2$ grows faster than $f(n)=n$: level costs grow by a factor $9/3=3$, and the leaves dominate. $n^3$ mistakes $a$ for the exponent.
alg-f-578 · level teorema · notes
Towards the Master theorem: in the tree of $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ one compares $f(n)$ with $n^{\log_b a}$. What happens when $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$?
  1. The root dominates: $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))$
  2. $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log n)$
  3. The leaves dominate: $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$
  4. $T(n)=\Theta(f(n)\cdot n^{\log_b a})$
Explanation. With equal costs per level one multiplies the cost of a level by the number of levels, as in $2T(n/2)+n=\Theta(n\log n)$. It is case 2 of the Master theorem.

16. The Master Theorem (36)

alg-f-124 · level facile · notes
Using the Master Theorem, what is the solution of $T(n)=9\,T(n/3)+n$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^3)$
  4. $\Theta(n^2)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_b a}=n^{\log_3 9}=n^2$ and $f(n)=n=\bigO(n^{2-\epsilon})$ with $\epsilon=1$: case 1, the leaves dominate.
alg-f-125 · level facile · notes
Using the Master Theorem, what is the solution of $T(n)=T(2n/3)+1$?
  1. $\Theta(1)$
  2. $\Theta(\log n)$
  3. $\Theta(n)$
  4. $\Theta(n^{\log_{3/2} 2})$
Explanation. $a=1$, $b=3/2$, hence $n^{\log_b a}=n^0=1$ and $f(n)=1=\Theta(n^0\log^0 n)$: case 2 with $k=0$, $T(n)=\Theta(\log n)$.
alg-f-126 · level facile · notes
To which of these recurrences does the Master Theorem not apply?
  1. $T(n)=2T(n/2)+n\log_2 n$
  2. $T(n)=4T(n/2)+n^2$
  3. $T(n)=3T(n/4)+n\log_2 n$
  4. $T(n)=2T(n/2)+n/\log_2 n$
Explanation. $n/\log_2 n$ is smaller than $n^{\log_2 2}=n$ but not polynomially, and it is not of the form $n\log^k n$ with $k\ge0$: it falls in the gap between cases 1 and 2 (solution $\Theta(n\log\log n)$). With $n\log_2 n$ for $a=b=2$ and $4T(n/2)+n^2$ we are in case 2, with $3T(n/4)+n\log_2 n$ in case 3.
alg-f-127 · level facile · notes
Why does the Master Theorem not apply to $T(n)=T(n-1)+n$?
  1. Because $a=1$, while the theorem requires at least two subproblems, i.e., $a\ge 2$.
  2. Because the subproblem has size $n-1$ and not $n/b$ with a constant $b>1$: the recurrence is not in standard form.
  3. Because $f(n)=n$ grows too much with respect to $n^{\log_b a}$ and is not asymptotically positive.
  4. Because $f(n)=n$ falls into case 3, but the regularity condition fails.
Explanation. The theorem requires $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ with constants $a\ge 1$ and $b>1$: $a=1$ is allowed. The recurrence is solved by expansion: $\Theta(n^2)$.
alg-f-128 · level facile · notes
In case 3 of the Master Theorem, besides $f(n)=\Omega(n^{\log_b a+\epsilon})$, which condition is required?
  1. $a\,f(n/b)\le c\,f(n)$ for some constant $c<1$ and all sufficiently large $n$ (regularity condition).
  2. $f(n/b)\le f(n)$ for all $n$, i.e., $f$ non-decreasing (monotonicity condition).
  3. $a\,f(n/b)\ge c\,f(n)$ for some constant $c>1$ and all sufficiently large $n$.
  4. $f(n)=\bigO(n^{\log_b a})$, so that root and leaves have the same order.
Explanation. The regularity condition says that the cost of the levels decreases geometrically from the root to the leaves, so the root dominates: $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))$.
alg-f-129 · level media · notes
Using the Master Theorem, what is the solution of $T(n)=7\,T(n/2)+n^2$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2)$
  2. $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^{\log_2 7})$
  4. $\Theta(n^3)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_2 7}\approx n^{2.81}$ and $f(n)=n^2=\bigO(n^{\log_2 7-\epsilon})$ with $\epsilon=\log_2 7-2>0$: case 1. This is the recurrence of Strassen's algorithm, which beats $\Theta(n^3)$.
alg-f-130 · level teorema · notes
Let $a\ge 1$, $b>1$ be constants and $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$. Which statement of the Master Theorem is correct?
  1. If $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^k n)$ for some constant $k\ge 0$, then $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^{k} n)$.
  2. If $f(n)=\bigO(n^{\log_b a})$, then $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$.
  3. If $f(n)=\Omega(n^{\log_b a+\epsilon})$ for some constant $\epsilon>0$, then $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))$, with no further condition.
  4. If $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^k n)$ for some constant $k\ge 0$, then $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^{k+1} n)$.
Explanation. This is case 2: all the $\log_b n$ levels cost of the order of $f(n)$, and one more factor $\log n$ appears. Case 1 requires $f$ to be polynomially smaller ($n^{\log_b a-\epsilon}$), case 3 also the regularity condition.
alg-f-131 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the base of the logarithms in the Master Theorem is correct?
  1. The base is irrelevant everywhere: $n^{\log_2 4}=\Theta(n^{\log_4 4})$.
  2. Inside $\Theta$ the base of the logarithm in $\log^k n$ is irrelevant, while the base $b$ in the exponent of $n^{\log_b a}$ is essential.
  3. The base is essential everywhere: $\log_2 n$ and $\log_3 n$ have different orders of growth.
  4. The theorem holds only with logarithms in base $2$.
Explanation. $\log_c n=\log_2 n/\log_2 c$ differs from $\log_2 n$ by a constant factor; instead $n^{\log_2 4}=n^2$ and $n^{\log_4 4}=n$, and $n^2\ne\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-132 · level ipotesi · notes
We want to prove the Master Theorem for $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ ($a\ge1$, $b>1$) when $n$ is an exact power of $b$. Which results are used?
  1. The bounds on $\lceil n/b\rceil$ and on $\lceil\lceil n/b\rceil/b\rceil$, needed to follow the sizes of the subproblems.
  2. Only the fact that the regularity condition $a\,f(n/b)\le c\,f(n)$ implies $f(n)=\Omega(n^{\log_b a+\epsilon})$.
  3. The substitution method with the guess $T(n)\le c\,n^{\log_b a}$, which covers the three cases together.
  4. $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})+\sum_{j=0}^{\log_b n-1}a^jf(n/b^j)$ (leaves plus levels), and a bound on the sum in the three cases.
Explanation. The first formula separates the cost of the leaves from that of the internal levels, the bound on the sum handles the three cases; then the two terms are compared. Ceilings are needed only to extend the result to every $n$.
alg-f-133 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $T(1)=d$ and $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ for $n$ a power of $b$. In the recursion tree, what is needed to write the cost of the leaves as $\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$?
  1. The geometric series with ratio $b^\epsilon$, which in case 1 is dominated by its last term.
  2. The expansion $T(n)=a^iT(n/b^i)+\sum_{j<i}a^jf(n/b^j)$ up to $i=\log_b n$, and $a^{\log_b n}=n^{\log_b a}$.
  3. The regularity condition $a\,f(n/b)\le c\,f(n)$ with $c<1$, applied level by level.
  4. The fact that there are $n$ leaves, one per element, each of cost $d$.
Explanation. With $i=\log_b n$ we reach $T(1)=d$ and the first term is $d\,a^{\log_b n}=d\,n^{\log_b a}$; the leaves are $n$ only when $a=b$. The geometric series and the regularity condition bound the sum over the levels.
alg-f-256 · level facile · notes
Karatsuba's algorithm multiplies two $n$-digit integers with $3$ recursive multiplications of $n/2$-digit integers and linear work to divide and combine. Writing the recurrence in standard form $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$, what are $a$, $b$ and $f(n)$?
  1. $a=2$, $b=3$, $f(n)=n$
  2. $a=3$, $b=2$, $f(n)=n$
  3. $a=3$, $b=3$, $f(n)=n$
  4. $a=3$, $b=2$, $f(n)=n^2$
Explanation. $a$ is the number of subproblems ($3$), $n/b$ their size ($n/2$), $f(n)$ the work outside the calls ($\Theta(n)$). The Master Theorem gives $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_2 3})$.
alg-f-257 · level facile · notes
A recurrence is in standard form if it can be written $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ with constants $a\ge 1$, $b>1$ and $f$ asymptotically positive ($n/b$ may also mean $\lfloor n/b\rfloor$ or $\lceil n/b\rceil$). Which of the following is not in standard form?
  1. $T(n)=2\,T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+n$
  2. $T(n)=T(n/2)+1$
  3. $T(n)=16\,T(n/4)+n^2\log_2 n$
  4. $T(n)=\frac12\,T(n/2)+n$
Explanation. With $a=\frac12<1$ there would be less than one subproblem: the condition $a\ge 1$ fails. Floors are allowed, and $a=1$ (binary search) is fine.
alg-f-258 · level media · notes
With the Master Theorem (case 2: if $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^k n)$ with $k\ge 0$ then $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^{k+1}n)$), what is the solution of $T(n)=4\,T(n/2)+n^2\log_2 n$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n^2)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2\log^2 n)$
  4. $\Theta(n^3)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_2 4}=n^2$ and $f(n)=n^2\log n$ has the form $n^{\log_b a}\log^k n$ with $k=1$: case 2 adds a logarithmic factor, $T(n)=\Theta(n^2\log^2 n)$. $f$ is not polynomially larger than $n^2$, so it is not case 3.
alg-f-259 · level media · notes
For $T(n)=2\,T(n/2)+n^2$ we have $f(n)=n^2=\Omega(n^{\log_2 2+\epsilon})$; case 3 of the Master Theorem also requires the regularity condition $a\,f(n/b)\le c\,f(n)$ with a constant $c<1$. Which constant satisfies it, and which solution is obtained?
  1. $c=\frac12$; $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  2. $c=\frac14$; $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$
  3. $c=2$; $T(n)=\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  4. None: the condition fails and the theorem does not apply
Explanation. $a\,f(n/b)=2\,(n/2)^2=\frac12 n^2$, so it holds with $c=\frac12$ (not with $c=\frac14$, which would require $\frac12 n^2\le\frac14 n^2$). In case 3 the root dominates: $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))=\Theta(n^2)$.
alg-f-319 · level ipotesi · notes
The proof of the Master theorem first works with $n$ an exact power of $b$. Which fact then allows extending the bounds to all $n$?
  1. Every natural is a power of $b$
  2. Floors and ceilings never change the value of $T$
  3. Case 3 needs no extension
  4. For regular functions, what holds on powers of $b$ holds for all $n$
Explanation. Between consecutive powers $b^j \le n<b^{j+1}$ monotone functions change by at most a constant factor, absorbed by $O$ and $\Omega$. Floors and ceilings are treated separately.
alg-f-320 · level teorema · notes
In the proof of the Master theorem, $g(n)=\sum_{j=0}^{\log_b n-1} a^j f(n/b^j)$ is the cost of the internal levels of the recursion tree, with $p=\log_b a$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If $f(n)=\Theta(n^p)$ then $g(n)=\Theta(n^p\log_b n)$
  2. $g(n)=\Theta(f(n))$ always
  3. If $f(n)=\Theta(n^p)$ then $g(n)=\Theta(n^p)$
  4. $g(n)$ does not depend on $f$
Explanation. In the first case the levels form a geometric series dominated by the leaves; in the second all $\log_b n$ levels cost $\Theta(n^p)$, hence the logarithmic factor.
alg-f-321 · level facile · notes
With the recurrence $T(n)=aT(\lceil n/b\rceil)+f(n)$ the subproblem sizes at level $j$ are $n_j$, with $n_0=n$ and $n_{j+1}=\lceil n_j/b\rceil$. With $n=10$ and $b=2$, what is $n_3$?
  1. $1$
  2. $1.25$
  3. $2$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $n_1=\lceil 10/2\rceil=5$, $n_2=\lceil 5/2\rceil=3$, $n_3=\lceil 3/2\rceil=2$. Without ceilings one would get $10/8=1.25$: ceilings add at most a constant.
alg-f-322 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the Master theorem with ceilings ($T(n)=aT(\lceil n/b\rceil)+f(n)$) is correct?
  1. The same three cases hold, for every $n$
  2. It holds only for $n$ a power of $b$
  3. Only case 2 holds
  4. The result changes by a factor $\log_b n$
Explanation. Ceilings change the subproblem sizes by a bounded amount, and the three cases stay the same. This is why floors and ceilings are ignored in applications.
alg-f-323 · level ipotesi · notes
In case 3 of the Master theorem the regularity condition $a\,f(n/b) \le c\,f(n)$ with $c<1$ is required. Observation: it implies $f(n)=\Omega(n^{p+\epsilon})$, with $p=\log_b a$. Why?
  1. Because $c<1$ makes $f$ decreasing
  2. Because $f$ is a polynomial
  3. Iterating it: $f(n) \ge (a/c)^j f(n/b^j)$
  4. Because regularity holds only for constant $f$
Explanation. With $j=\log_b n$ levels one gets $f(n) \ge \Theta(n^{\log_b(a/c)})$, and $\log_b(a/c)=p+\log_b(1/c)>p$. So regularity makes the second condition of case 3 redundant.
alg-f-579 · level facile · notes
RecursiveBinarySearch examines the middle element and then calls itself on one half only, with constant work per call. Which parameters does its standard-form recurrence $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ have?
  1. $a=2$, $b=2$, $f(n)=\Theta(1)$
  2. $a=1$, $b=2$, $f(n)=\Theta(n)$
  3. $a=2$, $b=1$, $f(n)=\Theta(1)$
  4. $a=1$, $b=2$, $f(n)=\Theta(1)$
Explanation. One subproblem ($a=1$) of half size ($b=2$), with one comparison and one computation of $mid$ ($f(n)=\Theta(1)$): $T(n)=\Theta(\log n)$. $a=2$ would also count the discarded half.
alg-f-580 · level teorema · notes
In the standard form $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ of the Master theorem one requires constants $a\ge 1$ and $b>1$. What do these two conditions guarantee?
  1. At least one subproblem, smaller than the instance
  2. That the solution $T(n)$ is always polynomial
  3. That the function $f(n)$ is increasing
  4. That the tree has exactly $a$ levels
Explanation. $a$ is the number of subproblems and $n/b$ their size: with $b>1$ the size decreases by a constant factor, and the tree has $\log_b n$ levels.
alg-f-581 · level facile · notes
Rule of thumb of the Master theorem for $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$: compare $f(n)$ with $n^{\log_b a}$, and the larger term wins (if they have the same order, multiply by $\log n$). What is the solution of $T(n)=3T(n/2)+n^2$?
  1. $\Theta(n^{\log_2 3})$
  2. $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $\Theta(n^3)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_2 3}\approx n^{1.58}$ is polynomially smaller than $n^2$: case 3, with regularity $3(n/2)^2=\frac34 n^2$. Hence $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$.
alg-f-582 · level facile · notes
With the rule of thumb of the Master theorem (compare $f(n)$ with $n^{\log_b a}$; for equal orders multiply by $\log n$), what is the solution of $T(n)=4T(n/2)+n^2$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n^2)$
  3. $\Theta(n^4)$
  4. $\Theta(n\log n)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_2 4}=n^2$, of the same order as $f(n)=n^2$: case 2, $\Theta(n^2\log n)$. Every level of the tree costs $n^2$, and there are $\log_2 n$ levels.
alg-f-583 · level teorema · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ there are $a^{\log_b n}$ leaves. Why does the term $n^{\log_b a}$ of the Master theorem measure the cost of the leaves?
  1. Because the tree has $n^{\log_b a}$ levels
  2. Because the root costs $n^{\log_b a}$
  3. Because $a^{\log_b n}=n^{\log_b a}$
  4. Because each level has $n$ nodes of cost $\Theta(1)$
Explanation. Both equal $2^{\log_2 a\cdot\log_2 n/\log_2 b}$. The Master theorem thus compares the cost of the leaves with the cost $f(n)$ of the root.
alg-f-584 · level media · notes
For $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+n^k$, level $j$ of the recursion tree costs $a^j(n/b^j)^k=(a/b^k)^j n^k$. With $a=8$, $b=2$, $k=2$, how do level costs change and what is the solution?
  1. They stay equal: $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  2. They double: $\Theta(n^3)$
  3. They halve: $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. They grow by a factor $8$: $\Theta(n^8)$
Explanation. $a/b^k=8/4=2>1$: costs grow geometrically towards the leaves, which are $n^{\log_2 8}=n^3$ (case 1). Ratio $1$ would give case 2, ratio below $1$ case 3.
alg-f-585 · level teorema · notes
Case 1 of the Master theorem asks $f(n)$ to be polynomially smaller than $n^{\log_b a}$. What does it mean?
  1. $f(n)=\bigO(n^{\log_b a})$, with any constant
  2. $f(n)=o(n^{\log_b a})$
  3. $f(n)\le n^{\log_b a}$ for every $n\ge 1$
  4. $f(n)=\bigO(n^{\log_b a-\epsilon})$ with $\epsilon>0$
Explanation. A margin of a factor $n^\epsilon$ is needed. Being only $o(n^{\log_b a})$ is not enough: $n/\log n=o(n)$ but it is not $\bigO(n^{1-\epsilon})$ for any $\epsilon>0$, and it falls into a gap between the cases.
alg-f-586 · level ipotesi · notes
For $T(n)=2T(n/2)+f(n)$ we have $n^{\log_2 2}=n$. With $f(n)=n^2/\log_2 n$, the ratio $f(n)/n=n/\log_2 n$ grows. Can case 3 be applied (it requires $f(n)=\Omega(n^{1+\epsilon})$ and regularity)?
  1. No: $f$ falls into a gap between the cases
  2. Yes: $n^2/\log_2 n=\Omega(n^{1.5})$, and regularity holds
  3. No: case 3 requires exactly $f(n)=n^2$
  4. Yes, but the solution is $\Theta(n\log n)$
Explanation. $\epsilon=\frac12$ suffices: $n^2/\log_2 n\ge n^{1.5}$ for large $n$. Regularity: $2f(n/2)=\frac{n^2}{2\log_2(n/2)}\le\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{n^2}{\log_2 n}\cdot\frac{\log_2 n}{\log_2 n-1}$, which is $\le\frac34 f(n)$ for large $n$. Hence $T(n)=\Theta(n^2/\log n)$.
alg-f-587 · level media · notes
The recurrence $T(n)=2T(n/2)+n\log_{10} n$ uses a base-$10$ logarithm. Knowing that the extended case 2 of the Master theorem gives $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^{k+1} n)$ when $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log^k n)$, $k\ge 0$, what is the solution?
  1. $\Theta(n\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n\log_{10} n)$
  3. It does not apply, because of base $10$
  4. $\Theta(n\log^2 n)$
Explanation. $\log_{10} n=\log_2 n/\log_2 10$ differs from $\log_2 n$ by a constant, so $f(n)=\Theta(n\log n)$: case 2 with $k=1$. Inside $\Theta$ the base of the logarithm does not matter.
alg-f-588 · level media · notes
With the Master theorem, what is the solution of $T(n)=2T(n/4)+\sqrt n$?
  1. $\Theta(\sqrt n)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(\sqrt n\,\log n)$
  4. $\Theta(\log n)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_4 2}=n^{1/2}=\sqrt n$, of the same order as $f(n)$: case 2, $\Theta(\sqrt n\log n)$. Forgetting the $\log n$ factor is the typical case-2 mistake.
alg-f-589 · level facile · notes
The Master theorem requires $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$ with constants $a\ge 1$ and $b>1$. For which recurrence does exactly the hypothesis on $a$ fail?
  1. $T(n)=\frac12 T(n/2)+n$
  2. $T(n)=T(n/2)+n$
  3. $T(n)=16T(n/4)+n$
  4. $T(n)=3T(n/3)+1$
Explanation. $a=\frac12<1$: fewer than one subproblem makes no sense for an algorithm. The others have $a=1,16,3$ and are solved by the theorem.
alg-f-590 · level facile · notes
The Master theorem does not apply to $T(n)=T(n-1)+n$ nor to $T(n)=T(n/3)+T(2n/3)+n$. How is the order of growth found then?
  1. It cannot be found by any method
