  2. With another method (expansion, tree, substitution)
  3. Case 2 of the theorem is applied anyway
  4. One rounds $n-1$ to $n/2$ and applies the theorem
Explanation. Expansion gives $T(n)=\Theta(n^2)$ for the first; the recursion tree gives $\Theta(n\log n)$ for the second. Rounding the recurrence would change the solution.
alg-f-591 · level facile · notes
The proof of the Master theorem first works with $n$ an exact power of $b$. Why does this hypothesis simplify the computation?
  1. Because then the function $f(n)$ is constant
  2. Because then $a$ and $b$ coincide
  3. Because the leaves become exactly $n$
  4. The sizes $n/b^j$ are all integers
Explanation. With $n=b^h$ every subproblem has integer size $b^{h-j}$ and all leaves are at level $h$. The general case then follows with floors and ceilings.
alg-f-592 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of $T(n)=a\,T(n/b)+f(n)$, with $n$ a power of $b$, how many nodes does level $j$ have and how much does each cost?
  1. $b^j$ nodes, each costing $f(n/a^j)$
  2. $a^j$ nodes, each costing $f(n/b^j)$
  3. $a^j$ nodes, each costing $f(n)/a^j$
  4. $j$ nodes, each costing $f(n/b)$
Explanation. Each node has $a$ children, and going down one level the size is divided by $b$. The total cost is $\Theta(n^{\log_b a})+\sum_{j=0}^{\log_b n-1}a^j f(n/b^j)$.
alg-f-593 · level facile · notes
In the proof of the Master theorem, $g(n)=\sum_{j=0}^{\log_b n-1}a^j f(n/b^j)$. In case 2, $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$. What is each term $a^j f(n/b^j)$, and hence $g(n)$?
  1. The terms decrease, so $g(n)=\Theta(f(n))$
  2. The terms increase, so $g(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$
  3. Each term is $\Theta(1)$, so $g(n)=\Theta(\log n)$
  4. Each term is $\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$, so $g(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a}\log n)$
Explanation. $a^j(n/b^j)^{\log_b a}=a^j\,n^{\log_b a}/a^j=n^{\log_b a}$: the $\log_b n$ levels all cost the same.
alg-f-594 · level teorema · notes
For $n$ a power of $b$, $T(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})+g(n)$, where $g(n)$ is the cost of the internal levels. In case 3 one proves $g(n)=\Theta(f(n))$, with $f(n)=\Omega(n^{\log_b a+\epsilon})$. Why then is $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))$?
  1. Because the cost of the leaves is $\bigO(f(n))$
  2. Because in case 3 there are no leaves
  3. Because $f(n)=\Theta(n^{\log_b a})$
  4. Because $g(n)$ is always larger than $T(n)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_b a}=\bigO(n^{\log_b a+\epsilon})=\bigO(f(n))$, so the sum of the two terms is $\Theta(f(n))$: the root dominates.
alg-f-595 · level media · notes
Case 3 of the Master theorem needs the regularity condition: $a\,f(n/b)\le c\,f(n)$ for a constant $c<1$ and large $n$. For $T(n)=3T(n/2)+n^2$, which constant $c$ is obtained?
  1. $c=\frac32$
  2. $c=\frac12$
  3. $c=\frac34$
  4. $c=3$
Explanation. $3\,(n/2)^2=\frac34 n^2$, so the condition holds with $c=\frac34<1$. $\frac32$ forgets to square the factor $\frac12$.

17. Recursive algorithms (57)

alg-f-134 · level facile · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$, otherwise RecursiveSum$(a,l,m)+$RecursiveSum$(a,m+1,r)$ with $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$. It is called on a sub-array of $n=8$ elements. How many calls does it execute in total and what is the recursion depth?
  1. $15$ calls, depth $3$
  2. $8$ calls, depth $4$
  3. $15$ calls, depth $4$
  4. $16$ calls, depth $8$
Explanation. The calls are $2n-1=15$ ($n$ base cases and $n-1$ additions); the tree has height $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil=3$, hence depth $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1=4$.
alg-f-135 · level facile · notes
RecursiveSumLinear$(a,n)$ returns RecursiveSumLinear$(a,n-1)+a[n]$ (with base case $n=0$); RecursiveSum recursively adds the two halves of the array. How much call-stack memory do they use, with frames of constant size?
  1. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  2. $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ for both
  3. $\Theta(n)$ for both
  4. $\Theta(1)$ and $\Theta(\log_2 n)$
Explanation. Both execute $\Theta(n)$ steps, but the recursion depth is $n$ for the decrease by one and $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1$ for the halving.
alg-f-136 · level facile · notes
MergeSort sorts $a[l..r]$ by recursively sorting the two halves and merging them with Merge, which copies the halves into two auxiliary arrays $L$ and $R$. Which statement is correct?
  1. It executes $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ steps and it is in-place, like Insertion Sort.
  2. It executes $\Theta(n^2)$ steps in the worst case, like Insertion Sort.
  3. It executes $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ steps in the worst case and uses $\Theta(n)$ extra memory: it is not in-place.
  4. It uses only $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ extra memory, that of the call stack.
Explanation. $S(n)=2S(n/2)+\Theta(n)$ gives $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$; Merge allocates the arrays $L$ and $R$, up to $n$ elements in the first call, plus $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ for the stack.
alg-f-137 · level facile · notes
Recursive binary search examines the central element and calls itself on one half, with $S(n)=S(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+c$ steps. On an array of $n$ entries of constant size, what is the worst-case running time as a function of the number of bits of the input $|I|$?
  1. $T(|I|)=\Theta(\log|I|)$
  2. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|)$
  3. $T(|I|)=2^{\Theta(|I|)}$
  4. $T(|I|)=\Theta(|I|\log|I|)$
Explanation. $S(n)=S(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+c$ gives $S(n)=\Theta(\log_2 n)$, and for an array $|I|=\Theta(n)$: the running time is logarithmic in the input size.
alg-f-138 · level facile · notes
Maximum subarray problem on $a[1..n]$: brute force tries all pairs $l\le r$ updating the sum, FindMaximumSubarray is divide and conquer (two halves plus the crossing subarrays), Kadane makes a single scan. What is their worst-case order of growth?
  1. Brute force $\Theta(n^3)$, FindMaximumSubarray $\Theta(n^2)$, Kadane $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$
  2. Brute force $\Theta(n^2)$, FindMaximumSubarray $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$, Kadane $\Theta(n)$
  3. All three $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$
  4. Brute force $\Theta(n^2)$, FindMaximumSubarray $\Theta(n)$, Kadane $\Theta(n)$
Explanation. The brute force tries all pairs $l\le r$ updating the sum; FindMaximumSubarray satisfies $S(n)=2S(n/2)+\Theta(n)$ (case 2); Kadane makes a single scan.
alg-f-139 · level teorema · notes
In the divide and conquer algorithm for the maximum subarray, FindMaxCrossingSubarray$(a,l,mid,r)$, with $l\le mid<r$ and $n=r-l+1$, handles the subarrays crossing the midpoint. Which statement is correct?
  1. It finds a maximum-sum subarray of the whole $a[l..r]$, in $\Theta(n)$ steps, with two scans.
  2. It finds the maximum-sum subarray ending at $a[mid]$, in $\Theta(\log n)$ steps.
  3. It finds a maximum-sum subarray among those containing $a[mid]$, in $\Theta(n^2)$ steps.
  4. It finds a maximum-sum subarray among those containing $a[mid]$ and $a[mid+1]$, in $\Theta(n)$ steps.
Explanation. The left and right parts of a subarray crossing the midpoint can be chosen independently, with two linear scans. The maximum over the whole $a[l..r]$ is obtained only by comparing with the two recursive results.
alg-f-140 · level teorema · notes
RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,l,r,m)$ returns $-1$ if $l>r$ (empty interval); otherwise it examines $mid=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ and, if $a[mid]\ne m$, calls itself on $[l,mid-1]$ or on $[mid+1,r]$. Which statement about the calls of RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,1,n,m)$, $n\ge 1$, is correct?
  1. At most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ calls are on a non-empty interval, and in total there are at most $\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+2$ calls.
  2. There are exactly $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$ calls on every input, both with $m$ present and with $m$ absent.
  3. There are at most $n/2$ calls, because each call discards at least two elements of the interval.
  4. There are at most $\log_2 n$ calls, including the one on an empty interval.
Explanation. Observation 17.7: the interval is at least halved at each call, and after the last non-empty call at most one call on an empty interval can follow. The bound is attained, for instance, with $m>a[n]$.
alg-f-141 · level ipotesi · notes
MergeSort$(a,l,r)$, if $l<r$, recursively sorts $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ and merges them with Merge. What is used to prove its correctness?
  1. The invariant of the inner loop of Insertion Sort, applied to each merge of the two sorted halves.
  2. The Master Theorem applied to $S(n)=2S(n/2)+\Theta(n)$, which also guarantees termination.
  3. Strong induction on the number $k=r-l+1$ of elements and the correctness of Merge, proved with a loop invariant.
  4. Simple induction from $k-1$ to $k$ elements, since each call removes at least one element.
Explanation. By the inductive hypothesis the two calls (with between $1$ and $k-1$ elements) sort $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$; so the precondition of Merge holds, and it produces $a[l..r]$ sorted. The Master Theorem concerns the number of steps, not correctness.
alg-f-142 · level ipotesi · notes
FindMaximumSubarray$(a,l,r)$ recursively looks for the maximum-sum subarray in the two halves, the one crossing the midpoint with FindMaxCrossingSubarray, and returns the best of the three. What is needed to prove its correctness?
  1. Strong induction; every subarray is on the left, on the right or crossing; correctness of FindMaxCrossingSubarray.
  2. Only the correctness of FindMaxCrossingSubarray: the maximum-sum subarray always crosses the midpoint.
  3. The invariant of Kadane: $cur$ is the maximum sum of a subarray ending at the current position.
  4. The assumption that the array contains at least one positive element, without which the maximum is undefined.
Explanation. By the inductive hypothesis the two calls find the maximum of the subarrays of the first two types, FindMaxCrossingSubarray that of the third type; the maximum of the three is the answer. The maximum does not always cross the midpoint.
alg-f-143 · level facile · notes
An algorithm is tail recursive if it returns unchanged the result of its last call. RecursiveBinarySearch makes a single call on one half; RecursiveSum adds the results on the two halves; MergeSort calls Merge after the two calls; FindMaximumSubarray compares three results. Which one is tail recursive?
  1. RecursiveSum
  2. MergeSort
  3. RecursiveBinarySearch
  4. FindMaximumSubarray
Explanation. RecursiveBinarySearch directly returns the value of its only call (Definition 17.8). RecursiveSum adds two results, MergeSort calls Merge after the calls, FindMaximumSubarray compares three results.
alg-f-260 · level facile · notes
FindMaximumSubarray solves the maximum subarray problem by divide and conquer: two calls on the halves and a linear scan for the subarrays crossing the midpoint, without auxiliary arrays. How many steps does it perform in the worst case and how much extra memory does it use (call stack included)?
  1. $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ steps and $\Theta(n)$ memory
  2. $\Theta(n)$ steps and $\Theta(1)$ memory
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$ steps and $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ memory
  4. $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ steps and $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ memory
Explanation. $S(n)=2S(n/2)+\Theta(n)$ gives $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$, as for MergeSort; but without the arrays $L$ and $R$ of Merge the memory is only the stack, proportional to the depth $\Theta(\log_2 n)$. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(1)$ are those of Kadane.
alg-f-261 · level facile · notes
Ignoring multiplicative constants: InsertionSort performs $n^2$ steps and MergeSort $n\log_2 n$, on a computer that executes $10^9$ steps per second. About how much time do they need with $n=10^6$?
  1. About $1$ second and about $0.001$ seconds
  2. About $1000$ seconds and about $0.02$ seconds
  3. About $1000$ seconds and about $20$ seconds
  4. About $10^6$ seconds and about $0.02$ seconds
Explanation. $n^2=10^{12}$ steps, i.e., $10^3$ seconds; $n\log_2 n\approx 10^6\cdot 20=2\cdot 10^7$ steps, i.e., $0.02$ seconds. Since $n\log_2 n=o(n^2)$, the gap grows with $n$.
alg-f-324 · level ipotesi · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$, otherwise with $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ it returns RecursiveSum$(a,l,m)+$RecursiveSum$(a,m+1,r)$. On which quantity is induction done to prove it correct?
  1. On the value of the sum
  2. On the number of elements $r-l+1$
  3. On the index $l$ only
  4. On the number of bits of $a[l]$
Explanation. Both calls work on sub-arrays with fewer elements (for $l<r$), so the strong inductive hypothesis applies to both; the base case is a single element.
alg-f-325 · level facile · notes
The call tree of RecursiveSum (which halves the sub-array until one element is left) on $n=10$ elements has height $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$. What is the recursion depth, that is, the maximum number of calls active at the same time?
  1. $4$
  2. $10$
  3. $19$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Height $\lceil\log_2 10\rceil=4$, depth $4+1=5$ (the initial call also counts). $19=2n-1$ is the total number of calls.
alg-f-326 · level ipotesi · notes
Merge merges the sorted $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$, writing at position $k$ the smaller of the two current elements. Which invariant, true at the start of every iteration, proves it correct?
  1. The two halves stay sorted and disjoint
  2. $a[l..k-1]$ holds, sorted, the $k-l$ smallest elements
  3. $k$ always equals $m$
  4. The array $a[l..r]$ is already sorted
Explanation. The invariant describes the partial result; at the end (all positions written) it gives the sorting of $a[l..r]$. The array is not sorted before the end.
alg-f-327 · level teorema · notes
Let $a[1..n]$ be sorted in nondecreasing order. Which statement about recursive binary search RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,left,right,m)$ is correct?
  1. It also works if the array is not sorted
  2. It always returns the first occurrence of $m$
  3. It does not terminate if $m$ does not occur
  4. It finds $m$ if it occurs in $a[left..right]$, else signals absence
Explanation. Correctness requires a sorted array: every comparison with the middle element discards a half that cannot contain $m$. With duplicates it may find any occurrence.
alg-f-328 · level ipotesi · notes
Kadane's algorithm scans the array keeping $cur$ (maximum sum of a sub-array ending at the previous position) and $best$ (best sum seen so far). Which update makes the invariant on $cur$ true at position $i$?
  1. $cur:=cur+a[i]$ always
  2. $cur:=\max\{cur,a[i]\}$
  3. $cur:=\max\{cur+a[i],\,a[i]\}$
  4. $cur:=best+a[i]$
Explanation. A sub-array ending at $i$ is $a[i]$ alone or a sub-array ending at $i-1$ plus $a[i]$; the better of the two is the maximum. Then $best:=\max\{best,cur\}$.
alg-f-596 · level ipotesi · notes
SumIterative$(a,n)$ starts from $s:=0$ and also works with $n=0$. RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ (returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$, otherwise adds up the two halves) instead requires $l\le r$. What would happen calling it on an empty sub-array, with $l>r$?
  1. The base case $l=r$ is never reached
  2. It returns $0$, the empty sum
  3. It returns $a[l]$, as in the base case
  4. It returns $-1$, as search does
Explanation. With $l>r$ one gets $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ and one of the two calls again receives an invalid interval: the recursion never reaches the base case, which is a one-element sub-array.
alg-f-597 · level facile · notes
To add up $n\ge 1$ numbers with additions of two numbers at a time, SumIterative (starts from $s:=0$ and adds $a[1],\dots,a[n]$) and RecursiveSum (one addition $s_1+s_2$ per non-base call) perform how many additions?
  1. $n-1$ and $n-1$
  2. $n$ and $2n-1$
  3. $n$ and $n-1$
  4. $n$ and $\log_2 n$
Explanation. SumIterative makes one addition per element, including the first $0+a[1]$. RecursiveSum has $2n-1$ calls, $n$ of them leaves: the $n-1$ internal calls make one addition each.
alg-f-598 · level facile · notes
SumIterative$(a,n)$: $s:=0$, $i:=1$, while $i\le n$ it runs $s\leftarrow s+a[i]$ and $i\leftarrow i+1$, then returns $s$. Counting each executed line once (the while condition is evaluated $n+1$ times), how many steps does it perform with $n=10$?
  1. $30$
  2. $33$
  3. $44$
  4. $34$
Explanation. $1+1+(n+1)+n+n+1=3n+4=34$. $33$ forgets the last evaluation of the condition, the false one that exits the loop.
alg-f-599 · level facile · notes
SumIterative$(a,n)$ adds up $a[1..n]$ with a loop and only the variables $s$ and $i$. How much extra memory does it use, besides the array?
  1. $\Theta(n)$
  2. $\Theta(1)$
  3. $\Theta(\log n)$
  4. $\Theta(n^2)$
Explanation. Two variables, whatever $n$ is, and no recursive call. RecursiveSum instead uses $\Theta(\log n)$ frames on the call stack.
alg-f-600 · level facile · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$, otherwise with $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ returns RecursiveSum$(a,l,m)+$RecursiveSum$(a,m+1,r)$. Which two calls does RecursiveSum$(a,1,7)$ make?
  1. $(a,1,3)$ and $(a,4,7)$
  2. $(a,1,4)$ and $(a,5,7)$
  3. $(a,1,3)$ and $(a,5,7)$
  4. $(a,1,6)$ and $(a,7,7)$
Explanation. $m=\lfloor 8/2\rfloor=4$: the left half has $\lceil 7/2\rceil=4$ elements, the right one $3$. The two halves cover all of $a[1..7]$ without overlapping.
alg-f-601 · level teorema · notes
In RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$, with $k=r-l+1\ge 2$ elements, the two halves $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ ($m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$) have $\lceil k/2\rceil$ and $\lfloor k/2\rfloor$ elements. Why does this guarantee termination?
  1. Because $k$ is always a power of $2$
  2. Because the right half is always empty
  3. Because at most two calls are made in all
  4. Each half has between $1$ and $k-1$ elements
Explanation. For $k\ge 2$, $1\le\lfloor k/2\rfloor\le\lceil k/2\rceil\le k-1$: each call receives a non-empty, shorter sub-array, down to the base case $l=r$.
alg-f-602 · level facile · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ first calls the left half $(l,m)$ and then the right one $(m+1,r)$, with $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$. In which order do the calls of RecursiveSum$(a,1,4)$ start?
  1. $(1,4),(1,2),(3,4),(1,1),(2,2),(3,3),(4,4)$
  2. $(1,4),(1,2),(1,1),(2,2),(3,4),(3,3),(4,4)$
  3. $(1,1),(2,2),(1,2),(3,3),(4,4),(3,4),(1,4)$
  4. $(1,4),(3,4),(4,4),(3,3),(1,2),(2,2),(1,1)$
Explanation. Calls are visited depth-first: call $(1,2)$ finishes with its children before $(3,4)$ starts. The second order is level by level, the third is the order in which calls finish.
alg-f-603 · level facile · notes
In the call tree of RecursiveSum on $n$ elements, the leaves are the calls on a single element and every internal node has two children. For $n=6$, how many internal nodes are there?
  1. $6$
  2. $11$
  3. $3$
  4. $5$
Explanation. A tree in which every internal node has two children and there are $n$ leaves has $n-1$ internal nodes: $5$, and $11=2n-1$ nodes in all.
alg-f-604 · level facile · notes
In the inductive step of RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$'s correctness one knows, by the inductive hypothesis, that $s_1=a[l]+\dots+a[m]$ and $s_2=a[m+1]+\dots+a[r]$. Why is $s_1+s_2$ the required sum?
  1. The two halves are disjoint and together form $a[l..r]$
  2. Because $s_1=s_2$ when $m$ is the midpoint
  3. Because addition is commutative and associative
  4. Because $m$ is always an even index
Explanation. $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ share no elements and leave none out: every element of $a[l..r]$ is counted exactly once. The two sums differ in general.
alg-f-605 · level teorema · notes
RecursiveSum always splits in half, yet it performs $\Theta(n)$ steps, like SumIterative. Why?
  1. Because the tree has $n$ levels
  2. Because each call scans its whole sub-array
  3. It makes $2n-1$ calls of $\bigO(1)$ steps
  4. Because the recursion depth is $n$
Explanation. The calls are the nodes of the tree ($n$ leaves and $n-1$ internal nodes), and each runs a fixed number of lines. The depth is only $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1$.
alg-f-606 · level facile · notes
The recursion depth of RecursiveSum on $n$ elements (halving down to one element) is $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1$. What is the largest number of frames on the call stack with $n=1000$?
  1. $11$
  2. $10$
  3. $1000$
  4. $1999$
Explanation. $\lceil\log_2 1000\rceil=10$, plus the initial call: $11$. $1999=2n-1$ is the total number of calls, which however are never all active together.
alg-f-607 · level teorema · notes
RecursiveSumLinear$(a,n)$ returns RecursiveSumLinear$(a,n-1)+a[n]$ (base case $n=0$); RecursiveSum splits in half. What do they have in common and what distinguishes them?
  1. Both depth $\Theta(\log n)$; steps $\Theta(n)$ against $\Theta(n\log n)$
  2. Both depth $n+1$; steps $\Theta(n^2)$ against $\Theta(n)$
  3. Both $\Theta(n)$ steps; depth $n+1$ against $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1$
  4. Nothing: steps and depth both differ
Explanation. Both do constant work per call and make $\Theta(n)$ calls. The decrease-by-one version, however, keeps all $n+1$ calls active together, so it uses $\Theta(n)$ stack memory.
alg-f-608 · level facile · notes
Among the sorting algorithms of the course (Insertion, Exchange, Selection Sort, Merge Sort), which performs $\bigO(n\log n)$ comparisons in the worst case?
  1. Insertion Sort
  2. Selection Sort
  3. Exchange Sort
  4. Merge Sort
Explanation. Merge Sort performs $\Theta(n\log n)$ steps on every input. The other three reach $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ comparisons in the worst case (Exchange and Selection Sort on every input).
alg-f-609 · level facile · notes
A sort is comparison-based if it accesses the keys only by comparing them in pairs and copying them. Is Merge Sort comparison-based?
  1. No: Merge adds up the keys of the two halves
  2. Yes: Merge compares $L[i]\le R[j]$ and copies keys
  3. No: Merge Sort uses keys as indices
  4. Yes, but only in the base case
Explanation. The only operations on keys are the comparison $L[i]\le R[j]$ and the copies among $a$, $L$ and $R$; the divide step computes $m$ from the indices, not from the keys.
alg-f-610 · level facile · notes
Merge copies the two sorted halves into $L$ and $R$ and, while both have elements, moves into $a$ the smaller of $L[i]$ and $R[j]$ (comparison $L[i]\le R[j]$); then it copies the rest. How many comparisons does it make with $L=[2,5,9]$ and $R=[3,4,10]$?
  1. $5$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $2,3,4,5,9$ are copied with one comparison each; then $L$ is exhausted and $10$ is copied with no comparison. At most $n_1+n_2-1=5$ comparisons are needed.
alg-f-611 · level teorema · notes
In Merge, for equal keys, the comparison $L[i]\le R[j]$ copies first the element of $L$ (the left half). Which property does this choice give Merge Sort?
  1. Being in-place
  2. Exactly $n-1$ comparisons
  3. Stability
  4. No extra property
Explanation. The elements of $L$ precede those of $R$ in the input. With $L[i]<R[j]$ an equal record from $R$ would jump ahead, and the sort would no longer be stable.
alg-f-612 · level facile · notes
The number of steps of MergeSort satisfies $T(n)=2T(n/2)+\Theta(n)$, where $\Theta(n)$ is the cost of Merge. What is the solution?
  1. $\Theta(n\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $\Theta(\log n)$
Explanation. $n^{\log_2 2}=n$, of the same order as $f(n)$: case 2 of the Master theorem. Every level of the tree costs $\Theta(n)$ and there are $\log_2 n$ levels.
alg-f-613 · level teorema · notes
On an already sorted array Insertion Sort performs $\Theta(n)$ steps. How many does Merge Sort perform, in the best and in the worst case?
  1. $\Theta(n)$ in the best, $\Theta(n\log n)$ in the worst
  2. $\Theta(n\log n)$ in the best, $\Theta(n^2)$ in the worst
  3. $\Theta(n\log n)$ in both cases
  4. $\Theta(n)$ in both cases
Explanation. Merge Sort always divides and merges the same way: Merge copies all elements into $L$, $R$ and back into $a$ anyway, so every level costs $\Theta(n)$ even on a sorted input.
alg-f-614 · level facile · notes
Merge copies $a[l..m]$ into $L$ and $a[m+1..r]$ into $R$ before merging, instead of working directly in $a$. Why?
  1. Because $L$ and $R$ make Merge Sort in-place
  2. Writing into $a[k]$ would overwrite elements not yet merged
  3. To be able to sort $L$ and $R$ with Insertion Sort
  4. Because $a$ cannot be modified inside a function
Explanation. The positions of $a$ are filled from the left while the elements of the right half are still there: the copies protect them. The price is $\Theta(n)$ extra memory.
alg-f-615 · level facile · notes
Insertion Sort performs $\Theta(n^2)$ steps in the worst case and $\Theta(n)$ in the best, Merge Sort $\Theta(n\log n)$ always. On which kind of large input is Insertion Sort faster?
  1. On an array in decreasing order
  2. On a random array
  3. On no input
  4. On an already sorted array
Explanation. On the sorted array Insertion Sort makes one comparison per element: $\Theta(n)$ against $\Theta(n\log n)$. On the decreasing array it makes $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$ shifts.
alg-f-616 · level teorema · notes
Comparing Insertion Sort and Merge Sort with respect to memory and stability (in-place: constant extra memory), which statement is correct?
  1. Both in-place; only Merge Sort is stable
  2. Both stable; only Insertion Sort is in-place
  3. Only Merge Sort is stable and in-place
  4. Neither is stable
Explanation. Insertion Sort moves keys within the array with a few variables; Merge Sort uses the arrays $L$ and $R$ ($\Theta(n)$ memory). Both keep the order of equal keys.
alg-f-617 · level facile · notes
RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,l,r,m)$ returns $-1$ if $l>r$; otherwise, with $mid=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$, returns $mid$ if $a[mid]=m$, calls itself on $(l,mid-1)$ if $a[mid]>m$, on $(mid+1,r)$ otherwise. With $a=[1,3,5,7,9,11,13]$ and $m=11$, how many calls in all and what does RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,1,7,11)$ return?
  1. $3$ calls, returns $6$
  2. $2$ calls, returns $5$
  3. $1$ call, returns $6$
  4. $2$ calls, returns $6$
Explanation. $mid=4$, $a[4]=7<11$: call on $(5,7)$; $mid=6$, $a[6]=11$: it returns $6$.
alg-f-618 · level facile · notes
In RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,l,r,m)$ on a sorted array, if $a[mid]>m$ with $mid=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$, on which interval does it call itself?
  1. On $(mid+1,r)$
  2. On $(l,mid)$
  3. On $(l,mid-1)$
  4. On $(mid,r)$
Explanation. If $a[mid]>m$, all elements to the right of $mid$ are greater than $m$ too: one searches left, excluding $mid$, already examined (with $(l,mid)$ the interval might not shrink).
alg-f-619 · level facile · notes
RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,l,r,m)$ (returns $-1$ if $l>r$; $mid=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$; left if $a[mid]>m$, right if $a[mid]<m$) searches $m=5$ in $a=[2,4,6,8]$. How many calls in all, including the initial one?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $4$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $(1,4)$: $mid=2$, $4<5$; $(3,4)$: $mid=3$, $6>5$; $(3,2)$: empty interval, returns $-1$. The last call is the base case, and it counts.
alg-f-620 · level facile · notes
RecursiveBinarySearch$(a,l,r,m)$ returns $mid=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ as soon as $a[mid]=m$. What does it return on $a=[2,2,2,2,2]$ with $m=2$?
  1. $1$
  2. $5$
  3. $-1$
  4. $3$
Explanation. The very first call finds $a[3]=2$. Binary search returns a position of $m$, not necessarily the first: the problem definition accepts any index $k$ with $a[k]=m$.
alg-f-621 · level facile · notes
In the inductive correctness proof of RecursiveBinarySearch on a sorted array, why can $a[l..mid]$ be discarded when $a[mid]<m$?
  1. Because $a[l..mid]$ is always empty
  2. Because every $a[i]$ with $i\le mid$ is $\le a[mid]<m$
  3. Because $m$ never occurs twice
  4. Because the left half is shorter
Explanation. From the non-decreasing order, $a[i]\le a[mid]$ for $i\le mid$, so none of those elements equals $m$; by the inductive hypothesis the call on $(mid+1,r)$ is correct.
alg-f-622 · level facile · notes
Recursive binary search has recursion depth $\Theta(\log n)$, the iterative one uses a while loop with the variables $left$, $right$, $mid$. How much extra memory do they use (first the recursive, then the iterative)?
  1. $\Theta(1)$ and $\Theta(1)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(1)$
  3. $\Theta(\log n)$ and $\Theta(\log n)$
  4. $\Theta(\log n)$ and $\Theta(1)$
Explanation. Each active recursive call takes one frame. The recursive version is tail recursive, however, so it turns into the while loop with no loss.
alg-f-623 · level facile · notes
The price of a stock on days $0,\dots,4$ is $[10,11,7,10,6]$. One buys on some day and sells on a later day. What is the largest gain?
  1. $5$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Buy at $7$ (day $2$) and sell at $10$ (day $3$). $5=11-6$ would sell before buying. The daily changes are $[1,-4,3,-4]$, and the largest gain is the maximum sum of a sub-array: $3$.
alg-f-624 · level teorema · notes
To maximise the gain by buying a stock and selling it on a later day, why is it not enough to buy at the lowest price and sell at the highest?
  1. Because the highest price is never the last one
  2. Because minimum and maximum can only be found in $\Theta(n^2)$ time
  3. Because the day of the minimum may come after that of the maximum
  4. Because the gain does not depend on the prices
Explanation. With $[10,11,7,10,6]$ the maximum $11$ is on day $1$ and the minimum $6$ on the last. This is why one moves to daily changes and to the maximum-sum sub-array.
alg-f-625 · level facile · notes
The maximum-subarray problem asks, given $a[1..n]$, for a non-empty sub-array $a[i..j]$ of maximum sum. What is the maximum sum for $a=[-2,1,-3,4,-1,2,1,-5,4]$?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $7$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $a[4..7]=[4,-1,2,1]$ has sum $6$. $7=4+2+1$ skips the $-1$, but a sub-array is made of consecutive elements; $12$ adds all the positives.
alg-f-626 · level facile · notes
In the maximum-subarray problem (non-empty sub-array of consecutive elements), what is the solution if all elements are negative, for instance $a=[-5,-2,-8]$?
  1. The empty sub-array, with sum $0$
  2. The whole array, $[-5,-2,-8]$
  3. The single largest element, $[-2]$
  4. The problem has no solution
Explanation. Adding a negative element lowers the sum, so a single element, the least negative, is best. The empty sub-array is not allowed by the definition.
alg-f-627 · level facile · notes
The brute-force algorithm for the maximum sub-array examines all index pairs $(i,j)$ with $1\le i\le j\le n$. How many pairs does it examine with $n=10$?
  1. $55$
  2. $45$
  3. $100$
  4. $10$
Explanation. The pairs with $i\le j$ are $\frac{n(n+1)}{2}=55$: the $45$ with $i<j$ plus the $10$ with $i=j$ (one-element sub-arrays).
alg-f-628 · level media · notes
Brute force for the maximum sub-array, for each $i$, updates the sum of $a[i..j]$ by adding $a[j]$ as $j$ grows, and performs $\Theta(n^2)$ steps. How many would it perform recomputing every sum from scratch?
  1. $\Theta(n^2)$
  2. $\Theta(n^3)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2\log n)$
  4. $\Theta(2^n)$
Explanation. There are $\Theta(n^2)$ pairs and adding up $a[i..j]$ from scratch costs $j-i+1$ steps: $\sum_{i\le j}(j-i+1)=\Theta(n^3)$ in all. The incremental update removes a factor $n$.
alg-f-629 · level facile · notes
FindMaximumSubarray$(a,l,r)$ splits $a[l..r]$ at the midpoint $mid$ and calls itself on the two halves. Which sub-arrays are NOT considered by the two recursive calls?
  1. Those made of a single element
  2. Those ending at the last element $a[r]$
  3. Those that have negative sum
  4. Those containing $a[mid]$ and $a[mid+1]$
Explanation. The calls see only sub-arrays entirely in $a[l..mid]$ or entirely in $a[mid+1..r]$. Those crossing the midpoint are found by FindMaxCrossingSubarray, in linear time.
alg-f-630 · level teorema · notes
FindMaximumSubarray returns the best of three candidates: the maximum in the left half, the one in the right half and the one crossing the midpoint. Why are the three candidates enough?
  1. Because the maximum sum is always in the left half
  2. Because crossing sub-arrays have zero sum
  3. Every sub-array lies left, right, or across the middle
  4. Because every sub-array has at most three elements
Explanation. A sub-array $a[i..j]$ with $i\le mid<j$ crosses the midpoint; otherwise $j\le mid$ or $i>mid$. By the inductive hypothesis the calls are correct, and the Lemma gives the best crossing one.
alg-f-631 · level facile · notes
FindMaximumSubarray makes two calls on the halves and one linear scan for the sub-array crossing the midpoint, so $T(n)=2T(n/2)+\Theta(n)$. What is its worst-case running time?
  1. $\Theta(n\log n)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $\Theta(\log n)$
Explanation. It is Merge Sort's recurrence: case 2 of the Master theorem. Better than brute force ($\Theta(n^2)$), worse than Kadane's algorithm ($\Theta(n)$).
alg-f-632 · level facile · notes
FindMaxCrossingSubarray$(a,l,mid,r)$ looks for the maximum-sum sub-array containing $a[mid]$ and $a[mid+1]$: it scans from $mid$ towards $l$ for the best left sum, and from $mid+1$ towards $r$ for the best right sum. How much does it cost, with $n=r-l+1$?
  1. $\Theta(n^2)$: all pairs are tried
  2. $\Theta(\log n)$: the interval is halved
  3. $\Theta(n)$: each element of $a[l..r]$ is examined once
  4. $\Theta(1)$: only $a[mid]$ and $a[mid+1]$ are looked at
Explanation. The two parts are independent: the best left part (ending at $mid$) and the best right part (starting at $mid+1$) are added. Two scans, of $mid-l+1$ and $r-mid$ steps.
alg-f-633 · level media · notes
Kadane's algorithm scans $a$ keeping $cur$, the maximum sum of a sub-array ending at the current position ($cur\leftarrow\max(a[i],\,cur+a[i])$), and $best$, the maximum of the values of $cur$. What does it return on $a=[2,-5,3,1,-2,4]$?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $10$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $cur$ takes $2,-3,3,4,2,6$: at $i=3$ it is better to restart from $3$ than $-3+3=0$. $best=6$, given by $[3,1,-2,4]$; $10$ adds only the positives.
alg-f-661 · level ipotesi · notes
RecursiveBinarySearch is correct on sorted arrays. What can be said if it is run on an UNSORTED array?
  1. It always returns $-1$, for every value of $m$
  2. Indices are right, but $-1$ may be wrong
  3. The recursion never ends, because $mid$ repeats
  4. It always returns a right index, or a right $-1$
Explanation. $mid$ is returned only after seeing $a[mid]=m$, so an index is always right; but discarding a half may remove $m$ (on $[5,1,4,2,3]$ with $m=1$ one gets $-1$). The interval shrinks anyway, so it terminates.
alg-f-684 · level ipotesi · notes
Kadane's algorithm looks for a NON-empty maximum-sum sub-array, with $best$ initialised to $a[1]$. A variant initialises $best:=0$ (as if the empty sub-array were allowed). What does it return on $a=[-5,-2,-8]$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-2$
  3. $-15$
  4. $-5$
Explanation. No sum exceeds $0$, so $best$ stays $0$, which is the sum of no non-empty sub-array ($-2$ is Kadane's answer). The variant solves a different problem (possibly empty sub-array): it differs only when all elements are negative.

18. Recursive versus iterative algorithms (44)

alg-f-144 · level facile · notes
Let $S(0)=d$ and $S(n)=S(n-1)+c$ for $n\ge 1$, with $c>0$ a constant. What is the order of growth of $S(n)$?
  1. $\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  2. $\Theta(n)$
  3. $\Theta(n^2)$
  4. $\Theta(2^n)$
Explanation. By Observation 18.1 $S(n)=d+\sum_{i=1}^n c=c\,n+d=\Theta(n)$: a decrease-by-one recursion costs as much as the corresponding loop.
alg-f-145 · level facile · notes
MergeSortBottomUp starts from $width=1$ and, while $width<n$, merges the adjacent blocks of $width$ elements (one pass), then doubles $width$. How many passes does it execute on an array of $n=10$ elements?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $10$
  4. $4$
Explanation. $width$ takes the values $1,2,4,8$, i.e., $\lceil\log_2 10\rceil=4$ passes; each costs $\bigO(n)$, for a total $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ without the call stack.
alg-f-146 · level facile · notes
PowerIt$(x,n)$ computes $x^n$ with $p:=1$, $y:=x$, $k:=n$ and, while $k>0$: if $k$ is odd $p\leftarrow p\cdot y$; then $y\leftarrow y\cdot y$ and $k\leftarrow\lfloor k/2\rfloor$. How many iterations does PowerIt$(x,100)$ execute?
  1. $6$
  2. $7$
  3. $8$
  4. $100$
Explanation. Each iteration replaces $k$ with $\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, i.e., removes one binary digit: the iterations are the binary digits of $100$, $\lfloor\log_2 100\rfloor+1=7$.
alg-f-147 · level facile · notes
For $F(n)$: FibRec uses the recursion $F(n)=F(n-1)+F(n-2)$; FibMemo is the same recursion with a table $memo[0..n]$ of the values already computed; FibIt is a loop keeping only the last two values. Which statement is correct?
  1. FibMemo and FibIt both use $\Theta(1)$ extra memory.
  2. FibRec, FibMemo and FibIt all execute $\Theta(n)$ steps.
  3. FibMemo executes an exponential number of calls, like FibRec.
  4. FibMemo executes $\Theta(n)$ steps with $\Theta(n)$ memory; FibIt executes $\Theta(n)$ steps with $\Theta(1)$ extra memory.
Explanation. Memoization computes each value once ($2n-1$ calls) but uses the table and the stack; FibIt keeps only $prev$ and $curr$. FibRec executes $\Theta(\varphi^n)$ steps.
alg-f-148 · level facile · notes
On an input of size $n$, the tree of calls of a recursive algorithm has one node per call and one child for each call made by that node. What is the recursion depth $D(n)$?
  1. The total number of calls executed, i.e., the number of nodes of the tree.
  2. The number of leaves of the tree of calls.
  3. The maximum number of calls active at the same time, i.e., the height of the tree of calls plus one.
  4. The number of recursive calls made directly by the first call.
Explanation. Definition 18.7: the stack memory is $\Theta(D(n))$ if the stack frames have constant size, while the number of steps depends on the number of nodes.
alg-f-149 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ be tail recursive, with calls of constant cost and frames of constant size, with recursion depth $D(n)$, and let $g$ be the iterative algorithm obtained by replacing the final call with a loop on the new parameters. Which statement is correct?
  1. $g$ gives the same result with steps of the same order, but uses $\Theta(1)$ memory instead of $\Theta(D(n))$.
  2. $g$ executes asymptotically fewer steps than $f$, for instance $\Theta(\log n)$ instead of $\Theta(n)$.
  3. $g$ uses the same memory as $f$, since the loop keeps all parameter values, but executes fewer steps.
  4. $g$ is obtained this way from every recursive algorithm, also with two calls such as Fib, with the same gain.
Explanation. The loop goes through the same parameter values as the successive calls: the gain is only in the stack memory. Fib is not tail recursive.
alg-f-150 · level teorema · notes
We compare recursive and iterative algorithms for the same problem (sum of an array, sorting, power), with calls of constant cost. Which statement is correct?
  1. A recursive algorithm is always asymptotically faster than its iterative version, thanks to the stack.
  2. The gain comes from dividing the problem, not from recursion: decrease by one and the loop have the same order.
  3. A recursive algorithm is always asymptotically slower than its iterative version, because of the cost of the calls.
  4. Every divide and conquer algorithm executes $\Theta(n\log n)$ steps, whatever the cost of dividing and merging.
Explanation. RecursiveSumLinear and the loop both execute $\Theta(n)$ steps; Merge Sort beats Insertion Sort because it divides the problem, and MergeSortBottomUp gets the same $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ without recursion.
alg-f-151 · level ipotesi · notes
FibMemo$(k,memo)$ returns $k$ if $k\le 1$; otherwise, if $memo[k]$ is undefined, it sets $memo[k]\leftarrow$FibMemo$(k-1)+$FibMemo$(k-2)$; it returns $memo[k]$. How do we prove that, with $memo$ all undefined and $n\ge 2$, it executes $2n-1$ calls?
  1. $M(k)=M(k-1)+M(k-2)+1$, as for the recursion without table, and then $M(n)=2n-1$ by induction.
  2. With the Master Theorem applied to $M(n)=2M(n/2)+1$, which gives $\Theta(n)$ calls.
  3. $M(k)=M(k-1)+1$: the table avoids the second call entirely, except in the two base cases.
  4. $M(2)=3$ and $M(k)=M(k-1)+2$: the second call finds $memo[k-2]$ already defined or is a base case.
Explanation. The first call fills $memo[2..k-1]$, the second returns immediately: hence $M(k)=M(k-1)+2$ and $M(n)=3+2(n-2)=2n-1$. $C(n)=C(n-1)+C(n-2)+1$ counts the calls of FibRec.
alg-f-262 · level facile · notes
At each function call the computer creates a record (stack frame) on the call stack. What does it contain, and when is it removed?
  1. Only the returned value; it is removed as soon as the next recursive call starts.
  2. The code of the called function; it is removed at the end of the whole program.
  3. Parameters and local variables; it is removed when the call makes another one.
  4. Parameters, local variables and return address; it is removed when the call terminates.
Explanation. All the calls that have started and not yet terminated keep their record on the stack: this is why the memory used is proportional to the depth of the recursion.
alg-f-263 · level media · notes
SumDecrease$(a,n)$ returns SumDecrease$(a,n-1)+a[n]$ (with SumDecrease$(a,0)=0$), so its depth of recursion is $n+1$. With records of $32$ bytes and a stack of $8$ MB $=8\cdot 2^{20}$ bytes, what is approximately the largest $n$ that does not cause a stack overflow?
  1. About $8\cdot 10^6$
  2. About $2.6\cdot 10^5$
  3. About $2.6\cdot 10^8$
  4. About $23$
Explanation. $8\cdot 2^{20}/32=262\,144$ records: beyond that the stack overflows, although the array would easily fit in memory. The halving version has depth $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ and does not have this problem.
alg-f-264 · level facile · notes
The best iterative algorithm of the course is compared with the recursive one for the same problem. For which problem is the recursive version asymptotically faster?
  1. The sum of an array: halving recursion performs $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ steps against $\Theta(n)$
  2. Binary search: the recursive version performs $\Theta(1)$ steps against $\Theta(\log_2 n)$
  3. Sorting: MergeSort $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ against $\Theta(n^2)$ of Insertion, Selection and Exchange Sort
  4. The Fibonacci numbers: recursion performs $\Theta(\log_2 n)$ steps against $\Theta(n)$ of the loop
Explanation. Only for sorting does divide and conquer give a better order (paid with $\Theta(n)$ memory). The sum stays $\Theta(n)$, binary search $\Theta(\log_2 n)$, and recursive Fib is exponential.
alg-f-265 · level facile · notes
For the maximum subarray problem one compares brute force (all pairs $l\le r$), the divide and conquer FindMaximumSubarray and Kadane's iterative algorithm (one scan). Which conclusion is correct?
  1. Divide and conquer ($\Theta(n\log_2 n)$) beats brute force ($\Theta(n^2)$), but Kadane ($\Theta(n)$ time, $\Theta(1)$ memory) beats both.
  2. Divide and conquer ($\Theta(n\log_2 n)$) is the best of the three, because recursion is always faster than a loop on the same problem.
  3. Brute force and divide and conquer have the same order $\Theta(n^2)$; only Kadane, with $\Theta(n)$ time, really improves the situation.
  4. Kadane is $\Theta(n\log_2 n)$ like divide and conquer, but it uses less memory, because it has no stack of recursive calls.
Explanation. A recursive algorithm can improve on brute force and still be beaten by a smarter loop: it must always be compared with the best iterative algorithm.
alg-f-266 · level media · notes
In an experiment random arrays of $n=10^4$ and then of $n=10^5$ elements are sorted with InsertionSort ($\Theta(n^2)$) and MergeSort ($\Theta(n\log_2 n)$). By which factors are the two running times multiplied, according to the theory?
  1. About $10$ and about $10$
  2. About $100$ and about $100$
  3. About $100$ and about $12.5$
  4. About $100$ and about $10$
Explanation. $(10^5/10^4)^2=100$; for MergeSort $10\cdot\frac{\log_2 10^5}{\log_2 10^4}=10\cdot\frac54=12.5$. In the experiment of the notes the measured factors are about $98$ and $12$.
alg-f-267 · level media · notes
The running time of the naive recursive function for the Fibonacci numbers is proportional to the number of calls, which grows as $\varphi^n$ with $\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}\approx 1.618$. By which factor is the time approximately multiplied when $n$ increases by $5$?
  1. About $11$
  2. About $5$
  3. About $32$
  4. About $25$
Explanation. $\varphi^5\approx 11.09$. $2^5=32$ would use base $2$ instead of $\varphi$; $5$ and $25$ would correspond to linear or quadratic growth. In a semi-logarithmic plot the curve is a straight line.
alg-f-268 · level facile · notes
According to the guidelines on the use of recursion, in which situation should it be avoided?
  1. When the problem splits into a few independent subproblems of size $n/b$, as for MergeSort.
  2. When the problem decreases from $n$ to $n-1$ with constant work, as for the factorial or linear search.
  3. When the depth of the recursion is small, typically $\Theta(\log_2 n)$, as for FastPower.
  4. When the recursive description is the natural one and no gain is possible, as for Hanoi.
Explanation. Decrease by one is a loop in disguise: same time, call overhead, $\Theta(n)$ stack memory and risk of stack overflow. The other three are the cases in which the guidelines recommend recursion.
alg-f-269 · level facile · notes
A naive recursive algorithm solves the same subproblems many times (overlapping subproblems, as for the Fibonacci numbers). What do the guidelines recommend?
  1. Rewriting the algorithm in tail-recursive form, which removes the repeated calls.
  2. Increasing the size of the call stack, to avoid the stack overflow.
  3. Applying the Master Theorem to choose the optimal size of the subproblems.
  4. Memoization or, better, a bottom-up loop (Dynamic Programming).
Explanation. With a table of the values already computed, or by computing them bottom-up, every subproblem is solved only once: for Fibonacci one goes from an exponential number of steps to $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-329 · level facile · notes
FibRec$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n \le 1$, otherwise FibRec$(n-1)+$FibRec$(n-2)$. The number of calls is $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1$, with $F$ the Fibonacci numbers ($F(0)=0$, $F(1)=1$). How many calls does FibRec$(5)$ make?
  1. $15$
  2. $8$
  3. $9$
  4. $31$
Explanation. $F(6)=8$, so $C(5)=2 \cdot 8-1=15$. $8$ is $F(6)$ without the factor $2$; $31=2^5-1$ would be a complete tree.
alg-f-330 · level teorema · notes
Let $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2 \approx 1.618$. Which statement about the growth of the Fibonacci numbers and the cost of FibRec is correct?
  1. $F(n)=\Theta(n^2)$, so FibRec is quadratic
  2. $\varphi^{n-2} \le F(n) \le \varphi^{n-1}$, so FibRec takes $\Theta(\varphi^n)$ steps: exponential time
  3. $F(n)=\Theta(2^n)$ exactly
  4. FibRec is linear since it makes one addition per call
Explanation. The number of calls is $2F(n+1)-1=\Theta(\varphi^n)$, and every call costs a constant. The iterative version computes $F(n)$ in $\Theta(n)$ steps.
alg-f-331 · level facile · notes
A recursive call is a tail call if it is the last operation of the function and its result is returned as it is. Which function is tail recursive?
  1. Factorial$(n)$: it returns $n \cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$
  2. Fib$(n)$: it returns Fib$(n-1)+$Fib$(n-2)$
  3. RecursiveSum: it returns the sum of the two calls on the halves
  4. RecursiveGCD$(n,m)$: if $m=0$ it returns $n$, otherwise it returns RecursiveGCD$(m,n \bmod m)$
Explanation. In GCD the result of the call is returned with no further operation; in Factorial the multiplication by $n$ remains, in Fib and RecursiveSum the addition.
alg-f-634 · level media · notes
SumDecrease$(a,n)$ returns SumDecrease$(a,n-1)+a[n]$ (base case $n=0$), so it keeps $n+1$ calls active together. With $n=10^7$ and about $32$ bytes per stack frame, how much stack memory would be needed?
  1. About $32$ bytes
  2. About $40$ MB
  3. About $8$ MB
  4. About $320$ MB
Explanation. $10^7\cdot 32$ bytes $=3.2\cdot 10^8$ bytes: far more than the typical $8$ MB stack, so the program crashes (stack overflow). $40$ MB is the array itself, which fits easily in memory.
alg-f-635 · level facile · notes
At each call the computer creates a stack frame (parameters, local variables, return address), removed only when the call ends. Which is NOT a hidden cost of recursion compared with a loop?
  1. The time to create and remove frames
  2. An asymptotically larger number of steps
  3. The memory taken by the active frames
  4. The risk of stack overflow
Explanation. For the same algorithm, recursion adds per-call costs (time), frames in memory and the stack limit; it does not change the order of growth of the steps, as SumDecrease and the loop show, both $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-636 · level facile · notes
The halving sum can be written without recursion using an explicit stack $P$ of pairs $(l,r)$: the top pair is popped and, if $l<r$, $(mid+1,r)$ and then $(l,mid)$ are pushed. With $a=[4,7,1,9]$, after popping $(1,4)$ and pushing $(3,4)$ and $(1,2)$, which pair is popped next?
  1. $(3,4)$
  2. $(1,1)$
  3. $(1,4)$
  4. $(1,2)$
Explanation. The stack returns first the last pair pushed: $(1,2)$. So the order of work is the same as in the recursion, which calls the left half first.
alg-f-637 · level teorema · notes
Every recursive algorithm can be turned into an iterative one by managing an explicit stack of the parameters of the calls still to run. For the halving sum on $n$ elements, how large does the explicit stack get at most?
  1. $\Theta(1)$
  2. $\Theta(\log n)$
  3. $\Theta(n)$
  4. $\Theta(n\log n)$
Explanation. The explicit stack does the job of the call stack: it holds the pending sub-arrays, at most a couple per level. One saves the cost of calls, not the memory.
alg-f-638 · level teorema · notes
SumDecrease$(a,n)$ (base case $n=0$; otherwise SumDecrease$(a,n-1)+a[n]$) has $S(n)=S(n-1)+c$ steps, like the loop that adds up $a[1..n]$. What advantage does the recursive version have?
  1. None: if anything it costs more memory
  2. It performs $\Theta(\log n)$ steps instead of $\Theta(n)$
  3. It uses less memory, because it has no variable $i$
  4. It can run in parallel on the two halves
Explanation. Both make $\Theta(n)$ steps, but the recursion uses $\Theta(n)$ frames (against $\Theta(1)$ variables) and pays for each call. Parallelism concerns splitting in half, not decreasing by one.
alg-f-639 · level teorema · notes
MergeSortBottomUp merges blocks of $1,2,4,\dots$ elements without any recursive call, and also performs $\Theta(n\log n)$ steps. What does it teach about the gain of Merge Sort over Insertion Sort?
  1. It comes from recursive calls, which are faster than loops
  2. It comes from Merge Sort being in-place
  3. It comes from halving and merging in linear time, not from recursion
  4. There is no gain: the two algorithms have the same order
Explanation. The iterative version has the same order of growth: what counts is the structure of $\log_2 n$ levels of linear merges. Recursion is just a convenient way to write it.
alg-f-640 · level facile · notes
The iterative fast power PowerIt$(x,n)$ halves the residual exponent $k$ at each iteration ($k\leftarrow\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, starting from $k=n$, while $k>0$), multiplying $p$ by $y$ when $k$ is odd and then squaring $y$. For $n=13=(1101)_2$, how many times is the multiplication $p\leftarrow p\cdot y$ executed?
  1. $4$
  2. $3$
  3. $13$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $k$ takes $13,6,3,1$: it is odd for $13$, $3$ and $1$, that is once for each digit $1$ of the binary representation of $n$. There are $4$ iterations, one per digit.
alg-f-641 · level facile · notes
For the maximum sub-array, the divide-and-conquer FindMaximumSubarray performs $\Theta(n\log n)$ steps with recursion depth $\Theta(\log n)$; Kadane's iterative algorithm performs $\Theta(n)$ steps with two variables. Which statement is correct?
  1. Divide and conquer is better in time
  2. They are equivalent: both $\Theta(n\log n)$
  3. Kadane is faster but uses $\Theta(n)$ memory
  4. Kadane is better both in time and in memory
Explanation. $\Theta(n)$ steps and $\Theta(1)$ memory against $\Theta(n\log n)$ steps and $\Theta(\log n)$ frames. Here a different idea (extending the best sum ending at $i$) beats halving.
alg-f-642 · level media · notes
On an array of $n=2^{20}\approx 10^6$ elements, FindMaximumSubarray performs about $n\log_2 n$ steps and Kadane about $n$ (ignoring constants). By about what factor is divide and conquer slower?
  1. About $10^6$
  2. About $20$
  3. About $2$
  4. About $1000$
Explanation. $\frac{n\log_2 n}{n}=\log_2 n=20$. The gap between $\Theta(n\log n)$ and $\Theta(n)$ grows only like $\log n$, much more slowly than that between $\Theta(n^2)$ and $\Theta(n)$ ($10^6$).
alg-f-643 · level teorema · notes
To multiply two $n$-digit integers one splits them into halves of $n/2$ digits. With four products of halves the recurrence is $S(n)=4S(n/2)+\Theta(n)$; Karatsuba uses three, $S(n)=3S(n/2)+\Theta(n)$. What are the solutions?
  1. $\Theta(n\log n)$ in both cases
  2. $\Theta(n^2)$ in both cases
  3. $\Theta(n^4)$ and $\Theta(n^3)$
  4. $\Theta(n^2)$ and $\Theta(n^{\log_2 3})$
Explanation. Case 1 of the Master theorem: $n^{\log_2 4}=n^2$ and $n^{\log_2 3}\approx n^{1.585}$ dominate $f(n)=\Theta(n)$. With four products nothing is gained over the schoolbook method; saving one lowers the exponent.
alg-f-644 · level media · notes
For numbers of $n=1024=2^{10}$ digits, one compares $n^2$ (schoolbook method) and $n^{\log_2 3}=3^{10}$ (Karatsuba), ignoring constants. By about what factor is Karatsuba faster?
  1. About $18$
  2. About $1.5$
  3. About $1000$
  4. About $3$
Explanation. $n^2=2^{20}\approx 1.05\cdot 10^6$ and $3^{10}=59049$: ratio $(4/3)^{10}\approx 17.8$. The advantage grows with $n$, like $n^{2-\log_2 3}\approx n^{0.415}$.
alg-f-645 · level facile · notes
FibMemo$(k,memo)$ computes Fibonacci numbers with the same recursion as FibRec, but uses a table $memo$. What does $memo[k]$ contain, and when is it used?
  1. The number of calls made with argument $k$
  2. The return address of the call on $k$
  3. The value $F(k)$, stored the first time and then read back
  4. The value $F(k-1)+F(k-2)$, recomputed at every call
Explanation. The first call on $k$ computes $F(k)$ and stores it; later ones read it with no new calls. So each subproblem is solved only once, and the calls become $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-646 · level facile · notes
To compute $F(n)$, FibMemo uses a table $memo[0..n]$ and a recursion of depth $\Theta(n)$; the iterative algorithm keeps only the last two values. How much extra memory do they use?
  1. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(1)$
  2. $\Theta(1)$ and $\Theta(1)$
  3. $\Theta(n)$ and $\Theta(n)$
  4. $\Theta(2^n)$ and $\Theta(n)$
Explanation. FibMemo's table and call stack are both $\Theta(n)$; the loop uses a fixed number of variables. The steps are $\Theta(n)$ in both cases.
alg-f-647 · level facile · notes
The recursion depth is the largest number of calls active together. FibRec$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n\le 1$, otherwise FibRec$(n-1)+$FibRec$(n-2)$. What is its depth, for $n\ge 1$?
  1. $2^n$
  2. $\log_2 n$
  3. $n$
  4. $F(n)$
Explanation. The longest path in the tree is FibRec$(n)\to(n-1)\to\dots\to(1)$: $n$ calls. The calls in all are exponentially many, but the stack memory is only $\Theta(n)$.
alg-f-648 · level facile · notes
In the chapter's experiment each time measurement is repeated until at least $0.2$ seconds have passed (and at least $3$ repetitions), reporting the average time. Why?
  1. To warm up the processor before measuring
  2. Because every algorithm must last $0.2$ seconds
  3. To limit the memory used by the stack
  4. To measure accurately even very short computations
Explanation. A computation of a few microseconds is shorter than the clock resolution: repeating it many times and dividing makes the measurement error negligible.
alg-f-649 · level facile · notes
In the experiment the random arrays are generated with a fixed seed ($12345$). What is it for?
  1. To make the arrays already sorted
  2. To make the experiment repeatable, with the same data
  3. To choose the worst case of each algorithm
  4. To reduce the time needed to generate the arrays
Explanation. With the same seed the generator always produces the same sequence: whoever repeats the experiment, and every algorithm compared, works on the same arrays.
alg-f-650 · level teorema · notes
In the sorting experiment the recursive mergeSort and mergeSortBottomUp (iterative) have very similar times, while insertionSort is much slower for large $n$. What is the conclusion?
  1. The order of growth matters, not recursion
  2. Recursion makes Merge Sort faster
  3. Insertion Sort is slow because it is iterative
  4. The times do not depend on the algorithm used
Explanation. $\Theta(n\log n)$ against $\Theta(n^2)$ makes the difference; between the two versions of Merge Sort, with the same order, the call overhead weighs little.
alg-f-651 · level media · notes
Ignoring constants, for $n=10^5$ Insertion Sort performs about $n^2$ steps and Merge Sort about $n\log_2 n$ (with $\log_2 10^5\approx 17$). By about what factor is Merge Sort faster?
  1. About $17$
  2. About $10^5$
  3. About $6000$
  4. About $10$
Explanation. $\frac{n^2}{n\log_2 n}=\frac{n}{\log_2 n}\approx\frac{10^5}{17}\approx 5900$. The ratio grows almost linearly with $n$.
alg-f-652 · level facile · notes

  2. $10946$
  3. $20$
  4. $39$
Explanation. $2\cdot 10946-1=21891$: already for $n=20$ tens of thousands of calls, against the $20$ iterations of fibonacciIt. $39=2n-1$ would be with memoisation.
alg-f-653 · level facile · notes
In the experiment fibonacciIt is measured by repeating it in blocks of $10^5$ calls, fibonacciRec is not. Why?
  1. Because fibonacciIt gives different results at each call
  2. Because fibonacciIt uses a lot of memory
  3. One call of fibonacciIt is too short to be measured alone
  4. Because fibonacciRec is faster
Explanation. fibonacciIt performs $\Theta(n)$ steps, a few dozen for $n\le 40$: nanoseconds. fibonacciRec for $n=40$ makes hundreds of millions of calls and takes seconds.
alg-f-654 · level facile · notes
With the default $8$ MB stack, sumRecDecrease (decrease by one) crashes for $n=10^6$, while sumRecHalving (halving) never crashes up to $n=10^7$. Why?
  1. sumRecHalving performs fewer steps
  2. sumRecHalving has at most $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil+1\le 25$ active frames
  3. sumRecHalving does not use the call stack
  4. sumRecDecrease has a mistake in its base case
Explanation. Both versions make $\Theta(n)$ steps, but the depth is $n+1$ against $\Theta(\log n)$: a million frames do not fit in $8$ MB, $25$ frames do.
alg-f-655 · level teorema · notes
In the sum experiment, sumIt, sumRecHalving and sumRecDecrease have the same order $\Theta(n)$: multiplying $n$ by $10$, times grow by about $10$. The recursive versions, however, are slower by a factor $3$–$13$. How is this interpreted?
  1. The recursive versions have a larger order of growth
  2. It is a measurement error, to be ignored
  3. The recursive versions are slower only for small $n$
  4. It is the constant cost of calls, which does not change the order
Explanation. A constant factor independent of $n$ does not change the class $\Theta(n)$, but it matters in practice: this is why a loop is preferred when recursion brings no advantage.
alg-f-656 · level facile · notes
According to the chapter's guidelines, in which situation is recursion the natural choice?
  1. A problem reducing to $n-1$ with very large $n$
  2. Divide and conquer with subproblems of size $n/b$
  3. Repeated subproblems, without memoisation
  4. When constant extra memory is required
Explanation. With fractional-size subproblems the depth is $\Theta(\log n)$ and recursive code follows the structure of the problem (Merge Sort, Karatsuba). In the other cases a loop or memoisation is preferable.
alg-f-657 · level facile · notes
An algorithm is tail recursive, but the language used does not eliminate tail calls, and the recursion depth can reach $10^6$. What should be done?
  1. Add a second base case
  2. Split the recursion into two calls on half the input
  3. Nothing: tail recursion does not use the stack
  4. Rewrite it as a while loop that updates the parameters
Explanation. Without automatic elimination each call still takes a frame, and a million frames risk a stack overflow. The transformation into a loop is immediate precisely because the call is in tail position.
alg-f-683 · level ipotesi · notes
A tail-recursive algorithm turns into a loop with $\Theta(1)$ memory. Does this also hold for Factorial$(n)=n\cdot$Factorial$(n-1)$ as written?
  1. Yes: the call is the last line of the function
  2. Yes, because it has a single base case
  3. No, because the result is not an integer
  4. No: after the call the multiplication by $n$ remains
Explanation. Tail position means that the result of the call is returned unchanged. Here it still has to be multiplied, so the caller's frame stays on the stack; with an accumulator, Factorial$(n,acc)$ becomes tail recursive.
