Each generator builds a new instance, with different numbers, at every start: a few examples here.

1. Representation of numbers (6)

Example 1 · alg-g-bin2dec · level facile · notes
Which natural number, in base ten, is represented by the sequence of digits $(1100100)_2$?
  1. $200$
  2. $19$
  3. $100$
  4. $116$
Explanation. The digit in position $i$ (counted from the right, starting at $0$) contributes $d_i\,2^i$: $2^{6}+2^{5}+2^{2}=100$. Reading the digits in reverse order or counting the positions from $1$ (twice the value) are the typical mistakes.
Example 2 · alg-g-bin2dec · level facile · notes
Which natural number, in base ten, is represented by the sequence of digits $(1110101)_2$?
  1. $113$
  2. $234$
  3. $87$
  4. $117$
Explanation. The digit in position $i$ (counted from the right, starting at $0$) contributes $d_i\,2^i$: $2^{6}+2^{5}+2^{4}+2^{2}+2^{0}=117$. Reading the digits in reverse order or counting the positions from $1$ (twice the value) are the typical mistakes.
Example 1 · alg-g-twos · level facile · notes
The sequence of $8$ bits $(11100100)_2$ is the two's complement representation of an integer. Which one?
  1. $-100$
  2. $228$
  3. $-28$
  4. $-27$
Explanation. The most significant bit is $1$: the integer is negative and equals $r-2^k=228-256=-28$, where $r=228$ is the natural value of the sequence.
Example 2 · alg-g-twos · level facile · notes
The sequence of $6$ bits $(101001)_2$ is the two's complement representation of an integer. Which one?
  1. $-22$
  2. $-9$
  3. $41$
  4. $-23$
Explanation. The most significant bit is $1$: the integer is negative and equals $r-2^k=41-64=-23$, where $r=41$ is the natural value of the sequence.
Example 1 · alg-g-minbits · level facile · notes
What is the minimum number of bits needed to represent the integer $-172$ in two's complement?
  1. $8$
  2. $9$
  3. $10$
  4. $11$
Explanation. We need the smallest $k$ with $-2^{k-1}\le -172\le 2^{k-1}-1$: $k=9$ ($\lceil\log_2|n|\rceil+1$). The value $\lfloor\log_2|n|\rfloor+1$ forgets the sign bit.
Example 2 · alg-g-minbits · level facile · notes
What is the minimum number of bits needed to represent the integer $-116$ in two's complement?
  1. $10$
  2. $9$
  3. $8$
  4. $7$
Explanation. We need the smallest $k$ with $-2^{k-1}\le -116\le 2^{k-1}-1$: $k=8$ ($\lceil\log_2|n|\rceil+1$). The value $\lfloor\log_2|n|\rfloor+1$ forgets the sign bit.
Example 1 · alg-g-twos-add · level media · notes
With $8$ bits in two's complement, $(10100001)_2$ and $(01001010)_2$ are added as natural numbers, keeping the $8$ least significant bits. Which integer does the result represent, and is there an overflow?
  1. result $-21$, no overflow
  2. result $21$, no overflow
  3. result $-21$, overflow
  4. result $235$, no overflow
Explanation. The operands are $-95$ and $74$, the true sum is $-21$; the $8$ bits of the result are $(11101011)_2$, i.e., $-21$. No overflow: $-21\in[-128,127]$.
Example 2 · alg-g-twos-add · level media · notes
With $8$ bits in two's complement, $(10111111)_2$ and $(11010001)_2$ are added as natural numbers, keeping the $8$ least significant bits. Which integer does the result represent, and is there an overflow?
  1. result $-112$, no overflow
  2. result $112$, no overflow
  3. result $144$, no overflow
  4. result $-112$, overflow
Explanation. The operands are $-65$ and $-47$, the true sum is $-112$; the $8$ bits of the result are $(10010000)_2$, i.e., $-112$. No overflow: $-112\in[-128,127]$.
Example 1 · alg-g-dec2bin · level facile · notes
Which sequence of digits in base $2$ represents the natural number $158$ (written in base ten)?
  1. $(10011110)_2$
  2. $(01111001)_2$
  3. $(10011101)_2$
  4. $(10011111)_2$
Explanation. Dividing repeatedly by $2$, the remainders read from the last to the first give $(10011110)_2$; reading them in the order they come out gives the reversed sequence $(01111001)_2$.
Example 2 · alg-g-dec2bin · level facile · notes
Which sequence of digits in base $2$ represents the natural number $118$ (written in base ten)?
  1. $(0110111)_2$
  2. $(1110111)_2$
  3. $(1110110)_2$
  4. $(1110101)_2$
Explanation. Dividing repeatedly by $2$, the remainders read from the last to the first give $(1110110)_2$; reading them in the order they come out gives the reversed sequence $(0110111)_2$.
Example 1 · alg-g-signmag · level facile · notes
In sign-magnitude representation with $k=8$ bits the most significant bit is the sign ($0$ for $n \ge 0$, $1$ for $n<0$) and the other $7$ bits are the magnitude $|n|$ in binary. How is $n=-70$ represented?
  1. $11000110$
  2. $10111010$
  3. $01000110$
  4. $10111001$
Explanation. Sign $1$ and magnitude $70=(1000110)_2$ on $7$ bits: $11000110$. $10111010$ is the two's complement representation ($2^8-70$), $01000110$ that of $+70$.
Example 2 · alg-g-signmag · level facile · notes
In sign-magnitude representation with $k=8$ bits the most significant bit is the sign ($0$ for $n \ge 0$, $1$ for $n<0$) and the other $7$ bits are the magnitude $|n|$ in binary. How is $n=-48$ represented?
  1. $11010000$
  2. $11001111$
  3. $10110000$
  4. $00110000$
Explanation. Sign $1$ and magnitude $48=(0110000)_2$ on $7$ bits: $10110000$. $11010000$ is the two's complement representation ($2^8-48$), $00110000$ that of $+48$.

2. Pseudo-code (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-trace-loop · level facile · notes
What is the value of the variable $c$ at the end of the following pseudo-code? <pre>x := 124 c := 0 <b>while</b> x > 1 <b>do</b> x ← ⌊x / 2⌋ c ← c + 1 <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $7$
  2. $6$
  3. $5$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Each iteration halves $x$ (rounding down): $x$ reaches $1$ after $\lfloor\log_2 124\rfloor=6$ iterations, hence $c=6$.
Example 2 · alg-g-trace-loop · level facile · notes
What is the value of the variable $c$ at the end of the following pseudo-code? <pre>x := 172 c := 0 <b>while</b> x > 1 <b>do</b> x ← ⌊x / 2⌋ c ← c + 1 <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $9$
  2. $6$
  3. $7$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Each iteration halves $x$ (rounding down): $x$ reaches $1$ after $\lfloor\log_2 172\rfloor=7$ iterations, hence $c=7$.
Example 1 · alg-g-collatz · level complessa · notes
What are the values of $k$ and $y$ at the end of the following pseudo-code? <pre>x := 26 y := 0 k := 0 <b>while</b> x ≠ 1 <b>do</b> <b>if</b> x mod 2 = 0 <b>then</b> x ← x / 2 <b>else</b> x ← 3x + 1 <b>end</b> k ← k + 1 <b>if</b> x > y <b>then</b> y ← x <b>end</b> <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $k=11$, $y=40$
  2. $k=10$, $y=40$
  3. $k=9$, $y=40$
  4. $k=10$, $y=20$
Explanation. The values of $x$ are $26,\ 13,\ 40,\ 20,\ 10,\ 5,\ 16,\ 8,\ 4,\ 2,\ 1$: there are $k=10$ iterations and the largest value assigned to $x$ in the loop (the initial value does not count, since $y$ is updated after $x$ changes) is $y=40$.
Example 2 · alg-g-collatz · level complessa · notes
What are the values of $k$ and $y$ at the end of the following pseudo-code? <pre>x := 19 y := 0 k := 0 <b>while</b> x ≠ 1 <b>do</b> <b>if</b> x mod 2 = 0 <b>then</b> x ← x / 2 <b>else</b> x ← 3x + 1 <b>end</b> k ← k + 1 <b>if</b> x > y <b>then</b> y ← x <b>end</b> <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $k=21$, $y=88$
  2. $k=19$, $y=88$
  3. $k=20$, $y=88$
  4. $k=20$, $y=58$
Explanation. The values of $x$ are $19,\ 58,\ 29,\ 88,\ 44,\ 22,\ 11,\ 34,\ 17,\ 52,\ 26,\ 13,\ 40,\ 20,\ 10,\ 5,\ 16,\ 8,\ 4,\ 2,\ 1$: there are $k=20$ iterations and the largest value assigned to $x$ in the loop (the initial value does not count, since $y$ is updated after $x$ changes) is $y=88$.

3. Sorting algorithms (5)

Example 1 · alg-g-insertion-pass · level facile · notes
InsertionSort sorts $a[1..n]$ in increasing order: for $i=2,\dots,n$ it inserts $\mathit{key}=a[i]$ into the prefix $a[1..i-1]$, shifting one place to the right the keys larger than $\mathit{key}$. It is executed on $a=[3, 6, 4, 5, 9, 7]$. What does the array contain at the end of the iteration with $i=3$?
  1. $[3, 4, 6, 5, 9, 7]$
  2. $[3, 4, 5, 6, 9, 7]$
  3. $[3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 9]$
  4. $[3, 6, 4, 5, 9, 7]$
Explanation. After the iterations $i=2,\dots,3$ the sub-array $a[1..3]$ contains the first $3$ elements sorted, and the rest is unchanged: $[3, 4, 6, 5, 9, 7]$.
Example 2 · alg-g-insertion-pass · level facile · notes
InsertionSort sorts $a[1..n]$ in increasing order: for $i=2,\dots,n$ it inserts $\mathit{key}=a[i]$ into the prefix $a[1..i-1]$, shifting one place to the right the keys larger than $\mathit{key}$. It is executed on $a=[7, 9, 5, 2, 8, 1]$. What does the array contain at the end of the iteration with $i=4$?
  1. $[2, 5, 7, 8, 9, 1]$
  2. $[5, 7, 9, 2, 8, 1]$
  3. $[2, 5, 7, 9, 8, 1]$
  4. $[1, 2, 5, 9, 8, 7]$
Explanation. After the iterations $i=2,\dots,4$ the sub-array $a[1..4]$ contains the first $4$ elements sorted, and the rest is unchanged: $[2, 5, 7, 9, 8, 1]$.
Example 1 · alg-g-selection-pass · level facile · notes
SelectionSort sorts $a[1..n]$ in increasing order: for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ it finds the position of the minimum of $a[i..n]$ and swaps it with $a[i]$. It is executed on $a=[2, 5, 3, 7, 8, 1]$. What does the array contain after the first $2$ iterations ($i=1,\dots,2$)?
  1. $[2, 3, 5, 7, 8, 1]$
  2. $[1, 2, 5, 7, 8, 3]$
  3. $[1, 5, 3, 7, 8, 2]$
  4. $[1, 2, 3, 7, 8, 5]$
Explanation. In each iteration the minimum of $a[i..n]$ is swapped with $a[i]$ (one swap per iteration): $[1, 2, 3, 7, 8, 5]$. Exchange Sort, instead, swaps every time it finds an element smaller than $a[i]$.
Example 2 · alg-g-selection-pass · level facile · notes
SelectionSort sorts $a[1..n]$ in increasing order: for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ it finds the position of the minimum of $a[i..n]$ and swaps it with $a[i]$. It is executed on $a=[6, 5, 8, 9, 2, 1]$. What does the array contain after the first $3$ iterations ($i=1,\dots,3$)?
  1. $[1, 2, 5, 9, 8, 6]$
  2. $[1, 2, 8, 9, 5, 6]$
  3. $[5, 6, 8, 9, 2, 1]$
  4. $[1, 2, 5, 6, 8, 9]$
Explanation. In each iteration the minimum of $a[i..n]$ is swapped with $a[i]$ (one swap per iteration): $[1, 2, 5, 9, 8, 6]$. Exchange Sort, instead, swaps every time it finds an element smaller than $a[i]$.
Example 1 · alg-g-exchange-pass · level facile · notes
ExchangeSort sorts $a[1..n]$ in increasing order: for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ and, inside, for $j=i+1,\dots,n$, it swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ if $a[i]>a[j]$. It is executed on $a=[8, 3, 2, 4, 1, 7]$. What does the array contain at the end of the first two iterations of the outer loop ($i=1,2$)?
  1. $[1, 2, 3, 4, 8, 7]$
  2. $[1, 2, 3, 8, 4, 7]$
  3. $[8, 7, 2, 3, 1, 4]$
  4. $[1, 2, 8, 4, 3, 7]$
Explanation. For every $j>i$, if $a[i]>a[j]$ the two elements are swapped: at the end of the iteration $a[i]$ is the minimum of $a[i..n]$, but the other elements have moved as well: $[1, 2, 8, 4, 3, 7]$.
Example 2 · alg-g-exchange-pass · level facile · notes
ExchangeSort sorts $a[1..n]$ in increasing order: for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ and, inside, for $j=i+1,\dots,n$, it swaps $a[i]$ and $a[j]$ if $a[i]>a[j]$. It is executed on $a=[6, 5, 8, 9, 2, 1]$. What does the array contain at the end of the first two iterations of the outer loop ($i=1,2$)?
  1. $[1, 2, 8, 9, 6, 5]$
  2. $[9, 8, 5, 6, 2, 1]$
  3. $[1, 2, 8, 9, 5, 6]$
  4. $[1, 2, 5, 9, 8, 6]$
Explanation. For every $j>i$, if $a[i]>a[j]$ the two elements are swapped: at the end of the iteration $a[i]$ is the minimum of $a[i..n]$, but the other elements have moved as well: $[1, 2, 8, 9, 6, 5]$.
Example 1 · alg-g-insertion-counts · level media · notes
InsertionSort, for $i=2,\dots,n$, sets $\mathit{key}=a[i]$, $j=i-1$ and, while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$, shifts $a[j]$ into $a[j+1]$ and decrements $j$; then it writes $\mathit{key}$ into $a[j+1]$. How many comparisons $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ and how many shifts does it perform on the array $[8, 3, 2, 4, 1, 7]$?
  1. $15$ comparisons, $9$ shifts
  2. $9$ comparisons, $9$ shifts
  3. $11$ comparisons, $9$ shifts
  4. $10$ comparisons, $9$ shifts
Explanation. The shifts are the pairs in reverse order: $9$. Each iteration makes one comparison per shift, plus one false comparison that stops it unless it reaches $j=0$: $11$ comparisons in total.
Example 2 · alg-g-insertion-counts · level media · notes
InsertionSort, for $i=2,\dots,n$, sets $\mathit{key}=a[i]$, $j=i-1$ and, while $j\ge 1$ and $a[j]>\mathit{key}$, shifts $a[j]$ into $a[j+1]$ and decrements $j$; then it writes $\mathit{key}$ into $a[j+1]$. How many comparisons $a[j]>\mathit{key}$ and how many shifts does it perform on the array $[6, 5, 8, 9, 2, 1]$?
  1. $12$ comparisons, $10$ shifts
  2. $10$ comparisons, $10$ shifts
  3. $15$ comparisons, $10$ shifts
  4. $11$ comparisons, $10$ shifts
Explanation. The shifts are the pairs in reverse order: $10$. Each iteration makes one comparison per shift, plus one false comparison that stops it unless it reaches $j=0$: $12$ comparisons in total.
Example 1 · alg-g-selection-stability · level complessa · notes
SelectionSort sorts by key an array of records (key, letter): for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ it looks for the minimum of $a[i..n]$ with the strict comparison $a[j]<a[\mathit{minIdx}]$ (on ties the first one found wins) and swaps it with $a[i]$. What is the final array for $(2,A),\ (2,B),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (1,E),\ (3,F)$?
  1. $(1,E),\ (2,B),\ (2,A),\ (3,D),\ (3,C),\ (3,F)$
  2. $(1,E),\ (2,B),\ (2,A),\ (3,F),\ (3,D),\ (3,C)$
  3. $(1,E),\ (2,A),\ (2,B),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (3,F)$
  4. $(1,E),\ (2,A),\ (2,B),\ (3,F),\ (3,D),\ (3,C)$
Explanation. Following the $5$ iterations (minimum of $a[i..n]$ with the strict comparison $a[j]<a[\mathit{minIdx}]$, then one swap) we obtain $(1,E),\ (2,B),\ (2,A),\ (3,D),\ (3,C),\ (3,F)$. A swap can move a record past a record with the same key: Selection Sort is not stable, and the stable order (the one of Insertion Sort) is different.
Example 2 · alg-g-selection-stability · level complessa · notes
SelectionSort sorts by key an array of records (key, letter): for $i=1,\dots,n-1$ it looks for the minimum of $a[i..n]$ with the strict comparison $a[j]<a[\mathit{minIdx}]$ (on ties the first one found wins) and swaps it with $a[i]$. What is the final array for $(3,A),\ (2,B),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (2,E),\ (3,F)$?
  1. $(2,E),\ (2,B),\ (3,F),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (3,A)$
  2. $(2,B),\ (2,E),\ (3,A),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (3,F)$
  3. $(2,B),\ (2,E),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (3,A),\ (3,F)$
  4. $(2,E),\ (2,B),\ (3,F),\ (3,D),\ (3,C),\ (3,A)$
Explanation. Following the $5$ iterations (minimum of $a[i..n]$ with the strict comparison $a[j]<a[\mathit{minIdx}]$, then one swap) we obtain $(2,B),\ (2,E),\ (3,C),\ (3,D),\ (3,A),\ (3,F)$. A swap can move a record past a record with the same key: Selection Sort is not stable, and the stable order (the one of Insertion Sort) is different.

4. Linear and binary search (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-binsearch · level facile · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m=16$ in $a=[3, 8, 14, 16, 22, 27, 32, 38, 40, 46, 48, 51]$ (indices $1$ to $12$): it starts from $left=1$, $right=12$ and, while $left\le right$, examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$, returns $mid$ if $a[mid]=m$, otherwise sets $left\leftarrow mid+1$ if $a[mid]<m$ and $right\leftarrow mid-1$ if $a[mid]>m$; at the end it returns $-1$. Which indices $mid$ does it examine, and what does it return?
  1. $mid$: $7,\ 4$; returns $4$
  2. $mid$: $6,\ 3$; returns $-1$
  3. $mid$: $6,\ 3,\ 4$; returns $4$
  4. $mid$: $6,\ 9,\ 11,\ 12$; returns $-1$
Explanation. With $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$, if $a[mid]<m$ we set $left\leftarrow mid+1$, otherwise $right\leftarrow mid-1$: indices $6,\ 3,\ 4$, returned value $4$.
Example 2 · alg-g-binsearch · level facile · notes
BinarySearch looks for $m=51$ in $a=[5, 8, 10, 13, 19, 24, 29, 35, 40, 45, 51]$ (indices $1$ to $11$): it starts from $left=1$, $right=11$ and, while $left\le right$, examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$, returns $mid$ if $a[mid]=m$, otherwise sets $left\leftarrow mid+1$ if $a[mid]<m$ and $right\leftarrow mid-1$ if $a[mid]>m$; at the end it returns $-1$. Which indices $mid$ does it examine, and what does it return?
  1. $mid$: $6,\ 9,\ 10,\ 11$; returns $11$
  2. $mid$: $6,\ 9,\ 11$; returns $11$
  3. $mid$: $6,\ 3,\ 1$; returns $-1$
  4. $mid$: $6,\ 9,\ 10$; returns $-1$
Explanation. With $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$, if $a[mid]<m$ we set $left\leftarrow mid+1$, otherwise $right\leftarrow mid-1$: indices $6,\ 9,\ 10,\ 11$, returned value $11$.
Example 1 · alg-g-search-compare · level media · notes
We look for $m=51$ in the sorted array $a=[1, 14, 22, 28, 29, 39, 40, 42, 43, 49, 51, 56]$ (indices from $1$ to $12$). LinearSearch compares $a[i]=m$ for $i=1,2,\dots$ until the first success. BinarySearch starts from $left=1$, $right=12$ and at each iteration examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$: it stops if $a[mid]=m$, otherwise it sets $left\leftarrow mid+1$ if $a[mid]<m$ and $right\leftarrow mid-1$ if $a[mid]>m$. How many comparisons does LinearSearch perform and how many iterations BinarySearch?
  1. LinearSearch $11$ comparisons, BinarySearch $4$ iterations
  2. LinearSearch $10$ comparisons, BinarySearch $3$ iterations
  3. LinearSearch $11$ comparisons, BinarySearch $3$ iterations
  4. LinearSearch $6$ comparisons, BinarySearch $3$ iterations
Explanation. $m$ is in position $11$, so LinearSearch performs $11$ comparisons. BinarySearch examines $mid=6,\ 9,\ 11$: $3$ iterations, no more than $\lfloor\log_2 12\rfloor+1=4$.
Example 2 · alg-g-search-compare · level media · notes
We look for $m=52$ in the sorted array $a=[13, 14, 15, 20, 28, 33, 37, 46, 52, 56, 57]$ (indices from $1$ to $11$). LinearSearch compares $a[i]=m$ for $i=1,2,\dots$ until the first success. BinarySearch starts from $left=1$, $right=11$ and at each iteration examines $mid=left+\lfloor(right-left)/2\rfloor$: it stops if $a[mid]=m$, otherwise it sets $left\leftarrow mid+1$ if $a[mid]<m$ and $right\leftarrow mid-1$ if $a[mid]>m$. How many comparisons does LinearSearch perform and how many iterations BinarySearch?
  1. LinearSearch $9$ comparisons, BinarySearch $2$ iterations
  2. LinearSearch $9$ comparisons, BinarySearch $4$ iterations
  3. LinearSearch $9$ comparisons, BinarySearch $1$ iterations
  4. LinearSearch $11$ comparisons, BinarySearch $2$ iterations
Explanation. $m$ is in position $9$, so LinearSearch performs $9$ comparisons. BinarySearch examines $mid=6,\ 9$: $2$ iterations, no more than $\lfloor\log_2 11\rfloor+1=4$.

5. Numerical algorithms (4)

Example 1 · alg-g-change-base · level facile · notes
ChangeOfBase$(n,b)$ repeatedly divides $n$ by the base $b$ until the quotient is $0$, and each remainder is the next digit starting from the least significant one. Which is the representation in base $8$ of $n=318$ (digits from the most to the least significant)?
  1. $(674)_{8}$
  2. $(476)_{8}$
  3. $(47)_{8}$
  4. $(506)_{8}$
Explanation. The remainders of the successive divisions by $8$ are $6,\ 7,\ 4$: they are the digits $d_0,d_1,\dots$, i.e., from the least significant one. Read in reverse order they give $(476)_{8}$.
Example 2 · alg-g-change-base · level facile · notes
ChangeOfBase$(n,b)$ repeatedly divides $n$ by the base $b$ until the quotient is $0$, and each remainder is the next digit starting from the least significant one. Which is the representation in base $8$ of $n=432$ (digits from the most to the least significant)?
  1. $(670)_{8}$
  2. $(66)_{8}$
  3. $(660)_{8}$
  4. $(066)_{8}$
Explanation. The remainders of the successive divisions by $8$ are $0,\ 6,\ 6$: they are the digits $d_0,d_1,\dots$, i.e., from the least significant one. Read in reverse order they give $(660)_{8}$.
Example 1 · alg-g-euclid · level facile · notes
EuclideanAlgorithm$(n,m)$ computes $\gcd(n,m)$: while $m\ne 0$, it replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\ n\bmod m)$ (one iteration of the while loop); then it returns $n$. With $n=128$ and $m=168$, which value does it return and after how many iterations?
  1. returns $8$ after $3$ iterations
  2. returns $8$ after $5$ iterations
  3. returns $8$ after $4$ iterations
  4. returns $40$ after $4$ iterations
Explanation. The values of $m$ are $168,\ 128,\ 40,\ 8,\ 0$: each iteration replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\ n\bmod m)$; after $4$ iterations $m=0$ and the algorithm returns $n=8=\gcd(128,168)$. With $n<m$ the first iteration just swaps the two numbers.
Example 2 · alg-g-euclid · level facile · notes
EuclideanAlgorithm$(n,m)$ computes $\gcd(n,m)$: while $m\ne 0$, it replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\ n\bmod m)$ (one iteration of the while loop); then it returns $n$. With $n=36$ and $m=66$, which value does it return and after how many iterations?
  1. returns $6$ after $4$ iterations
  2. returns $6$ after $3$ iterations
  3. returns $6$ after $5$ iterations
  4. returns $30$ after $4$ iterations
Explanation. The values of $m$ are $66,\ 36,\ 30,\ 6,\ 0$: each iteration replaces $(n,m)$ with $(m,\ n\bmod m)$; after $4$ iterations $m=0$ and the algorithm returns $n=6=\gcd(36,66)$. With $n<m$ the first iteration just swaps the two numbers.
Example 1 · alg-g-sieve · level media · notes
SieveOfEratosthenes$(n)$ sets $prime[2..n]$ to $1$; then for $p=2,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$, if $prime[p]=1$, it executes $prime[j]\leftarrow 0$ for $j=p^2,\,p^2+p,\,p^2+2p,\dots\le n$. With $n=47$, how many times is the assignment $prime[j]\leftarrow 0$ executed?
  1. $40$
  2. $31$
  3. $50$
  4. $44$
Explanation. The outer loop goes up to $\lfloor\sqrt{47}\rfloor=6$ and the inner loop runs only for $p$ prime ($p=2,3,5$), with $j=p^2,p^2+p,\dots\le 47$: $40$ assignments in total. A composite that is a multiple of several primes is marked several times, so the count is not the number of composites ($31$).
Example 2 · alg-g-sieve · level media · notes
SieveOfEratosthenes$(n)$ sets $prime[2..n]$ to $1$; then for $p=2,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$, if $prime[p]=1$, it executes $prime[j]\leftarrow 0$ for $j=p^2,\,p^2+p,\,p^2+2p,\dots\le n$. With $n=41$, how many times is the assignment $prime[j]\leftarrow 0$ executed?
  1. $27$
  2. $38$
  3. $34$
  4. $42$
Explanation. The outer loop goes up to $\lfloor\sqrt{41}\rfloor=6$ and the inner loop runs only for $p$ prime ($p=2,3,5$), with $j=p^2,p^2+p,\dots\le 41$: $34$ assignments in total. A composite that is a multiple of several primes is marked several times, so the count is not the number of composites ($27$).
Example 1 · alg-g-divisors · level facile · notes
A natural $d \ge 1$ is a divisor of $n$ if $n \bmod d=0$ ($1$ and $n$ included). How many divisors does $n=74$ have?
  1. $4$
  2. $2$
  3. $5$
  4. $3$
Explanation. The divisors are $1,2,37,74$: $4$ in total. Forgetting $1$ and $n$ removes two divisors; counting only those up to $\sqrt{n}$ forgets their companions $n/d$.
Example 2 · alg-g-divisors · level facile · notes
A natural $d \ge 1$ is a divisor of $n$ if $n \bmod d=0$ ($1$ and $n$ included). How many divisors does $n=45$ have?
  1. $7$
  2. $5$
  3. $4$
  4. $6$
Explanation. The divisors are $1,3,5,9,15,45$: $6$ in total. Forgetting $1$ and $n$ removes two divisors; counting only those up to $\sqrt{n}$ forgets their companions $n/d$.

6. Introduction to C++ (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-cpp-bitwise · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>int x = 20, y = 26;</code>, what is the value of the expression <code>x ^ y</code>?
  1. $30$
  2. $16$
  3. $14$
  4. $46$
Explanation. $20=(10100)_2$ and $26=(11010)_2$; with <code>^</code> a bit is $1$ if it is $1$ in exactly one of them: $(01110)_2=14$.
Example 2 · alg-g-cpp-bitwise · level facile · notes
In C++, with <code>int x = 27, y = 13;</code>, what is the value of the expression <code>x | y</code>?
  1. $40$
  2. $22$
  3. $9$
  4. $31$
Explanation. $27=(11011)_2$ and $13=(01101)_2$; with <code>|</code> a bit is $1$ if it is $1$ in at least one of them: $(11111)_2=31$.
Example 1 · alg-g-cpp-reference · level media · notes
In C++ a parameter declared with <code>&amp;</code> (for instance <code>int&amp; b</code>) is passed by reference, the others by value. What does the program print? <pre>int f(int&amp; a, int b) { a = a + 4; b = b * 4; return a + b; } int main() { int x = 6, y = 6; int z = f(x, y); cout &lt;&lt; x &lt;&lt; " " &lt;&lt; y &lt;&lt; " " &lt;&lt; z; return 0; }</pre>
  1. <code>6 6 34</code>
  2. <code>10 24 34</code>
  3. <code>10 6 30</code>
  4. <code>10 6 34</code>
Explanation. Inside <code>f</code> we have <code>a</code> $=6+4=10$ and <code>b</code> $=6\cdot 4=24$, so <code>z</code> $=34$. Only the parameter passed by reference (<code>a</code>) modifies the variable of the caller: <code>x</code> $=10$, <code>y</code> $=6$.
Example 2 · alg-g-cpp-reference · level media · notes
In C++ a parameter declared with <code>&amp;</code> (for instance <code>int&amp; b</code>) is passed by reference, the others by value. What does the program print? <pre>int f(int&amp; a, int b) { a = a + 2; b = b * 2; return a + b; } int main() { int x = 5, y = 8; int z = f(x, y); cout &lt;&lt; x &lt;&lt; " " &lt;&lt; y &lt;&lt; " " &lt;&lt; z; return 0; }</pre>
  1. <code>7 8 23</code>
  2. <code>5 16 23</code>
  3. <code>5 8 23</code>
  4. <code>7 16 23</code>
Explanation. Inside <code>f</code> we have <code>a</code> $=5+2=7$ and <code>b</code> $=8\cdot 2=16$, so <code>z</code> $=23$. Only the parameter passed by reference (<code>a</code>) modifies the variable of the caller: <code>x</code> $=7$, <code>y</code> $=8$.

7. Introduction to Python (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-py-divmod · level facile · notes
In Python 3, what are <code>26 // -6</code> and <code>26 % -6</code> (in this order)?
  1. $-5$ and $2$
  2. $-4$ and $2$
  3. $-5$ and $-4$
  4. $-4$ and $-4$
Explanation. In Python <code>//</code> rounds the quotient down: $\lfloor 26/(-6)\rfloor=-5$; the remainder is $26-(-6)\cdot(-5)=-4$ and has the sign of the divisor. C++ and Java truncate toward zero and would give $-4$ and $2$.
Example 2 · alg-g-py-divmod · level facile · notes
In Python 3, what are <code>-19 // 8</code> and <code>-19 % 8</code> (in this order)?
  1. $-2$ and $5$
  2. $-3$ and $-3$
  3. $-2$ and $-3$
  4. $-3$ and $5$
Explanation. In Python <code>//</code> rounds the quotient down: $\lfloor -19/8\rfloor=-3$; the remainder is $-19-8\cdot(-3)=5$ and has the sign of the divisor. C++ and Java truncate toward zero and would give $-2$ and $-3$.
Example 1 · alg-g-py-params · level media · notes
In Python a parameter receives the same object passed by the caller. What does the program print? <pre>def f(a, x): a[2] = a[2] + x x = x * 2 return x b = [6, 6, 8] k = 2 r = f(b, k) print(b, k, r)</pre>
  1. <code>[6, 6, 8] 2 4</code>
  2. <code>[6, 6, 10] 4 4</code>
  3. <code>[6, 6, 12] 2 4</code>
  4. <code>[6, 6, 10] 2 4</code>
Explanation. <code>a[2] = …</code> modifies an element of the caller's list, which becomes <code>[6, 6, 10]</code>; <code>x = x * 2</code> changes only the local name, so <code>k</code> stays $2$, while the returned value is $4$.
Example 2 · alg-g-py-params · level media · notes
In Python a parameter receives the same object passed by the caller. What does the program print? <pre>def f(a, x): a[0] = a[0] + x x = x * 2 return x b = [4, 8, 3] k = 4 r = f(b, k) print(b, k, r)</pre>
  1. <code>[8, 8, 3] 4 8</code>
  2. <code>[8, 8, 3] 4 4</code>
  3. <code>[4, 8, 3] 4 8</code>
  4. <code>[8, 8, 3] 8 8</code>
Explanation. <code>a[0] = …</code> modifies an element of the caller's list, which becomes <code>[8, 8, 3]</code>; <code>x = x * 2</code> changes only the local name, so <code>k</code> stays $4$, while the returned value is $8$.

8. Introduction to Java (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-java-divmod · level facile · notes
In Java, with <code>int x = 26, y = -6;</code>, what does <code>System.out.println(x / y + " " + x % y);</code> print?
  1. <code>-4 -4</code>
  2. <code>-5 -4</code>
  3. <code>-4 2</code>
  4. <code>-5 2</code>
Explanation. Between two <code>int</code> values, <code>/</code> truncates the quotient toward zero: $-4$; the remainder is $26-(-6)\cdot(-4)=2$ and has the sign of the dividend. $-5$ and $-4$ are the results of <code>//</code> and <code>%</code> in Python, which rounds down.
Example 2 · alg-g-java-divmod · level facile · notes
In Java, with <code>int x = -19, y = 8;</code>, what does <code>System.out.println(x / y + " " + x % y);</code> print?
  1. <code>-3 -3</code>
  2. <code>-2 5</code>
  3. <code>-3 5</code>
  4. <code>-2 -3</code>
Explanation. Between two <code>int</code> values, <code>/</code> truncates the quotient toward zero: $-2$; the remainder is $-19-8\cdot(-2)=-3$ and has the sign of the dividend. $-3$ and $5$ are the results of <code>//</code> and <code>%</code> in Python, which rounds down.
Example 1 · alg-g-java-concat · level media · notes
In Java, with <code>int x = 8, y = 2;</code>, what does <code>System.out.println(x + " " + y * 2 + x);</code> print?
  1. <code>8 12</code>
  2. <code>8 20</code>
  3. <code>8 228</code>
  4. <code>8 48</code>
Explanation. The operator <code>+</code> is evaluated from left to right: between two <code>int</code> values it is an addition, but as soon as one of the two operands is a <code>String</code> the other is converted into text and concatenated. <code>*</code> and the parentheses are evaluated first. The result is <code>8 48</code>.
Example 2 · alg-g-java-concat · level media · notes
In Java, with <code>int x = 5, y = 8;</code>, what does <code>System.out.println(x + y + " " + x + y);</code> print?
  1. <code>13 58</code>
  2. <code>58 13</code>
  3. <code>1358</code>
  4. <code>58 58</code>
Explanation. The operator <code>+</code> is evaluated from left to right: between two <code>int</code> values it is an addition, but as soon as one of the two operands is a <code>String</code> the other is converted into text and concatenated. <code>*</code> and the parentheses are evaluated first. The result is <code>13 58</code>.

9. Asymptotic notation (3)

Example 1 · alg-g-asym-compare · level facile · notes
Let $f(n)=(n+4)^2$ and $g(n)=n^2\,2^n$, functions of the natural variable $n$. Which asymptotic relation is true?
  1. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)=\Omega\big(g(n)\big)$
  3. $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)=\omega\big(g(n)\big)$
Explanation. We have $\lim_{n\to+\infty} f(n)/g(n)=0^+$; by the limit criterion $f=o(g)$, hence also $f=\bigO(g)$, $g=\omega(f)$ and $g=\Omega(f)$, but neither $\Theta$ nor $\Omega$ of $f$ with respect to $g$.
Example 2 · alg-g-asym-compare · level facile · notes
Let $f(n)=\ln(n^3)$ and $g(n)=n\log_2 n$, functions of the natural variable $n$. Which asymptotic relation is true?
  1. $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  3. $f(n)=\Omega\big(g(n)\big)$
  4. $g(n)=\bigO\big(f(n)\big)$
Explanation. We have $\lim_{n\to+\infty} f(n)/g(n)=0^+$; by the limit criterion $f=o(g)$, hence also $f=\bigO(g)$, $g=\omega(f)$ and $g=\Omega(f)$, but neither $\Theta$ nor $\Omega$ of $f$ with respect to $g$.
Example 1 · alg-g-theta-constants · level media · notes
To prove with the definition that $f(n)=4n^2+7n=\Theta(n^2)$ we need constants $c_1,c_2>0$ and a threshold $n_1\in\N$ with $c_1n^2\le f(n)\le c_2n^2$ for all $n\ge n_1$. Which choice works?
  1. $c_1=5$, $c_2=6$, $n_1=1$
  2. $c_1=5$, $c_2=15$, $n_1=7$
  3. $c_1=4$, $c_2=6$, $n_1=4$
  4. $c_1=4$, $c_2=4$, $n_1=7$
Explanation. The lower bound $c_1n^2\le f(n)$ holds for all $n$ if and only if $c_1\le 4$. The upper one is equivalent to $7n\le(c_2-4)n^2$, i.e., $(c_2-4)\,n\ge 7$: we need $c_2>4$ and $n_1\ge 7/(c_2-4)$. With $c_2=6$ we have $2\cdot 4\ge 7$; the other choices violate one of the conditions.
Example 2 · alg-g-theta-constants · level media · notes
To prove with the definition that $f(n)=3n^2+8n=\Theta(n^2)$ we need constants $c_1,c_2>0$ and a threshold $n_1\in\N$ with $c_1n^2\le f(n)\le c_2n^2$ for all $n\ge n_1$. Which choice works?
  1. $c_1=3$, $c_2=4$, $n_1=8$
  2. $c_1=1$, $c_2=10$, $n_1=1$
  3. $c_1=4$, $c_2=14$, $n_1=8$
  4. $c_1=4$, $c_2=5$, $n_1=1$
Explanation. The lower bound $c_1n^2\le f(n)$ holds for all $n$ if and only if $c_1\le 3$. The upper one is equivalent to $8n\le(c_2-3)n^2$, i.e., $(c_2-3)\,n\ge 8$: we need $c_2>3$ and $n_1\ge 8/(c_2-3)$. With $c_2=4$ we have $1\cdot 8\ge 8$; the other choices violate one of the conditions.
Example 1 · alg-g-bigo-n1 · level complessa · notes
To prove $f(n)=4n^2+25n=O(n^2)$ the constant $c=7$ is chosen. What is the smallest natural $n_1$ such that $f(n) \le 7\,n^2$ for every $n \ge n_1$?
  1. $10$
  2. $8$
  3. $9$
  4. $4$
Explanation. For $n \ge 1$: $4n^2+25n \le 7n^2 \iff 25 \le 3n \iff n \ge 25/3$, so $n_1=\lceil 25/3\rceil=9$. With $n_1-1$ the inequality does not hold yet.
Example 2 · alg-g-bigo-n1 · level complessa · notes
To prove $f(n)=3n^2+35n=O(n^2)$ the constant $c=4$ is chosen. What is the smallest natural $n_1$ such that $f(n) \le 4\,n^2$ for every $n \ge n_1$?
  1. $9$
  2. $36$
  3. $34$
  4. $35$
Explanation. For $n \ge 1$: $3n^2+35n \le 4n^2 \iff 35 \le 1n \iff n \ge 35/1$, so $n_1=\lceil 35/1\rceil=35$. With $n_1-1$ the inequality does not hold yet.

10. Asymptotic growth (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-growth-order · level facile · notes
Sort by order of growth, from the slowest to the fastest (each one is $o$ of the next), the functions $2^n,\ 3n+\log_2 n,\ n\log_2 n,\ \sqrt{n}$.
  1. $3n+\log_2 n,\quad \sqrt{n},\quad n\log_2 n,\quad 2^n$
  2. $\sqrt{n},\quad n\log_2 n,\quad 3n+\log_2 n,\quad 2^n$
  3. $\sqrt{n},\quad 3n+\log_2 n,\quad n\log_2 n,\quad 2^n$
  4. $\sqrt{n},\quad 3n+\log_2 n,\quad 2^n,\quad n\log_2 n$
Explanation. Hierarchy: polylogarithms $\prec$ powers $\prec$ exponentials $\prec$ $n!$ $\prec$ $n^n$; among powers the exponent matters, among exponentials the base, and constant factors or shifts do not change the order.
Example 2 · alg-g-growth-order · level facile · notes
Sort by order of growth, from the slowest to the fastest (each one is $o$ of the next), the functions $2^{2n},\ \log_2^3 n,\ n!,\ \log_2 n$.
  1. $\log_2 n,\quad 2^{2n},\quad \log_2^3 n,\quad n!$
  2. $\log_2^3 n,\quad \log_2 n,\quad 2^{2n},\quad n!$
  3. $\log_2 n,\quad \log_2^3 n,\quad n!,\quad 2^{2n}$
  4. $\log_2 n,\quad \log_2^3 n,\quad 2^{2n},\quad n!$
Explanation. Hierarchy: polylogarithms $\prec$ powers $\prec$ exponentials $\prec$ $n!$ $\prec$ $n^n$; among powers the exponent matters, among exponentials the base, and constant factors or shifts do not change the order.
Example 1 · alg-g-growth-transform · level media · notes
Let $f(n)=2^{n}$ and $g(n)=5^{n}$, functions of the natural variable $n$. Which asymptotic relation is true?
  1. $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. Neither is $\bigO$ of the other
  3. $f(n)=\omega\big(g(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
Explanation. It holds $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$: among exponentials the base matters. The notations $\Theta$, $o$ and $\omega$ are mutually exclusive.
Example 2 · alg-g-growth-transform · level media · notes
Let $f(n)=\log_2(n^{5})$ and $g(n)=\log_2 n$, functions of the natural variable $n$. Which asymptotic relation is true?
  1. $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$
  2. Neither is $\bigO$ of the other
  3. $f(n)=\omega\big(g(n)\big)$
  4. $f(n)=o\big(g(n)\big)$
Explanation. It holds $f(n)=\Theta\big(g(n)\big)$: $\log_2(n^k)=k\log_2 n$. The notations $\Theta$, $o$ and $\omega$ are mutually exclusive.

11. Input size (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-input-size · level facile · notes
The input is the array $a=[116, 158, 172, 22, 162]$ (without the number $n$). What is the input size $|I|$, i.e., the total number of bits needed to write it in binary?
  1. $36$
  2. $41$
  3. $40$
  4. $31$
Explanation. Each entry requires $\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1$ bits: $7+8+8+5+8=36$. The value $n(\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1)=40$ is only an upper bound.
Example 2 · alg-g-input-size · level facile · notes
The input is the array $a=[169, 63, 19, 80, 172]$ (without the number $n$). What is the input size $|I|$, i.e., the total number of bits needed to write it in binary?
  1. $34$
  2. $29$
  3. $40$
  4. $39$
Explanation. Each entry requires $\lfloor\log_2 a[i]\rfloor+1$ bits: $8+6+5+7+8=34$. The value $n(\lfloor\log_2 M\rfloor+1)=40$ is only an upper bound.
Example 1 · alg-g-input-zeros · level media · notes
The input size $|I|$ is the total number of bits needed to write the instance in binary: a natural number $x\ge 1$ needs $\lfloor\log_2 x\rfloor+1$ bits, and zero one bit. The input is the array $a=[158, 22, 125, 177, 0, 37]$ together with the number $n=6$ of its entries. What is $|I|$?
  1. $35$
  2. $39$
  3. $51$
  4. $38$
Explanation. The entries need $8+5+7+8+1+6=35$ bits (each zero counts one bit) and $n=6$ needs $3$ more: $|I|=38$.
Example 2 · alg-g-input-zeros · level media · notes
The input size $|I|$ is the total number of bits needed to write the instance in binary: a natural number $x\ge 1$ needs $\lfloor\log_2 x\rfloor+1$ bits, and zero one bit. The input is the array $a=[63, 0, 172, 132, 151, 174]$ (without the number $n$). What is $|I|$?
  1. $48$
  2. $45$
  3. $39$
  4. $42$
Explanation. The entries need $6+1+8+8+8+8=39$ bits (each zero counts one bit): $|I|=39$.

12. Running time (3)

Example 1 · alg-g-loop-count · level facile · notes
How many times is line $4$ executed? <pre>1 x := 0 2 i := 1 3 <b>while</b> i ≤ 14 <b>do</b> 4 x ← x + 1 5 i ← 2 · i <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $5$
  4. $7$
Explanation. Count line by line: $i=1,2,4,\dots,2^{3}\le 14\text{: }\lfloor\log_2 14\rfloor+1=4$.
Example 2 · alg-g-loop-count · level facile · notes
How many times is line $4$ executed? <pre>1 x := 0 2 <b>for</b> i := 1 <b>to</b> 18 <b>do</b> 3 <b>for</b> j := 1 <b>to</b> i − 1 <b>do</b> 4 x ← x + 1 <b>end</b> <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $289$
  2. $324$
  3. $153$
  4. $171$
Explanation. Count line by line: $\sum_{i=1}^{18}(i-1)=\frac{18\cdot 17}{2}=153$.
Example 1 · alg-g-findmax-steps · level media · notes
FindMaximum (pseudo-code below) is executed on $a=[18, 24, 26, 4, 25, 19, 19, 27]$, with $n=8$. Counting one step for each execution of a numbered line (return included), how many steps does it execute? <pre>1 max := a[1] 2 i := 2 3 <b>while</b> i ≤ n <b>do</b> 4 <b>if</b> a[i] > max <b>then</b> 5 max ← a[i] <b>end</b> 6 i ← i + 1 <b>end</b> 7 <b>return</b> max</pre>
  1. $32$
  2. $29$
  3. $27$
  4. $28$
Explanation. Lines $1,2,7$: once each; condition of line $3$: $n=8$ times; lines $4$ and $6$: $n-1=7$ times; line $5$: $t=3$ times (new maxima). Total $3n+t+1=28$; $4n$ is the worst case $t=n-1$.
Example 2 · alg-g-findmax-steps · level media · notes
FindMaximum (pseudo-code below) is executed on $a=[26, 10, 3, 12, 26, 24, 20]$, with $n=7$. Counting one step for each execution of a numbered line (return included), how many steps does it execute? <pre>1 max := a[1] 2 i := 2 3 <b>while</b> i ≤ n <b>do</b> 4 <b>if</b> a[i] > max <b>then</b> 5 max ← a[i] <b>end</b> 6 i ← i + 1 <b>end</b> 7 <b>return</b> max</pre>
  1. $22$
  2. $28$
  3. $21$
  4. $23$
Explanation. Lines $1,2,7$: once each; condition of line $3$: $n=7$ times; lines $4$ and $6$: $n-1=6$ times; line $5$: $t=0$ times (new maxima). Total $3n+t+1=22$; $4n$ is the worst case $t=n-1$.
Example 1 · alg-g-nested-loops · level complessa · notes
With $n=7$, how many times is the statement $x:=x+1$ executed in the fragment «for $i=1$ to $n$: for $j=i$ to $n$: for $k=1$ to $j$: $x:=x+1$» (every for includes both bounds; if the initial bound exceeds the final one the loop is not executed)?
  1. $28$
  2. $140$
  3. $49$
  4. $343$
Explanation. Sum over the pairs $i \le j$: $\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i}^{n} j=140$. Counting $n$ iterations for every loop ignores that the inner loops depend on $i$ and $j$.
Example 2 · alg-g-nested-loops · level complessa · notes
With $n=6$, how many times is the statement $x:=x+1$ executed in the fragment «for $i=1$ to $n$: for $j=i$ to $n$: for $k=i$ to $j$: $x:=x+1$» (every for includes both bounds; if the initial bound exceeds the final one the loop is not executed)?
  1. $216$
  2. $36$
  3. $56$
  4. $21$
Explanation. Sum over the pairs $i \le j$: $\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i}^{n}(j-i+1)=56$. Counting $n$ iterations for every loop ignores that the inner loops depend on $i$ and $j$.

13. Correctness (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-findmax-invariant · level facile · notes
FindMaximum (below) is executed on $a=[25, 7, 14, 18, 34, 31, 24]$, with $n=7$. What are the values of $i$ and $max$ when the condition of line $3$ is evaluated for the $3$-th time? <pre>1 max := a[1] 2 i := 2 3 <b>while</b> i ≤ n <b>do</b> 4 <b>if</b> a[i] > max <b>then</b> 5 max ← a[i] <b>end</b> 6 i ← i + 1 <b>end</b> 7 <b>return</b> max</pre>
  1. $i=4$, $max=18$
  2. $i=4$, $max=14$
  3. $i=3$, $max=25$
  4. $i=4$, $max=25$
Explanation. At the first evaluation $i=2$ and $max=a[1]$; each iteration increments $i$. At evaluation number $3$ we have $i=4$ and, by the invariant $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[i-1]\}$, $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[3]\}=25$.
Example 2 · alg-g-findmax-invariant · level facile · notes
FindMaximum (below) is executed on $a=[38, 13, 18, 25, 8, 36, 32]$, with $n=7$. What are the values of $i$ and $max$ when the condition of line $3$ is evaluated for the $5$-th time? <pre>1 max := a[1] 2 i := 2 3 <b>while</b> i ≤ n <b>do</b> 4 <b>if</b> a[i] > max <b>then</b> 5 max ← a[i] <b>end</b> 6 i ← i + 1 <b>end</b> 7 <b>return</b> max</pre>
  1. $i=5$, $max=38$
  2. $i=6$, $max=36$
  3. $i=6$, $max=8$
  4. $i=6$, $max=38$
Explanation. At the first evaluation $i=2$ and $max=a[1]$; each iteration increments $i$. At evaluation number $5$ we have $i=6$ and, by the invariant $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[i-1]\}$, $max=\max\{a[1],\dots,a[5]\}=38$.
Example 1 · alg-g-binsearch-interval · level media · notes
BinarySearch (below) looks for $m=6$ in $a=[6, 7, 8, 17, 24, 29, 40, 41, 44, 50, 56, 60, 62, 70]$, with $n=14$. What are $left$ and $right$ when the condition of line $3$ is evaluated for the $3$-th time? <pre>1 left := 1 2 right := n 3 <b>while</b> left ≤ right <b>do</b> 4 mid := left + ⌊(right − left) / 2⌋ 5 <b>if</b> a[mid] = m <b>then return</b> mid 6 <b>if</b> a[mid] &lt; m <b>then</b> left ← mid + 1 7 <b>else</b> right ← mid − 1 <b>end</b> 8 <b>return</b> −1</pre>
  1. $left=0$, $right=2$
  2. $left=1$, $right=3$
  3. $left=1$, $right=2$
  4. $left=1$, $right=6$
Explanation. At the successive evaluations the interval is $[1,14]$, $[1,6]$, $[1,2]$. Each step discards only a part that cannot contain $m$ (invariant: if $m$ is in the array, it is in $a[left..right]$).
Example 2 · alg-g-binsearch-interval · level media · notes
BinarySearch (below) looks for $m=6$ in $a=[4, 13, 16, 31, 33, 42, 46, 55, 61, 63, 69]$, with $n=11$. What are $left$ and $right$ when the condition of line $3$ is evaluated for the $2$-th time? <pre>1 left := 1 2 right := n 3 <b>while</b> left ≤ right <b>do</b> 4 mid := left + ⌊(right − left) / 2⌋ 5 <b>if</b> a[mid] = m <b>then return</b> mid 6 <b>if</b> a[mid] &lt; m <b>then</b> left ← mid + 1 7 <b>else</b> right ← mid − 1 <b>end</b> 8 <b>return</b> −1</pre>
  1. $left=1$, $right=6$
  2. $left=1$, $right=5$
  3. $left=0$, $right=5$
  4. $left=1$, $right=2$
Explanation. At the successive evaluations the interval is $[1,11]$, $[1,5]$. Each step discards only a part that cannot contain $m$ (invariant: if $m$ is in the array, it is in $a[left..right]$).

14. Recursion (5)

Example 1 · alg-g-rec-calls · level facile · notes
FastPower$(x,n)$ computes $x^n$: if $n=0$ it returns $1$; otherwise it sets $y:=$FastPower$(x,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ and returns $y\cdot y$ ($n$ even) or $x\cdot y\cdot y$ ($n$ odd). How many calls (including the first one) does FastPower$(x,60)$ execute?
  1. $6$
  2. $7$
  3. $8$
  4. $61$
Explanation. The exponents are $60,\lfloor 60/2\rfloor,\dots,1,0$: as many calls as the binary digits of $60$ plus the one with exponent $0$, i.e., $\lfloor\log_2 60\rfloor+2=7$.
Example 2 · alg-g-rec-calls · level facile · notes
Hanoi$(n,from,to,aux)$ moves a tower of $n$ disks: if $n=1$ it moves the disk; otherwise it moves $n-1$ disks to $aux$ with one call, moves disk $n$ to $to$ and brings the $n-1$ disks onto $to$ with another call. How many moves does it execute with $n=9$?
  1. $81$
  2. $255$
  3. $511$
  4. $512$
Explanation. $M(n)=2M(n-1)+1$ with $M(1)=1$, hence $M(n)=2^n-1=511$.
Example 1 · alg-g-rec-value · level facile · notes
What does the call R$(4)$ return, and how many calls of R does it execute in total (including the first one)? <pre><b>function</b> R(n) <b>if</b> n = 0 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> 2 <b>if</b> n = 1 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> 3 <b>return</b> 2 · R(n − 1) + 2 · R(n − 2) <b>end</b></pre>
  1. value $72$, $9$ calls
  2. value $26$, $9$ calls
  3. value $72$, $5$ calls
  4. value $72$, $10$ calls
Explanation. The values $R(0),\dots,R(4)$ are $2,\ 3,\ 10,\ 26,\ 72$. The tree of recursive calls is that of Fib: $C(n)=C(n-1)+C(n-2)+1$, $C(0)=C(1)=1$, hence $9$ calls (not $n+1$: the subproblems are repeated).
Example 2 · alg-g-rec-value · level facile · notes
What does the call R$(5)$ return, and how many calls of R does it execute in total (including the first one)? <pre><b>function</b> R(n) <b>if</b> n = 0 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> 0 <b>if</b> n = 1 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> 1 <b>return</b> 2 · R(n − 1) + 3 · R(n − 2) <b>end</b></pre>
  1. value $61$, $15$ calls
  2. value $61$, $6$ calls
  3. value $20$, $15$ calls
  4. value $61$, $16$ calls
Explanation. The values $R(0),\dots,R(5)$ are $0,\ 1,\ 2,\ 7,\ 20,\ 61$. The tree of recursive calls is that of Fib: $C(n)=C(n-1)+C(n-2)+1$, $C(0)=C(1)=1$, hence $15$ calls (not $n+1$: the subproblems are repeated).
Example 1 · alg-g-fastpower-mults · level media · notes
How many calls of FastPower (including the first one) and how many multiplications in total does FastPower$(x,71)$ execute? ($y\cdot y$ counts one multiplication, $x\cdot y\cdot y$ two.) <pre><b>function</b> FastPower(x, n) <b>if</b> n = 0 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> 1 y := FastPower(x, ⌊n/2⌋) <b>if</b> n mod 2 = 0 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> y · y <b>else</b> <b>return</b> x · y · y <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $8$ calls, $11$ multiplications
  2. $7$ calls, $11$ multiplications
  3. $8$ calls, $70$ multiplications
  4. $8$ calls, $14$ multiplications
Explanation. The exponents of the calls are $71,35,17,8,4,2,1,0$: $8$ calls. Each of the $7$ calls with positive exponent makes one multiplication, plus a second one if the exponent is odd ($4$ times, the $1$s in $(1000111)_2$): $7+4=11$, against the $70$ of the direct computation.
Example 2 · alg-g-fastpower-mults · level media · notes
How many calls of FastPower (including the first one) and how many multiplications in total does FastPower$(x,49)$ execute? ($y\cdot y$ counts one multiplication, $x\cdot y\cdot y$ two.) <pre><b>function</b> FastPower(x, n) <b>if</b> n = 0 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> 1 y := FastPower(x, ⌊n/2⌋) <b>if</b> n mod 2 = 0 <b>then</b> <b>return</b> y · y <b>else</b> <b>return</b> x · y · y <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $6$ calls, $9$ multiplications
  2. $7$ calls, $12$ multiplications
  3. $7$ calls, $9$ multiplications
  4. $7$ calls, $48$ multiplications
Explanation. The exponents of the calls are $49,24,12,6,3,1,0$: $7$ calls. Each of the $6$ calls with positive exponent makes one multiplication, plus a second one if the exponent is odd ($3$ times, the $1$s in $(110001)_2$): $6+3=9$, against the $48$ of the direct computation.
Example 1 · alg-g-hanoi-moves · level facile · notes
Hanoi$(n,from,to,aux)$ with $n \ge 2$ recursively moves $n-1$ disks from $from$ to $aux$, then disk $n$ from $from$ to $to$ (one move), then $n-1$ disks from $aux$ to $to$; with $n=1$ it makes a single move. How many moves does it make with $n=9$ disks?
  1. $511$
  2. $512$
  3. $17$
  4. $255$
Explanation. $M(1)=1$ and $M(n)=2M(n-1)+1$, hence $M(n)=2^n-1=511$. $2^n$ forgets the $-1$.
Example 2 · alg-g-hanoi-moves · level facile · notes
Hanoi$(n,from,to,aux)$ with $n \ge 2$ recursively moves $n-1$ disks from $from$ to $aux$, then disk $n$ from $from$ to $to$ (one move), then $n-1$ disks from $aux$ to $to$; with $n=1$ it makes a single move. How many moves does it make with $n=7$ disks?
  1. $128$
  2. $63$
  3. $127$
  4. $13$
Explanation. $M(1)=1$ and $M(n)=2M(n-1)+1$, hence $M(n)=2^n-1=127$. $2^n$ forgets the $-1$.
Example 1 · alg-g-hanoi-move · level complessa · notes
Hanoi$(k,from,to,aux)$: if $k=0$ it does nothing; otherwise it runs Hanoi$(k-1,from,aux,to)$, moves disk $k$ from $from$ to $to$, and runs Hanoi$(k-1,aux,to,from)$. Hanoi$(4,1,3,2)$ is called (disks $1,\dots,4$ from the smallest, all on peg $1$). Which is move number $9$?
  1. disk $1$ from peg $3$ to peg $2$
  2. disk $1$ from peg $2$ to peg $3$
  3. disk $4$ from peg $1$ to peg $3$
  4. disk $2$ from peg $2$ to peg $1$
Explanation. The first moves are $1$: $1$ ($1\to2$), $2$: $2$ ($1\to3$), $3$: $1$ ($2\to3$), $4$: $3$ ($1\to2$), $5$: $1$ ($3\to1$), $6$: $2$ ($3\to2$), $7$: $1$ ($1\to2$), $8$: $4$ ($1\to3$), $9$: $1$ ($2\to3$), $10$: $2$ ($2\to1$). The call tree is followed in order: first the whole left subtree, then the move of the node, then the right subtree.
Example 2 · alg-g-hanoi-move · level complessa · notes
Hanoi$(k,from,to,aux)$: if $k=0$ it does nothing; otherwise it runs Hanoi$(k-1,from,aux,to)$, moves disk $k$ from $from$ to $to$, and runs Hanoi$(k-1,aux,to,from)$. Hanoi$(4,1,3,2)$ is called (disks $1,\dots,4$ from the smallest, all on peg $1$). Which is move number $12$?
  1. disk $1$ from peg $1$ to peg $2$
  2. disk $1$ from peg $3$ to peg $1$
  3. disk $3$ from peg $2$ to peg $3$
  4. disk $3$ from peg $3$ to peg $2$
Explanation. The first moves are $1$: $1$ ($1\to2$), $2$: $2$ ($1\to3$), $3$: $1$ ($2\to3$), $4$: $3$ ($1\to2$), $5$: $1$ ($3\to1$), $6$: $2$ ($3\to2$), $7$: $1$ ($1\to2$), $8$: $4$ ($1\to3$), $9$: $1$ ($2\to3$), $10$: $2$ ($2\to1$), $11$: $1$ ($3\to1$), $12$: $3$ ($2\to3$), $13$: $1$ ($1\to2$). The call tree is followed in order: first the whole left subtree, then the move of the node, then the right subtree.

15. Recurrences (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-rec-expansion · level media · notes
Let $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=3T(n/2)+n$ for $n\ge 2$ a power of $2$. What is $T(32)$?
  1. $908$
  2. $422$
  3. $665$
  4. $503$
Explanation. Expanding, $T(n)=a^{k}T(1)+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}a^j f(n/2^j)$ with $k=5$: $243+32+48+72+108+162=665$.
Example 2 · alg-g-rec-expansion · level media · notes
Let $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=2T(n/2)+n^2$ for $n\ge 2$ a power of $2$. What is $T(8)$?
  1. $104$
  2. $128$
  3. $112$
  4. $120$
Explanation. Expanding, $T(n)=a^{k}T(1)+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}a^j f(n/2^j)$ with $k=3$: $8+64+32+16=120$.
Example 1 · alg-g-rec-floor · level complessa · notes
Let $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+T(\lceil n/2\rceil)+n$ for $n\ge 2$ (the recurrence of MergeSort with unit constants). What is $T(17)$?
  1. $87$
  2. $85$
  3. $81$
  4. $102$
Explanation. We compute bottom-up the values that are needed: $T(2)=4,\ T(3)=8,\ T(4)=12,\ T(5)=17,\ T(8)=32,\ T(9)=38,\ T(17)=87$. The formula $n\log_2 n+n$ holds only for $n$ a power of $2$, and replacing $T(\lceil n/2\rceil)$ with $T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ gives $81$.
Example 2 · alg-g-rec-floor · level complessa · notes
Let $T(1)=1$ and $T(n)=T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)+T(\lceil n/2\rceil)+n$ for $n\ge 2$ (the recurrence of MergeSort with unit constants). What is $T(14)$?
  1. $56$
  2. $70$
  3. $68$
  4. $48$
Explanation. We compute bottom-up the values that are needed: $T(2)=4,\ T(3)=8,\ T(4)=12,\ T(7)=27,\ T(14)=68$. The formula $n\log_2 n+n$ holds only for $n$ a power of $2$, and replacing $T(\lceil n/2\rceil)$ with $T(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)$ gives $48$.

16. The Master Theorem (2)

Example 1 · alg-g-master · level facile · notes
Using the Master Theorem, what is the order of growth of the solution of $T(n)=2\,T(n/4)+n$?
  1. $T(n)=\Theta\big(\sqrt{n}\big)$
  2. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n\big)$
  3. $T(n)=\Theta\big(\sqrt{n}\,\log n\big)$
  4. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n\,\log n\big)$
Explanation. Compare $f(n)=n$ with $n^{\log_b a}=n^{\log_{4} 2}=\sqrt{n}$: $f$ is polynomially larger and the regularity condition holds (case 3), hence $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))$.
Example 2 · alg-g-master · level facile · notes
Using the Master Theorem, what is the order of growth of the solution of $T(n)=3\,T(n/3)+n^{3}$?
  1. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\,\log n\big)$
  2. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n\,\log n\big)$
  3. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\big)$
  4. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n\big)$
Explanation. Compare $f(n)=n^{3}$ with $n^{\log_b a}=n^{\log_{3} 3}=n$: $f$ is polynomially larger and the regularity condition holds (case 3), hence $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))$.
Example 1 · alg-g-master-log · level complessa · notes
With the Master Theorem, what is the order of growth of the solution of $T(n)=9\,T(n/3)+n^{3}\,\log^{2} n$ (logarithms in base $2$)?
  1. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{2}\,\log^{3} n\big)$
  2. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\,\log^{3} n\big)$
  3. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\big)$
  4. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\,\log^{2} n\big)$
Explanation. We compare $f(n)=n^{3}\,\log^{2} n$ with $n^{\log_b a}=n^{\log_{3} 9}=n^{2}$. $f$ is polynomially larger than $n^{2}$ and the regularity condition holds ($a\,f(n/b)\le\frac{9}{27}f(n)$), hence (case 3) $T(n)=\Theta(f(n))=\Theta(n^{3}\,\log^{2} n)$.
Example 2 · alg-g-master-log · level complessa · notes
With the Master Theorem, what is the order of growth of the solution of $T(n)=27\,T(n/3)+n^{2}$ (logarithms in base $2$)?
  1. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{2}\,\log n\big)$
  2. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\big)$
  3. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{3}\,\log n\big)$
  4. $T(n)=\Theta\big(n^{4}\big)$
Explanation. We compare $f(n)=n^{2}$ with $n^{\log_b a}=n^{\log_{3} 27}=n^{3}$. $f$ is polynomially smaller than $n^{3}$ (case 1): $T(n)=\Theta(n^{3})$.

17. Recursive algorithms (4)

Example 1 · alg-g-merge · level facile · notes
Merge$(a,l,m,r)$ copies the sorted halves into $L=a[l..m]$ and $R=a[m+1..r]$; while neither is exhausted it compares $L[i]\le R[j]$ and copies the smaller into $a$; then it copies the rest with no comparison. With $L=[5, 6, 7, 18]$ and $R=[4, 8, 9, 15]$, how many times does it execute the comparison $L[i]\le R[j]$?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $8$
  4. $7$
Explanation. Each iteration of the first while loop makes one comparison and copies one element; the loop ends when one of the two halves is exhausted, and the rest of the other is copied without comparisons: $7$ comparisons ($n_1+n_2-1=7$ is only the maximum).
Example 2 · alg-g-merge · level facile · notes
Merge$(a,l,m,r)$ copies the sorted halves into $L=a[l..m]$ and $R=a[m+1..r]$; while neither is exhausted it compares $L[i]\le R[j]$ and copies the smaller into $a$; then it copies the rest with no comparison. With $L=[3, 6, 9]$ and $R=[1, 4, 5, 20]$, how many times does it execute the comparison $L[i]\le R[j]$?
  1. $7$
  2. $5$
  3. $3$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Each iteration of the first while loop makes one comparison and copies one element; the loop ends when one of the two halves is exhausted, and the rest of the other is copied without comparisons: $6$ comparisons ($n_1+n_2-1=6$ is only the maximum).
Example 1 · alg-g-mergesort-total · level complessa · notes
MergeSort$(a,l,r)$, if $l<r$, recursively sorts $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ with $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ and merges them with Merge, which compares $L[i]\le R[j]$ until one of the two halves is exhausted. For MergeSort$(a,1,8)$ on $a=[5, 4, 6, 8, 10, 7, 20, 3]$, how many comparisons $L[i]\le R[j]$ are executed in total?
  1. $6$
  2. $15$
  3. $24$
  4. $17$
Explanation. There are $4$ merges of pairs, $2$ of quadruples and a final one, each counting the comparisons until one half is exhausted: $15$ in total (the final merge makes $6$). $17$ is the maximum possible ($4\cdot1+2\cdot3+7$).
Example 2 · alg-g-mergesort-total · level complessa · notes
MergeSort$(a,l,r)$, if $l<r$, recursively sorts $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ with $m=\lfloor(l+r)/2\rfloor$ and merges them with Merge, which compares $L[i]\le R[j]$ until one of the two halves is exhausted. For MergeSort$(a,1,8)$ on $a=[17, 11, 4, 3, 5, 18, 1, 10]$, how many comparisons $L[i]\le R[j]$ are executed in total?
  1. $7$
  2. $16$
  3. $17$
  4. $24$
Explanation. There are $4$ merges of pairs, $2$ of quadruples and a final one, each counting the comparisons until one half is exhausted: $16$ in total (the final merge makes $7$). $17$ is the maximum possible ($4\cdot1+2\cdot3+7$).
Example 1 · alg-g-kadane · level complessa · notes
Kadane$(a,n)$ solves the maximum subarray problem: it returns the triple $(bl,br,best)$, where $a[bl..br]$ is a (non-empty, contiguous) subarray of maximum sum and $best$ is its sum (in the proposed instances the maximum-sum subarray is unique). What does Kadane$(a,8)$ return on $a=[9, -3, -3, -8, -5, 1, 4, 3]$?
  1. $(bl,br,best)=(1,1,10)$
  2. $(bl,br,best)=(1,8,-2)$
  3. $(bl,br,best)=(1,1,9)$
  4. $(bl,br,best)=(1,8,17)$
Explanation. Kadane scans the array keeping $cur$, the maximum sum of a subarray ending at the previous position: at position $i$ it restarts from $a[i]$ if $cur<0$, otherwise it extends with $a[i]$. The maximum is $a[1..1]$ with sum $9$. The sum of the positive entries only ($17$) does not correspond to a contiguous subarray.
Example 2 · alg-g-kadane · level complessa · notes
Kadane$(a,n)$ solves the maximum subarray problem: it returns the triple $(bl,br,best)$, where $a[bl..br]$ is a (non-empty, contiguous) subarray of maximum sum and $best$ is its sum (in the proposed instances the maximum-sum subarray is unique). What does Kadane$(a,8)$ return on $a=[-2, 6, -4, -8, -2, 7, 6, 3]$?
  1. $(bl,br,best)=(6,8,16)$
  2. $(bl,br,best)=(2,8,22)$
  3. $(bl,br,best)=(2,8,8)$
  4. $(bl,br,best)=(1,8,6)$
Explanation. Kadane scans the array keeping $cur$, the maximum sum of a subarray ending at the previous position: at position $i$ it restarts from $a[i]$ if $cur<0$, otherwise it extends with $a[i]$. The maximum is $a[6..8]$ with sum $16$. The sum of the positive entries only ($22$) does not correspond to a contiguous subarray.
Example 1 · alg-g-recsum-depth · level facile · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$; otherwise, with $m=\lfloor (l+r)/2\rfloor$, it returns RecursiveSum$(a,l,m)+$RecursiveSum$(a,m+1,r)$. Called on an array of $n=116$ elements, what is the recursion depth, that is, the maximum number of calls active at the same time (the initial call included)?
  1. $8$
  2. $7$
  3. $9$
  4. $231$
Explanation. The call tree has height $\lceil \log_2 n\rceil=7$ (the larger half has $\lceil k/2\rceil$ elements), so the depth is $7+1=8$. $2n-1=231$ is the total number of calls.
Example 2 · alg-g-recsum-depth · level facile · notes
RecursiveSum$(a,l,r)$ returns $a[l]$ if $l=r$; otherwise, with $m=\lfloor (l+r)/2\rfloor$, it returns RecursiveSum$(a,l,m)+$RecursiveSum$(a,m+1,r)$. Called on an array of $n=79$ elements, what is the recursion depth, that is, the maximum number of calls active at the same time (the initial call included)?
  1. $7$
  2. $157$
  3. $8$
  4. $9$
Explanation. The call tree has height $\lceil \log_2 n\rceil=7$ (the larger half has $\lceil k/2\rceil$ elements), so the depth is $7+1=8$. $2n-1=157$ is the total number of calls.

18. Recursive versus iterative algorithms (3)

Example 1 · alg-g-fib-memo · level facile · notes
FibRec$(k)$ returns $k$ if $k\le 1$, otherwise FibRec$(k-1)+$FibRec$(k-2)$. FibMemo$(k,memo)$ does the same, but if $memo[k]$ is already defined it returns it with no further call, and otherwise it stores the result in $memo[k]$. How many calls (including the first one) do FibRec$(8)$ and FibMemo$(8,memo)$ execute, with $memo$ all undefined?
  1. FibRec: $67$, FibMemo: $15$
  2. FibRec: $67$, FibMemo: $9$
  3. FibRec: $67$, FibMemo: $17$
  4. FibRec: $21$, FibMemo: $15$
Explanation. FibRec: $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1=67$ (overlapping subproblems). FibMemo: each value is computed once, $M(n)=M(n-1)+2$ with $M(2)=3$, hence $2n-1=15$.
Example 2 · alg-g-fib-memo · level facile · notes
FibRec$(k)$ returns $k$ if $k\le 1$, otherwise FibRec$(k-1)+$FibRec$(k-2)$. FibMemo$(k,memo)$ does the same, but if $memo[k]$ is already defined it returns it with no further call, and otherwise it stores the result in $memo[k]$. How many calls (including the first one) do FibRec$(7)$ and FibMemo$(7,memo)$ execute, with $memo$ all undefined?
  1. FibRec: $41$, FibMemo: $8$
  2. FibRec: $13$, FibMemo: $13$
  3. FibRec: $41$, FibMemo: $13$
  4. FibRec: $41$, FibMemo: $15$
Explanation. FibRec: $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1=41$ (overlapping subproblems). FibMemo: each value is computed once, $M(n)=M(n-1)+2$ with $M(2)=3$, hence $2n-1=13$.
Example 1 · alg-g-bottomup-pass · level complessa · notes
MergeSortBottomUp$(a,n)$ (below; Merge$(a,l,m,r)$ merges the sorted subarrays $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ into the sorted $a[l..r]$) is executed on $a=[3, 25, 30, 13, 26, 27, 10]$, with $n=7$. What does $a$ contain at the end of the pass with $width=2$? <pre>width := 1 <b>while</b> width &lt; n <b>do</b> l := 1 <b>while</b> l + width ≤ n <b>do</b> m := l + width − 1 r := min(l + 2·width − 1, n) Merge(a, l, m, r) l ← l + 2·width <b>end</b> width ← 2·width <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $[3, 13, 25, 30, 10, 26, 27]$
  2. $[3, 13, 25, 30, 26, 27, 10]$
  3. $[3, 10, 13, 25, 26, 27, 30]$
  4. $[3, 25, 13, 30, 26, 27, 10]$
Explanation. The pass with $width=1$ merges the pairs $a[1..2]$, $a[3..4]$, $a[5..6]$ ($a[7]$ stays alone): $[3, 25, 13, 30, 26, 27, 10]$. The pass with $width=2$ merges $a[1..2]$ with $a[3..4]$ and $a[5..6]$ with $a[7..7]$ (incomplete block, with $r=\min(8,7)=7$): $[3, 13, 25, 30, 10, 26, 27]$.
Example 2 · alg-g-bottomup-pass · level complessa · notes
MergeSortBottomUp$(a,n)$ (below; Merge$(a,l,m,r)$ merges the sorted subarrays $a[l..m]$ and $a[m+1..r]$ into the sorted $a[l..r]$) is executed on $a=[13, 5, 4, 30, 28, 7, 26]$, with $n=7$. What does $a$ contain at the end of the pass with $width=2$? <pre>width := 1 <b>while</b> width &lt; n <b>do</b> l := 1 <b>while</b> l + width ≤ n <b>do</b> m := l + width − 1 r := min(l + 2·width − 1, n) Merge(a, l, m, r) l ← l + 2·width <b>end</b> width ← 2·width <b>end</b></pre>
  1. $[4, 5, 13, 30, 7, 26, 28]$
  2. $[4, 5, 13, 30, 7, 28, 26]$
  3. $[4, 5, 7, 13, 26, 28, 30]$
  4. $[5, 13, 4, 30, 7, 28, 26]$
Explanation. The pass with $width=1$ merges the pairs $a[1..2]$, $a[3..4]$, $a[5..6]$ ($a[7]$ stays alone): $[5, 13, 4, 30, 7, 28, 26]$. The pass with $width=2$ merges $a[1..2]$ with $a[3..4]$ and $a[5..6]$ with $a[7..7]$ (incomplete block, with $r=\min(8,7)=7$): $[4, 5, 13, 30, 7, 26, 28]$.
Example 1 · alg-g-fib-calls · level facile · notes
FibRec$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n \le 1$, otherwise FibRec$(n-1)+$FibRec$(n-2)$. How many calls (the initial call included) does FibRec$(10)$ make?
  1. $177$
  2. $89$
  3. $55$
  4. $1023$
Explanation. The calls satisfy $C(0)=C(1)=1$ and $C(n)=1+C(n-1)+C(n-2)$, hence $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1=2 \cdot 89-1=177$: they grow as $\varphi^n$, not as $2^n$.
Example 2 · alg-g-fib-calls · level facile · notes
FibRec$(n)$ returns $n$ if $n \le 1$, otherwise FibRec$(n-1)+$FibRec$(n-2)$. How many calls (the initial call included) does FibRec$(8)$ make?
  1. $34$
  2. $255$
  3. $67$
  4. $21$
Explanation. The calls satisfy $C(0)=C(1)=1$ and $C(n)=1+C(n-1)+C(n-2)$, hence $C(n)=2F(n+1)-1=2 \cdot 34-1=67$: they grow as $\varphi^n$, not as $2^n$.
